# The Practice of Music in both types of song <note>original: "vtriusqꝫ cātus" - referring to both plainchant (cantus planus) and polyphony (cantus mensurabilis)</note> by the excellent Franchino Gaffurio of Lodi. In four books, most harmoniously arranged: and most recently printed with the highest diligence.

<meta>The catchword "lit" at the bottom of the page suggests the next page begins with the word "literas" or "literarum," continuing the thought on how music aids other studies.</meta>

# PREFACE

->To the most illustrious and excellent Prince and Lord, Lord Ludovico Maria Sforza<-
->the most invincible Duke of the Milanese. Franchino Gaffurio, professor of music,<-
->sends greetings.<-

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'Q' containing a circular medallion with a classical profile portrait of a man's head facing left.</image-desc>How great the profession of the musical art was among the ancients, most illustrious Prince—not only in authority but even in veneration—we are easily taught. This is shown by the example of the greatest philosophers who, as very old men, applied themselves to this discipline as if to place the final touch <note>original: "summam manum," literally "the highest hand" or finishing stroke</note> upon their studies. It is also shown by the institutions of the strictest republics which, though they took great care to excise whatever might publicly harm morals, nevertheless not only did not cast out this art, but even cultivated it with the greatest zeal as the parent and nourisher of character. To conclude, it is confirmed by the steady and consenting judgment of all peoples and nations, among whom nothing was ever celebrated with greater care. For what other discipline has been received with such great assent by mortals and such great harmony of every age and sex, that no person of any condition has yet been found who does not strive to console his troubles with at least some rude melody? Indeed, nothing else is so capable of leading minds to the most diverse emotions in a brief moment; this is proven not only by the testimony of the best men, but by "hidden faith" <note>original: "occultata fide," a common legal or philosophical expression for self-evident truth that doesn't require external proof</note> as they say, so much so that antiquity did not hesitate to call its professors not only Musicians, but also prophets and wise men. To this is added the authority of the poets, who feigned that Linus and Orpheus, born of gods, led forests and rocks because they reduced the hard and rustic life of certain men to a milder culture. But lest perhaps false faith be given to the words of poets—who, in their familiar style, extol everything to the highest degree—there have also been found professors of wisdom who did not hesitate to assert that not only sensible things, but even those which live without sense in any way, are soothed by musical modulations. And for this reason, they have judged that the most eminent author Virgil did not speak with poetic hyperbole in his *Bucolics* when he said that the peaks of the hard oaks moved to the song of Silenus. Indeed, if we believe Plato, who said that the soul of the world consists of musical modulation, I truly do not see why it can be doubted that other things living with any kind of soul—which it is clear was given to them from heaven—are affected and gladdened by things congruent to their nature, since it is already clearly established that "likeness is a friend to itself." For these reasons, most illustrious Prince, and also being impelled by nature (which we do not easily resist), I applied my mind to this discipline from my early youth, especially since it contributes to morals (as pleases the most skilled) and did not abhor my profession. Following the long-accepted opinion of religious men that this is the most important duty—that my studies should not benefit me alone—I published a book following the principles of the most ancient writers concerning the speculation of this art, which the Greeks call <term>Theorica</term> <note>The theoretical or contemplative branch of music, as opposed to practical performance.</note>, so that the reasoning of my studies might be established and the fruit of my labors more widely scattered. This work, renowned Prince, I dedicated to your name—steadfastly yet timidly, lest I should interrupt you with untimely trifles while you are occupied with the gravest cares and the weight of the greatest affairs. However, after I perceived with what favor and kindness you followed our little work (otherwise rude and uncultured), that liberality added a "spur to the running horse" <note>A classical idiom meaning to encourage someone who is already working hard.</note>, and my zeal was ignited in no small measure to approach the other part of this discipline, now secure in your favor. For Music, unlike the other disciplines of <term>Mathesis</term> <note>The mathematical sciences (Arithmetic, Geometry, Music, and Astronomy).</note>, does not only occupy itself with speculation, but issues into action and is joined to morality, as was said before. Nor did I seem to have sufficiently fulfilled my duty if I remained only in inquiry—judging that to look to the utility of only a few—unless our industry also toiled for the public benefit. For this part of music is not only useful for its own knowledge, but having pushed its roots further, it also assists the rest of [one's] studies; which is proved by the testimony of the most serious men, who [claimed] they [learned] nothing else from Music than lit...

<summary>Franchino Gaffurio dedicates his work to Ludovico Sforza, arguing for the moral and philosophical importance of music. He explains that after publishing a book on music theory, he is now moving into the practical application of the art.</summary>
<keywords>Franchino Gaffurio, Ludovico Sforza, music theory, music education, Plato, Orpheus, Virgil, Milan, Renaissance music, morality</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter, specifically the author's dedication to his patron and a laudatory poem by a contemporary scholar.</meta>

# PREFACE

<margin>Fabius Quintilianus <note>A celebrated Roman educator and rhetorician (c. 35–100 AD).</note></margin>
...they have professed to have learned letters. For Fabius Quintilianus, on the authority of Timagenes <note>A Greek historian and teacher of rhetoric in Rome.</note>, confirms that this art <note>Music</note> is the most ancient of all literary studies. However, when I speak of Music, I do not mean this theatrical and effeminate kind which corrupts public morals rather than shaping them; but rather that modest and manly music celebrated by the ancient heroes. This was the music brought to the tables of kings and the feasts of the wealthy, where, as the guests reclined, the lyre was passed around and sang of the extraordinary deeds of the most famous men. This was surely the greatest incentive toward the pursuit of virtue. Indeed, this art is raised even higher: it inserts itself into the very heavens, and by the testimony of the most celebrated poets, it describes the labors of the sun, the wandering Moon, and the Titanic stars. As if not content to have filled the spaces of the earth with its merits, it penetrates the heavens and joins with the mysteries of divine things.

Therefore, most clear-sighted Prince, I have dedicated this work—born near me under your leadership and auspices, and now printed in your name—with the veneration I owe you, to use the words of Pliny the Elder <note>original: "Plinii secundi"</note>. For to whom could I more suitably refer my labors than to him to whom not only all Italy, but even the most remote nations, are accustomed to refer the judgment and arbitration not only of their plans but also of their deeds, as if to an honorary judge? Nor did I fear that this little gift of ours would come as a burden to you while you are occupied with such great affairs; for I know you are accustomed to managing the greatest matters in such a way that you do not despise even the smallest, and by the felicity of your genius, you arrange all things so that they are carried out in an undisturbed course. Although, those things which look toward the advancement of studies should not be considered among the smallest matters to you. Witness to this are the illustrious gymnasiums of disciplines of every kind, but especially of Greek and Latin eloquence, which have been established by your great care throughout the cities of your empire. For who else of your strength and fortune encourages, protects, and cherishes the talents of his people with a greater command toward virtue?

As for what concerns me, I do not demand that my writings be taken in comparison with great authors, nor that they be granted the same authority as established writers; if there is anything of good in our work, I most willingly credit it to my learning. It will be enough for me if I merely strive. I wish for our industry to be commended only so far as I may be said to have provided for the progress of students with a harmonious and concise brevity; so that those things which would have to be sought scattered through the volumes of many authors may be found here collected in one work in a convenient order of subjects. For we have labored so that all matters, beginning from their first principles, might be led through a series to the conclusion, lest the reader be led around in confusion in an otherwise difficult art, and suffer more from the ineptitude of the writer than from the obscurity of the subject. We take only this for ourselves: we believe our industry should seek commendation from this point.

Therefore, most humane Prince, receive our little gift with the clemency you usually show in other matters, and allow it to be supported at least by the preface of your name. Thus it will deserve to be received; it cannot provide commendation to itself by its own merit, but it will follow your dedication. Since we otherwise credit all the goods of our fortune or genius to you, so whatever we have achieved through study and industry, we wish to be dedicated and consecrated to your name for all time.

---

## ¶ A Poem by Lucinus Conagus.

> ¶ It is a joy, and I would wish to render you the praises you deserve,
> But who, Ludovico <note>Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan (1452–1508).</note>, can extol you according to your merits?
> You give us golden and most happy ages, O Prince;
> No glory of leaders has ever been so great.
> And though I remain silent on the other monuments of your deeds,
> Which surpass the conversation and belief of men,
> When did the arts ever flourish so much in any age,
> Or when was Pallas <note>Athena/Minerva, goddess of wisdom.</note> so friendly to genius?
> We see followers of Isaeus, Euclid, and Pythagoras;
> Children of Apollo <note>original: "Phoebigenas"</note>, and those who seek the high stars.
> Nor are you lacking in followers of Chrysippus or learned Solons,
> And your age gives you the hands of Parrhasius <note>A famous painter of ancient Greece.</note>.
> Through you, Music has come forth, torn from the Stygian abode <note>The underworld; a reference to the myth of Orpheus.</note>,
> Nor has she brought only well-known melodies.
> Behold, Franchinus <note>Franchinus Gaffurius (1451–1522), the author of this treatise.</note> brings forth his riches, his gifts
> From the Muses <note>original: "Pieria"</note>; no one flourishes more brightly in his art.
> Seek these, reader: you will see nothing that is not laudable.
> From here you learn to practice, and from there to speak.
> Whatever you may do, you can learn to sing from this book,
> And you will be able to produce every sound correctly.
> But if the author of this gift is pleasing to you,
> Offer up pious prayers to the Lord God on his behalf.

->**T H E  E N D.**<-

A iii <note>Printer's signature mark.</note>

The page concludes with a laudatory poem by Lucinus Conagus praising the Duke's patronage of the arts and Gaffurius's musical mastery.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of Book One of Franchinus Gaffurius's *Practica Musicae* (1496). Gaffurius was a central figure in Renaissance music theory, serving as choirmaster at Milan Cathedral.</meta>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

# THE FIRST BOOK OF THE MUSIC IN PRACTICE
# BY FRANCHINUS GAFFURIUS OF LODI.

## ¶ On the necessary introduction to the practice of Music. Chapter I.

<image-desc>A large historiated drop cap 'E' featuring a seated figure (likely King David or a classical musician) playing a harp within a vaulted chamber or architectural frame.</image-desc>

<margin>
Orpheus
Amphion
Linus
Arion
Timotheus
Aristoxenus
The Philosopher
Tullius
</margin>
Although many have pursued the science of harmony <note>original: "harmoniam scientiam"</note> through theory alone—which is the concern of the theorist—more extensively than those who have practiced it, it is believed that no science could have reached such a great use of harmony without actual practice. For why should I mention those most excellent ancients who first wrote on theory? For posterity has celebrated Orpheus, Amphion, Linus of Thebes, Arion, and Timotheus, and the rest, because by their singing (by their practice, I say) one charmed wild beasts, another rocks and forests, another the creatures of the water, and others soothed rustic and unrefined minds. It is well known that these men became most famous both through the principles of the discipline itself and through its actual performance. Nor would I rashly bring forward the Pythagoreans, the Platonists, and the Peripatetics, by whose command the use of both the natural and the artistic voice is greatly commended for the instruction of youth. This was asserted for the following reason: since the musician and philosopher Aristoxenus—as Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> testifies in the first book of the *Tusculan Disputations*—affirmed that just as a certain tension of the body exists in song and on strings, which is called harmony, so from the nature and shape of the whole body various modes are produced like sounds in song. There are also those who have posited that powers are useless unless they are reduced to acts; for which reason they feel that the exercise of the melodic voice has contributed most to the consideration of harmony—not that it adds a varied multitude to it, but rather brings it to its very perfection. Therefore, the practice of music is the movement of sounds producing consonances and melody. Indeed, we gather these sounds in vain through reason and science unless they have been grasped by practice itself. Hence, it is necessary for their tensions and relaxations <note>pitch height and depth</note> and consonances to become known not only to the mind and through ratios, but through the habit of hearing and vocalizing.

<margin>
How Music differs from Grammar
</margin>
But I will not deny that this very practice of music is greatly at odds with Grammar. For in Grammar, when a short or long syllable must be used, we do so entirely by the authority of those who preceded us. However, it is necessary for the Musician to serve the rational measurement of voices <note>pitches/sounds</note>, and he must not pronounce this or that syllable before he knows he is permitted to do so by the measure of the voice and time; and although this is described in the first and second parts of this work:

<margin>
Augustine
Four types of musicians.
</margin>
nevertheless, the divine Augustine is known to have proved this with effective arguments in the second book of his *On Music*. Truly, four types of sonorous voices follow.

<margin>
Orators
Readers
Ambrosians
Gregorians
</margin>
The first type consists of those who deal with prose, expressing their thought through words rather than melody, such as Orators and readers, and those who even in divine services modulate antiphons and psalms; which (though improperly) our Ambrosian and Gregorian clerics call "plainchant" <note>original: "cantum planum"</note>. This is because they pronounce individual notes simply and "plainly" with an equal measure of short time. For it is not known to preserve the nature of concord <note>polyphony or complex harmony</note>, but because the limits and boundaries of their certain modes (which they also call tones) and the meetings and transitions of voices are observed according to the natural arrangement of the diatonic genus <note>the standard scale of whole and half steps</note>, the beginnings of the songs and of the entire modulation in...

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "ter" (likely "terminantur" or "tertio").</meta>

<summary>Gaffurius argues that musical theory is incomplete without physical practice and performance, citing ancient authorities like Orpheus and Cicero. He begins to categorize types of vocal music, distinguishing plainchant from other forms of musical expression.</summary>
<keywords>music theory vs practice, Orpheus, Aristoxenus, Cicero, St. Augustine, plainchant, Ambrosian chant, Gregorian chant, diatonic genus</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the practice of music, moving from the philosophical origins discussed on page 3 to the specific classification of performers and the foundational system of Guido of Arezzo.</meta>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

is for the most part proved by the act itself. Thus, I call that "sound-bearing reading"—as if it were a reading sustaining sounds or substituted for the sounds themselves—plainchant.
<margin>Aristotle</margin>
Furthermore, as Aristotle asserts in the twenty-eighth problem of the section on harmonics: the first observations of such voices, which they placed before the instruction of youth in music, they called "laws" <note>original: "leges", referring to the Greek "nomoi" or melodic formulas</note>. The second type is of those who pronounce not only the concept of the mind, but also the short and long syllables themselves (which belongs to the poets) by metric consideration; regarding this, we shall speak with more pleasing freedom in the first part of the second book. The third type is of those who, through the mutual resonance of one or the other, produce a melody and sweet song with certain dimensions of intervals; this is fully noted in the third volume of the present work. These he calls musicians and singers in the proper sense.
<margin>Who are the musicians and singers</margin>
The fourth type is usually dedicated to the theatrical and mimetic arts, and to those who are moved by gestures of the body to imitate the voice, such as in dances and choral rounds;
<margin>Theophrastus, Guido</margin>
to these, Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle</note> ascribed "music" as proven in both voice and bodily movement. But since this obscenity is not suited to our ceremonies, we turn our soul toward those things which concern divine praises. It is well established, therefore, that Guido of Arezzo <note>the 11th-century monk credited with inventing modern musical notation</note> described the introduction to this fruitful music using seven letters and six syllables to name all the strings <note>the notes of the scale</note>, adorned after the likeness of the fifteen strings of the natural and perfect <term>diatonic system</term> <note>the standard arrangement of whole and half steps in Western music</note>. Indeed, it contributes much to the action of the human voice to imitate the harmonious strings struck on a lyre, kithara, or <term>monochord</term> <note>a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals</note>; 
<margin>The Greeks</margin>
some of these the Greeks called "low" <note>original: "graves"</note>, others "high" <note>original: "acutas"</note>, and the rest "middle" <note>original: "medias"</note>. In truth, our churchmen distinguish this kind of tradition from Guido—which they call "the Hand"—into the low, the high, and the very high <note>original: "superacutum"</note>. Thus, there are twenty-two strings <note>notes</note> inscribed alternately on lines and in the intervals or spaces; namely, counting two joined strings for the sake of imitating the <term>synemmenon tetrachord</term> <note>original: "sinemenon", a specific grouping of four notes in the Greek system that allowed for the "soft" or flatted B</note>. They call the first eight "low," as if they were closest to silence and stillness. The eight placed above these they call "high," and the remaining six "very high." Yet, even within those eight low strings, there is a certain "highness," and "lowness" exists among the high strings. From this, it follows that a consonance leaps forth from each grouping of them; Boethius <note>an influential Roman philosopher and music theorist</note> used to define this as a mixture of high and low sound reaching the ears sweetly and uniformly. Therefore, the order of the voices, led through the lines and spaces by seven letters, is taken up as follows: for in the lowest place of the introduction, which they mark with a certain line, they inscribed the Greek letter Γ <note>Gamma</note>, and from that, the syllable *ut*.
<margin>Interval</margin>
In the neighboring space, they placed the letter A and the syllable *re*. On the second line following upward toward the high, the letter ♮ <note>B-quadratum or "hard" B</note>, joined conversely from a doubled Γ, and the syllable *mi*. But in the following interval upward, they placed the letter C and the syllable *fa*. For I understand an "interval" or "space" to be the empty margin lying between two lines that are contiguous in pitch. And with a similar progression, they marked the letters themselves and the seven syllables of the <term>hexachords</term> <note>original: "exacordorum", a six-note scale system</note> upon the strings. But this place would require many more things about these matters; since they have been clearly explained in the fifth book of the *Theorica*, we have decided to pass over them in silence for the sake of brevity. Let the diatonic introduction of Guido, described with letters and syllables through lines and spaces, be arranged according to this diagram <note>original: "parigraphia"</note>.

<sig>A iiii</sig>

<summary>Gaffurius categorizes performers into four types and introduces the Guidonian system of musical notation, explaining the arrangement of the "Hand" and the use of Greek-inspired pitch classifications.</summary>

<note>The solmization syllables (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) are inscribed within seven vertical columns (pipes) representing the overlapping hexachord system. The leftmost pipe corresponds to the lowest pitch (Gamma ut), and the rightmost pipes correspond to the highest pitches. This "Guidonian Hand" logic allowed singers to navigate the scale by shifting between these interlocking six-note patterns.</note>

<term>Diatonicum</term> — The diatonic genus of music, which uses a specific arrangement of whole tones and semitones.
<term>Guidonis</term> — Referring to Guido of Arezzo, the inventor of the "ut-re-mi" system.
<term>Pythagoras</term> — The Greek philosopher who discovered that musical intervals correspond to mathematical ratios (e.g., 2:1 for an octave).
<term>Tetrachord</term> — A scale of four notes used in ancient Greek music theory; the building blocks of this diagram.
<term>Hexachord</term> — A six-note scale (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) used in the Middle Ages to teach sight-singing.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical fundamentals, transitioning from the general history of musical notation to the specific Guidonian system of hexachords used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</meta>

->**BOOK ONE**<-

### On the Names of the Sound Syllables and Their Distances. Chapter II.

<margin>Gregory
Virgil
Guido</margin>
The authority of the poet Maro <note>A reference to Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> celebrated the seven essential strings described by Gregory with seven letters in this verse from the sixth book of the *Aeneid*: "There too the Thracian priest <note>Referring to Orpheus.</note> in his long robe... matches their measures with the seven distinctions of voices." <note>original: "Necnō traycius longa cum ueste sacerdos. Obloquitur numeris septem discrimina uocum."</note> From this, Guido, the founder of the system, completed his introductory work by combining seven <term>hexachords</term> (groups of six notes). A hexachord is the encompassing of six strings arranged in a <term>diatonic</term> (step-by-step) scale, the names of which are: **ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la**. Indeed, **ut** and **re** are low (as I agree with the Greeks); **sol** and **la** are high; and **mi** and **fa** are in the middle. However, the custom of the Church is to call **ut** and **re** the "low" notes, **mi** and **fa** the "high" notes, and **sol** and **la** the "super-high" notes. Guido himself arranged these so that he defined each interval of the perfect and diatonic system using only two such syllables. For between **ut** and **re**, the interval of a <term>tone</term> is enclosed by a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>A mathematical ratio of 9:8, used to define the distance of a whole step.</note> dimension. Between **re** and **mi** it is the same. But between **mi** and **fa** there is the distance of a minor <term>semitone</term> (half-step). Between **fa** and **sol**, and likewise between **sol** and **la**, a whole tone is extended. For this reason, **re** is higher than **ut** by a tone, and likewise **ut** is lower than **re** by a tone. **Re** is surpassed in height by **mi** by a tone, and **re** lies below it by a tone in depth. **Fa** is lower than **sol** by a tone, and **sol** exceeds **fa** in height by a tone. **La** is surpassed by **sol** in depth by the interval of a tone, but **la** exceeds **sol** in height by the same <term>epogdoan</term> <note>Another term for the 9:8 ratio or whole tone.</note> distance. Truly, **fa** is known to be higher than **mi** only by the space of a minor semitone; therefore, by that same interval, **mi** must be lower than **fa**. It is established, therefore, in each hexachord, that four tones—notated by different syllables—enclose a natural minor semitone in the middle position, which must be considered a result of necessity. For when you wish to consider the three forms of the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note> according to the variety of <term>tetrachords</term> (four-note scales), you could never find them diatonically within a single hexachord unless those four tones enclosed the semitone in the middle. This is something anyone can easily discover for themselves through what we are doing. And for that reason, in a perfectly arranged diatonic system, the individual natural semitones are recognized to fall between those two syllables, **mi** and **fa**; such was Guido's great care in the arrangement of the seven combined hexachords.

<margin>In praise of Guido</margin>
For he joined the beginning of each hexachord either to the first tetrachord of the preceding hexachord, or established it as separated from it by the interval of a tone. From this arrangement, in the <term>heptachord</term> (the seven-note scale), the first tetrachords of two hexachords are verified. But where the first tetrachord of the second hexachord ends, the third hexachord—called "**soft B**" <note>The "b molle" hexachord, which introduces the B-flat to avoid dissonant intervals.</note> (which can also be called "conjunct")—takes its beginning, added above so that the harshness of the <term>tritone</term> <note>An unstable, dissonant interval of three whole tones.</note> might become sweeter in melody, and so that the composition of certain tones can proceed through varied types of combined and acquired harmonies. The fourth hexachord, however, is separated from the first tetrachord of the second hexachord by the interval of a tone in the high direction. The fifth hexachord is joined to the first tetrachord of the fourth hexachord. Furthermore, the sixth hexachord—which can be called <term>synemmenon</term> <note>A Greek term for "connected" or "conjunct."</note> or conjunct—is connected to the first tetrachord of this fifth hexachord. The seventh hexachord is separated from the first tetrachord of the fifth hexachord by the distance of a tone in the high direction.

<margin>What a tone is</margin>
A **tone** is the legitimate space of a <term>sesquioctave</term> dimension circumscribed by two sounds. By a single division, it contains two nearby parts: one smaller, the other larger. They call the larger part the <term>apotome</term> (major semitone) and the smaller part the **minor semitone**. When the minor semitone is subtracted from the tone itself, the apotome is left behind, through which a discordant transition proceeds very with great difficulty in the voice. It is different with the minor semitone; for it provides a concordant and sweet temperament to those tones and harmonies, as is most notably seen in instrumental harmony.

<summary>This page explains the structure of the Guidonian hexachord system, defining the syllables (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la) and the mathematical intervals between them. It emphasizes the importance of the mi-fa semitone and explains how overlapping hexachords allow for melodic variety and the avoidance of harsh dissonances like the tritone.</summary>
<keywords>Guidonian hexachord, ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la, minor semitone, sesquioctave, apotome, diatessaron, soft B, music theory, Virgil, Pope Gregory</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals, specifically defining the semitone as a mathematical and auditory boundary, before transitioning into a detailed explanation of the visual shapes of musical notation, including simple notes and complex ligatures.</meta>

->**BOOK ONE**<-

...I shall prove. Since it is especially in the diatonic scale that it is naturally recognized to be placed before, after, or between two whole tones. 
<margin>
What a semitone is
What an interval is
</margin>
From this, the semitone itself may be defined as an interval which, when added to two <term>sesquioctaves</term> <note>A 9:8 ratio, the mathematical definition of a whole tone.</note>, results in a <term>sesquitertial</term> <note>A 4:3 ratio, the mathematical definition of a perfect fourth.</note> proportion; or it is the space which, when joined with two tones, completes the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth.</note> consonance between its highest and lowest sounding points. An interval or space, however, can be understood in this way as the distance between a high and a low sound. The sounding concepts of the mind are expressed by specific <term>notes</term> <note>original: "notulae"</note>; the extension, intensity, and release of these notes, arranged across a variety of lines and spaces, must be followed by the voice itself, and for this reason the notes themselves are called "signs of the voice." Because voices [sounds] cannot be written down, they are committed to memory through practice and exercise so they do not perish, for their pronunciation flows away into time past. Therefore, notes are described in three ways: simple, composite, and intermediate. A simple note is one that is not joined to another note; it is shaped with a square body in this way: [Image: a single black square musical note]. Sometimes it is also written with a small rod [a stem] descending on its right side, in the manner of a <term>longa</term> in <term>mensurable music</term> <note>Music with a specific, measured rhythmic value, as opposed to plainchant.</note>, as seen here: [Image: a square musical note with a vertical tail descending from the right side].

A composite note is one that is joined to another note, which then follows a different visual description. For when a <term>ligature</term> <note>A group of notes written as a single connected symbol.</note> ascends, having the second note higher, the first requires no stem on its side, as here:
[Image: A musical ligature consisting of two square notes in an ascending pattern.]

However, when a ligature descends, having the second note lower, a descending stem is assigned to the first on the left side, whether it is an oblique (diagonal) or square shape, in this way:
[Image: A set of musical ligatures showing downward movement, including a pair of descending square notes, an oblique (diagonal) form with a tail on the left, and two squares with a tail on the left.]

The authority of the ancients established that this consistency at the start of ligatures should be called <term>propriety</term> <note>A technical term in medieval notation describing the rhythmic value of the opening notes of a ligature.</note>. Propriety, therefore, is the appropriate description for the beginnings of ligatures as established by musical authors. 
<margin>
The Philosopher
</margin>
To the final joining of notes, they assigned <term>perfection</term> <note>A technical term describing the rhythmic value of the final note in a ligature.</note>, since according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle, who famously argued that the "perfect" or complete state of a thing is found in its end or purpose.</note> the perfection of all things is attributed to their end. This is denoted in three ways. Either the final note of the ligature is written with a square body below the second-to-last, as here:
[Image: A musical ligature with two square notes, the second placed directly beneath the first.]

Or it is placed directly above the second-to-last, as here:
[Image: A musical ligature with three square notes, the third placed directly above the second.]

¶ Or it is placed above the second-to-last in a direct step, shaped as a square with a descending stem on the right side, in this way:
[Image: A musical ligature with three square notes in an ascending step pattern with a descending tail on the right of the final note.]

¶ Therefore, "perfection" is the shaping suitable for the final notes of ligatures. Those who disagree with these positions depart from the authority of antiquity. Thus, those who write the last note of a ligature with an oblique body, like this: [Image: a single oblique (diagonal) musical note], are led into error. However, only two notes are permitted within a single oblique body, like this:
[Image: A musical ligature consisting of two oblique notes joined together.]

¶ Indeed, musical practice dreads an ascending oblique body, like this: [Image: an oblique musical note rising to the right].

<summary>This page defines the semitone and the mathematical nature of musical intervals before detailing the rules for "ligatures"—connected note symbols. It explains the concepts of "propriety" and "perfection" which govern how the beginning and end of these note groups are drawn.</summary>
<keywords>semitone, musical intervals, notation, ligatures, propriety, perfection, Aristotle, mensurable music, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of musical notation, transitioning from the shapes of individual notes to the rules governing "ligatures" (grouped notes) and the introduction of "clefs" (keys) used to determine pitch on a staff.</meta>

->**BOOK ONE**<-

¶ For the middle notes of <term>ligatures</term> <note>Groups of notes bound together in a single pen stroke, usually sung to one syllable.</note>—namely, those that are joined between the outer notes—no diversity of shape is assigned; for they are shaped either as simple squares or as oblique [diagonal] forms in this manner:

[A musical fragment on a four-line staff showing four square black notes joined by a horizontal bar. A second fragment shows two sets of descending thick diagonal ligatures.]

Furthermore, every ligature, no matter how many notes it contains, takes only a single syllable to be pronounced. Therefore, all musical notes of this progression, even if they are depicted with different shapes, should be pronounced with an equal measure of time. An intermediate note is that which takes the form of neither the simple nor the composite figures; rather, in its simplicity, it is compared by a certain similarity to an "oblique body." It is not written alone; instead, at least two or more are usually noted down in the form of a mensurable <term>semibreve</term> <note>Literally "half-brief," a diamond-shaped note value.</note> in this way:

[Musical notation on a four-line staff showing two diamond-shaped notes (semibreves) positioned vertically apart.]

<margin>Persius</margin>
¶ For the rest, they are equal in pronunciation and measure of time: although some measure them twice as strictly as the others; we believe this should be granted not by reason, but by the judgment of the singer. For many have wished to follow their own authority in the pronunciation and measure of plainchant notes. Hence the saying of <margin>Persius</margin> is not out of place: "To each their own will, nor is life lived by a single prayer." <note>original: "Velle suum cuique est: nec voto vivitur uno." From the Roman satirist Persius, Satires 5.53.</note> There are also those who describe and measure these notes of plainchant equally using the shapes of <term>mensurable</term> <note>Music with a specific, mathematically defined rhythm, as opposed to the more fluid rhythm of plainchant.</note> notation, such as <term>longs</term>, <term>breves</term>, and <term>semibreves</term>: as is evident in the <term>Cardinal's Creed</term> <note>original: "Symbolo cardineo"; likely referring to a specific setting of the Nicene Creed.</note> and in certain <term>proses</term> and hymns; the French especially follow this most famously, so that the ornamentation and pronunciation of the melodies are understood through that very diversity.

### ¶ On clefs and the pronunciation of notes. Chapter III.

<margin>
Musicians
Aristotle
</margin>
After this, it is necessary to reveal by which signs the names of the notes—as well as their low pitches, high pitches, and extensions—are perceived through lines and spaces. For every letter or "seat" placed in a linear or spatial location in the introduction is called a <term>clef</term> <note>original: "clavis," meaning "key."</note>; because all notes are "enclosed" <note>The author is making a play on words between "clavis" (key/clef) and "clauduntur" (enclosed or shut in).</note> by these very lines or spaces. But it is clear that it was not necessary to declare all lines and spaces with their own letters and clefs, specifically to avoid the mutual cluttering of adjacent descriptions. Therefore, <margin>Musicians</margin> have judged it appropriate to reveal all the "strings" of the introduction with fewer signs. For as <margin>Aristotle</margin> pleases, that which can be done with fewer things is done in vain with more. <note>A reference to the principle of parsimony, later known as Occam's Razor.</note> They are accustomed to distinguish the low, high, and super-high strings of the introduction with two signs called clefs. And the first sign (following the origins of the ancients) is a single line among four or five filled with a red color; when it holds a linear place in the introduction, it demonstrates low <term>F-fa-ut</term> <note>The F-clef.</note>; if it is described in a spatial location, it declares high <term>F-fa-ut</term>. The second sign is a single line among four or five filled with a <term>glaucous</term> <note>original: "glauco," a sea-green or bluish-grey color.</note> color; when this holds a linear position, they assign it as <term>C-sol-fa-ut</term> <note>The C-clef.</note>; but if it is described in a spatial location, it will indicate either low <term>C-fa-ut</term> or super-high <term>C-sol-fa</term>, which is also frequent in <term>Ambrosian</term> <note>Relating to the chant tradition of Milan, distinct from the Gregorian tradition.</note> notations. Add to this that the ancients observed a single string marked with a celestial [blue] color and described in a spatial location to be understood as high <term>B-fa</term>; but as super-high <term>B-fa</term> when on a line. And so they easily discerned all other subsequent voices and notes to be considered in the low and high registers through lines and spaces. More recent <term>Gregorians</term> and those who again describe mensurable songs, because they are accustomed to describe all lines in one and the same color, usually mark that linear clef of low <term>F-fa-ut</term> with three notes at the head of that line in this order:

[A musical example showing an F-clef represented by three stacked square notes at the beginning of a four-line staff. A second example shows a stylized, decorative version of the F-clef.]

<summary>This page explains the visual representation of ligatures and intermediate notes, emphasizing that they generally carry equal rhythmic weight despite their shapes. It also introduces the concept of clefs (keys), explaining how colors (red, glaucous, blue) and specific letter-signs were used to define pitch on a musical staff.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical notation, moving from the physical shape of notes to the function of "clefs" (keys) and the three methods of vocal performance used by singers and students of the era.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<columns>
¶ And at the head of the line for <term>C-sol-fa-ut</term> <note>original: "C solfaut". This refers to the pitch C, which occupied three different positions (sol, fa, and ut) across the various overlapping scales or hexachords of the medieval system.</note>, they assign two separate square notes in this manner:

[A small musical staff fragment showing a C-clef (positioned for alto or tenor ranges) and two square notes on a single line.]

<column-break/>

But the "high B-fa" <note>The note B-flat.</note> is indicated by the shape of the letter itself, as seen here:

[A small musical staff fragment showing a B-flat symbol, which looks like a rounded lowercase 'b', positioned on a staff line.]

<column-break/>

¶ And in this order, the clef is described where the first <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A grouping of four consecutive notes.</note> of each <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la), the fundamental building block of medieval music theory.</note> is determined toward the high range. Nor is it unfitting that, by common consent, musicians have assigned the clef to that note which leads down to the semitone nearest to it in the lower range. For the <term>semitone</term> itself is more skillfully crafted, and by its very proportion and breadth—both by nature and art—it is more difficult than the whole tone; hence, it requires more demonstration. Furthermore, there are three ways in which the pitches declared by the notes are usually pronounced.

¶ In the first way, by <term>solmization</term> <note>original: "solfizando". The practice of singing using the names of the notes.</note>—that is, by expressing the syllables and names of the voices: namely, *ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la*—as shown here:

[A musical staff with a C-clef showing an ascending six-note scale (hexachord). Below the notes are the labels: *ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la*.]

¶ They pass down this method of pronunciation as a rule to be set before boys beginning their studies.

¶ In the second way: by sounding the tones and voices alone, omitting entirely the letters, syllables, and words—something a practiced singer achieves easily in this manner:

[A musical staff showing a series of square notes representing a melodic phrase without text or solmization labels, intended for vocalizing on a single vowel.]

¶ In the third way: by pronouncing whatever texts, such as <term>antiphons</term> and <term>responsories</term> <note>Types of liturgical chants used in the Divine Office.</note>, and the words of the songs themselves written beneath the notes.

[A musical staff showing a short liturgical melody with the text "Salue rex glo ri e christe" written beneath the notes.]

->Hail, Christ, King of Glory<-
<note>original: "Salue rex glorie christe"</note>

¶ Toward which, as if to the goal of refined melody, the clergy are led, as shown here:

[A small vertical diagram showing a C-clef and several square notes stacked or arranged vertically on a staff, likely illustrating the range or "gamut".]

¶ But the notes themselves declare the pronunciation of the syllables in this order. For since *re* is a whole tone higher than *ut*, its sound must be raised by the interval of a tone, and *ut*, conversely, must be lowered from *re* by the interval of a tone, in this way.

¶ Likewise, the sound of the note *mi* is raised from *re* by a tone, and *re* is lowered from *mi* by the interval of the same tone, which is observed in these notes:

[A musical staff showing two pairs of notes illustrating the step-by-step movement between ut-re and re-mi, and their descent.]

¶ The sound of *fa* is stretched from *mi* by the interval of a minor semitone, and *mi* is brought back down from *fa* by the interval of the same semitone, as shown here:

[A musical staff featuring diamond-shaped notes (known as a "punctum effictum") often used to denote semitones or specific rhythmic values, illustrating the mi-fa interval.]

¶ The syllable *sol*, however, stretches the sound above *fa* by the interval of a tone, and *fa* is brought back down from *sol* by the interval of the same tone, which these notes declare:

[A musical staff showing the intervals between fa and sol, using square notes.]

# BOOK ONE

¶ Likewise, the sound of the syllable *la* is higher than *sol* by the interval of a whole tone, and *sol* is lower than *la* by that same tone; this is easily perceived by the arrangement of these notes.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff with square notation illustrating the interval of a tone between 'la' and 'sol' in ascending and descending motion.</image-desc>

¶ Again, *mi* is stretched upward from *ut* by the interval of a <term>ditone</term> <note>original: "ditoni". A ditone consists of two whole tones, which we today call a major third.</note>, and *ut* is lowered from *mi* by the same distance. This occurs in two ways: "composite" and "simple." It is composite when the intervals of the two whole tones are bounded by the two outer notes with a middle and common note written in between, as seen here:

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff showing the composite ditone interval 'ut-re-mi' (ascending) and 'mi-re-ut' (descending).</image-desc>

¶ Or it is "incomposite" when the distance of two tones is concluded by only the two outer notes, with no middle note written in between, which is clearly perceived by these notes. <term>Boethius</term> <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> and <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>An ancient Greek astronomer and music theorist.</note> ascribed this naturally harmonious interval to the "enharmonic" <term>tetrachords</term> <note>A grouping of four notes.</note>.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff showing the incomposite ditone interval 'ut-mi' (ascending) and 'mi-ut' (descending).</image-desc>

<margin>
Boethius
Ptolemy
What a
ditone is
Semiditone
Pythagoras
Chromatic
Enharmonic
</margin>
For a ditone is a "heap" of two whole tones. A <term>semiditone</term> <note>A minor third, consisting of one whole tone and one semitone.</note>, however, is a mixture of a tone and a minor <term>semitone</term> <note>A half-step.</note>; it is called "semi-" as if it were an imperfect or diminished ditone. Boethius proposes this as an "incomposite trihemitone" <note>literally "three half-tones."</note> for the "chromatic" tetrachords, though it is naturally harmonious. For by the investigation of <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>The ancient Greek mathematician who discovered the numerical ratios of musical intervals.</note>, it was found that in each "diatonic" and natural tetrachord (which sounds dissonant to the ears), a certain "incomposite" harmony was discovered by relaxing the middle strings. Since this was understood through two considerations—the ditone and the <term>trihemitone</term>—these two "genera," the Chromatic and Enharmonic, were placed between the natural Diatonic. However, we shall treat these more extensively in the section on instrumental harmony.

¶ Therefore, the sound *fa* moves away from *re* toward the high range by the interval of a semiditone, and similarly *re* is relaxed from *fa* toward the low range. This also happens in two ways: "composite" and "incomposite," as anyone can perceive for themselves from these descriptions.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff showing the interval between 're' and 'fa' in composite (re-mi-fa) and incomposite (re-fa) forms.</image-desc>

¶ Furthermore, the sound *sol* is higher than *mi* by the interval of a semiditone, and *mi* is lower than *sol*; this is also considered in composite and incomposite ways. However, this interval differs from the one between *re* and *fa*. The former (re-fa) keeps the whole tone higher than the semitone <note>In the sequence re-mi-fa, the tone (re-mi) comes before the semitone (mi-fa).</note>, while the latter (mi-sol) keeps the semitone higher than the tone <note>In the sequence mi-fa-sol, the semitone (mi-fa) comes before the tone (fa-sol).</note>, as is proven by these notes.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff showing the interval between 'mi' and 'sol' in composite (mi-fa-sol) and incomposite (mi-sol) forms.</note>

¶ In each <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale.</note>, the sound *fa* is raised up from *ut* by the consonance of a <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The ancient Greek term for a perfect fourth.</note> or an <term>epitrito</term> interval <note>A mathematical ratio of 4:3, which defines the perfect fourth.</note>. And the sound of the syllable *ut* is lowered from *fa* by the same distance. This occurs in four ways: either by three distinct intervals surrounded by four notes (two whole tones and a semitone); or by two intervals (a ditone and a semitone); or by a single, "incomposite" interval between the outer notes, as shown in these examples.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff showing four different constructions of the 'ut-fa' interval (a perfect fourth): fully composite (ut-re-mi-fa), two partially composite forms (ut-mi-fa and ut-re-fa), and an incomposite form (ut-fa).</image-desc>

# BOOK ONE

¶ Indeed, the sound of *sol* is sustained higher than *ut* by the interval of a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapentes"</note>: namely, three whole tones and a semitone. This is stretched through various intervals with varied notation, as is considered in these <term>derivations</term> <note>original: "deductionibus"; the melodic sequences used to teach the intervals.</note>.

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing mensural notation (square black notes). It illustrates the interval of a fifth between "sol" and "ut," showing various melodic ways to bridge the interval.</image-desc>

¶ But the sound of *la* is derived higher than *ut* by the distance of a fifth and a tone <note>A major sixth.</note>: consisting of four tones and a minor semitone. This can be sustained in various intervals both from low to high and from high to low, as shown in these examples.

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing mensural notation. It illustrates the interval of a sixth ("la" from "ut"), showing several melodic patterns ascending and descending between these notes.</image-desc>

### Chapter 4. On the Properties and Mutations of Vocal Syllables.

<margin>
Marchettus
Tinctoris
</margin>

**P**roperty in vocal notes, according to <term>Marchettus of Padua</term> <note>An influential 14th-century music theorist.</note>, is the derivation of several voices from one and the same starting point. <term>Tinctoris</term> <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), a major Renaissance theorist.</note>, however, says that property is the singular quality of voices to be derived. For "proper" and "property" are not the same: "proper" is concrete, whereas "property" is abstract, just like "White" and "Whiteness." For a "proper" thing is a certain substance joined with a quality—or if you prefer, with an <term>accident</term> <note>In philosophy, a property that is not essential to the nature of a thing.</note>—and this is concrete. "Property," however, is a certain passion, quality, or accident; when it is abstracted from the substance in which it dwells, it is called abstract, such as "property" or "whiteness." Indeed, we call the "property" of this kind of melody the singular <term>derivation</term> <note>original: "deductionem"</note> of each individual <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) used in medieval music education.</note> arranged in the introductory scale. A derivation is the <term>diatonic</term> <note>Standard scale movement without chromatic changes.</note> and natural progression of these six syllables: ascending in this order: *ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la*; and descending: *la, sol, fa, mi, re*. Indeed, they call these seven hexachords "properties" or "qualities," of which they assign three to the <term>square-B</term> <note>original: "♮ quadræ". The "hard" hexachord (durum) starting on G, which uses the square-shaped natural sign.</note> or "hard-B," two to nature <note>The "natural" hexachord starting on C.</note>, and two to the <term>round-B</term> <note>original: "b rotundæ". The "soft" hexachord (molle) starting on F, which uses the rounded flat sign.</note> or "soft" hexachord. Those called "square-B" take their beginning from the letter G. The "natural" ones begin on the letter C. But the "soft-B" ones are known to possess their beginning on the letter F, which is clearly perceived from the order of the introductory scale. Therefore, the lowest string—namely the Greek letter <term>Gamma</term> (Γ)—produces the first form of the hexachord, which the line of the "hard-B" quality is known to support. The third letter-note <note>The letter C</note> is known to produce the second form of the hexachord, placing the fourth string in a space; they call this the "natural" property or quality. Finally, the sixth letter-character <note>The letter F</note> produced the third form of the hexachord in correct order, reaching the seventh string; they call this the "soft-B" quality. It is established that these repeated properties become known through the description of the introductory scale. Furthermore, I am in no way pleased with the reasoning of <term>Anselm</term> <note>Likely Anselm of Parma, a theorist known for unconventional views on the musical scale.</note> because he placed the natural property in the middle between the square-B and the round-B—or I should better say, between the "hard" and the "soft"—as if nature itself...

<margin>
Against Anselm
</margin>

<summary>This page defines the larger musical intervals of the fifth and sixth and introduces the theoretical concept of "property" in hexachords, contrasting the definitions of Marchettus and Tinctoris.</summary>
<keywords>fifth, sixth, diapente, hexachord, Marchettus of Padua, Tinctoris, hard-B, soft-B, natural property, Anselm of Parma, gamut, solmization</keywords>

# BOOK ONE

The property of nature is smoother when arranged according to the <term>chromatic genus</term> <note>original: "chromaticum genus"; one of the three ancient Greek tunings, using semitones.</note>. However, the property of the <term>square B</term> <note>original: "♮ quadræ"; the "hard" B-natural.</note> is harsher in the <term>diatonic genus</term>, while the property of the <term>soft B</term> <note>original: "♭ mollis"; the "soft" B-flat.</note> is sweeter when led into the <term>enharmonic</term> <note>original: "Enarmonico"; the third Greek genus, using microtones.</note>. For every <term>hexachord</term> <note>original: "exachordum"; a six-note scale.</note>—whether of the "hard" (♮) quality, the "natural" quality, or even the "soft" (♭) quality—is known to be measured according to the diatonic genus. Hence, I am convinced that the quality of nature itself is inscribed upon the fourth string, because it contains the distance of the prior and lower <term>Hypaton</term> <note>The lowest of the ancient Greek tetrachords.</note> diatonic tetrachord, which is the natural genus. Indeed, the "soft B" softens the harshness of the <term>tritone</term> by completing its first tetrachord at the <term>Trite synemmenon</term> <note>original: "tritessinemenon"; the "third string of the conjunct tetrachord," our B-flat.</note>. Conversely, the "hard B" makes the tone between the <term>Mese</term> <note>The central note, A.</note> and the <term>Paramese</term> <note>The note above the center, B-natural.</note> harsher through the <term>disjunct</term> <note>original: "disiunctam"; referring to the separation between tetrachords.</note> interval of B-natural <note>original: "♮ mi"</note>. If I establish this string as the middle point of the <term>monochord</term> <note>original: "chordotoni"; a one-stringed instrument used to measure musical ratios.</note>, the first turn of the compass will change it into its own lower equivalent and unison—namely, B-natural—through the double ratio of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapasson".</note>. Since this "mi" is the third note of that hexachord and holds the third place of the <term>introductory scale</term> <note>original: "introductorii"; likely referring to the Guidonian Hand or the basic gamut.</note> on the first and lowest string, namely <term>Gamma-ut</term> <note>original: "Γ ut"; the lowest G in the medieval system.</note>, it must necessarily take up the beginning of its own quality. To which properties or qualities all vocal syllables are assigned can be easily perceived through these three figures:

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *La* on E | *La* on A | *La* on D |
| *Sol* on D | *Sol* on G | *Sol* on C |
| Every *fa* is sung on C | Every *fa* is sung on F | Every *fa* is sung on B-flat (♭) |
| *Mi* on B-natural (♮) <note>original: "♮ quadrā"</note> | *Mi* on E through nature | *Mi* on A through the soft B (♭) |
| *Re* on A | *Re* on D | *Re* on G |
| *Ut* on G | *Ut* on C | *Ut* on F |

---

<margin>
What is mutation?
Baccheus
Gregory
</margin>

¶ Furthermore, clerics attest to many kinds of mutations of sounds. For according to <term>Baccheus</term> <note>Bacchius Geron, an ancient Greek music theorist.</note>, mutation is the "alteration of subjects" or "the transposition of something similar into a dissimilar place." Hence, in his *Moralia*, <term>Gregory</term> <note>Pope Gregory the Great.</note> says: "To be changed is to go from one thing into another and not to be stable in oneself; for every single thing tends toward another by as many steps as it is subject to the movements of its own mutability."

<margin>
Martianus
Bryennius
</margin>

But <term>Martianus</term> <note>Martianus Capella, 5th-century author of "The Marriage of Philology and Mercury."</note> calls this kind of mutation a "transition," which he interprets as the variation of the voice into another figure of sound. <term>Bryennius</term> <note>Manuel Bryennius, a 14th-century Byzantine theorist.</note>, however, said that mutation is "the alienation of the underlying system and the character of the voice." Mutation occurs according to genus when, for example, in the diatonic tetrachord, the <term>Lichanos</term> or the <term>Paranete</term> <note>Greek names for specific notes in a tetrachord.</note> string is lowered by a semitone into the low register, passing into a chromatic figure, or by a whole tone, passing into an enharmonic figure. This variation from low to high never happens, 

<margin>
Boethius
Aristotle
</margin>

as our <term>Boethius</term> <note>The most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages.</note> explained in his fourth book. <term>Aristotle</term> himself wished this to be understood in his *Musical Problems* when he said: "That which tastes of the nature of the high pitch frequently passes into the low, but that which tastes of the nature of the low does not change into the high." There is also another consideration of mutation in voice and sound; for when they are in motion and become only voices 

<margin>
Peter of Abano
Marchettus
</margin>

and sounds, they are believed to belong to the genus of <term>successive beings</term> <note>original: "entium successivorum"; a philosophical term for things that exist only in a sequence through time, like sound or time itself.</note>, as the commentator <note>Referring to Peter of Abano.</note> explained in the twenty-seventh problem. Therefore, the generation of the voice and sound itself consists in a certain "becoming" and "being transformed." Truly, in this introductory work, it follows the mutation defined by <term>Marchettus</term> <note>Marchettus of Padua, early 14th-century theorist.</note>. For he says: "Mutation is the variation of the name of a voice into another on the same sound." For the syllables themselves, assigned to their voices and strings (that is, their notes), are said to be equal in quantity if they consist in one and the same line or space, but diverse in quality or property. Thus, since mutation is the quality of one hexachord [changing] into the quality of another...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "mutation" from the previous page, specifically detailing how singers transition between different hexachords (six-note scales) while maintaining the same pitch.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<margin>Anselm</margin> [The quality] is transferred while the pitch of the voice remains the same: as <note>Anselm of Parma, a 15th-century theorist who proposed a system of eight hexachords.</note> Anselm testifies in the third book of his *Music*. Hence, I define **mutation** <note>original: "mutationem"; the technique of shifting from one hexachord to another to extend the vocal range.</note> as the alternating movement from one voice to another, perceived through a uniform tension of the sound. By "voices," I mean the syllables of the <term>hexachords</term> <note>The six syllables used in singing: Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La.</note>. Therefore, a voice is not changed into another by raising or lowering the pitch, but one syllable is changed into another, and one property or quality into another. Thus, when we express the syllables through a melodic transition, the mutation itself fits. The letter-characters <note>The alphabet names for notes: A, B, C, D, E, F, G.</note> observed in the introduction are neither spoken nor changed during singing. Furthermore, a syllable that occupies a line or a space alone is not suitable for mutation. Therefore, in Gamma-ut, in A-re, in B-mi, and in E-la, no mutation occurs; if it should happen that necessity requires it, you will repeat the original order of the joined hexachords. In **C-fa-ut**, two mutations occur alternately: the first by changing the preceding syllable into the following one—namely, *fa* into *ut*. This is called an **ascending mutation** from the "square B" <note>The "hard" hexachord containing B-natural.</note> into the "natural" property, as the description of these notes explains.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a four-line staff with a C-clef (UT) on the third line. It shows an ascending sequence of square notes labeled "In C-fa-ut". The notes transition from 'fa' (in the square/hard hexachord) to 'ut' (in the natural hexachord). Lines connect the syllables to show they share the same pitch.</image-desc>

¶ The **second mutation** occurs when, in singing, we change the following syllable into the preceding one—namely, *ut* into *fa*. This is called a **descending mutation** from the "natural" property into the "square B," as is clear here.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a four-line staff with a C-clef on the third line. It illustrates a descending melodic line. Labels indicate a mutation from 'ut' (natural hexachord) to 'fa' (hard hexachord) on the same note.</image-desc>

¶ It is established that mutation must occur for three reasons. First, so that above and below each hexachord, the voices themselves can be raised into the high register and lowered into the low register through a melodic transition. Second, to achieve a sweeter melodic transition; for often, the varied quality of the syllables makes the song no less honeyed and sweet than the changed pitch of the sung sound. Sometimes the "soft B" quality <note>B-flat.</note>, brought into the place of the "square B" (which the <term>Ambrosians</term> <note>Followers of the Milanese or Ambrosian musical rite, known for specific melodic traditions.</note> observe more often), makes the melody sweeter. Third, for an easier harmony of the figures—namely, the transition of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diateffaron"</note> and <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapentes"</note> intervals arranged within the mixture of tones. In **D-sol-re** and likewise in **E-la-mi**, two similar mutations occur. In **F-fa-ut**, however, two mutations arise: the first, for the sake of ascending, is made by changing the "natural" quality into the "soft B," or the first syllable into the second—namely, *fa* into *ut*—as the present description of notes indicates.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a four-line staff with a C-clef on the second line. The staff shows an ascending melodic progression with square and diamond-shaped notes. The mutation is marked between 'fa' (natural) and 'ut' (soft B-flat hexachord).</image-desc>

¶ The **second mutation** regards descending from "soft B" into "nature" by changing the second syllable into the first—namely, *ut* into *fa*—as can be easily understood by this arrangement of notes.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a four-line staff with a C-clef on the second line, showing a descending phrase. It demonstrates the mutation from 'ut' (soft B hexachord) back to 'fa' (natural hexachord).</image-desc>

¶ From **G-sol-re-ut**, six mutations arise. The first occurs from the melodic transition of the first syllable into the second—namely, from *sol* into *re*—ascending from "nature" into "soft B," as is perceived by this arrangement.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a four-line staff with a C-clef on the second line. It features an ascending melodic line. Textual annotations show the mutation from 'sol' (natural hexachord) to 're' (soft B hexachord).</image-desc>

# BOOK ONE

¶ The **second mutation** is the reverse: changing the second syllable into the first—specifically, *re* into *sol*—while descending from the <term>soft B</term> <note>original: "b molli"; the Soft Hexachord starting on F, which uses B-flat.</note> into the <term>natural</term> <note>The Natural Hexachord starting on C.</note>, as is shown here.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the second mutation: a descending sequence of square and diamond-shaped notes (re to sol) moving from the soft hexachord (b-molle) to the natural hexachord.</image-desc>

¶ The **third mutation** occurs by converting the first syllable into the third for the sake of an intended ascent, namely *sol* into *ut*: moving from the natural into the <term>square ♮</term> <note>original: "♮ quadram"; the Hard Hexachord starting on G, which uses B-natural.</note>, as perceived in this example.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the third mutation: an ascending sequence of notes (sol to ut) moving from the natural hexachord to the hard hexachord (♮ quadram).</image-desc>

¶ The **fourth** is the reverse, seeking a descent <note>original: "remissionem"; literally a "relaxation" or lowering of pitch.</note> by changing *ut* into *sol* from the square ♮ into the natural, as is shown here.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the fourth mutation: a descending sequence of notes (ut to sol) moving from the hard hexachord to the natural hexachord.</image-desc>

¶ The **fifth mutation** occurs when, during the course of a melody, the second syllable is changed into the third—namely *re* into *ut*—for the sake of ascending: from the soft ♭ B into the square ♮, as here.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the fifth mutation: an ascending sequence of notes (re to ut) moving from the soft hexachord (♭ b molli) to the hard hexachord (♮ quadram).</image-desc>

¶ The **sixth** happens by changing the third syllable into the second—namely *ut* into *re*—for the sake of descending from the hard ♮ into the soft B: as is declared by this example.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the sixth mutation: a descending sequence of notes (ut to re) moving from the hard hexachord (b-mollem).</image-desc>

¶ This sixth mutation—namely of the third syllable into the second (*ut* into *re*)—frequently occurs in regards to an ascent at the first step of <term>disjunction</term> <note>A "disjunction" refers to a point in the scale where hexachords do not overlap naturally, requiring a leap or a specific shift in solmization syllables.</note>; in that case, I call it an **irregular** or **indirect mutation**, which is clearly noted by this example.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff showing an irregular or indirect mutation with notes moving across hexachords.</image-desc>

<margin>Direct
mutation

Indirect
mutation</margin>
¶ A **direct and regular mutation** is one that opposes the preceding and following <term>unison</term> <note>A mutation occurring on the same pitch.</note> mutation; for example: when the first is for the sake of ascending, the second for descending, the third for ascending, the fourth for descending, the fifth for ascending, and the sixth for descending.

However, an **indirect and irregular mutation** is so called when it leads through a similar motion of "property" or "quality" <note>In medieval theory, "property" (proprietas) refers to which hexachord is being used (Hard, Soft, or Natural).</note> to the preceding or following unison mutation. An example is this sixth type when used for ascending: it is made similar in motion to the preceding fifth mutation, which was also directed toward an ascent. For indeed, an **ascending mutation** is when, after the name of the unison note has been changed, the first movement of the voice tends...

B

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of "mutations" within the hexachord system. It completes the description of mutations at the pitch A (A-la-mi-re) and introduces the complex problem of the "permutation" required to move between B-flat and B-natural, before beginning the mutations for the pitch C (C-sol-fa-ut).</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...into the high pitch. When descending, however, when the first voice is changed, it returns to the low pitch. In the note **A-la-mi-re**, six mutations are made, and they are similar to those that were laid out in **G-sol-re-ut**.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff featuring square notes and a C-clef on the third line. The melody follows a descending and then ascending pattern, illustrating the transition between hexachords at the pitch A.</image-desc>

And the sixth mutation again proceeds irregularly and indirectly; that is, it is very similar to the preceding fifth mutation, ascending from the <term>soft hexachord</term> <note>original: "b molli"; the hexachord starting on F which uses B-flat.</note> into the <term>hard hexachord</term> <note>original: "♮ duram"; the hexachord starting on G which uses B-natural.</note>, as shown here.

¶ In the pitch **B-fa ♮-mi**: because both syllables do not represent the same sound, most theorists agree that no mutation can occur there. They are, in fact, separated from one another by a <term>major semitone</term> <note>In the tuning systems of this era, the distance between B-flat and B-natural was a large semitone, making them distinct pitches rather than just two versions of the same note.</note>. For while *B-fa* is separated from the *mi* of *A-la-mi-re* by a <term>minor semitone</term> in the high direction, *B-natural-mi* is a whole tone higher than the *re* of that same *A-la-mi-re*. Since a whole tone exceeds a minor semitone by a major semitone, it is established that *B-natural-mi* is sharper than *B-flat-fa* by a major semitone. 

Therefore, when compelled by necessity, we perform a transition in *B-fa ♮-mi*, and this involves a change of both quality and quantity. By quality, I mean the property of the <term>soft hexachord</term> changing into the <term>hard</term>, by changing *fa* into *mi* for the sake of ascending, or conversely for the sake of descending. By quantity, I mean passing from *fa* to *mi*—from a lower sound to a sharper one—by the interval of an <term>apotome</term> <note>The technical term for a major semitone.</note>, or conversely, descending from *mi* to *fa* from a sharp sound to a lower one. 

Because this transition is difficult and quite dissonant, the school of musicians teaches that it should be avoided with all skill. <margin>Marchetto and Anselm</margin> <note>Refers to Marchetto da Padova and Giorgio Anselmi, famous music theorists of the 14th and 15th centuries.</note> Marchetto and Anselm call this a **permutation**. An irregular and indirect mutation was devised to avoid the dissonant transition of this kind of permutation, which must sometimes occur due to the arrangement of the notes, as is clear in this example.

<image-desc>A longer musical example on a five-line staff with various ligatures and square notes. It illustrates the complex transition between b-flat (b-fa) and b-natural (b-mi), showing how the singer must navigate the "permutation."</image-desc>

<margin>What a permutation is</margin>
¶ A **permutation**, therefore, is a mutual variation of quality and quantity between one another.

<image-desc>A small musical fragment with four square notes on a five-line staff illustrating a brief melodic movement.</image-desc>

¶ In the pitch **C-sol-fa-ut**, six mutations are performed. The **first** occurs when, while singing, we change the first syllable into the second—namely, *sol* into *fa*—ascending from the soft hexachord into the hard hexachord, as shown here.

<image-desc>Musical notation showing an ascending sequence of notes: C, D, E, F, G, illustrating the shift from the F-hexachord to the G-hexachord at the pitch C.</image-desc>

¶ Furthermore, this first mutation is sometimes made indirectly and irregularly: namely, descending in the first movable transition to avoid that dissonant and unsuitable permutation in **B-fa ♮-mi**, as is proven by this arrangement of notes.

<image-desc>Musical notation showing a descending melodic sequence on a five-line staff, demonstrating a workaround for the difficult B-flat to B-natural transition.</image-desc>

¶ The **second mutation** occurs when, while singing, the second syllable is changed into the first—namely, *fa* into *sol*—for the sake of descending, moving from the <term>square ♮</term> <note>The Hard Hexachord.</note> into the soft hexachord, as is clear from this example.

<image-desc>Final musical example on the page showing notes on a staff, demonstrating the descending mutation from the "hard" hexachord (using B-natural) to the "soft" hexachord (using B-flat).</image-desc>
