<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of "mutations"—the methods by which a singer shifts between the three standard six-note scales (hexachords) of medieval and Renaissance music theory. It completes the list of mutations for the pitch C (C-sol-fa-ut), provides general rules for other pitches, and begins a new chapter on the interval of the fourth.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

¶ The third mutation occurs when, in singing, we change the first syllable into the third: specifically, changing **sol** into **ut** while ascending from the <term>soft hexachord</term> <note>original: "ex b molli"; the scale beginning on F which uses B-flat.</note> to the <term>natural hexachord</term> <note>the scale beginning on C.</note>, as is perceived in this example.
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff illustrating the third mutation between the soft hexachord and natural hexachord, showing notes on the staff corresponding to the syllables 'sol' and 'ut' at the pitch C.</image-desc>

¶ The fourth mutation occurs when, in singing, we change **ut** into **sol** while descending from the natural hexachord into the soft hexachord, as is evident here.
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff illustrating the fourth mutation from the natural hexachord to the soft hexachord, showing notes corresponding to the syllables 'ut' and 'sol' at the pitch C.</image-desc>

¶ The fifth mutation occurs when, in singing, we change the second syllable into the third: **fa** into **ut** while ascending from the <term>hard hexachord</term> <note>original: "ex ♮ quadra"; the scale beginning on G which uses B-natural, symbolized by the "square" or "hard" B.</note> into the natural hexachord, as is established here.
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff illustrating the fifth mutation from the hard hexachord to the natural hexachord, showing the transition from 'fa' to 'ut' at the pitch C.</image-desc>

¶ The sixth and final mutation occurs when, in singing, the third syllable is changed into the second: namely, **ut** into **fa** while descending from the natural hexachord into the hard hexachord, as is perceived in this example.
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff illustrating the sixth mutation from the natural hexachord to the hard hexachord, showing the transition from 'ut' to 'fa' at the pitch C.</image-desc>

¶ Many indirect and irregular mutations can also be made, as anyone can easily consider for themselves.
¶ In the pitch **D-la-sol-re**, six similar mutations are conducted. There are two in **E-la-mi**, and two in **F-fa-ut**. Likewise in the others, for those examining similar movements of mutations, one should refer each individual case to its similar counterpart. Furthermore, theorists say that a plurality of mutations should be avoided, since a progression of singing is properly arranged if it is managed with a single mutation. They also maintained that a mutation should be delayed and pushed as far off as possible. Sometimes a "disjunct" transition of mutation occurs when, in singing, a transition is made beyond the order of the <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la).</note>—for instance, by ascending or descending through seven or eight notes, or even more—which is observed more frequently in <term>measured songs</term> <note>Polyphonic music with specific rhythmic values, as opposed to plainchant.</note>. Similarly, many mutations could be made through "conjunctions" if the syllables of the hexachords were arranged by the intervals of whole tones, dividing those tones into two unequal <term>semitones</term> <note>Half-steps.</note>. But since that belongs to either the <term>chromatic</term> or <term>mixed genus</term> <note>Ancient Greek musical categories; the author is distinguishing between the complex "chromatic" style and the standard "diatonic" style used in church music.</note>, the present study rejects it, referring only to the <term>diatonic</term> system of **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo (d. 1050), the monk credited with inventing the staff and the solmization system.</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the consonance of the fourth and its types. Chapter Five.

The <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth.</note> is a <term>consonance</term> <note>A pleasing musical interval.</note> formed by four sounds encompassing two whole tones and a minor semitone, which natural singing requires in each <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A scale segment of four notes.</note>. It is known to have three "figures" <note>Also called species; these are the different ways a fourth can be built depending on where the half-step (semitone) falls.</note>, since every consonance—whether a fourth, a fifth, or an octave—always has one fewer species or figure than it has notes, and an equal number of intervals naturally and diatonically arranged. For it is called *diatessaron* <note>original: "Dicta. n. est diatessaron"; from the Greek "dia tessarōn" meaning "through four [notes]".</note>...

<summary>This page concludes the rules for "mutations" (shifting between hexachords) at the pitch C and general guidelines for other pitches, emphasizing the avoidance of unnecessary shifts. It then introduces Chapter Five, defining the "diatessaron" (the perfect fourth) and its mathematical structure.</summary>

# BOOK I

...by four. In one genus, indeed, but of different species; it therefore comprises three different species or figures, as has been said. The first passes from the low to the high by the intervals of a tone, a semitone, and again a tone, within the boundary of these syllables: **re mi fa sol**. It takes its beginning in **A re**—where the first and "added" string <note>original: "acquisita"; referring to the *proslambanomenos*, the lowest note of the Greek Greater Perfect System</note> of the perfect fifteen-string system resides—and ends in **D sol-re**. Moreover, wherever a similar progression occurs, you will always recognize it as the first form of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>. For this proceeds either by the individual distinct intervals of the diatonic genus, or by a composite tone and semitone, or a composite semitone and tone, or by the composite fourth itself, as perceived in these figures.

<image-desc>A musical notation diagram illustrating the "Prima diatessaron figura" (First figure of the fourth). It contains three short musical examples on five-line staves with a C-clef on the fourth line. Each example shows a sequence of four square notes representing the interval of a fourth, with semitones and tones in specific positions (A-B-C-D, E-F-G-A, and a descending pattern).</image-desc>

¶ The second figure of the fourth proceeds from low to high by a semitone and two contiguous tones, arranged distinctly and compositely with these syllables: **mi fa sol la**. It follows the beginning of the first, namely starting at **♮ mi** <note>B-natural</note> according to the diatonic measurement of the first <term>hexachord</term> <note>the six-note scale</note>. Conversely, it descends from high to low by two tones and a semitone; and a similar transition of intervals and syllables everywhere produces the second form of the fourth, which is arranged with various intervals just like the first, in this manner.

<image-desc>A musical notation diagram illustrating the "Secunda diatessaron figura" (Second figure of the fourth). Three musical examples on five-line staves use square notation to show a diatessaron pattern starting with a semitone (e.g., B-C-D-E), with phrases ascending and descending.</image-desc>

¶ The third form of the fourth extends from low to high through the diatonic sequence of joined hexachords from **C fa-ut** to **F fa-ut**, with these syllables: **ut re mi fa**, specifically by a tone, a tone, and a semitone. But conversely, from high to low, it moves by a semitone and two tones. Therefore, a similar consideration of both syllables and intervals always brings about the third form of the fourth; the following descriptions declare how it can be extended through various intervals.

<image-desc>A musical notation diagram illustrating the "Tertia diatessaron forma" (Third form of the fourth). Three musical examples on five-line staves show the diatessaron pattern of tone-tone-semitone (e.g., C-D-E-F), using square notation and ligatures.</image-desc>

¶ However, these three figures of the fourth can be considered in each species with a varied measurement of the middle strings, as I will most notably demonstrate in instrumental harmony through a most equal division and the annotation of numbers. But here, let us demonstrate what we are saying only through syllables, so that it may be understood in this way.

<image-desc>A complex musical diagram on a five-line staff. It features several square notes with multiple solmization syllables (re, mi, fa, sol, la) printed both above and below each note. This diagram illustrates the "mutation" or overlap between different hexachords (natural, durum, and molle) in the musical system.</image-desc>

# ¶ On the consonance of the fifth and its species. Chapter Six.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring an indoor scene with a seated figure, possibly a scholar or philosopher, at a desk or bench.</image-desc>

**D**iapente <note>the interval of a fifth</note> is a consonance of five sounds comprising three tones and one minor semitone across four diatonic intervals. For it is named "by five" <note>original: "per quinque"</note>. And it has four species or different figures, whose positions the wandering semitone transition, moving here and there, is known to declare. For the first figure of the fifth is made from the first species of the fourth plus a tone in the high range; it retains the natural semitone in the second interval, proceeding with these syllables: **re mi fa sol la**. It takes its beginning in **D sol-re** and ends in **A la-mi-re** <note>The OCR reads "Ela mi re," which is likely a typo for the pitch A, the fifth above D.</note>. Furthermore, a similar transition of intervals and notes will always establish the first species of the fifth. However, it is also condensed by various intervals, as is evident here.

# BOOK ONE

<header>FIRST</header>

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff with a C-clef on the fourth line, showing five notes in ascending order from E to B natural (the syllables mi, fa, sol, la, mi), representing the second species of the diapente.</image-desc>

¶ The second figure of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; an interval spanning five notes</note> is composed of the second species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; an interval spanning four notes</note> and a whole tone added at the top. It contains a semitone in its first and lowest interval, and is guided by these syllables: **mi fa sol la mi**. Its beginning is placed at low **E-la-mi** and it ends at high **B-natural** <note>original: "♮ mi acutam"</note>. A similar arrangement of voices and intervals is always recognized as the second species of the fifth; this diagram shows how it can be understood across different pitch levels.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff showing five notes in ascending order from F to C (the syllables fa, sol, la, mi, fa), representing the third species of the diapente.</image-desc>

¶ The third figure of the fifth is formed from a <term>tritone</term> <note>an interval of three whole tones</note> and a semitone at the top. It has the semitone joined at the fourth interval, and proceeds with these syllables: **fa sol la mi fa**. It begins at low **F-fa-ut**, extending to high **C-sol-fa-ut**. Every progression of similar voices and intervals always presents a similar species of the fifth, which can be extended through various intervals according to the <term>diatonic</term> <note>the standard scale consisting of tones and semitones</note> genus, as is clear here.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff showing five notes in ascending order from G to D (the syllables ut, re, mi, fa, sol), representing the fourth species of the diapente.</image-desc>

¶ The fourth figure of the fifth is arranged through the third species of the fourth and a tone added at the top, proceeding from **G-sol-re-ut** to **D-la-sol-re**. It uses these syllables: **ut re mi fa sol**, and contains a semitone in the third interval. Wherever a similar succession of voices and intervals occurs, the fourth species of the fifth will always result; they often arrange this through various intervals in this manner:

¶ It will likewise be possible to verify the formula of each individual species in every figure of the fifth. We have decided that this must be demonstrated in instrumental harmony by the actual measurement of strings and the notation of numbers. Here, however, we describe the first figure of the fifth, set apart from the others, to clarify the proposed consideration in this order:

# BOOK TWO

<header>SECOND BOOK</header>

### ¶ On the consonance of the octave and its species. Chapter VII.

<margin>Ptolemy</margin>
<margin>Bacchius</margin>
The <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval spanning eight letter-names</note> is a consonance of eight sounds arranged according to the <term>diatonic genus</term> <note>the standard musical scale consisting of whole tones and semitones</note>. It is produced by a span of five whole tones and two minor semitones. It is formed by the joining of a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, with a shared middle note acting as the boundary of the connection. I call this note "common" because it serves as the end of the fourth and the beginning of the fifth, or vice versa. The octave is so named <note>original: "diapason," from the Greek "dia pason," meaning "through all"</note> because it sustains the melody—or the effect of the modulation—through all the distinct sounds. It is established that there are only seven distinct sounds; the eighth sound, however, is a repetition of the first, being very similar to it, which is why it is called "equal-sounding" <note>original: "aquisona"</note>. Indeed, as <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, an influential 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist</note> said, the octave creates such a union of voices that the two sounds seem to be uttered as one and the same. According to <term>Bacchius</term> <note>Bacchius Geron, an ancient Greek writer on music</note>, there are as many forms of consonances as there are species of the octave, which provide the fullness of all melody. One type consists of an <term>arithmetic division</term> <note>a way of dividing an interval where the smaller interval is on the bottom</note>; this occurs when the lowest, highest, and the common middle strings are struck together such that the lower interval is a fourth and the upper is a fifth. This structure is found in the <term>collateral tones</term> <note>also known as "plagal" modes</note>. Another type is joined according to a <term>harmonic division</term> <note>where the larger interval is on the bottom</note>; this happens when the strings are struck so that there is a fifth between the low and middle notes, and a fourth between the middle and high notes. This occurs in the <term>leading</term> or <term>authentic tones</term>. Thus, these three strings played together prove a harmonic consistency. The logic of this lies in the proportioned agreement of the outer notes and their differences on the <term>monochord</term> <note>original: "chordotono"; a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals</note>, which will be explained more clearly in the section on instrumental harmony.

The consonance of the octave contains seven figures or species. The **First** is formed from the first species of the fourth (stretching from low **A-re** to **D-sol-re**) and the first species of the fifth (from **D-sol-re** to high **A-la-mi-re**). It is divided at **D-sol-re** by an arithmetic arrangement. It contains five whole tones and two minor semitones; the first of these semitones is located in the second interval, and the second is in the fifth interval of the diatonic progression. A similar progression of voices and intervals always indicates the first figure of the octave, which can be extended across various pitches as seen here:

<image-desc>A musical diagram illustrating the "first figure" (prima figura) of the octave. On the left, a three-note chord on a five-line staff represents the notes A, D, and A. Brackets indicate the intervals: "diatessaron" (fourth) between the bottom and middle note, "diapente" (fifth) between the middle and top note, and "diapason" (octave) encompassing the outer notes. To the right, the intervals are expanded into a sequence of notes demonstrating the scale structure (A-B-C-D-E-F-G-A).</image-desc>

¶ The **Second figure** of the octave is formed from the second species of the fourth (from low **B-natural** <note>original: "♮ mi graui"</note> to low **E-la-mi**) and the second species of the fifth (from low **E-la-mi** to high **B-natural**). It is divided at low **E-la-mi** according to the arithmetic mean. Again, a similar process of voices and intervals will always produce the second form of the octave, with the first semitone in the first interval and the second semitone in the fourth interval. The present layout indicates how it is drawn across various intervals.

<image-desc>A second musical diagram illustrating the "second figure" (secunda figura) of the octave. It shows a chord (B, E, B) on the left with interval labels for "diatessaron," "diapente," and "diapason." The right side displays the sequence of notes on the staff (B-C-D-E-F-G-A-B), showing the specific distribution of tones and semitones for this species starting on B.</image-desc>

<summary>This page defines the octave (diapason) as a combination of a fourth and a fifth, explaining its arithmetic and harmonic divisions. It begins a catalog of the seven species of the octave, detailing the first and second figures and their specific intervallic structures.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, fourth, diatessaron, fifth, diapente, Ptolemy, Bacchius, arithmetic division, harmonic division, authentic modes, plagal modes, musical species, diatonic genus</keywords>

Arithmetic
mediation.
Third
Octave
</margin>
¶ The third form of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> is formed from the third species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> created between low C <note>original: "C fa ut"</note> and low F <note>original: "F faut"</note>, and the third species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> drawn from low F up to high C <note>original: "C sol faut"</note>. It is divided at low F by <term>arithmetic division</term> <note>original: "arythmetica consideratione"; in music theory, this refers to a division where the smaller interval (the fourth) is placed below the larger interval (the fifth)</note>, containing the first semitone at the third interval and the second at the seventh. Thus, a similar arrangement of voices and intervals proves the third form of the octave, which can be explained through many types of intervals, as is established here.

[A musical diagram consisting of two four-line staves with square Gregorian-style notation. The notes illustrate the 'third figure of the octave'. Vertical lines connect notes across the staves to show the division and structure of the octave.]

<margin>
Harmonic
mediation.
Fourth
Octave
</margin>
¶ The fourth form of the octave is composed of the first species of the fifth drawn from D <note>original: "D sol re"</note> to high A <note>original: "A la mi re"</note>, and from the first species of the fourth created from high A to high D. It is divided <term>harmonically</term> <note>original: "harmonice"; harmonic division places the larger interval (the fifth) below the smaller interval (the fourth)</note> at high A, holding the first semitone in the third interval and the second in the sixth. A consistent movement of sounds and intervals always produces the fourth form of the octave, which can also be supported by diverse intervals, as is evident here.

[A musical diagram illustrating the 'fourth octave form' with square notes on four-line staves. Horizontal and vertical lines indicate the structure of the intervals described in the text.]

mediation.
Fifth
Octave

¶ The fifth form of the octave is formed from the second species of the fifth drawn from low E <note>original: "E la mi"</note> to high B-natural <note>original: "♮ mi"</note> and from the second species of the fourth held between high B-natural and high E. It is divided at high B-natural according to the <term>harmonic proportion</term> <note>original: "harmonicam mediocritatem"</note>, containing the first semitone in the first interval and the second in the fifth. Every such progression of sounds and intervals will always complete the fifth species of the octave, which can also contain different intervals, as shown here.

[A musical diagram mapping the 'fifth octave form', with square notes on four-line staves. It visually demonstrates the second species of the fifth and fourth as described in the text.]

Octave

¶ The sixth form of the octave is constructed from the third species of the fifth drawn from low F <note>original: "F faut"</note> to high C <note>original: "C sol faut"</note>, and from the third species of the fourth created from high C to high F. It is divided at high C according to the harmonic middle; it contains the first semitone in the fourth interval and the second in the seventh. Such a consideration of sounds and intervals will always and everywhere prove the sixth formula of the octave, which can be varied by many kinds of intervals, as here.

<header>BOOK 4</header>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines using square notation. It illustrates the seventh species of the octave, starting on G and divided at D, showing an ascending and descending sequence of notes.</image-desc>

The seventh and final form of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> is conceived from the fourth species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> created from low G <note>original: "G solreut graui"</note> to high D <note>original: "D solre acutā"</note>, and from the first species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> drawn from high D <note>original: "D lasolre acutā"</note> to the highest G <note>original: "G solreut acutā"</note>. It is divided at high D by <term>harmonic division</term> <note>original: "harmonica consistentia"; in this arrangement, the larger interval (the fifth) is placed below the smaller interval (the fourth)</note>, possessing the first semitone at the third interval and the second at the sixth. A similar arrangement of both sounds and intervals will always produce the seventh form of the octave, whose intervals vary in this distinct pattern, as is clear here.

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines using square notation, showing an eight-note scale in square notes, with a C-clef on the fourth line from the bottom.</image-desc>

<margin>
Ancient
musicians
Ptolemy
</margin>
It is therefore established that these seven forms of the octave differ from one another: partly by the local variety of their semitones, and partly by the diversity of the formulas for the fourths and fifths. One species of the octave is also higher than another by a whole tone or a semitone according to the sequential distribution of sounds in the <term>diatonic</term> <note>The standard musical scale consisting of five whole tones and two semitones.</note> system; another is lower. Furthermore, in each octave, while the outer sounds remain fixed, the other internal formulas can be discovered by varying the dimensions of the intervals, which will indeed be demonstrated on a musical instrument. The ancient musicians called these octave formulas "constitutions," as if each species, consisting of a joining of harmonies, conducted a full body of melody, as it were. <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>original: "Ptholomeus"; Claudius Ptolemy, an influential Greco-Roman mathematician and music theorist.</note>, however, called them "modes," distinguished by their own names. So that he might fill out the whole system of the most perfect fifteen-string scale with these melodies, he added an eighth mode at the high end. This mode is distant from the first species of the octave not by its species, but by the full interval of that first species; he named this the **Hypermixolydian**, as if to say "above the Mixolydian." However, we shall explain all of these and their properties precisely on the harmonic instrument itself. Nevertheless, it is pleasing to list them here by name.

<margin>
Hypodorian
Hypophrygian
Hypolydian
</margin>
The first species of the octave, which we have drawn from low A <note>original: "Are graui"</note> to high A <note>original: "Alamire acutam"</note>, and which is divided at D <note>original: "D sol re"</note>, they called the **Hypodorian**. It is named as if it were "subject to the Dorian," or "plagal," or "subjugated" to the Dorian because its <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A scale of four notes.</note> flows back into the bass from the middle string, which the clergy call the **final**. The second species is called the **Hypophrygian**, as if it were "under the Phrygian"; it is drawn from low B <note>original: "♮ mi graui"</note> to high B <note>original: "♮ mi acutam"</note> and is divided at its own final, namely low E <note>original: "Elami graue"</note>. The third species of the octave they called the **Hypolydian**, as if "under the Lydian," since it subverts the high tetrachord of the Lydian in the opposite direction; we have drawn this from low C <note>original: "C faut graui"</note> to high C <note>original: "C solfaut acutam"</note>, divided <term>arithmetically</term> <note>An arithmetic division in music theory places the smaller interval (the fourth) below the larger interval (the fifth).</note> at its final, low F <note>original: "F faut grauem"</note>.

<margin>
Dorian
</margin>
The fourth species of the octave, drawn from low D <note>original: "D solre graui"</note> to high D <note>original: "D lasolre acutā"</note>, and <term>harmonically</term> divided at its co-final, namely high A <note>original: "Alamire acutā"</note>, was called the **Dorian** by those who first established this kind of melody, or (as some would have it) by those whom it most delighted.

<margin>
Phrygian
</margin>
The fifth species of the octave, which we have drawn from low E <note>original: "Elami graui"</note> to high E <note>original: "Elami acutā"</note>, harmonically divided at its co-final high B <note>original: "♮ mi acutam"</note>, they called the **Phrygian**, whose melody...

<summary>This page describes the seventh species of the octave and identifies the seven octave species with their classical Greek names (Hypodorian, Dorian, etc.). It explains how these modes are formed by different placements of fourths and fifths and mentions Ptolemy's addition of the Hypermixolydian mode.</summary>
<keywords>octave species, diapason, Ptolemy, Hypodorian, Hypophrygian, Hypolydian, Dorian, Phrygian, harmonic division, arithmetic division, modes, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the species of the octave. The previous page ended with a description of the seventh octave species (G to G) and mentioned how it is divided. This page identifies the sixth species as the Lydian mode, discusses the classification of the modes into "authentic" and "plagal" pairs, and introduces the four "maneriae" used in church music (Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus).</meta>

### BOOK ONE

<margin>The Lydian Mode</margin>
It is said that the Phrygians were the ones who both discovered and made the most use of it. The sixth species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>, which we have arranged between low F <note>original: "F faut grauem"</note> and high F <note>original: "F faut acutam"</note>, and which is <term>harmonically divided</term> <note>original: "harmonice mediata"; the octave is split into a fifth and a fourth</note> at its <term>co-final</term> <note>original: "confinalem"</note> C <note>original: "C solfa ut"</note>, was named the **Lydian** mode. This is because the Lydian people themselves were greatly delighted by its melody. The authority of the ancients commends these three—the **Dorian**, **Phrygian**, and **Lydian**—as being the most famous and most suitable for stirring the emotions of the soul. For this reason, they call them "leaders" <note>original: "duces"</note> and **authentic** <note>original: "autenticos"</note>. However, their "collaterals," because they render the emotions of the soul more relaxed, are called "peaceful" <note>original: "placales"</note> and "companions" <note>original: "comites"</note>. They are called "collateral" because they are led by similar "sides"—namely, the same species of <term>fourths</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and <term>fifths</term> <note>original: "diapentes"</note>—or because of the inversion of one side (the fourth) taken separately above. I will explain the natural harmonies of all these and their inventors more extensively in the section on instrumental harmony.

<margin>The Ambrosians</margin>
The seventh species of the <term>octave</term>, drawn between low G <note>original: "G solreut grauem"</note> and high G <note>original: "G solreut acutam"</note> and harmonically divided at D <note>original: "D lafolre"</note>, is called the **Mixolydian**, meaning "mixed Lydian." Frequently, the harmony is rendered sweeter by an alternating change between the Lydian and Mixolydian melodies. Our **Ambrosians** <note>followers of the liturgy and chant of St. Ambrose of Milan</note> observe this most especially in the church modes when they are accustomed to sing the fifth and seventh modes as if they were mixed, by changing the quality of the "hard B" (B-natural) to "soft B" (B-flat), using the species of the fifth or fourth. As was mentioned, **Ptolemy** established that an eighth octave figure, which remained after the seven different ones, should be called the **Hypermixolydian**. This is drawn from the **Mese** to the **Netenhypaboleon** and <term>arithmetically divided</term> <note>original: "Arythmetice mediata"; in this division, the smaller interval, the fourth, is placed below the larger fifth</note> at the **Paranetendiezeugmenon** <note>these are technical terms from the Greek Greater Perfect System, used here to define the pitch boundaries of the mode</note>. 

However, there are eight modes which some call **tones**, as if they were drawn from tones and their parts (namely, semitones). Others call them **tropes** because of the accidental conversion of one tone or semitone into another by raising or lowering it, or because of the alternating mixture of the species of fifths and fourths. Church authority has distinguished these into four **maneriae** <note>original: "maneriebus"; a classification system grouping an authentic and a plagal mode together</note>. They called the first class **Protus**, the second **Deuterus**, the third **Tritus**, and the fourth **Tetrardus** <note>these names are derived from the Greek words for first, second, third, and fourth</note>, assigning to each its own octave and fourth. Thus, the *Protus* observes the limits and boundaries of the **Dorian** and **Hypodorian**, as we see in many church melodies—for example, the Lenten salvation of the Virgin, *Salve Regina*. The *Deuterus* contains the **Phrygian** and **Hypophrygian**; the *Tritus*, the **Lydian** and **Hypolydian**; and the *Tetrardus*, the **Mixolydian** and its companion, which we can call the **Hypomixolydian** by mirroring the lower fourth of the others. 

Later generations divided these classes so as not to deviate from the order established by ancient authority. For the *Protus*, which is Dorian, they placed as first; the **Hypodorian** as second. They call the *Deuterus* third (which is the **Phrygian**), and the **Hypophrygian** fourth. The *Tritus* is fifth (namely, the **Lydian**), and the **Hypolydian** sixth. The *Tetrardus* or **Mixolydian** is seventh in order. The eighth does not have its own proper name, unless perhaps I call it the **Hypomixolydian** in imitation of the others. Later church musicians numbered these in pairs. Since one (the unit) is the first odd number, the first of each pair are called "odd" and "authentic" or "leaders." The second of each pair are "even" (from the number two) and are called "collateral" or "companions." They derive this famous order as follows: 

*   First: Odd and Authentic.
*   Second: Its even "peaceful" companion.
*   Third: Odd and Authentic.
*   Fourth: Its even "peaceful" companion.
*   Fifth: Odd and Authentic.
*   Sixth: Its even "peaceful" companion.
*   Seventh: Odd and Authentic.
*   Eighth and last: Its even "subjugated" companion.

<margin>What a tone is according to Guido</margin>
According to **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century music theorist</note>, a tone of this kind is a rule that judges all written and notable melodies by their ascent, descent, and final note. But we must speak about each of these and their properties one by one.

<summary>This page defines the Lydian and Mixolydian modes and explains the medieval church system of eight modes (tones) organized into four "maneriae" (Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus). It distinguishes between "authentic" (leader) and "plagal" (companion) modes and cites authorities like Ptolemy, the Ambrosian tradition, and Guido of Arezzo.</summary>

<keywords>Lydian mode, Mixolydian mode, authentic modes, plagal modes, church modes, Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus, Guido of Arezzo, Ambrosian chant, octave species, Ptolemy</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the Lydian mode and the general classification of modes into authentic and plagal pairs. It transitions from general theory to the specific technical construction of the First Mode (Dorian) and the definitions of how modes can be modified or "irregular."</meta>

### BOOK ONE

### ¶ Chapter VIII. On the various properties of the modes, and on the formation of the first mode.

<margin>
Roman and Ambrosian clerics
</margin>

The melody of the **first mode** <note>original: "primi toni"</note> is formed from the first species of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> between low D <note>original: "D solre"</note> and high A <note>original: "Alamire"</note>, arranged with the first species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> from high A to the even higher D <note>original: "D la sol re acutam"</note>. This is indeed the fourth form in the order of the **octave** <term>diapason</term>, in which we have placed the **Dorian** mode. It ends regularly on low D, which is where its fifth takes its beginning. Likewise, the melodies of the remaining modes have assigned the beginnings of their own proper fifths to their respective ending notes. This was done because the human voice becomes more exhausted when led into the high range than into the low; for as it moves from the high to a relaxed low range, it becomes more resonant. Thus, the ancients decided that the four "final" strings <note>The "final" is the note on which a chant ends, acting as a tonal home base.</note> of the modes should be chosen from the lower order of voices, specifically those where the conjoined **four-note scale** <term>tetrachord</term> of each "collateral" <note>Also known as the "plagal" mode.</note> mode could fit appropriately in the low range. 

Churchmen, however, following the Roman and Ambrosian traditions, place one note at the interval of a whole tone below the "final" note of any **authentic** <note>The "leading" or higher-range version of a mode.</note> melody. They do this as if there were a ninth note among the first eight notes of the octave, resulting in a **whole tone** <note>original: "sesquioctaua," referring to the 9:8 mathematical ratio of a whole tone.</note> relationship. While this arrangement naturally and diatonically suits the three "leaders"—namely the first, third, and seventh modes—the diatonic movement of sounds proves dissonant for the **fifth mode**. This is because below its final string, low F <note>original: "F faut graui"</note>, a **half-step** <note>original: "semitonium"</note> immediately and naturally falls. For this reason, some assert that the note itself should be lowered between low D and low E <note>original: "Elami"</note>—specifically, a major semitone below E—so that it stands a full tone away from the final in the low range. We, however, proceeding diatonically, believe that the natural arrangement of voices in the introductory scale should not be overturned at all, especially since those eight voices of the octave consonance are distinguished by a double dimension: five whole tones and two minor semitones. Therefore, I do not think the substitution of such a note is necessary as if it were essential to the harmony of the octave; rather, it is superfluous, though it may be approved by ecclesiastical authority.

The beginnings of the melody of the **first mode** usually follow their own rules: starting on C <note>original: "C faut"</note> is considered superfluous; on low D, low F, or low G <note>original: "G solreut"</note>, and on high A, they are considered natural and perfect. They are **commixed** on low E, but **mixed** and also superfluous on low A <note>original: "A re"</note> and low B-natural <note>original: "♮ mi"</note>. 

A mode is called **commixed** if it is authentic and contains the species of a mode other than its own collateral <note>e.g., if a Dorian melody uses pieces of a Phrygian scale structure.</note>. If, however, it is a **plagal** mode <note>original: "placalis," meaning "peaceful" or "subordinate."</note>, it is called commixed when it contains the consonant forms of a leader other than its own partner. 

A mode is called **mixed** if it is authentic and touches either the entire lower tetrachord of its plagal partner, or at least two of its strings. 

An **imperfect** mode, whether authentic or plagal, is one that does not fill its proper octave shape, falling short either in its fifth, its fourth, or both; they properly call such a mode **diminished**. 

They consider a mode to be **pluperfect** or **superfluous** when (if it is authentic) it holds one or two notes diatonically beyond the octave in the high range. If it is a plagal mode, then it oversteps in the low range. 

There are also those who call the melodies of individual modes **irregular** when they end on their **co-final** string. There are four co-final strings, corresponding to the combinations of the eight modes. A co-final string exists in any category of music at that voice where the formula of the fifth ends upward; thus, the co-final of any mode is distant from its final by a full interval of a fifth. 

For the first and second modes end regularly on low D, but irregularly on high A. 
The third and fourth modes end regularly on low E, but irregularly on high B-natural <note>original: "♮ mi acutam"</note>. 
The fifth and sixth modes end regularly on low F, but irregularly on high C <note>original: "C solfaut"</note>. 
The seventh and eighth modes end regularly on low G.

<summary>This page defines the technical construction of the First Mode (Dorian) and explains the various "accidents" or modifications of modes, including commixed, mixed, imperfect, and pluperfect forms. It also introduces the concept of "regular" versus "irregular" endings (finals vs. co-finals) based on the interval of a fifth.</summary>

<keywords>First Mode, Dorian, fifth, fourth, octave, authentic, plagal, commixed mode, mixed mode, imperfect mode, pluperfect mode, final, co-final, Roman tradition, Ambrosian tradition, musical theory</keywords>

# FIRST BOOK

<margin>
Gregorian
Ambrosian
</margin>

...they end on the low [D]. They may end irregularly on high D <note>original: "D la sol re acuta"</note>, although in Antiphons, Graduals, and other Gregorian melodies, they rarely allow a **co-final** <term>confinalis: a secondary note where a chant may end instead of its standard 'final' note</term>; for they say these always end regularly. The followers of Saint Ambrose <note>The Ambrosian rite is a North Italian liturgical tradition distinct from the Roman rite</note>, however, more often end this seventh mode on its co-final, but the eighth mode rarely. Furthermore, every mode can be conceived in an introductory sense wherever its "sides" or "species" <note>The specific patterns of whole steps and half steps that make up the fourths and fifths of the scale</note> are known to extend; when a mode is drawn outside its natural and primary arrangement, we can call it **fictitious** or **acquired** <term>musica ficta: the use of notes outside the standard hand-hexachord system</term>.

One must also consider a distinction in the melodies of these modes which **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo (d. 1033), the most influential music theorist of the Middle Ages</note> wanted to be understood: as much as is pronounced continuously in any chant until the voice rests. This is clearly declared through **neumes** <term>neuma: the early system of musical notation used before the modern five-line staff</term>. 

<margin>
Distinction
Guido
Neuma
</margin>

A **neume** is a grouping of voices or notes to be pronounced properly in a single breath. *Neuma* in Greek is usually interpreted as "nod" or "sign" in Latin. Notators describe these in Antiphons, nocturnal responsories, and graduals with a certain line—acting as a pause that ends the melodies—which encompasses all the intervals of the lines and divides the "distinctions" [musical phrases]. This line indicates the breathing of the voice itself. One should not breathe before the last syllable of any word, unless several notes are placed over a single syllable; in that case, by urgent necessity, the singer may breathe after a syllable that is not the last. Furthermore, according to Guido, these distinctions should be made and ended where the mode in which they are set can most often and most fittingly take its regular beginnings.

It is also a wonder (as Guido’s own opinion suggests) how that most holy high priest **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I (the Great), traditionally credited with the collection and standardization of 'Gregorian' chant</note>, in the manner of those who are sleepy, seems to exhort us to keep watch in the nocturnal responsories with a heavy and relaxed style. In the Antiphons, he sounds plain and sweet. In the Introits, however, he cries out and calls us to the divine office as if with the voice of a herald. In the Alleluia and the Verses, which were ascribed to the Ambrosian melody, he seems to rejoice sweetly in divine jubilation. But in the Tracts and Graduals, he is known to proceed in a plain, extended, and humble voice. In the Offertories and Communions, he revealed how much he excelled in the emotions of this melody. For in them, there is every kind of elevation, arrangement, extension, and doubling: a sweet delight for those who know it, and a refreshment from labor for those learning it. It is a wonderful arrangement, far distant from the systems of other musicians. Not only did he introduce this art of music, but he is known to have contributed authority and growth to the discipline of music. 

Guido’s own words testify that Saint Ambrose wonderfully sought out only the sweetness of the melody. Many others also contributed various gifts to his treasury, just as they received them from the Lord. This example illustrates the ecclesiastical melody of the first mode:

...proceeding by a note above the final of the antiphon using these syllables: *fa, sol, la*. These are **mediated** <term>mediatio: the melodic inflection or pause at the midpoint of a psalm verse</term>. Likewise, these psalm tunes are mediated in different ways and are ended in various ways according to the notation of their *World without end, Amen* <note>original: "Seculorum Amen"</note> or their **Euouae** <note>original: "Euouae"; a mnemonic word formed from the vowels of "saeculorum Amen," used in choral books to indicate the melodic ending, or "difference," of a psalm tone</note>. For *Euouae* represents nothing other than "world without end, Amen"; all its vowels are collected into one for the sake of brevity. ¶ To whichever tone the chants of the **Graduals** <term>graduale: a chant sung after the first scripture reading in the Mass</term>, the **Alleluia**, and the **Responsories** <term>responsorium: a type of chant where a soloist’s verse is answered by a choir</term> are assigned, their verses are also to be ascribed to that same tone. Thus, just as the beginning of the *Euouae* relates to the end of the antiphon, so the beginning of the verses relates to the preceding ending of their chants. When a chant belongs to an **authentic mode** <term>autenticus tonus: the "higher" version of a musical mode (in this case, the Dorian)</term>, its verse serves that same mode; and vice-versa. However, a verse of a **plagal** <term>plagalis: the "lower" version of a mode, which shares the same final note but has a different range</term> chant often reaches the melodic shapes of an authentic modulation, and vice-versa. In that case, both are considered as a single **maneria** <term>maneria: a medieval classification that pairs an authentic and a plagal mode together (e.g., Modes 1 and 2)</term>. 

Furthermore, the beginning of the *Euouae* of the first tone is distant by the interval of a **fifth** <term>diapente: the musical interval of a perfect fifth</term> above the final note of the preceding antiphon. Indeed, from the verses of the **Nocturn Responsories** <note>chants used during the night-time office of Matins</note> of the first tone, the followers of Gregory usually select certain necessary notes by which the *Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit* <note>original: "Gloria patri & filio & spiritui sancto"</note> is sung most fittingly when needed, in this way:

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the First Mode (Dorian) to the technical definition and practical application of the Second Mode (Hypodorian). # BOOK ONE

## On the formation of the second tone. Chapter Nine.

<margin>Marchetto</margin>
The melody of the second tone is formed from the first common figure of the **fifth** <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term>, which also belongs to the first tone. It is also formed from the first species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> extending from the note **A re** to **D sol re**. For this is the very figure of the **octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> which we have placed as the first in the order between **A re** and the high **A la mi re**, which is indeed ascribed to the **Hypodorian** mode <term>Hypodorio: the "lower Dorian" or second mode, which uses the same notes as the first mode but sits a fourth lower</term>. I call that a "common species" which remains unchanged in any **authentic** mode <term>autentico: the primary version of a mode</term> and its **plagal** mode <term>placali: the "side" or related mode that shares the same final note but has a lower range</term> when considered on the same strings. Therefore, it is clear that the fourth in the authentic mode and its **collateral** mode <note>original: "collaterali"; another name for the plagal mode</note> is not a common species, as **Marchetto** <note>Marchetto of Padua, an influential 14th-century music theorist</note> asserts; this is because the fourth occupies a higher position in the authentic mode and a lower position in the plagal mode. In the latter, the fourth is known to be placed below the fifth, while in the former, it is above the fifth. Indeed, the notes that provide the beginnings for the second tone are the low notes **A re**, **C fa ut**, **D sol re**, **E la mi**, and **F fa ut**. However, in its extension or surplus (though rarely), they have placed its beginning on **ut**. In the note **D sol re**, it has regularly obtained its proper final ending, the formula of which this illustration proves:

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff with a C-clef on the third line. It contains square notation neumes representing a plainchant melody.</image-desc>

->In honor of the apostles, Bassianus built a new temple for the Lord.<-

¶ Many also agree that, by "ecclesiastical license," the plagal tones may reach a single note higher than the fifth in the high range. Therefore, if an **antiphon** <term>antiphona: a short chant sung before and after a psalm</term> ends on **D sol re**, and the start of its *world without end* <note>original: "seculorum"; referring to the beginning of the ending formula for the psalm tone</note> is on the low **F fa ut**, it shall be judged by the law of the second tone. They begin its **psalmody** <term>psalmodiam: the melodic formula for singing a psalm</term> by proceeding from a single note lowered from the final, using these notes: *re, fa*. This is shown in the following illustration:

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff with a C-clef on the third line. It contains square notation including rhombic shapes and standard square neumes.</image-desc>

->The second tone begins thus, is mediated thus, and is finished thus. **Euouae** <note>a mnemonic used to indicate the vowels of "saeculorum Amen," showing the melody of the ending</note>.<-

¶ It is also customary for the verses of the **nocturnal responsories** <note>chants used in the night office of Matins</note> of this second tone to proceed with their own melody, starting one note below the final note of the preceding chant; specifically with these notes: *ut, re, mi, fa*. Likewise, the notes by which the *Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit* is appropriately sung are also usually drawn from these melodies in this manner:

It also provides the specific musical formulas for starting and ending psalm verses in this mode.</summary>

## On the formula of the third tone. Chapter Ten.

The melody of the third tone is formed from the second species of the **fifth** <term>diapentes: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> leading from low **E-la-mi** to high **B-mi**; and from the second species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> made from high **B-mi** to higher **E-la-mi**. For this is the fifth figure of the **octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> which we have named the **Phrygian**. For this tone, four beginnings occur: the notes **E-la-mi**, **F-fa-ut**, and **G-sol-re-ut** in the lower register, and high **C-sol-fa-ut**. Its final note is low **E-la-mi**. The following formula is suitable for its composition:

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff with a C-clef on the second line from the top. The melody is written in square neumes.</image-desc>

> ¶ The priest Ambrose baptizes Augustine; together they immediately sing "We praise
<note>original: "Baptizat Augustinum sacerdos Ambrosius ambo statim modulant' te deū"</note>
<note>This refers to a popular medieval legend that Saints Ambrose and Augustine spontaneously composed the "Te Deum" hymn during Augustine’s baptism in 387 AD.</note>

<image-desc>A short segment of the musical staff concluding the melody above.</image-desc>

> Thee, O God."
<note>original: "lau da mus"</note>

¶ The **Euouae** <term>Euouae: a mnemonic for the vowels of "seculorum Amen," the final words of the "Gloria Patri," used in liturgical books to show how a psalm tone should end</term> of the third tone begins on **C-sol-fa-ut**—that is, on the sixth note above the "final" <note>the concluding note of the melody</note> of the antiphon according to the **Gregorians** <note>followers of the Roman chant tradition associated with St. Gregory the Great</note>, where the **four-note scale** <term>tetrachordum: a sequence of four notes</term> taken at the beginning of the psalm-singing is concluded. However, the **Ambrosians** <note>followers of the Milanese chant tradition associated with St. Ambrose</note>, proceeding more sweetly, place the beginning of this third tone's **Euouae** on the fifth note above the final of the antiphon—the note on which its figure of the **fifth** <term>diapentes: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> ends—treating it as a sweeter harmonic division, just as they do in the other **authentic** <term>autenticis: the primary or "master" modes (1, 3, 5, and 7), which typically have a higher range than their "plagal" counterparts</term> modes.

<summary>This page defines the Third Mode (Phrygian) using the mathematical relationships of the fourth and fifth intervals and discusses the different liturgical traditions for ending psalm tones.</summary>
<keywords>Third Mode, Phrygian, Euouae, Gregorian chant, Ambrosian chant, interval of a fifth, interval of a fourth, St. Augustine, St. Ambrose, musical theory</keywords>

### ¶ On the formula of the fourth tone. Caput undecimum.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap 'Q' depicting a seated figure in a study with a window overlooking a landscape.</image-desc>The melody of the fourth tone is formed from the second species of the **fifth** <term>diapentes: a perfect fifth</term>, which we also assigned to the third tone; and from the second species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron: a perfect fourth</term> drawn from low **B-natural** <term>mi graue: the note B at the bottom of the medieval scale</term> to low **E** <term>E la mi: the note E</term>. For it is the second species in the order of the **octave** <term>diapason: an octave</term>, drawn from low **B-natural** to middle **B-natural** <term>mi acutam</term>, which they call the **Hypophrygian** <note>The "plagal" or lower-range version of the Phrygian mode.</note>. The fourth tone, moreover, adopts six starting notes: namely, the low notes **C**, **D**, **E**, **F**, and **G** <note>original: "C fa ut, D sol re, E la mi, F fa ut, & G sol re ut"</note>, as well as middle **A** <term>A la mi re</term>. It ends regularly on low **E**, as the present example demonstrates.

¶ Marcellinus the priest and Peter the exorcist,
martyrs of Christ, intercede for us.
<note>original: "Marcellinus sacerdos & Petrus exorcista martyres Christi intercedant pro nobis." These were early Christian martyrs whose feast day is June 2nd.</note>

---

¶ This fourth tone <note>The Hypophrygian mode.</note> often ends on the high *A-la-mi-re* <note>In the medieval hexachord system, this is the note A above middle C.</note>, proceeding through what are called "acquired" species <note>This refers to transposition, where a mode is moved from its natural position to a different pitch level.</note>. For it is the second form of the *diapente* <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> suitable to it, led from that same *A-la-mi-re* to the high *E-la-mi* with the quality of a "soft B" <note>original: "b mollis," meaning the use of a B-flat.</note>; or, as it were, from the *Mese* <term>Mese: the "middle" note in the Ancient Greek musical system</term> to the *Nete Diezeugmenon* <term>Nete Diezeugmenon: the "highest of the disjunct," the top note of the third tetrachord</term>, not proceeding through the *synemmenon* <term>synemmenon: the "conjunct" tetrachord, which introduced the B-flat</term> tetrachord. Furthermore, the second form of the *diatessaron* <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> matching this type of fourth tone is led from the low *E-la-mi* to the *A-la-mi-re* itself—which is then its proper final—or from the *Hypate Meson* <term>Hypate Meson: the "lowest of the middle" notes in the Greek system</term> to the *Mese*. Every *Euouae* <note>original: "Euouae" — a shorthand mnemonic representing the vowels in the Latin "saeculorum Amen," used to show the ending of a psalm tone.</note> of the fourth tone begins on the fourth note above the final of the preceding antiphon. Its psalmody is taken up on that same pitch, proceeding with these notes: *la, sol, la*, as is perceived in this description.

<image-desc>Two musical staves. The first staff illustrates the intonation ("sic incipit"), mediation ("sic mediatur"), and termination ("& sic finitur") of the fourth tone. The second staff shows two variations of the "Euouae" (differentia).</image-desc>

->The fourth tone begins thus | is mediated thus | & is finished thus<-
->World without end, Amen | World without end, Amen<-

---

¶ But if the verse of a responsory that ends on low *E-la-mi* begins on high *A-la-mi-re* (that is, on the fourth note above the final of the preceding chant), it will be of the fourth tone. Alternatively, when the responsory ends on high *A-la-mi-re* and the beginning of the verse is on *D-la-sol-re*—following the "soft B" <note>B-flat</note> quality—it will again be of the "acquired" fourth tone. Its natural melody for "Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit" proceeds with these notes:

<image-desc>The start of a musical staff at the bottom of the page showing the plainchant for the Doxology.</image-desc>

<note>original: "Gloria patri & filio & spi-"</note>

### ¶ Chapter 12: On the Construction of the Fifth Tone.

The melody of the fifth tone <note>Commonly known as the Lydian mode.</note> is formed from the third species of the *diapente* <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> extending from low *F-fa-ut* to high *C-sol-fa-ut*, and from the third species of the *diatessaron* <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> which is formed from *C-sol-fa-ut* to high *F-fa-ut*. This is, in fact, the sixth form of the *diapason* <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> in order, which is called the Lydian mode. Four starting notes are suitable for the fifth tone: low *F-fa-ut* and *G-sol-re-ut*, and high *A-la-mi-re* and *C-sol-fa-ut*. It regularly ends on low *F-fa-ut* <note>In medieval music, the "final" is the note on which a melody concludes, defining its mode.</note>, as is noted in this example:

<image-desc>Four-line musical staff with square notation and a C-clef. The text below the staff reads: "¶ Precibus & meritis beati blasii martiris defende nos deus"</image-desc>

¶ Through the prayers and merits of the blessed martyr Blaise, defend us, O God,
<note>original: "Precibus & meritis beati blasii martiris defende nos deus." Saint Blaise is the patron saint of throat ailments; this chant was likely used in liturgies involving the blessing of throats.</note>

<image-desc>Four-line musical staff with square notation and a C-clef. The text below the staff reads: "ab omni malo gutturis"</image-desc>

from every evil of the throat.
<note>original: "ab omni malo gutturis."</note>

¶ The *Euouae* <note>A mnemonic for the vowels in "saeculorum Amen," the conclusion of the "Gloria Patri." It is used to show the specific ending of a psalm tone.</note> of the fifth tone, however, begins on *C-sol-fa-ut*—that is, on the fifth note above the "final" of the antiphon. Its psalmody <note>The specific melodic formula used for singing the verses of a psalm.</note>, beginning in unison with the final note, proceeds with these notes: *fa-la-fa*; as is noted in this transition:

<image-desc>Four-line musical staff with square notation and a C-clef. The text below the staff reads: "¶ Quintus tonus sic incipit sic mediatur & sic finitur Euouae:"</image-desc>

¶ The fifth tone begins thus, is mediated thus, and is finished thus: *Euouae*.
<note>This is a standard pedagogical formula used to teach students how to sing the beginning (intonation), middle (mediation), and end (termination) of a psalm tone.</note>

¶ And likewise, those beginnings of verses that start on *C-sol-fa-ut*, whose responsories <note>A type of chant where a soloist and choir alternate.</note> have ended on low *F-fa-ut*, are assigned to the fifth tone. The melody of these verses—"Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit"—proceeds in this manner.

### ¶ Chapter 13: On the Formula of the Sixth Tone.

The melody of the sixth tone <note>Commonly known as the Hypolydian mode, the "plagal" or lower-range counterpart to the Lydian mode.</note> is formed from the third species of the *diapente* <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term>, which is also assigned to the fifth tone; and from the third species of the *diatessaron* <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> brought from low *C-fa-ut* to low *F-fa-ut*. This is, in fact, the third figure of the *diapason* <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> arranged between low *C-fa-ut* and high *C-sol-fa-ut*, which they call the Hypolydian. Four specific starting notes are assigned to it: low *C-fa-ut*, *D-sol-re*, and *F-fa-ut*, as well as high *A-la-mi-re*. It regularly ends on low *F-fa-ut*. The progression of this tone is perceived in this composition:

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff with square and diamond-shaped neumes. This represents a chant addressed to Saint Erasmus.</image-desc>

->O Holy Erasmus, renowned martyr, pour out prayers to the Lord for our salvation.<-
<note>original: "Sancte Herasme martyrī clite funde p̄ces ad dominum nostra pro salute." Saint Erasmus, also known as Saint Elmo, was a 3rd-century martyr and one of the Fourteen Holy Helpers.</note>

¶ When, however, an antiphon <note>A short chant sung before and after a psalm.</note> ends on low *F-fa-ut*, and the beginning of its *Euouae* <note>A mnemonic representing the vowels of "seculorum Amen," used to indicate the melodic ending of a psalm tone.</note> is held on the third note above the final—namely, on high *A-la-mi-re*—it is to be categorized in the sixth tone. Its psalmody <note>The melodic formula used for singing the verses of a psalm.</note> begins in unison, proceeding with these syllables: *fa, sol, la*, as shown here:

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff illustrating the melodic formula for the sixth tone (Sextus Tonus).</image-desc>

->The sixth tone begins thus, mediates thus, and ends thus.<-
<note>original: "Sextus tonus sic incipit sic mediatur & sic finit." This is a standard pedagogical formula used to demonstrate the intonation (beginning), mediation (middle pause), and termination (end) of a specific church mode.</note>

¶ Similarly, the verses of the responsories of the sixth tone begin on <term>A-la-mi-re: a specific pitch in the medieval system of solmization</term>: which is the third note <note>original: "chorda" (string); theorists often referred to notes as "strings" based on the monochord used for teaching.</note> above the final note of the preceding chant. ## ¶ Chapter XIV: On the Formula of the Seventh Tone.

The melody of the seventh tone is formed from the fourth species of the <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> extending from low <term>G-sol-re-ut</term> to high <term>D-la-sol-re</term>; and from the first species of the <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> made from high <term>D-la-sol-re</term> to high <term>G-sol-re-ut</term>. This is, in fact, the seventh figure of the <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> which we have arranged between low <term>G-sol-re-ut</term> and high <term>G-sol-re-ut</term>; it is called the **Mixolydian**. For the seventh tone has five specific starting notes: namely, low *G-sol-re-ut*, *A-la-mi-re*, *B-natural-mi*, and high *C-fa-ut* and *D-la-sol-re*. Also, low *F-fa-ut* is permitted by ecclesiastical arrangement; and it regularly ends on low *G-sol-re-ut*, as is marked in this arrangement.

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation on four-line staves with square neumes. The lyrics read: The same faith and passion truly made Protasius and Gervasius to be brothers.</image-desc>
<note>Protasius and Gervasius were twin brothers martyred in the 2nd century; they are the patron saints of Milan.</note>

¶ The <term>Euouae: a mnemonic representing the vowels of "saeculorum Amen," the end of the Doxology</term> therefore beginning on *D-la-sol-re*: whose antiphon ends on low *G-sol-re-ut*: is usually assigned to the seventh tone. The ancients began its psalmody on the fifth note above the final note of the antiphon, proceeding with these notes: *sol, fa, sol*. Later generations, however, have frequently used the fourth note above the final for the beginning of this kind of psalmody: singing with these syllables: *fa, mi, fa, sol*: as is established here.

<summary>This page concludes the musical rules for the sixth tone and introduces the seventh tone (Mixolydian), detailing its range, its construction from a perfect fifth and fourth, and its standard starting and ending pitches.</summary>
<keywords>Seventh Tone, Mixolydian mode, diapente, diatessaron, diapason, Guidonian solmization, Euouae, Protasius and Gervasius</keywords>

¶ Furthermore, there are other endings for this seventh psalmody, which you will find marked in the notation itself. There also exists another psalmody of the seventh tone which the <term>Gregorians: those who follow the Roman style of plainsong</term> observe for the <term>antiphon: a short chant sung before and after a psalm</term> *We who live, we bless the Lord* <note>original: "Nos qui vivimus benedicimus dominum"</note>, ending on the <term>confinal: a secondary or alternative ending note for a mode</term>, since it is led by an acquired note and diminished in the high register by a single voice, as is perceived in the present formula.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff with square and diamond-shaped (liquescent) neumes. There is a C-clef on the third line and a B-flat accidental. The text below corresponds to the antiphon "We who live" and the start of Psalm 113.</image-desc>

antiphon: We who live, we bless the Lord. psalm: When Israel went out

from Egypt, the house of Jacob from a barbarian people. <note>original: "israel de egypto domus iacob de populo barbaro"</note>

¶ For this psalmody retains a melody very similar to the fourth tone at the beginning and the sixth tone in the middle, because the antiphon is mixed with it; it includes its own species of the <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term>, namely the third species. At the end, it resembles the third tone. Truly, even if the <term>Ambrosians: those who follow the musical tradition of St. Ambrose of Milan</term> follow the same notes and clefs for this antiphon, they nevertheless celebrate a different melody for this psalmody; for they begin on the <term>confinal: co-final</term> note of the antiphon itself, namely in low <term>G-sol-re-ut: the pitch G</term>, where their figure of the <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> ends in the high register, just as they observe in the other <term>authentic: the primary, higher-ranging musical modes</term> tones, in this manner.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff for an Ambrosian chant, featuring square and diamond-shaped neumes. C-clef on the fourth line.</image-desc>

antiphon: We who live, we bless the Lord. psalm: When Israel went out

Israel from Egypt, the house of Jacob from a barbarian people. <note>original: "yſrael ex gypto domus iacob de populo barbaro"</note>

¶ However, I shall first present this antiphon according to the proper keys of the seventh tone from Saint Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397) was the Bishop of Milan and is traditionally credited with establishing the Ambrosian chant tradition, which differs from the Roman Gregorian chant.</note>, where they demonstrate both the seventh <term>octave species: the specific arrangement of whole and half steps within an octave</term> and the Mixolydian mode itself. An ancient book of antiphons revealed to me that it was ended on low G <note>original: "G solreut"</note> and on D <note>original: "D lasolre"</note> as its proper <term>confinal: an alternative note where a chant can end, usually a fifth above the final</term>—which he established most frequently in the <term>authentic modes: the four primary modes (1, 3, 5, 7) that have a higher range than their "plagal" counterparts</term>—as is noted in this sequence.

<image-desc>Musical notation on two four-line staves with square neumes and C-clefs.</image-desc>

antiphon: We who live, we bless the Lord. <note>original: "Nos qui vivimus benedicimus dominum"</note> psalm: When Israel went out <note>original: "In exitu"</note>

Israel from Egypt, the house of Jacob from a barbarian people. <note>original: "yſrael ex egypto domus iacob de populo barbaro"</note>

¶ We also propose the same judgment concerning the antiphon *Not unto us, O Lord, but to your name give glory* <note>original: "Nomini tuo da gloriam domine"</note>, which we Ambrosians sing alternately over the same psalm *When Israel went out of Egypt* during Sunday Vespers.

¶ Furthermore, our Ambrosians often assign shorter antiphons—whose notes ascend only a <term>ditone: a musical interval of two whole tones, equivalent to a major third</term> from the final note—to the authentic tone, because the psalmody is arranged for this antiphon.

antiphon: Where charity and love are, there is God. <note>original: "Ubi charitas et amor ibi deus est"</note> psalm: Behold how good <note>original: "Ecce quam bonum"</note>

and how pleasant it is for brothers to dwell together in unity. <note>original: "et quam iocundum habitare fratres in unum"</note>

# BOOK
[Page 159]

¶ On the other hand, some judge differently when the small notes have taken an upward interval of a minor third <note>original: "trihemitonieam", an interval of three semitones</note> from the final note; for then they sustain those chants with a plagal <term>plagal: a mode where the melody moves above and below the final note, often sounding more "settled" or "subdued" than its authentic counterpart</term> consideration. There are also some chants—for instance, the verses of the short Responsories and the Responsories themselves—whose notes are arranged with an equal distance above and below the final note: for example, one below the final and one above the final. In that case, if the note placed below is separated by a wider interval, such as a whole tone <note>original: "tonia"</note> from the final, and the higher note stands at a narrower distance, namely a semitone <note>original: "semitoniæ"</note>, from the same final, it shall in my opinion be ascribed to a plagal melody. 

However, it is ascribed to the authentic <term>authentic: the primary version of a mode, typically characterized by a higher melodic range</term> when the upper note exceeds the final by a whole tone in height, and the lower note is a semitone below it in depth. But when the distance of both the higher and lower notes from the final note itself is equal—for instance, a whole tone lower below the final and likewise a whole tone higher above the final—I feel the chant or melody should be ascribed to its own <term>maneria: a medieval system of grouping modes into pairs (authentic and plagal) based on their shared final note</term>, as if it were a participant of both tones, namely the plagal and the authentic. This we can properly call a mixed tone <note>original: "tonum promiscuum"</note>. From the verses of the nocturnal Responsories of the seventh tone, the notes are taken with which the Gregorians appropriately sing the "Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit" <note>original: "Gloria patri & filio & spiritui sancto"</note> in this manner:

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff featuring square notation for a Gregorian chant.</image-desc>

->Glo ry be to the Fa ther and to the Son and to<-

<image-desc>Continuation of the musical staff with square notation.</image-desc>

->the Ho ly Spi rit.<-

### ¶ On the composition of the eighth tone. Chapter XV.

The melody of the eighth tone is formed from the fourth species of the fifth <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term>, which is also assigned to the seventh tone, and from the first species of the fourth <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> leading from low D <note>original: "D solre"</note> to low G <note>original: "G solreut"</note>. Because it occupies the lower <term>tetrachord: a scale of four notes</term> of the first tone, they say it shares in that nature. However, it receives no name in the order of the octave modes <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> unless perhaps by a subsequent reversal, as we said in the seventh chapter, I should call it the Hypomixolydian <note>The plagal counterpart to the Mixolydian (seventh) tone</note>. It has, moreover, five starting notes: namely low D, F, and G, and high A and C; and it ends regularly on low G <note>original: "G solreut"</note>, as this arrangement shows.

<image-desc>A four-line musical staff featuring square notation for a Gregorian chant. The melody contains several ligatures and complex neumes.</image-desc>

->O Vir gin of vir gins, Ma ry, star of the sea, suc[cour]<-

# FIRST [BOOK]

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff using square and diamond-shaped notes (neumes). Below the staff is the text: Help us in our misery <note>original: "curre nobis miseris"</note>.</image-desc>

For it is clear from the derived principles of the tones that those pleasing chants [reaching] to the fifth above their final—namely, where their fifth-form <term>diapentes: an interval of a fifth</term> ends—have not had their principles assigned by the church authorities. However, the *Euouae* <note>A shorthand for the vowels in "seculorum Amen," used to indicate the melodic ending of a psalm tone</note> of the eighth psalmody begins on C *sol-fa-ut*: raised indeed by the interval of a fourth <term>diatessaron: an interval of a fourth</term> above the final note of the antiphon. Its psalmody begins on the final itself, proceeding with these little notes: *ut re fa*, or *ut re ut* in solemn [chants], as is evident here.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a four-line staff divided into sections. The first section is labeled "The eighth tone begins thus". The middle section is labeled "it mediates thus". The final section is labeled "and it ends thus. Euouae."</image-desc>

From the preceding, therefore, it is easily perceived that no psalmody should be initiated in a higher voice than that which is known at the beginning of its *seculorum amen* <note>the ending of the Gloria Patri</note>. Nevertheless, they perform ferial <term>ferial: relating to ordinary weekdays in the church calendar, which usually required simpler music</term> and minor psalmodies with a faster measure of time, and solemn ones more slowly. Consequently, a psalmody follows the tone of its antiphon; for in Greek, *Anti* is interpreted in Latin as "for" and "against." Likewise, in composition, they say it signifies "equality." Thus, an Antiphon is, as it were, a modulation equal with the psalmody, or a symphony <note>original: "simphoniam", used here to mean a harmonious agreement</note> of one and the same tone arranged within the chant and its psalm. Indeed, from the verses of the nocturnal responsories of this eighth tone, the Gregorians take a suitable modulation for "Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit" <note>original: "Gloria patri & filio & spiritui sancto"</note> in this manner.

->C iiii<-

<margin>The Ambrosians</margin>
<margin>Augustine</margin>

¶ Church singers are also accustomed in chants—such as the Alleluia and the Verse <note>original: "Versus"</note>, and many others of that genre—to modulate around one and the same vowel with a continuous and perpetual passage. Those of the Ambrosian rite <note>followers of the liturgical tradition of St. Ambrose of Milan</note> call this by the common name "melody" <note>original: "melodiam"</note>; for they (as they say) run through the divine Holy Trinity and angelic harmony in mind and spirit during these passages. These same singers likewise generally begin the psalmody <term>psalmody: the act of singing psalms</term> of any tone on that note where the beginning of its *Euouae* <note>a mnemonic for the vowels of "seculorum Amen," used to indicate the melodic ending of a psalm tone</note> has been marked. They continue it with the same pitch without the interruption of any variation <note>original: "differentie"</note> until the *Euouae* itself, the notation of which follows the end of the psalmody. For, as Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo</note> testifies, the end is that to which all things are referred, and for the sake of which the rest are made. Sometimes, in certain psalmodies (especially of the first tone), these same Ambrosians solemnly modulate the beginnings and mediations <term>mediation: the melodic inflection or pause in the middle of a psalm verse</term> of the Gregorian rite <note>the Roman liturgical tradition attributed to St. Gregory the Great</note>. Indeed, the endings of such psalmodies, which Ambrosian modulations more frequently accompany, are brought to light in these following descriptions.
