FIRST [BOOK]

<image-desc>Musical notation on four-line staves with square neumes. The first staff contains two examples of the "Euouae" (liturgical shorthand for the doxology ending) for the seventh tone. Below the staff, the text "Euouae of the seventh tone" is printed twice, corresponding to the segments above.</image-desc>

->Euouae of the seventh tone. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Euouae of the seventh tone.<-

<image-desc>A second musical staff with square neumes, showing two examples for the eighth tone. Below the staff, the text "Euouae of the eighth tone" is printed twice.</image-desc>

->Euouae of the eighth tone. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Euouae of the eighth tone.<-

¶ There are, furthermore, several melodies written with very few notes. For this reason, they are rendered imperfect by a solemn diminution <note>a "diminution" in this context refers to a reduction or shortening of the standard melodic form</note>. If their *Euouae* <note>original: "Euouae"; a mnemonic representing the vowels of "seculorum Amen," used to indicate the specific melodic ending of a psalm tone</note> does not follow them, it is difficult for anyone to judge whether they should be assigned to a plagal tone <term>plagal tone: a mode where the range typically sits lower, circling around or below the final note</term>. Because of this, some have granted this kind of discernment to certain "judicial notes" <term>judicial notes: specific pitches used as markers to diagnose the mode of a chant</term>. For the first maneria <note>original: "maneriei"; a system of grouping an authentic and a plagal mode together based on their shared final note</note>, in which the first and second tones conclude, they assign low F-f-ut <note>The note F below middle C</note>. To the second, which rules the third and fourth tones, they assign low G-sol-re-ut. To the third, in which the fifth and sixth tones are found, they assign high A-la-mi-re. To the fourth, which contains the seventh and eighth tones, they assign high B-natural-mi. This is done so that each judicial note stands apart from the final of its own maneria by an interval of a third upwards, counting the notes inclusively according to the diatonic arrangement. And for this reason, the judicial notes of the first and second maneria stand at the interval of a semitone <note>meaning a minor third from the final, such as D to F</note> upwards from their finals. However, the judicial notes of the third and fourth maneria are separated from their own finals by the distance of a ditone <term>ditone: an interval of two whole tones, or a major third</term> upwards.

Indeed, the natural and diatonic arrangement of the notes requires this. Therefore, when in a melody of any maneria there are more notes arranged above that judicial note (that is, in the higher range) than there are notes brought down into the lower range, that melody is assigned to the authentic classification <term>authentic: a mode whose range primarily extends a fifth or an octave above the final note</term>. It is otherwise, however, if the lower notes fill out a greater number; for then they consider such a melody to be plagal. Yet they have established that notes written exactly upon the judicial notes themselves should be counted for neither side. Nevertheless, while the truth of this kind of judgment often shines through in more extensive melodies—those written with many notes—in those melodies which a scarcity of notes expresses (such as the Antiphon *Where Charity and Love* <note>original: "Vbi charitas & amor"</note> when it is proposed), this kind of judgment is fallacious <note>original: "commentitium"</note>. 

By the consensus of many, however, a figure of its fifth <term>diapente: the interval of a fifth</term> repeated several times upwards from the final will, by that single interval most especially, declare the melody to be authentic. But they think a song is plagal if there is such a repetition only of the fourth note upwards from its final. As for the melodies of the *Glory be to the Father and the Son* <note>original: "Gloria patri & filio"</note>, the psalmodies of the Gregorian Introits, and the Ambrosian psalmodies, we leave these for each person to investigate for themselves for the sake of brevity.

¶ The first book of the musical practice of Franchino Gaffurio of Lodi ends successfully.

# THE END.

<meta>This page begins the second book of Franchino Gaffurio’s treatise. While the first book focused on the theory of the modes and plainsong, this book shifts toward "measured music" (mensural music), exploring how time and rhythm are structured. It begins by grounding musical rhythm in the classical tradition of poetic meter.</meta>

[THE SECOND] BOOK

### ¶ Poets and Musicians established a short and long measure of time in the voice. Chapter One.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'M' depicting a man, likely a classical musician or King David, seated and playing a harp within a detailed landscape of rolling hills and trees.</image-desc>Music, which is the action of the voice, was established in the previous volume according to an equal measure of time arranged in individual notes. Now, poets and musicians, pursuing all vocal time with sound judgment, have established that it consists of various figures according to the quantity of time: the short and the long. From this, they consider all syllables to be either short or long. Therefore, they assigned a measure of one time <term>one time: the basic unit of duration, similar to a single beat or a quarter note</term> to a short syllable, but a quantity of two times to a long syllable. For duality is the first number to follow unity, counting it twice. Thus it is that the short syllable was discovered before the long syllable, as the grammarian Diomedes <note>Diomedes Grammaticus was a 4th-century Latin grammarian whose work was a standard reference for meter and rhythm in the Renaissance</note> says; for one comes before two. They consider a syllable to be perfectly short either by its nature or because of a following vowel, and they recognize a long syllable exactly either by nature, by position, or even by its own authority <note>In Latin prosody, "nature" refers to the intrinsic length of a vowel, while "position" refers to a vowel followed by two consonants, which makes the syllable long</note>. They also posit that certain syllables are common; for instance, when a syllable is short by its own nature but is followed by a "liquid" and a "mute" consonant <note>original: "liquida mutā"; a linguistic rule where a short vowel followed by certain consonant pairs (like 'tr' or 'pr') can be treated as either short or long in verse</note>, then that syllable is placed indifferently, sometimes as short and sometimes as long. Examples of this, such as "darkness" <note>original: "tenebre"</note> and "of the father" <note>original: "patris"</note>, are gathered from many examples among both Greek and Latin poets. And so, they constructed every kind of poem with a mixture of different "feet" <term>feet: the basic repeating rhythmic units in a line of poetry</term> to the sound of the lyre or the cithara. This was first discussed as theoretical music in instrumental harmony; it will be explained more extensively when I lead you to the first authors of the musical modes. Truly, all types of poems are known to be arranged with different feet, and these feet are arranged with different times. For the dactyl <term>dactyl: a rhythmic foot consisting of one long and two short beats (— ∪ ∪)</term> (to mention the quantities of some) sounds three syllables together: the first is long, and the remaining two are short, such as "armor-bearer" <note>original: "armiger"</note> and "of the prince" <note>original: "principis"</note>. Therefore, it is sustained by four times <note>One long (2) + two shorts (1+1) = 4 total units of time</note>. Likewise, the spondee <term>spondee: a foot of two long beats (— —)</term> has four times arranged in two long syllables, such as "happy" <note>original: "fœlix"</note> and "summer" <note>original: "astas," likely a variant of "aestas"</note>. The iamb <term>iamb: a foot of one short followed by one long beat (∪ —)</term>, called the "swift foot," has three times led by two syllables: the first is short and the last is long, such as "pious" <note>original: "pius"</note>. It is established that the trochee <term>trochee: a foot of one long followed by one short beat (— ∪)</term> is the opposite of the iamb, as it also concludes two syllables in three times, but the first is long and the last is short, such as "muse" <note>original: "musa"</note>. The anapest <term>anapest: a foot of two shorts and one long (∪ ∪ —)</term>, called the "anti-dactyl" by the Greeks because it is the opposite of the dactyl, leads three syllables: two short and the last long, such as "piety" <note>original: "pietas"</note> and "Erato" <note>Erato was the Greek Muse of lyric and love poetry</note>. The pyrrhic <term>pyrrhic: a foot of two short beats (∪ ∪)</term> consists of two short syllables, such as "wretched" <note>original: "miser"</note> and "father" <note>original: "pater"</note>. Three short syllables are ascribed to the tribrach <term>tribrach: a foot of three short beats (∪ ∪ ∪)</term>, such as "lord" <note>original: "dominus"</note>. Furthermore, they attribute three syllables to the amphibrach <term>amphibrach: a foot of short-long-short (∪ — ∪)</term>: the first short, the second long, and the third short, such as "keel" <note>original: "carina"</note>. The cretic or amphimacer <term>cretic: a foot of long-short-long (— ∪ —)</term> comprises three syllables: the first long, the second short, and the third long, such as "islands" <note>original: "insulæ"</note>. The bacchius <term>bacchius: a foot of one short followed by two longs (∪ — —)</term> consists of three syllables: the first short and the remaining two long, such as "Achates" <note>original: "Achates"; the faithful companion of Aeneas in the Aeneid</note> and "Ulysses" <note>original: "Vlixes"</note>. The proceleumatic <term>proceleumatic: a foot of four short beats (∪ ∪ ∪ ∪)</term> (which mostly suits lyric song) has four short syllables, such as "little bird" <note>original: "auicula"</note>. They constructed the dispondee <term>dispondee: a foot of four long beats (— — — —)</term with eight times, namely with four long syllables, such as "orators" <note>original: "oratores"</note>. The choriamb <term>choriamb: a foot of long-short-short-long (— ∪ ∪ —)</term> consists of four syllables: the first long, the two following short, and the fourth long, such as "powerful in arms" <note>original: "Armipotens"</note>. They lead the biiamb <term>biiamb: a foot consisting of two iambs (∪ — ∪ —)</term> with four syllables: the first short, the second long, the third short, and the fourth long, such as "proximity" <note>original: "propinquitas"</note>. The epitrites, which Diomedes also calls "hippios," they place as four types. The first <term>first epitrite: a foot of short-long-long-long (∪ — — —)</term> consists of four syllables: the first short and the remaining three long, comprising seven times, such as "priests" <note>original: "sacerdotes"</note>. The second epitrite also has four syllables: the second [syllable] short...

<meta>The text breaks off mid-sentence, with the catchword "bre" indicating the next word is likely "brevis" (short), continuing the definition of the second epitrite.</meta>

It then bridges the gap between ancient grammar and the physical reality of rhythm, citing both classical authors and the biological pulse as the foundation of musical time.</meta>

## [THE SECOND BOOK]

...[the second epitrite]: the remaining three are long: as in the word *conditores*. In the **third epitrite**, four syllables are arranged: the third is short and the three others are long: as in *Demosthenes*. They form the **fourth epitrite** with four syllables, specifically with the fourth being short and the remaining three long, as in *Fesceninus*.

<margin>
Diomedes
Aristides
Augustine
</margin>
Of these and the rest, they perceive some as simple, like the Spondee and Iamb, and others as composite, like the Dispondee and Biiamb. Indeed, **Diomedes** himself, **Aristides** in the first book of his *Music*, and more fully **Augustine** in his second book, are known to have treated the movement and nature of all these. But musicians themselves have assigned certain figures, as if they were their own proper and fitting names, by which they might compose every song from the resulting diversity of measured times—not unlike how every poem is composed of multiple feet—according to the quantities of the voices.

<margin>
Natural Philosophers
Arsis
Thesis
Diastole
</margin>
Furthermore, natural philosophers <note>Original: *Physici*. In the Renaissance, this referred to those studying nature and medicine.</note> agree that the correct measure of a short time should be adapted to the equal movements of the pulse. They confirm **Arsis** and **Thesis**—which they call **Diastole** and **Sistole**—equally in the measure of each pulse. However, it is well known that the pulses of those with a fever undergo an increase or alteration in the unequal proportion of Diastole and Sistole, which is a matter of concern to those same natural philosophers.

<margin>
Sistole
Poets
</margin>
**Diastole** in Greek is interpreted in Latin as "dilation" or "elevation." **Sistole**, however, is "contraction." Poets, moreover, have Arsis and Thesis—that is, the "lifting" and "setting down"—as properties in their feet as well; they use them in pronunciation so that the poem may strike the ears with a more pleasing sound and soothe the mind. Although these can be found in any feet that make up a verse, a fit and elegant joining of words makes them especially clear and aids in graceful delivery. Furthermore, the connection of words occurs in different ways according to various types of poetry, suited to the delivery, so that the very texture of the verses reveals the "numbers" (which are a kind of rapid flow), the joining of words in a certain order, and a sweet rhythm flowing of its own accord.

<margin>
Rhythm
Quintilian
Bede
Time in rhythm
</margin>
**Rhythm**, as **Quintilian** <note>A famous Roman rhetorician whose work on education and oratory was rediscovered in the Renaissance.</note> pleases, consists of a space of times; and since time is the dimension of motion and rest, I take that very motion to be the act of pronouncing. However, **Bede** <note>The "Venerable Bede," an 8th-century English monk and scholar.</note>, in that treatise which he composed on figures and meters, interpreted Rhythm as a melodic composition made not by metrical rule, but examined by the number of syllables according to the judgment of the ears, like the songs of common poets. Rhythm seems to be very similar to meters. And indeed, by itself, it cannot exist without meter. For meter is "reason with melody," while Rhythm is "melody without reason" <note>In this context, "reason" refers to the strict mathematical rules of ancient Greek and Latin quantitative meter, whereas "rhythm" refers to the more natural, stress-based or syllable-counting patterns of medieval and vernacular song.</note>. Nevertheless, you will often find by some chance even "reason" in Rhythm, preserved not by artificial control, but led by the sound and the melody itself; common poets do this by necessity in a rustic way, while the learned do it skillfully.

<margin>
Greeks
Aristoxenus
Nicomachus
</margin>
The Greeks, however, assert that Rhythm consists of arsis and thesis and time, which some used to call "empty." **Aristoxenus** <note>A student of Aristotle and a highly influential writer on music and rhythm.</note> said this is time divided into each of those things which can be composed numerically. According to **Nicomachus**, Rhythm is an ordered composition of times. Concerning the structure and context of this kind, it is not our place to prescribe rules and canons for everything. For we leave to the poets their own specific matters; yet I would especially wish them to have "good ears" so that they might achieve this elegance in a poem. Let it be permitted, however, for us to submit an example, lest more things be found lacking <insert>there</insert>, and so that those meditating on examples through examples might have an easier transition to the rest by easy conjecture. The heroic hexameter loses much of its grace whenever the second foot ends a part of speech; it is less offensive if it ends in the third place before a monosyllabic word. It closes more honorably and pleasingly in the first, fourth, or fifth place by a part of speech, and prevents the poem from becoming ungraceful and jolting with interrupted speech, as in that line: *Dono ducite: doque volentibus hoc magnis diis* <note>Original Latin: "Bring it as a gift: and I give this to the great gods who wish it."</note>. It also has a spondee in...

<summary>Gaffurio explains the classical foundations of rhythm, drawing parallels between poetic feet, the human pulse (systole and diastole), and the definitions of rhythm provided by ancient authorities like Quintilian and Bede.</summary>
<keywords>epitrite, pulse, Arsis, Thesis, Diastole, Sistole, Rhythm, meter, Quintilian, Bede, Aristoxenus, Nicomachus, poetic feet</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the aesthetics of poetic rhythm and transitions into the formal classification of musical time and notation, building on the previous page's introduction of poetic feet and the biological pulse.</meta>

...fifthly: which hurts the ears more. Some also contribute to this reasoning the variety of feet, so that spondees <note>A poetic foot consisting of two long syllables.</note> are tempered with alternating dactyls <note>A poetic foot consisting of one long syllable followed by two short ones.</note>. It is also useful to note that heroic verse <note>Hexameter, the meter of epic poetry like the *Aeneid*.</note> ending in a trisyllable seems to rise more strongly than one ending in a monosyllable—unless that word is an enclitic <note>A word that "leans" on the preceding word for its accent, like *que* in Latin.</note>, which breaks the flow. It ends poorly in a polysyllable, but more gracefully in a bisyllable. The frequent clashing of vowels, and especially the persistence of the same vowel, letter, and syllable, is to be avoided and shunned. Dissonance and sibilance <note>Hissing sounds, like the repeated 's'.</note> offend the ears and impede harmonic pronunciation, or at least render it less sweet. This same thing must be considered in every variety of song. For how much difference is there between these two pentameters—of which it is for the listener to judge: *Icarean dogs and Herculean beasts* <note>Original Latin: "Icareosque canes herculeasque feras."</note> and *there are three servants, two mules, and as many horses* <note>Original Latin: "sunt famuli tres mulæ duæ equi totidem." This example is likely chosen for its clunky, repetitive rhythm compared to the first.</note>. And these Sapphic verses: *you run to the twin seat of the mountain; these are not your spectacles; yield to the Muses* <note>Original Latin: "ad sori curris geminum sedile. Non tua hæc spectacula cede Musis."</note>. And these Asclepiads: *I hate the common crowd and their riches, and I keep them away* <note>Original Latin: "Odi diuicias uulgus & arceo." A reference to Horace's Odes.</note>. *Nor did the son of Semele move the Corybantes* <note>Original Latin: "Nec mouit corybantes Semeles puer." The son of Semele is Bacchus/Dionysus.</note>. From these notes, therefore, it is clear that if the connection of words is harmoniously joined through **Arsis** and **Thesis** <note>The rising and falling of the musical beat or poetic meter.</note>, it can more conveniently show its own power, so that without any harshness, the song almost announces, sustains, and pours itself forth.

### On the various figures of the ancients and their measure. Chapter II.

Musicians have proclaimed the measure of sounds through various figures <term>Notation: the visual representation of musical notes</term> in both pronunciation and division. For measure is twofold: one which consists in the continuous quantity of the sound itself, which they ascribe to **time**. It is established that this was assigned to each figure by the authors themselves, both by reason and by the will of the founder—a custom also held by the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed the universe was governed by numerical proportions.</note>, who considered the authority of the teacher equal to reason itself. The other type of measure is arranged solely by reason in the intervals or distances of sounds according to proportional dimension, as in musical instruments. Therefore, the measure of time is the arrangement of the quantity of each figure (to be considered in two ways). First, when we measure the **essential** measure of each individual note according to the primary arrangement of musicians (for we use "figures" and "notes" interchangeably). Second, when we count the quantities of figures **accidentally**, which proceeds from a double consideration: both according to the accidents of this describeable notation—such as **alteration, imperfection, diminution, division**, and the like—and also by the accidents of various proportions that are in any way commensurable. These are fully described in the fourth volume of this work. Therefore, it is established that this discipline of figures or measures is derived from essence and accidents; for the figures and rests take up the essence itself in their own quantities—namely, in mode, time, and prolation. But those things which happen to the figures beyond these three are ascribed to accidents. For accidents and necessary things cannot exist by themselves, as **Aristotle** says in the first book of the *Posterior Analytics*. Every measurable description is marked either by notes, or by rests (which show omitted and measurable voices), or by a sign or a canon. Indeed, the Greeks arranged various figures in rhythm

g <image-desc>Medium length horizontal line with a dot above</image-desc>

Musicians
</margin>

in this order: they marked a **Breve** of one time in this way (a); a **Minor Long**, which they called two-time, thus (b); a **Long of three times** thus (c); a **Long of four times** thus (d); and a **Long of five times** thus (e). They understood **Arsis** by a point added to each figure, in this way (f, g, h). **Thesis**, however, was declared by each note without a point. They expressed consonant tensions—namely, fourths, fifths, octaves, whole tones, semitones, and the rest accommodated to harmonic songs—with certain descriptions; which we have decided to omit for the sake of brevity, as they are absurd and foreign to our modern customs. Our musicians, however...

SECOND [BOOK]

<margin>
<image-desc>Vertical diagram showing the evolution of musical notation marks, labeled i through t:
i — a short note (breve) shown as a solid black square.
k — a long note with a stem exactly four times the height of the square.
l — a long note with a stem three times the height of the square.
m — a long note with a stem twice the height of the square.
n — a "perfect" long of three beats, shown as a square with one-third of its body hollowed out.
o — an alternative version of the hollowed long.
p — a "double long" (maxima), a solid black rectangle twice the width of a square.
q — a "triple long," a solid black rectangle three times the width of a square.
r — a composite symbol (ligature) representing several long notes joined together.
s — a composite symbol of hollow notes representing several short notes joined together.
t — a complex symbol combining long and short notes into a single figure.</image-desc>
</margin>
...our [predecessors] formed the short note <term>brevis: a note of moderate length, traditionally one "time" unit</term> of one "time" <note>original: *temporis*</note> as a full square in this way: **i**. Indeed, they formed the Long <term>longa: a musical note of greater duration than a breve</term> of two times as a square with a small line [stem] descending or ascending on the right side, of such length as would be equal to the four sides of the square itself, in this way: **k**. Some, however, because of the excessive deformity of its length, drew the stem from the square note equal only to the length of three sides of the square, as here: **l**. Others arranged the stem equal in length to only two sides of the square, in this way: **m**. They also described a Long of three times as a rectangle in the same fashion, but with the third part of the square body hollowed out, as here: **n**, or thus: **o**. Furthermore, they marked a Long of four times as a full rectangle with the square doubled in width, in this way: **p**. This they called a double long <note>original: *duplicem longam*, later known in music theory as a *maxima*</note>. Finally, they placed the triple long with a triple extension of the square, considered as six times, in this way: **q**. There were also some who encompassed several longs in a single figure, indicated by individual stems, in this way: **r**. Later writers, however, overturning this order of notation, wrote these same notes as hollow shapes, bringing together several square breves into a single body in this way: **s**. Uniformly also, they wrote longs with breves, and breves with longs, in a single figure [a ligature] in this way: **t**. But since these types of descriptions have fallen out of use, it was pleasing to speak of them briefly, so that the styles of more recent <term>Neoterics: a term used to describe "modern" or contemporary writers and composers</term> may become more easily known to everyone.

### On the consideration of the five essential figures. Chapter III.

<margin>
<image-desc>Diagram of musical terms and symbols:
A box containing three horizontal lines representing rests (the "omitted voice").
Direct Voice (Vox recta) — the sound itself.
Omitted Voice (Vox omissa) — the silence or rest.
Breve — shown as a black square (u).
Long — shown as a black square with a stem (x).
Musical Poets (Poetæ Musici) — associated with the diamond-shaped notes.
Semibreve — shown as a diamond/rhombus symbol (y).
Minim — shown as a diamond with an upward stem (z).</image-desc>
</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'F' featuring vine-like ornamental patterns.</image-desc> A figure is a representation of the direct and the omitted voice. For we call that a "direct voice" which is singable or pronounceable in a certain measure. The "omitted voice," however, is considered in that very silence by a certain measure of time. The figures of the direct voice are the notes themselves. The omitted voice is declared through rests <note>original: *pausas*</note>. Indeed, among the notes, one is short and the other long; for shortening <note>original: *correptio*</note> and lengthening <note>original: *productio*</note> are naturally known to exist in these sounds, just as in syllables. Therefore, Musicians first handed down the short note with a square body in this way: **u**. Since it contains the measure of a single time, they call it a "time" <note>original: *tempus*</note>. They also marked the Long as a square with a stem on the right side, deriving from the square itself and going up or down, which they led to be equal in quantity to the four sides of the square. From this, it was also called a "double breve," as here: **x**. However, writers of songs often arrange the measurable quantity of such a stem irrationally. Since in the arrangement of meters, the poets themselves placed a limit on long syllables as a sort of greater mode in the quantity of the voice, which no other measure of time would exceed, musicians called that same long note a "mode" <term>modus: a rhythmic division defining the relationship between longs and breves</term>. From there, partitioning the square breve into two equal parts diagonally, musicians led it into the semibreve <note>original: *semibrevem*</note> with its corners turned upward and downward, which they call "prolation" <term>prolatio: the relationship between semibreves and the smaller minims</term>, assigning to it half the quantity of a breve, in this way: **y**. Finally, the Neoterics assigned a measure of one time to the direct semibreve, concluding the *diastole* and *systole* <note>Terms for the expansion and contraction of the heart/pulse, used here for the upbeat and downbeat</note> within the sound of each semibreve. And since the *diastole* and *systole*, or *arsis* and *thesis* <note>Greek terms for the lifting and setting down of the foot in poetic rhythm</note>, which are contrary, are considered in the pulse as the smallest measure of time, they divided the semibreve itself (arranged as a whole measure of time) into two equal parts: as if one contains the quantity of the *diastole* in the measure of the pulse as if in sound, and the other the quantity of the *systole*. To this, they assigned the smallest fullness of the voice, naming it the Minim <term>minima: literally "the smallest"; the shortest note value in this system</term>. They describe its figure as a semibreve with a stem added to one of the angular summits, often sharper, in this way: **z**. But the short note, which at first [was] only one time, and the long...

BOOK [TWO]

<margin>
Aristides
Anselm
</margin>
...consists of two times: in the time of measurable sound itself, they are called the most proper elements, whose magnitudes are equal to the agreeable distances of a tone. For a tone, as Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, a 3rd-century Greek music theorist.</note> and Anselm <note>Anselm of Parma, a 15th-century theorist who sought to reconcile ancient Greek theory with modern practice.</note> posited, is divided into four agreeable enharmonic quarter-tones <term>diesis: in ancient Greek theory, the smallest interval, often a quarter of a whole tone</term>. And thus, a long note is resolved into four semibreves, and a breve into four minims. Therefore, the quantity of a minim occupies the fourth part of a breve, as if flowing from one of the four corners of the square breve itself—or, as some prefer, from one side of the square breve. Since, therefore, everything is completed by the smallest of its kind: just as by unity itself—by which (since it is the smallest unit) every number receives its increase, and into which every resolution of numbers is made—and just as every line grows from a point, and the quantity of every line decreases down to the termination of the point itself: it is certain that the minim note itself completes every measure of musical time. Since it consists in the very act of speaking <note>original: *prolatione*</note> as the most essential part of the poet's semibreve, they called it a part of "prolation" <term>prolatio: the relationship between the semibreve and the minim in mensural notation</term>. Hence, the minimum musical time (to use Franco's <note>Franco of Cologne, the 13th-century creator of "Franconian" notation which first assigned specific durations to note shapes.</note> opinion) is not called just any minimum, but that which
<margin>
Franco
Maxima
Spondee
Similar
</margin>
is the minimum in the fullness of the voice. This is what Aristides, in the first book of his *Music*, seems to assert with these words: "Time is indivisible and the minimum, which he also calls a sign." Moreover, it is called a "minimum" in relation to us because it is the first thing perceptible by the senses; but it is called a "sign" because it is indivisible. For even the Geometers have named that which is indivisible among them a "sign" [point], and this indivisible unit holds the place of unity. Finally, musicians introduced the "double long" in the tenors of motets, containing four breve times. They call it the **Spondee** <note>In poetry, a foot consisting of two long syllables.</note>
<margin>
<image-desc>A square musical note (maxima) with a long descending stem on the right side.</image-desc>
</margin>
because it consists of two longs, not unlike it; since it excels the rest of the figures in the quantity of its times and the size of its body, they call it the **Maxima** [the Greatest], described in this way: **a**.

Because there is in it a greater temporal modulation of sounds than in the others, they call it the "Greater Mode." By its very addition, it is compared to the *proslambanomenos* <note>The lowest note in the Ancient Greek "Greater Perfect System."</note> string, which produces a lower sound than the other strings of the perfect system, owing to its temporal slowness because of the greater length of the string. And the remaining figures can likewise easily be compared to the other strings. Truly, since it encompasses the times of four breves in various ways, they judge that the **Maxima** corresponds to different four-beat poetic feet: for when you consider in it one long and two breves, you make it similar to a **Dactyl**; if, however, two breves and one long, it will agree with an **Anapest**; if four breves, with a **Proceleumatic**. The progression of these types of figures is therefore in this order: **Minim**, **Semibreve**, **Breve**, **Long**, and **Maxima**. Others, however, place them in a retrograde order from the Maxima to the Minim. For if we compare these to the theoretical order of consonances <note>intervals</note>: the Minim will hold the place of the **Tone**; the Semibreve, the **Fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>; the Breve, the **Fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>; the Long, the **Octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>; and the Maxima, the **Double Octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note>.

[BOOK] SECOND

24

...he, however, calls the smallest parts led in quadruple proportion [minims]. Most people, however (which pleases us more), have interpreted the first turnings of this kind—namely, the parts near the minim—as **semiminims**, as if they were "disjoined" and "separated" minims. Again, they called the primary parts of these semiminims "minor semiminims" <note>original: *seminimimas*</note> based on the diminution of time and the measure of quantity. And so, the semiminim follows the minim just as a major semitone follows a whole tone. Indeed, the semiminim relates to the minim itself just as a minor semitone relates to the whole tone itself. 

The third and most diminished particle of a minim, which Tinctoris <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), a hugely influential music theorist from the Low Countries.</note> wished to be called a "minim in octuple proportion," others call a "minim-semiminim." Some even call it a **comma** <term>comma: a very small musical interval, here applied to a note value</term>. We have thought it should be called a **diesis**, which is the smallest agreeable particle in the division of a tone. Most people describe this semiminim as a "full" note <note>meaning a filled-in or black notehead</note> with the top of the stem twisted to the right, and furthermore with the angle twisted downward, as seen here: **e**.

<margin>
**e** [semiminim with a flag to the right]
**f** [semiminim with a flag to the left]
Franco.
Philippus.
Anselm.
</margin>

Truly, since it accords with the descriptions of other such diminutions with a quite strange deformity, we mark the full note with the top of the stem twisted to the left in this way: **f**. For the right side is considered more "increased" and "perfect" than the left in the consideration of such notes. 

There are also those who handed down various descriptions for these notes. For **Franco** <note>Franco of Cologne, who revolutionized notation in the 13th century.</note> and **Philippus de Caserta**, as well as **Johannes de Muris** and **Anselm of Parma** in the third "diet" <note>original: *dieta*, meaning a day's journey or a section of a book</note> of his music, draw the ascending "plica" <term>plica: a 'fold' or melodic ornament indicated by a stroke on a note</term> and the breve with stems on both sides. Again, Anselm posited three types of breves: one greater, another lesser, and another middle. He figured the **greater breve** as a square with a descending stem on the left side, in this way: **g**.

<margin>
**g** [square note with left descending stem]
**h** [square note with left ascending stem]
**i** [square note]
**k** [square note with two right stems]
**l** [square note with two left stems]
**m** [square note with vertical stroke through center]
</margin>

The **lesser breve** he figured as a square with an ascending stem on the left, in this way: **h**. The **middle breve**, however, was a square without a stem, in this way: **i**. Likewise, he described the **greater semibreve** as a square with two ascending stems—that is, one ascending and the other descending on the right side, in this way: **k**. The **lesser semibreve** as a square with two stems on the left side, as here: **l**. The **middle semibreve** he marked as a square with a stem drawn straight through the middle of the square, both upward and downward, in this way: **m**. 

With similar consideration, he also varied the remaining notes. The **Moderns** <term>neoterici: the "modern" theorists of the author's own time</term> have judged that these descriptions, which have fallen out of use, should be rejected rather than approved.

### ¶ Chapter V. On the Ligatures of Figures.

A **ligature** is the ordered conjunction of simple figures by means of proper strokes. The notes that can be ligated are four: the **Semibreve**, the **Breve**, the **Long**, and the **Maxima**. Just as a simple note that is "ligatable" can be tied, so also a tied or "joined" note can be loosened and separated into a simple figure—except for the semibreve, whose primary and simple figure is known to be different from its compound one. And those, likewise, which they write with a single oblique body, the description of which does not at all fit simple figuration. 

Ligatures, therefore, are arranged in some cases with a single body:
<image-desc>A fragment of a musical staff showing a ligature formed by two notes joined by a thick diagonal bar.</image-desc>

In others, with many and diverse bodies, as here:
<image-desc>A fragment of a musical staff showing several notes, some linked as ligatures and some standing alone.</image-desc>

¶ Also, of ligatures: some are **ascending**, others **descending**. An ascending ligature is so called when the second note is above the first, as here:
<image-desc>A fragment of a musical staff showing an ascending two-note ligature.</image-desc>

¶ Nor does it matter if in a ligature of this kind there are many descending notes, since the ascent and descent in ligatures is determined by the first movable step between the second and the first note.
<image-desc>A fragment of a musical staff showing a ligature of three notes descending and then ascending.</image-desc>

...the path is considered. A descending ligature is said to occur when the first note sits above the second, in this way:
<margin>The beginnings of ligatures. Some are "with property." Others are "without property."</margin>
<image-desc>A musical staff with four sets of ligatures, demonstrating various "properties" (proprietates) in mensural notation.</image-desc>
...said to be "with property." Others "with opposite property."
¶ For every ascending ligature whose first note has no tail—neither ascending nor descending—is said to be **with property** <term>proprietas: literally "property"; a technical term describing whether the first note of a ligature follows the standard rules</term>, as seen here:
¶ Likewise, every descending ligature whose first note has a descending tail attached to the left side is said to be **with property**, as here:
<image-desc>A musical staff with two examples of descending ligatures.</image-desc>
¶ Indeed, every ligature "with property" makes the first note a **breve** <note>original: *breuem*; a note value usually half the length of a "long"</note>. Therefore, those are in error who, in an ascending ligature, set the first note of an oblique body as a **long** in this way:
<image-desc>A musical staff with four examples of oblique ligatures (ligaturae obliquae).</image-desc>
<margin>Franco.</margin>
<image-desc>A manicula (pointing hand) pointing to the right.</image-desc> Moreover, according to **Franco** <note>Franco of Cologne, a highly influential 13th-century music theorist</note>, "property" is an ordered arrangement and positioning for the beginnings of ligatures attributed by the first authorities of **plainchant** <term>plainchant: monophonic church music, like Gregorian chant, which does not use the complex rhythms of "mensural" music</term>. For this reason, every ligature in plainchant possesses "property" alone. Ligatures "without property" and those "with opposite property" never occur in plainchant notes; as they differ essentially from a ligature "with property," they create figures that are different in both name and quantity. This is in no way admitted in plainchant, as musicians have arranged its notes to have equal measures of time.
Every ascending ligature whose first note has a tail descending from the right side is called **without property**, as here: <image-desc>Musical notation showing an ascending ligature with a right-side tail.</image-desc> Likewise, they have said that every descending ligature whose first note has no tail is **without property**, as here:
¶ Truly, every ligature "without property" makes the first note a **long**. Furthermore, every ligature—whether ascending or descending—whose first note has an upward <note>original: *descendentem*; the Latin text says "descending," but in standard music theory of this period, an "opposite property" tail must point *upward* to distinguish it. This is likely a printing error in the original or a slip by the author.</note> tail on the left side is said to be **with opposite property**, as here:
<image-desc>A musical staff showing ligatures without property.</image-desc>
¶ And every ligature "with opposite property" always makes the first note a **semibreve**, and the second note as well—not because of its own nature, but by consequence. For a single semibreve note is unsuitable for a ligature, but two are very aptly joined together:
<image-desc>Musical notation showing ligatures with opposite property (cum opposita proprietate).</image-desc>
It is established from what has been deduced: the **Long**, the **Breve**, and the **Semibreve** fit the beginnings of ligatures most suitably. The **Maxima**, however, because it is always described as having a long and rectangular body, is never varied by any change in quantity. It is known to fit at the beginnings, the middles, and the endings of ligatures, as here:

---

<image-desc>A full-width musical staff displaying various complex ligatures, including binary, ternary, and quaternary forms in square mensural notation.</image-desc>

¶ Furthermore, it is known that **perfection** and **imperfection** <term>perfection: a rule determining if the final note of a ligature is a "long" or a "breve"</term> apply to the final figures of ligatures. For every ligature, whether ascending or descending, that has its final note as a square directly above the penultimate note, in this way:
<image-desc>A two-note ascending ligature where the final note is directly above the first.</image-desc>
¶ Or in a directly descending ligature with a tail on its right side, as here:
<image-desc>A descending ligature with a downward tail on the final note indicating perfection.</image-desc>
<margin>It is declared "with perfection"; and musicians [call] that final note in such ligatures a long.</margin>
¶ Or with a square body below the penultimate note in this way:
<image-desc>A musical staff showing a ligature where the final note is placed below the penultimate note.</image-desc>

# SECOND [BOOK]

<margin>a</margin>
...they have placed. Indeed, every ligature, whether ascending or descending, having the last note above the penultimate in an oblique body <note>An "oblique body" refers to a thick, slanted line connecting two pitches, common in medieval and Renaissance notation.</note> in this way:
<image-desc>Musical staff with two ligatures: the first an ascending oblique form, the second a descending oblique form.</image-desc>
Or in a square-line with an indirect stem as here <image-desc>Two ligatures on a four-line staff with vertical stems descending from the right.</image-desc> in this way. It is said to be **without perfection** <term>sine perfectione: a technical state where the final note of a ligature is treated as a breve instead of a long</term>.
Or below the penultimate in an oblique body <image-desc>Four examples of ligatures on a staff.</image-desc>
Musicians have established that the last note of these ligatures is always a **breve**. From this it is established that the **long** and the **breve** can be placed not only at the beginning but also at the end of a ligature.
The **semibreve**, however, is not used at the end at all, except in a ligature of **opposite property** <term>proprietas opposita: indicated by an upward stem at the start, making the first two notes semibreves</term>; for when only two semibreves are joined, then the first and the last are called semibreves, as here:
<image-desc>Two ligatures with upward stems on the left, indicating semibreves.</image-desc>
In the middle notes of ligatures, however, there is no essential difference, since they all agree in quantity; hence they must necessarily be given a single name. For, as it pleases **Franco** <note>Franco of Cologne, the 13th-century father of rhythmic notation.</note> and **Anselm** <note>likely Anselm of Parma, a 15th-century theorist.</note> and all musicians, all middle notes are **breves**. Therefore, those who conclude that a middle note is a **long** are mistaken; for example, in this way: <image-desc>Three musical ligatures with varying stem directions.</image-desc> The long, indeed, belongs only to the beginning and the end of ligatures.
<margin>The semibreve of opposite property</margin> The semibreve belongs to the beginning and the middle when more than two are joined in a ligature of opposite property. To the beginning and the end, if there are only two.
The **breve**, however, as has been deduced from the previous considerations, has obtained an appropriate place at the beginning, middle, and end of ligatures. Musicians, however, have never been accustomed to join the **minim** <note>original: *minimam*; the smallest standard note value of the period.</note> into a ligature. Indeed, whatever "accidents" simple figures undergo—such as **alteration**, **imperfection**, and the like—these same things are usually imitated by the ligatures themselves.

---

### On rests. Chapter VI.

A rest, which the Greeks call **quiet** <note>original: *quietem*, translating the Greek *pausis*.</note>, is a sign showing a skillful stopping of the song, which indicates to the singers that they should restrain themselves from singing. Musicians established this both for timely rest and the refreshment of the voice after a laborious lifting [of sound], and for the sweetness of the song. For just as a preacher of the Divine Word or an orator often soothes a weary listener with a certain jest, rendering him more grateful and attentive, so a singer, by interspersing certain delays in the voices, makes the listeners more attentive to the remaining parts of the song. For a rest is a figure of a line or a certain stroke drawn through a space, or spaces, or part of a space, added to no note but entirely separated from the notes. The ancients placed four rests in songs, which, because they declare the omitted and measurable voices of the notes themselves, have been given their names. For of these: one is the **Breve**; another the **Long**; another the **Semibreve**; and another the **Minim**.
A breve rest is a stroke drawn in the writing of a song from one line to the nearest line, encompassing the interval or middle space. A long rest [of two times]—namely, of two breves—is a stroke encompassing two [spaces]; a whole rest of three lines encompasses [two] intervals. There is also a rest of three "times" <note>A "time" or *tempus* is the basic unit of measurement, equal to one breve.</note>, which, touching four lines with its own ends, occupies three full spaces; this they call a **perfect long**, because it passes through three equal times of silence, which are also called breves.
<margin>The number three is perfect.</margin>
For the number three is considered perfect in the quantities of such figures: both by reason of its property—for it shows the first even number, two (which is considered feminine), and the first odd number, which is itself (and is called masculine), and from their alternating union all other numbers are procreated—and by reason of all its diverse parts which make it up (for the unit and the number two lead to it); and also by reason of the concord naturally arranged in these first three consequences, as **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, whose *De Musica* was a foundational text for medieval theory.</note> says in the first book of his *Music*...

<sig>D</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the rules for note values within ligatures and begins a new chapter on rests. The author defines various types of rests and provides a philosophical justification for the "perfection" of the number three in music, citing St. Augustine.</summary>
<keywords>ligatures, breve, long, semibreve, minim, rests (pausae), perfect long, number three, Franco of Cologne, Anselm, St. Augustine, musical notation</keywords>

# BOOK

<margin>Mathematicians</margin>
...those middle notes. For indeed, the ternary <note>The number three.</note> is made complete from one and two, between which no other natural number falls; this does not happen with other numbers. For example, two and three do not precisely result in four, nor do three and four precisely result in five (the number naturally following them); and it is the same for the rest. This is both by reason of ternary equality—for in the ternary itself, the beginning, middle, and end are arranged equally—for which reason we profess the divine Trinity in the highest God—and also by reason of mutual combination. For if you multiply the ternary by two, or the two by three, the number six <note>original: *senarius*</note> immediately arises, which **Mathematicians** often proclaim as "perfect" in its own parts <note>In medieval mathematics, a "perfect number" (like 6) equals the sum of its divisors: 1+2+3=6.</note>. A pause greater than three measures <note>original: *temporum*</note> is not measured by the other small notes of the song, but declares the termination of the harmony <note>original: *concentus*</note>. When there is a need for two or three such "perfect" **long rests** according to the composer’s arrangement—namely, to be measured by a silence of six or nine beats—two or three rests of three spaces each must be written, and even more at the discretion of the creator.

A **breve rest** also generally passes three semibreves in silence; musicians assign these to a "perfect" breve, calling it "perfect time." A **semibreve rest** is a small stroke which, clinging to any line, descends to the middle of the adjacent space. There are those who wish for it to contain a third part of one beat, provided it covers only a third part of the space; and moreover, to contain two parts of the beat when it covers two-thirds of the interval; but the **Moderns** <note>original: *Neoterici*</note> disagree. For they judge that two contiguous semibreve rests more correctly contain two-thirds of a "perfect" breve, which is the practice the more recent authors frequent. A **minima rest** is a small stroke placed on a line, ascending to the middle of the neighboring interval. (I call an "interval" or "space" the distance or uncomposed vacuum between two lines). This rest possesses a measurable quantity of half a semibreve. It often represents a third part of a semibreve, namely when "perfect prolation" <term>prolatio perfecta: a rhythmic setting where the semibreve is divided into three minims instead of two</term> governs the semibreve. But if you should care to rest for two-thirds of that value, they agree that two contiguous minima rests must be written. From these things, it is easily observed by singers that a semibreve note and its rest are resolvable into three minims (which is ascribed to perfect prolation). And likewise, by two semibreve rests placed together, it is known that the breves themselves are consistent with a ternary division (which belongs to "perfect time").

More recent authors describe a **seminima rest**—unknown to ancient musicians—as a minima rest with the tip turned to the right. This follows the duration of half a minima. However, a rest for the **semiminima** <note>A further subdivision, often called a fusa or eighth note in later terms.</note>, derived by no author anywhere else, should be shaped like a minima rest but with the tip turned to the left. This possesses the breathable quantity of half a seminima rest. It is indeed established in the consideration of musical figures that the left side is distinct from the right; for when you add a descending or ascending stroke to the right side of a square **breve** note, you will immediately see a **long** note prefigured; whereas if the descending or ascending stroke clings to its left side, a **breve** or **semibreve** note will result. Therefore, the descriptions of the rests themselves are in this manner:

From left to right: a thick block spanning three spaces (Perfect Long); a thick block spanning two spaces (Imperfect Long); a vertical line spanning one full space (Breve); a short line hanging from a staff line (Semibreve); a short line rising from a staff line (Minima); a rising line with a right-hand hook (Seminima); and a rising line with a left-hand hook (Semiminima).</image-desc>

### ¶ Chapter 7: On Mode.

<margin>
Pythagoras.
Perfect mode.
</margin>
**Mode** <note>original: *Modus*; in this context, it refers to the relationship between different note values, specifically how many breves make up a long.</note> is the rule or order of arranged quantity in a **long** <note>original: *longa*</note> according to the division of the whole into its immediate parts. Some, however, say that mode is the measurement of **breves** in **longs**, and of **longs** in **maximas**. For indeed (just as in all other things), mode must always be observed in musical notes according to the measure of time and the number of the notes themselves; they believe the term is derived from "moderate modulation" <note>original: *moderata modulatione*</note>. Others say it comes from "modifying" <note>original: *modificando*</note>, as the consideration of varied timings produces different types of modes. By "timings" <note>original: *tempora*</note>, I mean **breve** notes. It was previously stated that in poetic structures, a long is equal to two breves; therefore, since two is an even and "feminine" number (as **Pythagoras** judged), they consider this binary grouping of notes to be "imperfect." They deem the ternary <note>groups of three</note> to be "perfect" and "masculine," for males excel females in their perfection. Therefore, **imperfect mode** exists when only two beats—or two breves—consist within the long itself. Ancient writers of songs used to declare this by two dots placed inside a square figure in this way: **a**

<margin>
a <image-desc>A square music note (longa) containing two vertically aligned dots.</image-desc>
</margin>
However, **perfect mode** in a long—that is, when it contains three breves—was signaled by three dots drawn in a square in this way: **b**

<margin>
b <image-desc>A square music note (longa) containing three vertically aligned dots.</image-desc>
</margin>
More recent theorists call this measurement of the long the **minor mode** <note>original: *modum minorem*</note>, in relation to the mode existing in the **maxima**. Those ancient writers, however, established a single mode for both the maxima and the long, as if one could not be established without the other once perfection was calculated. Following them, musicians established a rest of three beats <note>original: *pausam trium temporum*</note> to represent the **perfect minor mode** in an existing long; for the sake of ease and convenience, this is placed before the notes, so that it is known that three breves are contained in a single long. They say the **imperfect mode** of that same long is demonstrated solely by the shape of the long note itself. For every long note belongs to some minor mode; when preceded by the sign of perfection (the three-beat rest), they ascribe it to the perfect minor mode. However, when the sign is lacking—as if the consideration moves from "privation" to "habit" <note>A philosophical concept where the "habit" (the presence of a quality) is more perfect than its "privation" (its absence).</note>—they intended the long itself to be of the minor and imperfect mode, assigning only two breves to it. From this, they believed the **major mode**, which they intended to reside in the double-long note <note>the maxima</note>, was imperfect because that double-long contains only two longs; because no sign of perfection is attached to it, they named this figure the **imperfect major mode maxima**.

<margin>
Major mode.
</margin>
However, when two rests of three beats are applied—which we called **perfect long rests**—they judged that the **perfect major mode** resided in the maxima note. Indeed, it was not inconsistent: since a single three-beat rest was assigned to the perfect minor mode, they proposed two such rests to identify the perfect major mode. For two rests are greater in the measure of quantity and time than one; therefore, it is necessary that they indicate the major mode. Truly, the maxima itself, having three longs, will contain nine breves or beats if each such long carries three breves. Because of this, all musicians agree—as **Anselm** <note>Likely referring to the theorist Anselm of Parma.</note> testifies—that at most nine such beats can be contained in a single figure. Even by the breath alone <note>original: *hiatu*</note>, a singer cannot easily exceed this with their own voice. It is also clear that the ternary arrangement of longs is joined to the consistency of those nine beats by a very certain comparison of perfection; for the ternary is the first side of "oddness," which, when multiplied by itself, proves its own odd square: namely, nine. If this...

<margin>
The Musicians: Anselm.
</margin>

# BOOK <note>original: *LIBER*; likely the Second Book of the treatise</note>

<margin>
Ausonius. 
Eloy.
</margin>
...secure in the ternary: he would immediately convert threes into ternaries. For this reason, it was not without merit that **Ausonius** <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD), a Roman poet</note> described this kind of ternary consideration with incredible praise in his *Riddle of the Ternary Number* <note>original: *in crippo [gripho] ternarii*; a famous poem celebrating the number three</note> addressed to Symmachus. **Eloy** <note>likely the composer Eloy d'Amerval (fl. late 15th century)</note>, therefore, being most learned in the modes, declared **Perfect Major Mode** <note>original: *modum maiorem perfectū*; a rhythm where a Maxima equals three Longs</note> in his mass *The Disciples Said* <note>original: *Dixerunt discipuli*</note> by using two rests of perfect longs. Furthermore, he showed the perfection of **Minor Mode** <note>original: *minoris modi perfectionem*; where a Long equals three Breves</note> with a single rest of three beats. **Dunstable** <note>John Dunstable (c. 1390–1453), a hugely influential English composer</note> also arranged this in the tenor of *Come Holy Spirit* <note>original: *Veni sancte spiritus*</note> in the following manner:

<margin>TENOR</margin>
<image-desc>A single musical staff of mensural notation with the part label "TENOR". It contains various ligatures and long notes (maximae and longae).</image-desc>

->**Come Holy Spirit.**<-

It is customary for many to mark rests of this kind of long among the notes of a song both **essentially** and **indicatively**. I say **essentially** when they measure out as many **breves** <note>the basic unit of time</note> to be omitted—in coordination with the notes of another part, such as the highest voice—as there are gaps between the lines of the staff. However, I say **indicatively** when those two rests indicate **Perfect Major Mode**, and a single rest indicates **Perfect Minor Mode**, which is expressly proven in the aforementioned tenor of *Come Holy Spirit*. Often, however, these are arranged as **incommensurable** <note>original: *icōmensurabiles*; meaning they do not count toward the actual duration of the piece</note>, namely **indicatively** only. This occurs when they precede the time signature—that is, the circle or semicircle <note>the Renaissance equivalent of a time signature</note>—written at the beginning of the song. In that case, two rests of three beats declare only the **Perfect Major Mode**, and a third rest declares the **Minor Mode**. **Tinctoris** <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), a leading music theorist of the Renaissance</note> fittingly demonstrated this in the tenor of his masterly motet, *It delights others to compose difficult songs* <note>original: *Difficiles alios delectat pangere cantus*</note>. <margin>Tinctoris.</margin> It does not matter whether these three rests are drawn across the same intervals between lines or different ones. Indicative and incommensurable rests of this kind are described in this tenor in the following way:

<margin>CANTVS <note>The melody or highest voice</note></margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation for the "CANTVS" part, featuring diamond-shaped notes (semibreves and minims) on a five-line staff.</image-desc>

<margin>TENOR <note>The foundation voice, often holding long, structural notes</note></margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation for the "TENOR" part, featuring longer rectangular notes and rests on a five-line staff.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A third system of musical notation continuing the exercise or example, featuring a mix of diamond-shaped notes and ligatures.</image-desc>

Through this description, anyone will very easily perceive the quantities of all **maximas** and **longs** <note>the two largest note values in this system</note> to be considered in any mode.

<image-desc>A musical notation diagram showing four examples on five-line staves. Below each staff is a label: "Perfect Major Mode", "Imperfect Major Mode", "Perfect Minor Mode", and "Imperfect Minor Mode".</image-desc>

### ¶ Chapter VIII. Concerning Time.

<margin>Perfect Time</margin>
Musicians intended for "Time" <term>Tempus: the level of rhythmic measurement governing the relationship between the breve and the semibreve</term> to be understood as the quantity assigned to breve notes. It is, in fact, twofold: perfect and imperfect. **Perfect Time** assigns three semibreves to a breve note; this is indicated by a circle <note>the symbol 'O'</note> written at the beginning of a song. Musicians believe that by placing a circle, the perfect ternary division of the breve itself is declared. This is because, in the circumference itself, the beginning, middle, and end exist equally; or (which pleases me more) because that spherical and circular circumference is naturally known to be produced by the number six—which is a perfect number—according to its distance from the middle point (which they call the center). For if the center is distant from the circumference by a single finger's breadth, the circumference itself will be measured by a quantity of six fingers.
<margin>Why the compass is called a "sixth" by mathematicians</margin>
For this reason, mathematicians call the compass <note>original: *circinum*; the tool used to draw circles</note> itself a "sixth." There are also those who prove the perfect and ternary division in breve notes by two pauses <note>rests</note> of semibreve notes written at the beginning of the song, which they count along with the other following figures in this manner: <image-desc>musical notation: two vertical rest marks followed by a diamond-shaped semibreve note, another vertical rest, and another semibreve</image-desc>. By these, it can easily be perceived that a rest of one breve is divisible into three equal parts <insert><image-desc>diagram of four square units arranged in a 2x2 grid, representing rhythmic divisions</image-desc></insert> inherent to perfect time. Two of these are its third parts; when it happens that two semibreve rests of imperfect time are arranged, most assert that a single breve rest is more correctly suited. I add: unless one of those two semibreve rests must be counted with the preceding note and the other with the following. Likewise, some perceive the perfect division of time in songs by three full breve notes—that is, notes accidentally made imperfect by such fullness <note>referring to "coloration," where notes are filled in with ink to change their value</note>.

<margin>Imperfect Time</margin>
**Imperfect Time**, however, distinguishes a breve figure into two semibreves. Indeed, they derived "semibreve" from *semis* <note>Latin for "half"</note>, because it is half; and it leads the breve as if it were half of the breve's quantity. Moreover, the placement of a semicircle <note>the symbol 'C'</note> declares this kind of binary resolution of breves in songs. You should not think of the semicircle as a "half-circle," but as "imperfect," for *semum* <note>a Latin root</note> sounds like "imperfect." Therefore, they wished for imperfect time in breves to be expressed by the semicircle itself—not indeed so that it confers half the quantity of perfect time to each breve note (which would be a semibreve and a half), but rather as two-thirds of that perfect time, acting as an imperfect division of time. This can certainly be considered mathematically, since the semicircle itself preserves precisely two-thirds of the circular circumference. This is demonstrated most clearly by placing an equilateral triangular figure within the circumference of the circle in this way: <insert><image-desc>Diagram of a circle with an inscribed equilateral triangle.</image-desc> In this way, writers may truly describe the semicircle.</image-desc> Therefore, the demonstrations of **Perfect** and **Imperfect Time** are arranged in this very well-known example.

<sig>D iii</sig>

The author uses geometric and mathematical symbolism—specifically the circle and the number six—to justify why the circle represents rhythmic perfection.</summary>

BOOK 2

<image-desc>

### PERFECT TIME
### IMPERFECT TIME

Furthermore, those are in error who call a semibreve of imperfect time "major" because it contains half of a breve, while they think the one that contains a third part of a perfect breve is "minor." In truth, every semibreve calculated in the same prolation <term>prolatio: the rhythmic level governing the division of the semibreve into minims</term> is always equal to any other semibreve. It is no obstacle that one possesses a half and the other a third part of a breve note, since the breves themselves are arranged in dissimilar quantities <note>meaning the "beat" remains the same, but the "measure" (the breve) changes its length</note>. Many people assert that the quantities of such perfect and imperfect notes are expressed by the characters of the numbers three and two, preceded by a circle for quantity when it is arranged in a perfect number, or a semicircle if imperfect. They consider the subsequent perfect notes by the character of the number three and the imperfect by the number two. For example, if a song was composed in perfect Major Mode, perfect Minor Mode, and perfect Time, it would be known by these signs: **O 3 3**. If, however, it were in perfect Major Mode, imperfect Minor Mode, and perfect Time, it would follow this order: **O 2 3**. In perfect Major Mode, perfect Minor Mode, and imperfect Time, it uses these signs: **O 3 2**. But when they lead a song in imperfect Major Mode and perfect Minor Mode and Time, they place these signs before the notes: **C 3 2**. If, however, in imperfect Major Mode and imperfect Minor Mode and perfect Time, they write these signs: **C 2 3**. And they do this in various ways, referring individual signs to individual quantities of the figures. When they place only two signs, they want the Minor Mode and Time itself to be understood, such as perfect Minor Mode and perfect Time in this way: **O 3**. And perfect Minor Mode and imperfect Time in this way: **O 2**. There are also those who do not only declare perfect or imperfect time by the character of the number three or two, but also consider a <term>sesqualtera: a ratio of 3:2</term> or double proportion <note>2:1 ratio</note> in the semibreves. Again, they show imperfect Minor Mode and imperfect Time by these two signs: **C 2**. And imperfect Minor Mode and perfect Time in this way: **C 3**. We, however, have decided that these signs for the aforementioned quantities ought to be rejected.

<margin>The Philosopher</margin>
According to the Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophus"; a standard reference to Aristotle</note>, "It is in vain to do with more what can be done with fewer" <note>This is the principle of parsimony, often called Ockham’s Razor</note>. For if the perfect Major Mode is distinguished from the imperfect by a single sign—namely, by two rests of three times <note>three-beat rests</note>—while the imperfect is proven by no sign but only by its own shape; and by the same consideration the perfect Minor Mode is perceived by a single sign, namely a single rest of three times (as if by a certain habit); and the imperfect by none (as if by privation) but is considered only by the description of its own shape: it is clear that different signs should not be written for different quantities of the same name, but should be established only for the perfect ones themselves. It is also for this reason that while ancient and more recent musicians have assigned the circle and semicircle to perfect and imperfect time, we separate them from the consideration of the Modes. Furthermore, because some people inconsiderately believe the characters of the numbers three and two create sesqualtera and double proportions—with whom I reasonably disagree in the first [chapter] of the fourth [book]—to remove all hesitation, I judge those numbers to be foreign to the quantities of the Minor Mode or Time.

<meta>The catchword "Verum," (meaning "But" or "Truly") at the bottom right indicates the first word of the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the notation and mathematical logic of rhythmic "Time," arguing against the use of complex numerical codes (like O 3 3) to indicate modes. The author invokes Aristotle's principle of simplicity, suggesting that perfection should be marked while imperfection is understood by its absence.</summary>
<keywords>tempus perfectum, tempus imperfectum, mensural notation, semibreve, breve, Aristotle, prolation, musical proportions, Ockham's razor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical mensuration (rhythmic notation) from Book 2, moving from the level of "Time" (the division of the breve) to the level of "Prolation" (the division of the semibreve), using geometric analogies to explain why certain shapes represent "perfection."</meta>

SECOND [BOOK]

<margin>Musicians as Geometers</margin>
Musicians have wished to signify the perfect quantities themselves in the note shapes, written with certain and continuous quantity, through geometric agreement. For the first and most powerful considerations among geometers are the point, the circle, and the line. Since a point is a certain minimum and the beginning of continuous quantity—just like "one" is the unit in numbers and a single sound is the unit in music—and since the circle and line grow together from the clustering of points, it is recognized to denote a power of perfection in continuous things. For each thing (as was deduced in the third chapter) is perfected by the smallest unit of its own kind. 

Hence, to describe the perfect quantity of the smaller shapes—which they call **prolation** <term>prolatio: the rhythmic level governing how many minims make up a semibreve</term>—musicians instituted that the unit should be inscribed within the sign of both types of Time <note>referring to the dot placed inside the circle or semicircle</note>. Moreover, because the circle itself is a perfect figure, they ascribed "Perfect Time" (as we have said) to the **breves** <term>brevis: a note shape that usually represents the basic pulse</term>. From there, they taught that the "Perfect Minor Mode" in **longs** <term>longa: a note value longer than a breve</term> is to be calculated by a rest covering three spaces. Finally, they demonstrated mathematically the perfect quantity of the **maximas** <term>maxima: the longest note value in this system</term>, in which the "Major Mode" consists, by using two rests that each cover three spaces.

### On Prolation. Chapter IX.

<margin>Perfect Prolation, Imperfect Prolation</margin>
**P**rolation is the essential quantity ascribed to **semibreves** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note</term>, showing the division of the whole into its immediate parts. For it maintains a division similar to the other essential quantities. One type accepts a ternary [three-way] section in the semibreve, dividing it into three **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value at this time</term>; this they call "perfect." The other is binary, ascribing only two minims to each semibreve; this they call "imperfect." 

There are also those who call this ternary and perfect prolation "Major," and the binary one "Minor," which does not please me. Since both prolations are considered within a single shape—namely the semibreve—a semibreve is not inherently different from another semibreve in the way a maxima is from a long (where "Major" and "Minor" modes are known to exist separately). However, it pleased some of the ancients to ascribe this kind of prolation to both semibreves and minims. They called it "Major Prolation" when semibreves were divisible into minims, and "Minor Prolation" when minims were divisible into **semiminims**. 

For they even described the minim as divisible into three equal parts, as <margin>Anselm</margin> Anselm <note>Anselm of Parma, a 15th-century music theorist</note> shows. Because of this, they called the one belonging to semibreves "Major Prolation," while they ascribed "Minor" to the minims. Modern musicians, however, have omitted the ternary division of the minim itself, assigning the minim and its subdivisions only to binary [two-way] division. 

Therefore, a dot inscribed within the sign of Time <note>original: "punctus temporali signo inscriptus"</note> usually declares Perfect Prolation. Imperfect is easily recognized by the absence of such a dot. Some have been accustomed to express Perfect Prolation by using two minim-rests placed together next to the notes of the melody. There are also those who think the "dot of division," placed before or after a minim or between two of them, is a sign of Perfect Prolation. This is because it attaches the minim to the nearest semibreve to be calculated in a ternary division. Even if such a dot indicates a ternary calculation of minims, in Perfect Prolation a group of three is usually arranged anyway, where a dot of division is not needed for recognition—for example, when I have arranged three semibreves or three minims in a melody. For every semibreve contains three minims in Perfect Prolation, and those three minims are calculated together. In such a case, a dot of division is not even appropriate; wherefore if most...

BOOK [II]

...[if the circle or semicircle] alone were the sign of perfect prolation, the **perfect semibreve** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note value</term> would not be admitted: just as a similar note is perfect before its peer <note>original: "similis ante sibi similem." This refers to the rule of *similis ante similem perfecta*, where a note is automatically considered "perfect" (triple) if followed by another note of the same value.</note>: which would be unsuitable. It is clear, therefore, that a **point** <note>the dot in the center of a mensuration sign</note> placed within the sign of Time was attributed by the authors to **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: a rhythmic setting where one semibreve equals three minims</term>. From this, it can be most clearly understood that prolation cannot be established without Time, nor the Major Mode without the Minor, nor the Minor without Time in musical songs. Thus, the signs themselves prove the perfection of both Mode and Prolation, acting as a kind of outward quality. However, the note shapes themselves carry imperfection when the signs are lacking. It is different for both: for **Time** <term>tempus: the rhythmic level governing how many semibreves make up a breve</term> is usually declared as **Perfect** by a circle and **Imperfect** by a semicircle, so that in both cases the perfection could be noted by a point.
