### ¶ On the parts of the note shapes. Cap. X.

Of the note shapes, one is called the **proximate part**. Another is **remote**. Another **more remote**. Another **most remote**. The **proximate part** is that into which the whole is immediately resolved: such as the **long** <term>longa</term> in respect to the **maxima**; the **breve** <term>brevis</term> in respect to the long; the **semibreve** in respect to the breve; and the **minim** <term>minima</term> in respect to the semibreve. The **remote part** is that where a single natural intermediate value falls between it and its whole: such as the **breve** in respect to the **maxima**, between which only the **long** resides; the **semibreve** in respect to the **long**, between which only the **breve** is placed; and the **minim** in respect to the **breve**, which only intercept the **semibreve**. The **more remote part** is that where two larger notes are arranged in natural order between it and its whole: such as the **semibreve** in respect to the **maxima**, which (having conceived the ends) surround the intermediate **long** and **breve**. The **most remote part** is that where three larger shapes naturally reside between it and its whole: such as the **minim** in respect to the **long** <note>The author likely means the maxim, as there are three levels between a minim and a maxim: semibreve, breve, and long.</note>, between which the long, breve, and semibreve are naturally enclosed. The same judgment is also drawn concerning the subdivisions of the **minim** in respect to the individual shapes. From this kind of consideration, it is established that the **maxima** is always a "total" figure, and the **diesis** <term>diesis: here referring to the smallest possible rhythmic division</term> is always a "partial" figure; but the six remaining—namely the **long**, **breve**, **semibreve**, **minim**, **semiminim**, and [the other] **semiminim**...

<meta>catchword: which [li q̄]</meta>

[BOOK] SECOND

...total and partial. Since those three paths [of division] cannot receive a ternary <note>triple</note> section when starting from the smallest unit, we will propose no smaller figure beyond the **minim** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value, which cannot be further divided into three</term> for our consideration of what can be made imperfect. For among the modern composers, the minim itself marks the end of triple division. Therefore, these attributes of **mensural music** <term>musica figurabilis: music where the shape of the note indicates its specific duration</term> are ascribed only to the five primary note shapes.

### On the Imperfections of the Note Shapes. Chapter XI.

<margin>First notable point
Second notable point
Anselm
Imperfection</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative drop-cap initial 'I' depicting two figures, possibly scholars or musicians, in a room with architectural details.</image-desc>
Among the note shapes that can be made imperfect, one type is only "passive" <note>it can only be reduced by others</note>. Another is only "active" <note>it can only reduce others</note>. A third type is both "active" and "passive." The only purely passive shape is the **maxima** <term>maxima: the longest note value in the hierarchy</term>. Although this note can be made imperfect in many ways by various smaller notes, it never makes another note imperfect itself. This is because it never precedes or follows a note of greater quantity in a song to which it could be applied as a missing third part. From this, two things must generally be noted. First: a note that makes another imperfect will always be smaller than the one it affects. Second: a shape capable of being made imperfect must always be considered in the context of its "perfect" <note>triple</note> quantity, or at least a part of it. This is what Anselm asserted in the third "diet" of his music treatise with these words: "The quantity of the subtraction is the third part of the measure indicated by the figure from which it is taken; for these measures stop their division at the number three." <note>original: "Quantitas autem detractionis est pars tertia mensurae..." Anselm is likely referring to Anselm of Parma, a 15th-century theorist.</note> Therefore, **imperfection** is a certain reduction of a third part from its whole, based on the triple arrangement previously considered in that note. However, when note shapes are arranged in a song according to the logic of double (imperfect) quantity, they often decorate them with a specific **dot of augmentation**; by this, the quantity of each note is increased by half, thus acquiring a ternary section equivalent to perfection. The only purely "active" shape is the **minim**; for although it may make other notes—namely **semibreves**—imperfect in "perfect prolation" <term>prolatio perfecta: a setting where a semibreve contains three minims</term>, it can never be made imperfect by another note because it does not allow for a triple division of its own quantity. The shapes that are both "active" and "passive" are those that can both cause and receive imperfection. These are three: the **longa**, the **brevis**, and the **semibrevis**; for each of these can make a larger note imperfect and be made imperfect by a smaller one. Every shape capable of being made imperfect can be affected either from the front, or from the back, or from both sides. For this reason, a shape whose third part cannot be divided into three equal parts (such as a semibreve in "perfect prolation," whose third part is a minim that does not fulfill a triple division) can be made imperfect from only one side. Furthermore, every note that is made imperfect by the application of its subtracted third part is said to be "imperfected as to the whole." When a note is reduced by the subtraction of a third part of its "proximate" <note>immediate</note> part, it is said to be "imperfected as to a proximate part" by its "remote" <note>distant</note> part. In the same way, a shape can be made imperfect by a more remote part in relation to a remote part, or by the most remote part in relation to a more remote part—such as a **maxima** being affected by a **minim** in perfect prolation. Generally, musicians have also established that any note placed before another of its own kind (when arranged in perfect quantity) is always perfect <note>original: "similis ante similem... semper esse pfectã." This is the famous rule that a note followed by an identical note cannot be reduced to a double value.</note>. It can never be made imperfect by the reduction of a subtracted part; therefore, a note capable of being made imperfect is necessarily placed before a larger or smaller shape if it is to be reduced.

<margin>There are three signs of imperfection.
The first way to recognize imperfection.</margin>
There are indeed three things that demonstrate an "imperfectible" note shape is actually imperfect: **Numerical imperfection**, **Punctual division** <note>use of dots</note>, and the **Fullness of the notes** <note>coloration, where notes are filled in/blackened to show a change in value</note>. Regarding the first: whenever smaller notes are written before or after a larger one, and the triple count of notes is either diminished or exceeds the required number, then that larger note is made imperfect by the nearest smaller note (unless that smaller note was already grouped with others), or by proximate remote parts, or more remote parts, or by both proximate and remote parts together—and so on in various ways, as is clear in this **tenor** <term>tenor: the structural voice part of a composition</term>.

<summary>This page explains the rules of 'imperfection' in Renaissance music, classifying notes as active (reducing others), passive (being reduced), or both. It defines imperfection as the removal of a third part from a triple value and lists the three ways to identify it: the number of notes, dots, and coloration.</summary>

¶ In this tenor (as can be perceived): the first **breve** <term>brevis: a note value that, in "perfect" time, is worth three semibreves</term> makes the following **longa** <term>longa: a note value longer than a breve, usually worth two or three breves</term> imperfect <note>original: "imperficit"; meaning it reduces the triple value to a double value</note> from the front <note>original: "a parte ante"</note> with respect to the whole: because it is its third part and is adjacent. Truly, the first **semibreve** <term>semibrevis: a note value smaller than a breve</term> makes the preceding breve imperfect from behind <note>original: "a parte post"</note>: with respect to the whole. Moreover, these two **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value in this system</term> placed between a breve and a longa make that same breve imperfect from behind: with respect to the whole. Even if these are its "remote" parts <note>referring to notes two levels down in the hierarchy</note>, since they are equivalent to one of its "adjacent" parts <note>the level immediately below</note>, they are known to produce the effect of a single adjacent part. The longa following those two minims is made imperfect: with respect to the whole from behind by the first breve. The first breve, under the sign of **perfect time** <term>tempus perfectum: a musical meter where the breve is divided into three semibreves</term> and **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: a musical meter where the semibreve is divided into three minims</term>, is made imperfect from behind: namely, with respect to the whole by the adjacent part, and with respect to a part by the remote part, which [is] of that same adjacent part...

# [BOOK] SECOND

is the third part. Again, a figure capable of being made imperfect <note>meaning a "perfect" note worth three units that can be reduced to two</note> can be made imperfect from the front and from the back <note>original: "a parte ante & a parte post"</note> at the same time: namely, when before it there is only a single third part of its adjacent part, and after it likewise follows a third part of another adjacent part. Then it is said to be made imperfect "with respect to its parts." For this reason, according to the instructions of musicians, these figures can be made imperfect in several ways, as is clearly demonstrated in this tenor.

<image-desc>Four staves of Renaissance-era mensural musical notation. The first stave is labeled "TENOR" in the right margin. The second stave is labeled "CANTVS" <note>The melody or highest voice</note> in the right margin. ¶ In this tenor, the first **semibreve** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note value</term> makes the following **longa** <term>longa: a long note, here potentially worth three breves</term> imperfect from the front with respect to its adjacent part <note>original: "quo ad partem propinquam"</note>; for that semibreve is the third part of the longa's own adjacent part. Likewise, the semibreve following it makes the longa imperfect from the back with respect to the longa's adjacent part. 

By a similar consideration, the first **breve** <term>brevis: a square-shaped note</term> written under the sign of **perfect time** <term>tempus perfectum: a musical meter where a breve equals three semibreves</term> and **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: where a semibreve equals three minims</term> is noted; for it is declared to be made imperfect by "remote" parts—namely, **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value</term>—with respect to two adjacent parts from the front and from the back. 

The first of two joined longas is not made imperfect, because "a like note before a like note" <note>original: "similis ante sibi similem"; a fundamental rule of mensural music where a note followed by one of equal value remains "perfect" or triple</note> cannot be made imperfect. The second longa is made imperfect from the back with respect to the whole: namely, by the adjacent part which is a breve. And that breve itself, while making the longa imperfect, is also made imperfect by the following semibreve with respect to the whole. 

It must meanwhile be understood that a like note before its like can never be made imperfect when dealing with similar figures that are physically separated and which are "totals" <note>independent, full note values</note>. However, this does not apply to notes united in a whole; for example, two breves of **imperfect time** <term>tempus imperfectum: duple meter</term> considered within a longa of **minor imperfect mode** <term>modus minor imperfectus: where a longa equals two breves</term>. In that case, even if a breve is placed before a breve, they can be made imperfect. This indeed happens when a figure is made imperfect from the front and from the back with respect to both parts:

<summary>This page explains complex rules of "imperfection" in music theory, where the duration of notes (longas and breves) is reduced by neighboring smaller notes. It highlights the rule that "like notes before like notes" remain perfect, except in specific cases of "united" note values.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical "imperfection" from the previous page, using a specific "tenor" (the structural base of a melody) as an example. It focuses on how notes like the longa and breve can be reduced in value by smaller notes placed before or after them.</meta>

# BOOK [SECOND]

as is also evident in the **longa** <term>longa: a long note value, often worth three breves in "perfect" meters</term> of the previously mentioned tenor, which is diminished from the front and from the back by two subtracted **semibreves** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note value, here worth one-third of a breve</term>. And in a **breve** <term>brevis: a square note value, usually the basic unit of time</term> of **perfect time** <term>tempus perfectum: a meter where the breve is divided into three semibreves</term> and **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: a meter where the semibreve is divided into three minims</term>, it is made imperfect by those two surrounding **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value, shaped like a semibreve with a stem</term>. 

Truly, if a note were considered as a single body and could not be made imperfect before another note like itself, then no figure would ever be capable of imperfection from the front, whether in regards to its adjacent or even its remote part; anyone can easily observe this for themselves. 

Furthermore, it is not to be accepted that a single minim can make another figure imperfect except in the arrangement of perfect prolation—although <margin>Guillaume</margin> **Guillaume de Machaut** <note>original: "Gulielmus de Mascadio," the famous 14th-century French composer and poet</note> posited that a breve of perfect time and imperfect prolation could be made imperfect by a single minim. This is absurd; for a note is usually made imperfect either by a third part of itself—such as a longa of minor perfect mode being reduced by a single breve—or by a third part of its half-part—such as a longa of imperfect mode and perfect time being reduced by a single semibreve—or by a third of its third part—such as a breve of perfect time and perfect prolation being reduced by a single minim. 

There are also those who would have a breve of perfect time and perfect prolation be made imperfect by two minims preceding or following it together; this is not permitted unless those two minims make the said adjacent breve imperfect as a whole, of which they are an adjacent part (that is, a third) through **alteration** <term>alteratio: the doubling of the value of the second of two notes of the same denomination to fill a triple grouping</term> of the second minim, as happens in the second tenor regarding alteration and in many songs. Or (which is little used) unless the first minim made the first unified semibreve imperfect, and the second minim made the third semibreve imperfect through **reduction** <term>reductio: the grouping of notes across larger values to maintain the meter</term>.

It is further asserted that a figure divisible into three triple parts, when it is made imperfect from the front and from the back by "remote" parts <note>parts two levels down in value, like a minim is to a longa</note> with respect to its "adjacent" parts, then the first unified adjacent part is made imperfect from the front as a whole, and the third part is made imperfect from the back. But the second such unified part remains in its perfection; this can be seen in the first longa of the preceding tenor, surrounded by those two semibreves. 

Truly, if two remote parts precede a figure divisible into three triple adjacent parts, and one remote part follows it, then it is said to be made imperfect with respect to all its adjacent parts. For example, when there are two minims before a breve of perfect time and perfect prolation, and a single minim follows it: the first of those two minims makes that breve imperfect in its first adjacent part; the second minim makes it imperfect in its second part; and the following minim makes that breve imperfect in its third adjacent part. 

Add to this that if those two preceding minims were to make that breve imperfect as a whole (of which they would be the third part through the alteration of the second minim), then the following minim will make it imperfect with respect to the third adjacent part from the back, and the second adjacent part remains perfect. And thus the whole breve, which normally contains nine minims, by this kind of subtraction, leaves four minims of its own quantity <note>This technical calculation demonstrates how complex rhythmic subtraction could become in Renaissance music theory</note>. 

By a similar consideration, many agree that a note can be diminished by a third of itself, which they call imperfect as a whole; then also by a third part of an adjacent part (namely, with respect to that part), and again by a third part of another adjacent part. Furthermore, they make the note imperfect with respect to its remote parts, so that a larger portion of the value of the figure is subtracted than a half. For instance, if from a longa of perfect minor mode, perfect time, and perfect prolation—which contains twenty-seven minims—I were to subtract a perfect breve (which is its third part, amounting to nine minims), and then a semibreve (the third part of an adjacent part, amounting to three minims), and then another semibreve...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical rules for "imperfection" in music theory, detailing how specific note values (longas and breves) are reduced when surrounded by smaller units (semibreves and minims). It notably critizes Guillaume de Machaut's views on the subject as "absurd" based on the strict proportional logic of the time.</summary>

# SECOND [BOOK]

...of the adjacent part, a third part comprising three **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value in this system</term>. Likewise, four minims are taken from the four remaining **semibreves** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note, here worth one-third of a breve</term>: so that out of the twenty-seven minims which the full **longa** <term>longa: a long note value, here at its maximum value</term> by right contains, nineteen are removed by a reducible subtraction. Truly, the consideration of various kinds of this imperfection has come little into the light and use, and we have said it is rather to be avoided than approved: because imperfection is the subtraction of a third part, or a smaller part, to be applied to the whole use, in order to complete the ternary division of quantity. For nature itself dictates that when we take away a third part of any whole, a binary <note>original: "binaria"; referring to a two-part grouping</note> consideration of parts is left. For this reason, every figure arranged in its perfect quantity according to the ternary grouping of its adjacent parts—if it is made imperfect by the subtraction of its third part—must necessarily exist as imperfect: and it inheres in a binary division as if substituted for its imperfect quantity. However, an imperfect figure, which is divisible into only two perfect adjacent parts, is said to be made imperfect from the front and from the back with respect to both parts: namely, when its single remote parts <term>pars remota: a note value two levels below the current note (e.g., a semibreve is a remote part of a longa)</term> have preceded it, and single ones have followed it. And likewise when both those remote parts that make it imperfect have preceded together or followed together: whether they exist as simple and disjointed notes, or as **ligatures** <term>ligatura: a group of notes tied together into a single symbol</term>: the first makes the first imperfectible part imperfect, and the second makes the second [imperfect]; which most people assert in the notations of songs, as is very clearly perceived in the present **tenor** <term>tenor: the structural foundation of a polyphonic composition</term>.

---

<margin>CANTVS</margin>

In this tenor, the first longa is made imperfect from the back with respect to both its adjacent parts <note>The "adjacent parts" of a longa are the breves that compose it</note> by the two following tied semibreves: of which the first makes the first united **breve** <term>brevis: a square note value, usually the basic unit of time</term> imperfect from the back, and the second [makes] the second [imperfect]. The second longa is made imperfect...

# BOOK [TWO]

...[is made imperfect] from the front <note>original: "a parte ante"; meaning the smaller note preceding a larger one reduces its value</note> with respect to both adjacent parts by the two preceding **semibreves** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note, here worth one third of a breve in "perfect" time</term>: of which the first makes the first joined **breve** <term>brevis: a rectangular note, the building block of this system</term> imperfect from the front, and the second makes the second [breve] imperfect. The third **longa** <term>longa: a long note value, here composed of multiple breves</term> is made imperfect from the front and from the back with respect to both adjacent parts. For a preceding semibreve makes the first joined breve imperfect from the front, while the following semibreve is denoted to make the second joined breve imperfect from the back <note>original: "a parte post"; meaning the smaller note following a larger one reduces its value</note>. The fourth longa is perfect with respect to both adjacent parts, because it is placed before another note similar to itself that is not joined; even though it may be of the lesser imperfect mode. The fifth longa is made imperfect from the back with respect to both joined adjacent parts: by a semibreve and by the two following **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value in this system</term>. For the semibreve completes <note>original: "perficit"</note> the first joined breve from the back. However, the two minims (equal to the value of one semibreve) similarly make the second joined breve imperfect from the back. Truly, the first breve—following the sign of **imperfect time and perfect prolation** <term>imperfect time / perfect prolation: a rhythmic setting where the breve is divided into two semibreves, but each semibreve is divided into three minims</term>—is made imperfect from the front and from the back with respect to both joined adjacent parts. It is established that the first of these is made imperfect from the front, and the second from the back. However, the following breve is made imperfect from the back only, with respect to both parts, by the two following minims, of which the first makes the first joined part imperfect, and the second [makes it imperfect] from the back.

<margin>
The second way to recognize imperfection.
</margin>

¶ Regarding the second point: Whenever a **point of division** <term>punctus divisionis: a small dot used to separate notes and determine their rhythmic grouping, similar to a modern bar line</term> is applied to any note, whether for that note alone or for itself and others, then that note makes the larger preceding or following note imperfect, if it is capable of being made imperfect; as is demonstrated in this **tenor** <term>tenor: the structural foundation voice of a polyphonic composition</term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical imperfection and rhythmic grouping in mensural notation. # BOOK [TWO]

...by the two **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value in this system</term> preceding it. However, a **point of division** <term>punctus diuisionis: a dot placed between notes to separate them into different rhythmic groups, ensuring they are counted in threes</term> written between two **semibreves** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note, worth one third of a breve in "perfect" time</term> placed between the last **breves** <term>brevis: a rectangular note, the building block of this system</term> indicates that the fifth breve is made imperfect <note>original: "imperfici"; to reduce a note's value from three units to two</note> from the back <note>original: "a parte post"; meaning the smaller note following a larger one reduces its value</note> and the sixth [is made imperfect] from the front <note>original: "a parte ante"; meaning the smaller note preceding a larger one reduces its value</note>. But if that larger figure preceding or following the smaller note where the dot was placed cannot be made imperfect, then the smaller note itself will be reduced or transferred to the prior note where it can be regularly joined in a ternary <note>original: "ternaria"; a grouping of three, which was considered "perfect"</note> grouping. The same also occurs when a smaller note without a dot precedes a larger one that cannot be made imperfect; for then the smaller note itself is transferred to the prior place to complete a ternary count, as is perceived in the present tenor.

<image-desc>Two staves of Renaissance musical notation illustrating mensural theory. The top staff is for the "TENOR" part and the bottom staff for the "CANTVS" part. The notation consists of black square and diamond notes (breves and semibreves), dots of division, and ligatures (notes connected by a thick bar).</image-desc>

->TENOR<-

->CANTVS<-

¶ In this tenor, the dot placed between the third and fourth semibreve shows that the fourth itself must be counted with the third breve of the following **ligature** <term>ligature: two or more notes joined together in a single symbol</term>, since it cannot be added to either the first or second; for a similar note placed before a similar one (as has been explained) cannot be made imperfect. For the same reason, a semibreve preceding three final separate breves without a dot is reduced and makes that third breve imperfect. The same judgment should also be applied when a point of division is placed by any breve in relation to **longas** in the **lesser perfect mode** <term>modus minor perfectus: a rhythmic system where one longa equals three breves</term>, or by any longa in relation to **maximas** in the **greater perfect mode** <term>modus maior perfectus: a system where one maxima equals three longas</term>, as well as by any minim in relation to semibreves in **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: a system where one semibreve equals three minims</term>. And also if they must be transferred without a dot to more distant figures capable of receiving this kind of imperfection. 

What should be said if four semibreves are arranged between two breves of **perfect time** <term>tempus perfectum: a system where one breve equals three semibreves</term>? According to the ancients, I say that the first of these semibreves is reducible and must be counted with the breve preceding it, which it completes from the back—as far as the whole is concerned—especially when the breve following those four semibreves is perfect; that is, either marked with a **point of perfection** or placed before a similar note. It is otherwise, however, if that breve is capable of being made imperfect; for then the first breve preceding those four semibreves will be perfect in itself, and the fourth semibreve makes the following breve imperfect from the front. Yet, to remove doubt, I recommend that a preceding breve that is perfect in itself should be marked with a point of perfection, unless a point of division is placed between the third and fourth semibreve, as the description of the present tenor proves.

It provides specific instructions for avoiding ambiguity in notation when multiple small notes appear between larger ones.</summary>

# BOOK

<image-desc>Four horizontal musical staves featuring Renaissance mensural notation. The top staff is preceded by the label 'TENOR' and uses a C-clef on the fourth line. The following three staves are grouped together with the label 'CANTVS' on the left; they use a C-clef on the third line. ->CANTVS<-

In this **tenor** <term>tenor: the fundamental melodic line in a polyphonic composition, often carrying the long-held notes of a cantus firmus</term>, the first **breve** <term>brevis: a rectangular-shaped note that serves as a primary unit of time</term> is made imperfect by the first **semibreve** <term>semibrevis: a diamond-shaped note, worth one-third of a "perfect" breve</term>: because the second **breve** cannot be made imperfect. The third **breve** is made imperfect from the back <note>original: "a parte post"; meaning the smaller note following a larger one reduces its value from three units to two</note>: as to the whole, by the first **semibreve** following it. The fourth **breve** is perfect. The fifth **breve** is made imperfect from the front <note>original: "a parte ante"; meaning the smaller note preceding a larger one reduces its value</note> as to the whole, by the **semibreve** immediately preceding it. The sixth **breve** is perfect, because it sits before another note like itself <note>The rule "similis ante similem perfecta est" means a note is always perfect (three units) if followed by a note of the same value</note>. The seventh **breve** is perfect: marked with a **point of perfection** <term>punctus perfectionis: a dot placed after a note to ensure it is counted as three units rather than two</term>. The eighth **breve** is made imperfect from the front, as to the whole, by the preceding **semibreve**. The ninth **breve** is perfect, because it sits before another like itself. The tenth **breve** is perfect: because a complete ternary <note>original: "ternarius"; a grouping of three</note> number of **semibreves** follows, contained between it and the **point of division** <term>punctus diuisionis: a dot used to separate rhythmic groups to maintain the triple meter</term>. The last **breve** is made imperfect from the front, as to the whole, by the **semibreve** preceding it, which is indicated by that same **point of division** placed before the **semibreve** itself. 

The same is also to be understood regarding four **minims** <term>minima: the smallest standard note value in this system, represented by a diamond with a stem</term>: with two **semibreves** of **perfect prolation** <term>prolatio perfecta: a rhythmic setting where a semibreve is divided into three minims</term> placed between them; and regarding four **breves** written between two **longas** <term>longa: a note value longer than a breve, usually worth two or three breves</term> of the **lesser perfect mode** <term>modus minor perfectus: a system where a longa is worth three breves</term>. If, however, a single **minim** and a single **semibreve** immediately follow a **breve** of **perfect time** <term>tempus perfectum: a system where a breve is worth three semibreves</term> and **imperfect prolation** <term>prolatio imperfecta: a system where a semibreve is worth only two minims</term>: then that **breve** cannot be made imperfect by its immediate neighbor, which is one-third of its value, following it as to the whole: since that following neighboring part—namely the quantity of the **semibreve**—is divided into dissimilar, non-connective parts: namely, into a **minim** and into a half-part of the following **semibreve** part: as the present **tenor** shows.

# BOOK TWO

E

<image-desc>A line of mensural musical notation on a five-line staff, featuring square and diamond-shaped notes, ligatures, and clefs.</image-desc>

->TENOR AND CANTVS<-

¶ Regarding the third point: Whenever a note is filled <note>original: "impletur"; this refers to "coloration," the practice of blackening a note head to indicate a change in its rhythmic value, typically making it "imperfect."</note> in a ternary and perfect grouping of quantity <term>perfect quantity: a rhythmic system where a large note is divided into three smaller units, similar to triple time</term>, it is then made **imperfect** <term>imperfection: the reduction of a note's value from three units to two</term> by a third part of its own value. It is necessary that this third part follows with a similar fullness; this fullness is confirmed by **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of grouping a smaller, "leftover" note with a larger one to complete a perfect rhythmic unit</term> alone. Nor does it matter whether that reducible third part precedes or follows the larger note—as its whole—either immediately or mediately <note>original: "imediate an mediate"; meaning either directly next to the note or separated by other notes</note>, provided that it is reduced to that larger note for the sake of completing the rhythmic count. For the smaller part is always accustomed to be led toward the larger part as if toward its own whole, as is perceived in the present **tenor** <term>tenor: the structural "holding" voice in Renaissance polyphony</term>.

<summary>This page explains the third rule of mensural notation, focusing on how "filled" (colored) notes are rendered imperfect in triple meter. It details how smaller notes are mathematically "reduced" or grouped with larger notes to maintain the rhythmic structure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "coloration" (filled-in notes) in mensural notation and begins a new chapter on the use of the "dot" (punctus). It builds on the previous page's explanation of how notes are grouped into threes (perfection) or twos (imperfection).</meta>

# BOOK
<i>i</i>

<margin>Bonadies <note>Johannes Bonadies was a 15th-century priest and music theorist who was the teacher of Franchinus Gaffurius, the author of this treatise.</note></margin>
¶ Indeed, when three similar figures are filled <note>original: "implentur"; this refers to coloration, where the usually "void" or white note-heads are blackened to signal a change in rhythm.</note> either mediately or immediately: then the first part is joined to the second note and reduced to the first; the second part of the third following filled note is applied to complete the ternary <term>ternary: a grouping of three beats, similar to triple time</term> division. There are also those who fill half the body of a **long** <term>long: a note value (longa) that is normally twice or three times the length of a "breve"</term>; they consider the final **breve** <term>breve: a standard note value (brevis) in this system</term> contained within it to be made **imperfect** <term>imperfection: the reduction of a note's value from three units to two</term> by its subsequent third part. Even if the figure has a void space in one part of its area and is filled in the other: in my opinion, however, that **long** would not be permitted to signify this. For "void" and "full" are, by their very nature, wonderfully repugnant to one another and do not demonstrate one and the same thing. Therefore, I would prefer both of them—the void and the full—to be marked figuratively as distinct bodies. This, however, does not require a most abstruse dispute, since it is clearly established that these things are left to the judgment of those writing. Figures arranged with consideration for imperfect quantity are also often written as filled; musicians usually equate these to a *sesquialtera* <term>sesquialtera: a proportional relationship where three notes are played in the time of two</term> diminution, as will be shown in the fourth book, where *sesquialtera* is discussed most amply. But if only a single figure of this kind, or only two, are written with such fullness: they are diminished by a part of their proper quantity, standing in for the primary—that is, the **double** <note>original: "dupla"</note>—proportion (which is rational), as my teacher **Bonadies** posited. Furthermore, it was the custom among ancient musicians to describe all notes existing in their essential quantities as full; but those which were accidentally made imperfect, they marked as void.

### On the Dot. Chapter XII.

<margin>
Dot of division
<br/>
Dot of transportation
<br/>
Dot of division
</margin>
A **dot** <note>original: "punctus"</note> is the smallest and invisible quantity of any continuum. More fittingly, however, we call a dot a certain minute sign which is placed before, after, or between notes as an accident; it is the beginning of a continuous quantity, like that of a line or a circle. For the dot is twofold: namely, of **division** and of **perfection**. A **dot of division** is that which, placed before or after any note, neither increases nor diminishes it; rather, it indicates that the note is to be applied and counted with the preceding or following note, for the sake of completing a ternary division among the notes. This happens in two ways: mediately and immediately. A note is said to be counted and applied to a preceding or following one "mediately" by a dot when the dot itself, placed before or after a note, divides it so that it is not to be counted with the most closely preceding or following notes. To this, the term **dot of transportation** or **translation** <note>original: "punctus transportationis seu translationis"</note> is properly applied, since it transfers the note to which it is attached to more distant figures for the purpose of calculation. It is said to be applied "immediately," however, when the note to which the dot is attached is counted with the more immediate note or notes. This I properly call a **dot of division**, as is shown in this **tenor**.

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation on five-line staves. The first system is labeled TENOR and uses a C-clef on the fourth line; it illustrates mensural notes (breves, longs, and semibreves) in both void and full forms, interspersed with dots of division and perfection to demonstrate the rhythmic principles discussed in the text. The second system is labeled CANTVS and uses a C-clef on the first line, showing similar mensural notation examples with varied note shapes and dots.</image-desc>

**CANTVS**

# BOOK II

<image-desc>Musical notation in mensural style on a five-line staff. The notes include breves, semibreves, and minims, with dots (punctus) placed among them to indicate rhythmic values.</image-desc>

¶ In this **tenor**: a **dot** <term>dot: known in Latin as a "punctus," it serves several rhythmic functions in this system, unlike the modern dot which only adds half a note's value</term> placed after the first **semibreve** <term>semibreve: a diamond-shaped note value</term> following the first **breve** <term>breve: a square-shaped note value</term> indicates that the semibreve itself must be joined to that breve to complete the accumulation of **time** <term>time: "tempus," the level of measurement describing the relationship between the breve and the semibreve</term>. However, a dot placed immediately before the semibreve that precedes the second breve causes it to be counted with that breve to complete a ternary <note>triple</note> division of time. Truly, a dot placed before a semibreve which immediately precedes three breves placed in sequence shows that the semibreve is to be transferred to the last of those breves: and it is to be counted with it to complete the ternary division of time. For it cannot be joined to the first breve, nor to the second semibreve; for "like before its like cannot be perfected" <note>original: "similis ante sibi similem non potest perfici"; a fundamental rule of music theory stating that a note is naturally "perfect" (3 units) when followed by another of the same value, and thus cannot be made perfect by an external dot or addition.</note>. Furthermore, a dot written between two semibreves placed between two breves proves that in the division, the first semibreve is joined to the first breve, and the second to the second. It should also be noted that a **dot of transportation** <note>original: "punctus transportationis"</note> ordains that the note to which it is attached is to be transferred not to the preceding notes, but to the following ones: namely, to the first place it is able to occupy. There are also those who surround a note to be transported in this way with two dots on either side, as is perceived in this tenor.

---

<margin>TENOR</margin>
<margin>CANTVS</margin>
<image-desc>Two musical examples in mensural notation. The first is a single staff labeled "TENOR" to the right. The second consists of two staves labeled "CANTVS" (the highest voice part) to the right. The notation includes various ligature forms and rhythmic signs.</image-desc>

¶ Therefore, the **dot of division** <note>original: "punctus diuisionis"</note> is applied only to notes brought forth in their **perfect** <note>triple</note> quantities. When it is applied to a **long** <term>long: "longa," a large note value</term> note, it perfects the **mood** <term>mood: "modus," the measurement of the relationship between the long and the breve</term>; if to a **breve**, the **time**; if to a **semibreve**, the **prolation** <term>prolation: "prolatio," the measurement of the relationship between the semibreve and the minim</term>; if to a **minim** <term>minim: the smallest standard note value in this system</term>, the prolation likewise. However, such a dot does not attach to a **maxima** <term>maxima: the largest note value</term>, since no larger figure is written for which the maxima itself could serve as a third part. Furthermore, the **dot of perfection** <note>original: "punctus perfectionis"</note> is that which, placed after a note, perfects it, rendering it divisible into three equal parts. This is also considered in two ways. First, when—

<sig>E ii</sig>

<summary>This page defines the different roles of the "dot" (punctus) in Renaissance music theory, specifically explaining how it groups notes into triple meters and how the "dot of transportation" moves a note's value to a later position in a phrase.</summary>
<keywords>punctus, dot of division, dot of transportation, dot of perfection, breve, semibreve, ternary division, mensural notation, Franchinus Gaffurius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the dot (punctus) in mensural music theory. The previous page ended while explaining how a dot placed between two semibreves indicates a division of time. This page transitions into the specific definitions of the "dot of perfection," "dot of augmentation," and "dot of division," illustrating them with musical examples for the Tenor and Cantus parts.</meta>

# BOOK II

[The dot] is placed after a note that is arranged in its perfect quantity: in that case, even if the note could be made imperfect by a smaller note preceding or following it, the dot causes it to remain in its own perfection. For this reason, it is called the **dot of perfection** <term>dot of perfection: Latin "punctus perfectionis"; a dot that prevents a "perfect" note (one worth three beats) from being reduced to two beats by surrounding smaller notes</term>. 

Secondly: when it is placed after a note that is arranged according to its imperfect quantity: in that case, it is known to increase that note by exactly half of its own value. For this reason, they call it the **dot of augmentation** <term>dot of augmentation: Latin "punctus augmentationis"; this function is the closest ancestor to our modern musical dot, which adds 50% to a note's duration</term>. Since it adorns the note with a ternary <note>triple</note> division, making it equal to a perfect figure, it has presumed to claim a certain perfection for itself; hence, they also call it the dot of perfection, which is distinguished by the present discourse.

---

<margin>TENOR <note>the structural base melody of a polyphonic composition</note></margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation for the Tenor part on a single five-line staff. It uses white mensural notation with various note shapes (maxima, longa, brevis, semibrevis) and punctus (dots).</image-desc>

<margin>CANTVS <note>the "song" or highest voice part</note></margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation for the Cantus part across two systems of five-line staves. It uses white mensural notation and includes various ligatures and punctus.</image-desc>

---

In this **tenor**, the dot placed after the first **breve** <term>breve: a square-shaped note value</term> causes it to remain in its perfection. Unless it were "perfected" by the dot, it could be made imperfect <note>reduced from three beats to two</note> by a third of its value—specifically, by the dotted **minim** followed by a **semiminim** <term>semiminim: a smaller note value, the ancestor of the modern quarter note</term>. The dot placed after the third breve does the same thing. 

However, the dot placed after the second breve under the sign of **imperfect time** <term>imperfect time: a measurement system where notes are divided into two parts rather than three, similar to modern 4/4 or 2/4 time</term> provides an augmentation of half its own value, showing that the note can be resolved into three equal parts. The same result is achieved by the dot placed after the third **semibreve** <term>semibreve: a diamond-shaped note value</term> under the same sign: for it increases it by a full half of its value, subjecting it to a ternary <note>triple</note> division. For this reason, it is said to be equivalent to a semibreve of **perfect prolation** <term>perfect prolation: a rhythmic setting where the semibreve is divided into three minims</term>. 

The same thing will happen to **maximas** and **longas** <term>large note values</term> when this kind of dot is applied: if they are arranged in their perfect quantity, they will maintain their own perfection; but in an imperfect quantity, they will receive augmentation. 

One and the same dot is often considered to be both a **dot of division** <term>dot of division: Latin "punctus divisionis"; a dot used to separate groups of notes to ensure they are measured correctly</term> and a dot of augmentation. For example, when it is placed immediately after a semibreve before a breve of perfect time (which cannot be made imperfect), and is followed immediately by a single minim and two semibreves in any arrangement: then the dot both measures the semibreve against the breve and increases it by half its value. Because of this, this kind of dot is commonly called both a dot of division and a dot of augmentation. 

Therefore, a dot placed *before* notes indicates only division; but when placed *after* a note, it can indicate both division and perfection. But a note adorned with a dot of perfection (which...

<summary>This page defines the three primary functions of the dot (punctus) in Renaissance music theory: perfection, augmentation, and division. It explains how these dots alter note values depending on whether the music is set in perfect or imperfect time.</summary>
<keywords>punctus perfectionis, dot of augmentation, dot of division, mensural notation, breve, semibreve, perfect time, imperfect time, tenor, cantus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the properties of musical notes and the "dot." It concludes a philosophical argument regarding the impossibility of a note being both perfect and imperfect simultaneously before moving into a detailed explanation of "Alteration" in Chapter Thirteen. The text cites the 14th-century theorist Johannes de Muris and provides the mathematical rules for doubling note values to maintain triple meter.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

<margin>The Philosopher <note>A common title for Aristotle in Renaissance academic texts.</note></margin>
...with the permission of some, I would say, it [the note] can sustain the imperfection of a third or any other part assigned to it: since perfection and imperfection are contrary to one another, and the Philosopher does not allow contraries in the same subject.

---

<margin>Johannes de Muris <note>A highly influential 14th-century French mathematician and music theorist whose works remained standard textbooks for centuries.</note></margin>
## Chapter Thirteen: On Alteration.

Alteration in measurable figures is defined by Johannes de Muris as the doubling of the proper value according to the form of the note: for it is called "alteration" <note>original: "alteratio"</note> as if it were the "action of another" <note>original: "alterius actio." This is a medieval etymological pun, suggesting the note acts like its "other" (the larger note value).</note>. It occurs indeed in notes arranged in their perfect quantity, and in those in imperfect quantity when they are the close parts of their perfect whole: such as a semibreve <term>semibreve: in this period, the basic unit of time, ancestor to our "whole note"</term> of imperfect prolation in perfect time; and a breve <term>breve: a note usually worth two or three semibreves</term> of imperfect time in the minor perfect mode; and so forth. For a minim <term>minim: the smallest standard note value at this time, ancestor to our "half note"</term> is altered to complete the division of perfect prolation <note>"Prolation" refers to the relationship between the semibreve and the minim.</note>. A semibreve is altered to complete the division of perfect time <note>"Time" (tempus) refers to the relationship between the breve and the semibreve.</note>. A breve is altered to complete the numerical integrity of the minor perfect mode <note>"Mode" (modus) refers to the relationship between the long and the breve.</note>. A long <term>longa: a note of very long duration, typically worth two or three breves</term> is usually altered to fulfill the ternary <note>triple</note> arrangement of the major perfect mode. Whenever only two minims are found between two semibreves of perfect prolation—or between the figured quantities of those two semibreves, for instance between two semibreve rests, or between a semibreve and a rest, or between a rest and a semibreve—the second is always altered to the equivalent quantity of two minims, unless a dot of division <note>a "punctus divisionis," which forces the notes to be counted separately</note> has been placed between them. The same thing happens when two semibreves are enclosed by two breves of perfect time or their equivalent quantities. Similarly, when two breves are found described between two longs of the minor perfect mode or their quantities. Likewise, when two longs are described between two maximae <term>maxima: the longest possible note in this system of notation</term> of the major perfect mode or their figured quantities: the second is always altered unless (as was said before) a dot of division is placed in between, as is clearly seen in the present Tenor.

<image-desc>Three horizontal staves of Renaissance mensural music notation. The music features various note shapes typical of the period, including maxims, longs, breves, semibreves, and minims, set on five-line staves. It includes C-clefs and points of division (punctus divisionis) to illustrate the textual rules regarding the alteration of note values in perfect and imperfect mensurations.</image-desc>

It explains how this applies to various note values—minims, semibreves, breves, and longs—and notes that a "dot of division" can be used to prevent this doubling.</summary>

-># BOOK TWO<-
<sig>D 2</sig>

<image-desc>
Three staves of mensural music notation on five-line staves. The notation includes longae (long notes), breves (square notes), and semibreves (diamond-shaped notes), with some notes in black (filled) and some in white (void) notation. It features various ligatures (connected notes) and rhythmic configurations illustrating the principles of alteration and imperfection described in the text.
</image-desc>

¶ Now, if a **rest** <term>rest: a symbol indicating a period of silence equal to a specific note value</term> of any note value and a similar **note** are placed between two larger notes as mentioned before: then either the rest precedes the note, or the note precedes the rest. If the rest precedes the note, the note itself is **altered** <note>Meaning its value is doubled to maintain the triple meter.</note>. However, if the note precedes the rest, **alteration** will not occur, since it is always the second note—and never the first—that is altered, and a rest can never be altered. There have been some, however, who attributed alteration to rests as well, but the common school of musicians rejects them.

There are, moreover, two very powerful reasons why they allowed the alteration of the second of two small notes rather than the first. The first reason is that when a sounding string is divided into two equal parts, the proportion in the second part will always be greater than in the first, as if in respect to the end: for they are accustomed to assign the greater and more perfect part to the conclusion.

<margin>Arithmetic mean <note>original: "Arithmetica medietas." This refers to a mathematical way of dividing an interval into two parts with equal differences.</note></margin>
The **arithmetic mean** clearly proves this, as it cuts the interval of an **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> on the monochord <note>original: "chordotono," a single-stringed instrument used to teach musical intervals.</note> into two equal differences: for the first and lower part will contain a **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron," with a 4:3 ratio (epitrita)</note> while the second part will contain a **fifth** <note>original: "diapenten," with a 3:2 ratio (hemiolia)</note>.

The second reason is that the first of two notes of this kind—let us say **semibreves**—could be changed into another shape in order to assume double its own value; for it could be written as a square figure. It would then be called a **breve**, and to complete the three-fold division, the second semibreve would then be attached to it. For this reason, it would be more fitting for a square **breve** to be made **imperfect** <note>original: "imperfecta," meaning its normal value of 3 is reduced to 2 by the following shorter note.</note> by the following semibreve, rather than having that first semibreve be altered. 

But the **second** semibreve of this kind is altered because it cannot take another form to grow into double its own quantity. For if it were to take the square shape of a breve, it would be equal not just to the value of two semibreves, but to **three**, since it would then happen to stand before another note similar to itself, which cannot be made imperfect. This is something any singer can easily see for themselves.

It should also be noted that when two similar notes—whether simple or in a **ligature** <note>A series of notes joined together in a single symbol, common in medieval and Renaissance notation.</note>—make a larger note **imperfect** <note>That is, they subtract from its value.</note> in regards to the two parts into which it can be divided, they are not subject to alteration; that is, the second of them is not doubled. But if those two smaller notes make the larger note imperfect in regards to the whole, which is divisible into three perfect or ternary parts, and they constitute the third part, then the second of them will be **altered**, as is shown in this example.

<summary>This page explains why the second of two notes is doubled (altered) in triple meter and why rests cannot be altered. The author provides mathematical proof using string proportions and octave divisions to justify these rhythmic rules.</summary>

<keywords>alteration, musical rest, arithmetic mean, octave, fourth, fifth, semibreve, breve, imperfection, triple meter, music theory</keywords>

# BOOK TWO
<sig>E 4</sig>

<image-desc>Four horizontal staffs of music in mensural notation with square and diamond-shaped notes. The first staff is labeled TENOR on the right. The second staff is labeled CANTVS on the right. The staffs include various ligatures and clef signs.</image-desc>

¶ In the present **tenor** <term>tenor: the fundamental voice part in medieval music that "holds" the melody</term>, the second **semibreve** <term>semibreve: a diamond-shaped note, ancestor to the whole note</term> following the first **long** <term>long: a note of long duration, usually equal to two or three breves</term> is not **altered** <note>Meaning its value is not doubled.</note>. This is because both semibreves do not **imperfect** <term>imperfect: to reduce the value of a "perfect" (triple) note by one-third</term> that long as a "proximate part" <note>A direct subdivision.</note>, since the music is in **imperfect minor mode** <term>imperfect minor mode: a rhythmic system where a long is worth two breves rather than three</term>. Instead, they relate to both parts. However, the second of those two semibreves written before two **ligated** <term>ligated: notes physically joined together in the manuscript</term> **breves** <term>breve: a square-shaped note, the standard unit of time</term> is altered, because that second note stands between a distinct value of one breve and another breve. The second of two **minims** <term>minim: the smallest standard note value in this system</term> following a single breve—specifically under the signs of **perfect time** and **perfect prolation** <note>Signs indicating a triple meter at multiple levels of the rhythm.</note>—is also altered. This is because both minims together imperfect that breve in its third part, which is called the "proximate part."

<margin>Johannes de Muris <note>Johannes de Muris (c. 1290–1350) was a French philosopher and one of the most influential music theorists of the Middle Ages.</note></margin>
Finally, we have seen fit to challenge the idea that an altered note can be "imperfected" from the front, as Johannes de Muris asserts. We believe it is in no way possible for the number three <note>original: "tris," referring to the triple value of a perfect or altered note.</note> to be divisible in such a manner, nor has it ever been accepted that a "like" note can be imperfected by another "like" note <note>A rule in mensural music: a note cannot be reduced in value by a note of its own same denomination.</note>.

***

->### Chapter Fourteen: On Diminution###<-

**Diminution** in **mensural song** <term>mensural song: music where every note has a precisely defined rhythmic duration</term> is the taking away of a certain quantitative value from the musical shapes themselves. It is usually demonstrated by musicians in three ways: First, **Canonically**. Second, **Proportionally**. Third, **Virgularly** <note>By use of a "virgula" or stroke through the time signature.</note>. 

**Canonical** diminution is considered to occur when the values of the notes decline and are varied in measurement according to a "canon" <note>original: "canonicis," here meaning a written rule or instruction.</note> or a written governing sentence. For example, a rule might state: "Let the **maxima** be a **long**. Let the **long** be a **breve**," and so on. In such a case, the **maxima** <term>maxima: the largest note value</term> is treated as a long, the long as a breve, the breve as a semibreve, and the semibreve as a minim. Furthermore, diminution is often understood in the opposite sense by this same method of "canon," such as: "Let the **breve** be a **maxima**. Let the **semibreve** be a **long**, and the **minim** be a **breve**." Because this varies according to the arbitrary choice of the musician, this type of "diminution" is named improperly, as it produces the effect of **augmentation** <note>Increasing the value</note> rather than diminution. For this reason, this type of diminution...

<summary>This page concludes the rules on "alteration" (doubling notes), specifically arguing against Johannes de Muris on whether triple-value notes can be subdivided, and introduces "diminution," the systematic reduction of note values.</summary>
<keywords>alteration, diminution, Johannes de Muris, mensural notation, imperfect minor mode, canon, musical proportions, perfect time</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Diminution from Chapter Fourteen, providing formal definitions and distinguishing between proportional diminution (using numbers) and "virgulary" diminution (using a slashed time signature). It concludes with Chapter Fifteen on Syncopation.</meta>

we call a variation of measurable quantity according to the primary arrangement of notes. Or thus: Diminution is the variation of the proper and primary quantity according to the shape of the note. Diminution taken **proportionally** is that which is established by the specific characters of numbers <note>original: "numerorum characteribus"</note> proving a certain proportion. For this reduces the shapes themselves according to the consideration of the arranged proportion. Conversely, this diminution is taken "improperly" when, for instance, a proportion of "lesser inequality" is maintained; for then, the shapes may actually undergo an increase in quantity <note>The author is noting a paradox where certain "proportions" might actually lengthen notes, even though the term "diminution" implies shortening.</note>.

<margin>Grammarians</margin>
Diminution is, however, sometimes named from a contrary sense, not unlike how **Grammarians** use the term "grove" <note>Latin: *lucus*</note> because it lacks "light" <note>Latin: *lux*; this is a famous medieval etymological joke, *lucus a non lucendo*.</note>, or a "pool" <note>Latin: *piscina*</note> when it is deprived of fish. We ascribe the fourth book of our consideration of musical shapes to this kind of proportional diminution. **Virgulary** <term>virgulary: from *virgula*, a small rod or stroke; referring to the vertical slash through a time signature, like the modern "cut time" symbol 𝇂</term> arranged diminution is that which is declared in this measurable description of shapes by a small stroke cutting through the sign of time. This properly belongs to the temporal measure, not to the shapes themselves; for by such a sign, the measure itself is diminished, not the number of the notes. For a **breve** <term>breve: a square-shaped note, usually the standard unit of time</term> of **perfect time**, whether it is performed in a diminished or whole manner, always contains three **semibreves** <term>semibreve: a diamond-shaped note, a subdivision of the breve</term>, with its whole perfection preserved. In the same way, a breve of **imperfect time** is always known to possess two semibreves, even when subject to diminution itself, which is noted in the present example.

---

¶ But since the **duple proportion** <note>A 2:1 ratio, doubling the speed.</note> is better known and easiest among the other proportions in both division and pronunciation, this "virgulary" diminution of the measure—which is twice as fast and equivalent to a duple proportion—is more frequently observed by singers.

### ¶ On Syncopation. Chapter Fifteen and the Last.

**Syncopation** <term>syncopation: in this context, the displacement of a note's value across a larger unit of time, creating an "off-beat" effect</term> in measurable song is the reduction of a note across a larger note or notes to another note or notes with which it agrees in the counting. For syncopation occurs in songs whenever a sequence of several shapes proceeds under a diminished count of the notes to be numbered—whether their quantitative arrangement is binary <note>two-part</note> or ternary <note>three-part</note>—as can be most clearly understood in this **tenor**.

<summary>This page defines Diminution as the reduction of note values, distinguishing between numerical proportions and the use of the "cut" time signature (virgula). It also introduces Syncopation as the displacement of notes across rhythmic boundaries.</summary>
<keywords>diminution, proportion, cut time, breve, semibreve, syncopation, tenor, cantus, mensural notation</keywords>

-># **SECOND**<-

<image-desc>Three musical staves of five lines each printed with white mensural notation. The first staff is labeled "TENOR" on the right side. The second staff is labeled "CANTVS" on the right side. The notation includes breves, semibreves, minims, ligatures, and rests of various durations (longa, brevis), illustrating the rhythmic principles of syncopation described in the text.</image-desc>

¶ Authors have agreed <note>original: "Convenere autem auctores"</note> that a **minim** <term>minim: a note value in mensural notation; at the time of its naming, it was the "smallest" (minima) value</term> note should not be carried across a **breve rest** <note>original: "pausam brevem"</note> via syncopation, but only across a **semibreve rest** (and even then, only rarely). Similarly, a **semibreve** note should rarely be brought across a breve rest, and never across a **long rest** <note>original: "pausam longae"</note>. They maintained that the remaining notes should be considered in a similar order. However, many of the more recent composers of songs disagree with these rules. For they not only lead a semibreve note across a breve rest through syncopation, but even—what is a more extreme practice—they establish that the semibreve itself may be transferred across a long rest through syncopation, arranging the other notes with similar shifts. 

But if some matters which you might consider necessary to both ecclesiastical <note>referring to plainchant or "Cantus Planus"</note> and measurable <note>referring to polyphonic music or "Cantus Mensurabilis"</note> instruction seem to have been omitted, believe that it was done intentionally. For I rely upon the diligence of my readers; indeed, I believe they will remain ignorant of nothing pertaining to this system of instruction if they studiously examine these volumes. It is difficult for the remaining parts not to be known to those by whom all, or at least most, things have already been very well understood.

<meta>This page begins Book I of a new section or treatise by Franchinus Gaffurius, following the conclusion of the previous book on rhythmic proportions. Here, the author shifts focus from the duration of notes to the nature of harmony and the fundamental definitions of counterpoint.</meta>

-># **BOOK I**<-

## ¶ On counterpoint and its elementary voices. Chapter I.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'H' containing a stylized six-petaled flower surrounded by leafy flourishes.</image-desc>

Harmonic modulation, according to the author Bacchius <note>Bacchius Geron (c. 4th century CE), a Greek writer on music theory</note>, is a universal type that indicates various "ideas"—that is, models or different compositions of song—which we indeed call **counterpoint** <term>counterpoint: from the Latin "contrapunctum," meaning "point against point," referring to the practice of placing one musical note (point) against another</term>. It is, as it were, a concordant sounding together of the outer notes <note>referring to the highest and lowest voices</note> corresponding to one another through the artful placement of notations. Even though it is established by certain rules, it is judged to be all the more beautiful the more noble the use to which it is put. Counterpoint is, therefore, the art of shaping singable sounds through proportional dimension and the measurement of time. For melody consists of voices, intervals, and times. Of voices, however, one is "pedestrian," by which we read orations and speak. Another is "equestrian," <note>original: "equæstris"; a metaphor for the rhythmic "gallop" of poetic meter</note> by which we recite poetic verses according to **Arsis and Thesis** <term>Arsis and Thesis: the "lifting" and "setting down" of the voice, referring to the upbeat and downbeat of rhythmic feet</term>. Another is "modulated," by which we produce something through natural singing or on instruments according to harmony. These modulated voices have intervals determined by a fixed dimension; pedestrian voices have indefinite ones, while equestrian voices are, in a way, a mixture of both. 

Regarding intervals: some encompass a "double dimension" <note>the 2:1 ratio of the octave</note> between equal-sounding outer voices. Others are consonant, such as the **sesquialtera** <note>original: "hemiolia"; the 3:2 ratio of a Perfect Fifth</note> and the **sesquitertia** <note>original: "epitrita"; the 4:3 ratio of a Perfect Fourth</note>. Others consist of a mixture of both in a triple or quadruple arrangement. Others (the smallest indeed) are a **sesquioctave** <note>the 9:8 ratio of a whole tone</note>. Others still are intervals concluded by outer voices of an uncertain and irrational dimension; we shall pursue a longer explanation of these in our study of instrumental harmony. 

<margin>
Bryennius.
Boethius.
Ptolemy.
</margin>
Regarding these, **Guido** <note>Guido d'Arezzo, the influential 11th-century monk who developed the staff and solfège</note> writes as follows: "The ditone <note>Major Third</note>, the semiditone <note>Minor Third</note>, and the semitone, although they join voices in singing, they receive no further division." We therefore call these intervals irrational and uncertain, for on the **monochord** <term>chordotono: a one-stringed scientific instrument used to measure musical ratios</term> they are separated from the first three multiples and set apart from the first two superparticulars <note>ratios like 2:1, 3:2, and 4:3 were considered "pure," while others were seen as "irrational" by strict Pythagoreans</note>; for the **Pythagoreans** ascribed all melodic consideration primarily to multiplicity or to superparticularity. These, indeed, are the intervals suitable for this art: namely, the intervals of the simple third and the simple sixth, from whose extremities concordances arise which we may (not without reason) call "muted." 

Of joined sounds that meet the ears in a certain concordance, there are three differences. 
1. Some are joined in such a way that they are perceived by the ears only as "fitting," such as the simple third and the simple sixth. 
2. Others not only suit the hearing but also produce a sense of similarity to themselves, such as the simple **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> and the simple **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: "diapaſon diapente"; a compound twelfth</note>. 
3. Others truly have reached such a point that they seem to produce a single sound to the ears, even though they are of unequal pitch, such as the simple **octave** <note>original: "diapaſon"</note> and the **double octave** <note>original: "biſdiapaſon"</note>. 

<margin>
Of concordances.
</margin>
Truly, **Bryennius** <note>Manuel Bryennius, a 14th-century Byzantine theorist</note> calls the sounds that only suit the ears **emeles** <note>original: "emeles"; from the Greek for "melodic" or "in tune," but not yet "consonant"</note>, meaning "melodic." He calls those that share a similarity **symphonic** <note>consonant</note>. But those that seem the same, he calls **homophonic** <note>unison</note> or **antiphonic** <note>octave</note>. 

<margin>
Some are perfect.
Some are imperfect.
Some are intermediate.
</margin>
**Boethius**, agreeing with **Ptolemy**, calls the first of such joined sounds—those which are rightly adapted to melody by the judgment of the ears—"emeles." Those that share a similarity arising from precise superparticular consideration, he calls **consonant**; but those which seem to be the same, consisting in multiplicity, he named **equal-sounding**. 

<margin>
Franco.
Guido.
</margin>
Posterity itself has derived a distinction from all of these in the art of counterpoint, such that it calls the **antiphonic** or equal-sounding voices "Perfect Species" of counterpoint. It calls the "emeles" (melodic) "Imperfect Species," and the consonant ones "Intermediate Species." This has indeed been asserted by **Franco** <note>Franco of Cologne, 13th-century theorist</note> and is known to have been declared by **Guido** in this opinion: "No sound accords perfectly with its fifth, and no..."

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental principles of counterpoint, defining it as the harmonious relationship between different voices and classifying musical intervals based on classical Greek and medieval authorities.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, harmony, Bacchius, Pythagoras, Guido d'Arezzo, Boethius, musical intervals, consonance, ratios, diapason, diapente, species of counterpoint</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the general principles of music theory to the specific rules of counterpoint. The author, Franchinus Gaffurius, begins Book Three by defining the scope of counterpoint and its relationship to mathematical proportions, citing classical and medieval authorities like Plato, Bacchius, and Anselmi of Parma.</meta>

-># **BOOK THREE**<-

->**THIRD**<-

<margin>
Anselmus:
The effect and property of counterpoint.
What an interval is.
Bacchius.
Plato.
</margin>
...to perfect concordances. And no voice creates a perfect concordance with another except for the perfect octave. Furthermore, the Art of counterpoint itself is finite, even though the songs themselves vary. For its mandates are not arbitrary and changing, but common and well-known. For even if it happens that the modes and diversities of songs are varied to infinity, the Art of counterpoint does not differ from other arts which have finite and common mandates, and are limited to a few rules, even while partial and singular instances proceed to infinity, as Anselmus <note>Giorgio Anselmi of Parma (1386–c. 1449), an Italian theorist whose work *De musica* influenced Gaffurius</note> asserts. Therefore, in this art, the more distinct the figures are and the larger their number—provided they are not confused by their own additions—the more aptly each voice can be disposed to melody and sung in their proper times, and the more easily and usefully the singers themselves may commit them to memory. Thus, the faculty of counterpoint transmits joined sounds that are concordant through the proportional measurement of intervals. Intervals, however, since they are certain silent transitions from sound to sound, are not audible (as Bacchius <note>Bacchius Geron, a Greek music theorist of late antiquity</note> says) but are intelligible <note>perceived by the mind rather than just the ear</note>. Therefore, the divine Plato, in the seventh book of the *Laws*, thinks that every concert ordered by art—even if the sweetness of the voice is lacking—is far better than when it is without order.

### ¶ Chapter II. On the nature and the naming of the species of counterpoint.

<margin>
Unison.
</margin>
It is necessary that the species or elements of counterpoint—whether on the strings of instruments or in vocal concert—proportionally follow the mixture of low and high sounds from which harmonic melody arises. Although this happens least of all to the **unison** <term>unison: two voices singing the same pitch</term>, which is simple and of a single nature, it nevertheless contributes the most increase to the other concordances (for they derive from it), just as unity does for numbers and a point for a line. Musicians, observing it in the place of unity, have harmoniously ascribed it to the concordances. They have imposed names upon these based on the number of sounds of each according to the arrangement of the diatonic genus: thus, they call the **ditone** <note>original: "ditonum"; here meaning the interval of a Third</note>, which consists of three sounds, a **third**. The **diapente** <note>original: "diapeten"; the interval of a Fifth</note>, drawn across five voices, they name a **fifth**. The diapente with a tone, consisting of six sounds, they call a **sixth**. The **diapason** <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an Octave</note>, proven by eight sounds, they call an **octave**, and the rest in the same manner. Among concordances of this kind, some are simple and primary: those which are conceived between the seven more essential and distinct sounds, such as the unison, the third, the fifth, and the sixth. Others are replicated and secondary, such as the octave, the tenth, the twelfth, and the thirteenth. These are **equisonant** <term>equisonant: sounding the same or equivalent, specifically across octaves</term> with the preceding ones, being conceived in a double dimension; for the octave is equisonant with the unison, the tenth with the third, the twelfth with the fifth, and the thirteenth with the sixth. Others are tripled and tertiary, such as the fifteenth, the seventeenth, the nineteenth, and the twentieth. For the fifteenth is equisonant with the unison and the octave; the seventeenth with the third and the tenth; the nineteenth with the fifth and the twelfth; and the twentieth with the sixth and the thirteenth. Furthermore, among these concordances, some sustain a consonant middle string <note>a note that falls between the two outer notes of the interval and remains harmonious</note>, while others do not. Of those that sustain a consonant middle string, some have it disjoined from the extremities by a narrower interval and distance, while others have it more distant. And the more acute and variable the interval of the middle between these extremities is, the more such a mixture perceives the nature of harmony. But if there is only a single middle string (however variable)—for instance, in that species which they call a **third**, for which only the second voice provides a middle—it is far removed from the nature of harmonic sweetness because of its simplicity. Indeed, they place this first in the order of joined and concordant voices, as if it were the first to depart harmoniously from the simplicity of the unison, just as the number two is the first to depart from unity itself. Those mixtures of voices which contain no middle string arranged according to the diatonic rule depart from the law of counterpoint, such as the tone and the semitone. Thus the third...

<margin>
Third.
</margin>

<vocab>counterpoint, unison, third, fifth, sixth, octave, equisonant, ditone, diapente, diapason</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental definitions of counterpoint, establishing it as a finite art governed by mathematical proportions and intervals. Gaffurius classifies musical intervals into primary, secondary, and tertiary categories, explaining how composite intervals (like the tenth or twelfth) relate back to their simple roots (the third or fifth).</summary>
