<meta>This page continues Gaffurius’s systematic classification of musical intervals used in counterpoint. Having established the definition of counterpoint in the previous page, he now examines the specific properties of the Third, Fifth, Sixth, and Octave, distinguishing between "perfect" and "imperfect" (or "wandering") species based on their mathematical proportions and acoustic stability.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

->**THE BOOK**<-

The third is arranged by the ditone <note>original: *ditono*; an interval of two whole tones, known as a major third</note>: namely, and the semitone: although when it is a major third <note>original: *ditonalis*</note> it may be transformed into the form of a minor third <note>original: *semiditoniã*</note> by the subtraction of a semitone; and when it is a minor third, it may be converted into a major third by the addition of a semitone; yet it is not said to change its proper concordance and its natural name. Indeed, that which is a ditone they call a "major third" from its larger interval; and that which is a semiditone they call a "minor third." And therefore, because of this kind of variety and its uncertain measurement, they have seen fit to call the third itself a "wandering" <note>original: *uagantē*</note> and "imperfect" species of counterpoint. Now, they assert that the minor third, which they call the semiditone or trihemitone, is a suitable distance. Through the diligence of the ancients (led by nature), from the change of the diatonic genus into the chromatic, they took a concordant interval from each tetrachord as an uncomposite larger interval; for which reason some call it the "chromatic third." But the diatonic third they call "enharmonic" because it encompasses the very rare and uncomposite interval of the enharmonic tetrachord, as was treated in the third chapter of the first book; but I shall explain these things more extensively in *Instrumental Harmony*. From this variation of the third, therefore, which occurs by the subtraction or addition of a semitone, it is certain that a transformation from one genus to another takes place.

<margin>The Fifth</margin> The Fifth <note>original: *Quinta*; also called the *diapente*</note>, however, which is an integral fifth made of three tones and a minor semitone, is produced in a sesquialtera <note>the ratio of 3:2</note> dimension. It occupies a middle concordant string between the extremes. For it is composed of the first two simple concords: namely, the minor third and the major third, with a concordant middle being preserved. From this, it derives a sweeter concordance at the extremities, as if it adheres to the harmonic middle by a certain imitation. If the string of this middle is intensified into the high pitch by the interval of a sixth <note>original: *exachordi*</note>, it will conclude a sharper harmonic middle between those extremities, completing the composite resonance of the octave <note>original: *diapaſon*</note>. And therefore, because the fifth itself contains the larger and most powerful interval of this harmonic consistency, the sesquialtera dimension of the fifth itself, arranged according to the diatonic genus, confers a more pleasing and sweet form of harmonic melody. The Sixth is named from six strings arranged diatonically: because it does not acquire a fixed measurement on the monochord <note>original: *chordotono*; a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals</note>

<margin>The Sixth</margin> it is imperfect. For the monochord is the string holding all the consonances arranged by musical proportions. Again, it happens that the sixth itself—produced by four tones and a minor semitone—is varied and diminished by the subtraction of a major semitone; although it does not lose its proper sound and name, one is called "major" and the other "minor." The major sixth is made of a fifth and a tone. The minor, however, is made of a fifth and a semitone. Hence, by this kind of variation, we can call the sixth itself "wandering," just like the third. For the sixth alone has a middle and suitable string: namely, that which is a third to the lower note and sounds a fourth <note>original: *diateſſaron*</note> below the higher note. For the consonance of the fourth, even if it is dissonant when sounded alone, yet when joined in a concordant mixture, it creates a concordant middle with the extremes, as if it were a participant and close relative of the harmonic middle. But frequently such a mediated sixth—namely, when the middle has sounded a diatonic major third to the lower note—sometimes requires the middle string itself to be relaxed (as we are taught by the experience of instruments); for then, by a certain continuation of sound, that fourth is bent somewhat sweetly toward participation in the fifth, and that major third is somewhat divided, being turned into a continuation of the sweeter minor third and the semitonal third. It is not, therefore, incongruous to name the third and the sixth "imperfect" and "wandering" concordances, since they are participants, in a certain way, of one and the same consideration and progression. The Octave, which is the equal-sounding

<margin>The Octave</margin> *diapason*, consists of five tones and two minor semitones, and is established by the double <note>the ratio 2:1</note> dimension of the monochord; it is a perfect species of counterpoint, and indeed the first of the harmonic species.

# BOOK THREE

...consisting of a harmonic mean <note>original: *harmonica medietas*; a mathematical proportion where the ratio of the differences between three terms is equal to the ratio of the outermost terms</note>. For in the same proportion that its longer string (emitting a lower sound) maintains [a ratio] with the shorter string (which produces the highest sound and is equal-sounding <note>original: *æquisonū*; referring to the octave</note>), in that same way, the interval or difference between the longer and the middle string—which is a fifth <note>original: *diapente*</note>—is known to be derived in relation to the difference or interval of the middle and the higher string, which completes a fourth <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>. Again, the larger interval, which is defined by larger numbers, possesses the greater consonance: namely, the fifth. But the smaller distance, led by fewer numbers, contains the smaller consonance: namely, the fourth; which indeed is a property of the harmonic mean, as I shall mathematically demonstrate in *Instrumental Harmony*. It is therefore established that the mean is the constitution of harmony, as the interpreter Peter of Abano <note>original: *Petrus de habano*; a 13th-century physician and philosopher who wrote a famous commentary on Aristotle's *Problems*</note> testifies in the twelfth problem of the harmonic section.

<margin>
Peter of Abano.
Aristotle.
</margin>

For which reason, since the octave is the first and smallest equal-sounding interval that is harmonically mediated, Aristotle himself, in the eighteenth problem, asserts that it alone consonates perfectly. In addition to this, between the lowest and the middle string, it receives in turn a certain middle and concordant one: namely, the major and minor third; which has been touched upon before in the consideration of the fifth itself. Thus, the harmonically mediated octave is that first and simple harmony by which all musical song shines forth with a more pleasing and sweeter modulation.

<margin>
Tenth.
</margin>

The tenth <note>original: *decima*</note>, however, which is an octave added to a third, tastes of the nature of the third itself: for it is imperfect and wandering; since by the subtraction of a semitone it is minor, and by the addition, major. For it does not receive a resonant middle string between itself and the octave, because it is the first species departing from it, just as the simple third is from the unison. For the tenth itself supports three concordant middle sonorities: namely, the third, the fifth, and the octave. And although these conclude a harmonic consistency between the first (lower) note and the fifth and octave, nevertheless, by the admixture of the wandering imperfect intervals—namely, of the third relative to the lower note, and the sixth arranged between the fifth and the higher tenth itself—they dull it and make it less sweet. For everything well and rightly arranged, when it receives something misshapen and incongruous, will suffer an alteration of its own nature.

<margin>
Twelfth.
</margin>

The twelfth <note>original: *duodecima*</note>, however, being equal-sounding to the fifth, consists of eight whole tones and three minor semitones; for it is made from an octave and a fifth. Since it preserves only the octave as a mean, sustaining a double and a sesquialter dimension <note>The ratio 3:1 can be broken into 2:1 (octave) and 3:2 (fifth)</note>, it will comply with the triple harmonic mean, derived from the consideration of the extremes and the differences. This is proven by these numbers: 12, 6, 4. For if I assign the number 12 to the lowest and longest string which is Gamma <note>original: *Γ*; the lowest note of the medieval system</note>, and the number 6 to its octave (namely, G-sol-re-ut), and then the number 4 to the twelfth itself (namely, D-la-sol-re), the extreme terms to one another and the differences between them are preserved in one and the same proportion. For just as twelve to four creates a triple proportion, so the difference of twelve to six (which is six) preserves a triple comparison to the difference of six to four (which is two). Likewise, there is a larger proportion in the larger numbers, and thus a greater consonance; and in the smaller numbers, a smaller consonance; which (as we said before) is a property of the harmonic mean. It also supports a fifth string arranged by harmonic mean between the first and the octave, to which the twelfth itself is an octave. But if you interpose a third string to it, and an octave of that third (which is a tenth to the first note), because they are dull <note>original: *surdæ*; often used to describe intervals that do not fit perfectly into Pythagorean ratios</note> and irrational, they suspend the sweetness of that harmonic consistency.

<margin>
Thirteenth.
</margin>

The thirteenth imitates the nature of the sixth, since it is distant from it by an octave in the high register; for it is imperf[ect]...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical basis of musical intervals using the 'harmonic mean,' specifically analyzing why the octave and twelfth are 'perfect' while the tenth and thirteenth are 'imperfect' or 'wandering.'</summary>

# BOOK THREE

...and varied: minor indeed by the subtraction of a semitone, and major by its addition. It is mediated harmoniously by the third, sixth, octave, and tenth, influencing the sweetness of the harmonic composition.

<margin>The fifteenth</margin> 

The fifteenth—an equal-sounding double octave <note>original: *bisdiapason*; an interval of two full octaves</note>—contains ten tones and four minor semitones. Furthermore, it preserves within itself the entire power and nature of harmonic melody, for it supports and governs the seven species of harmonic composition, namely the species of the octave <note>original: *diapason*</note>. For this reason, antiquity ascribed the arrangement of fifteen essential strings to the "most perfect system" <note>This refers to the *Systema Teleion* of Ancient Greek music theory, which spanned two octaves</note>. This interval, arranged by a continuous halving of a single string, is known to align with geometric proportionality because of its physical magnitude, which it obtains from two equal measurements of the equal-sounding octave. For by whatever proportion the larger number maintains the middle, that same proportion occurs from the middle to the lesser. 

And again, the difference between the larger and the middle is related to the difference between the middle and the lesser by an equal consideration, which is the property of geometric proportionality <note>In a geometric progression like 16, 8, 4, the ratio 16:8 is the same as 8:4, and the difference (8) has the same ratio to the second difference (4)</note>. For example, when I mark the longer string of the monochord <note>original: *chordotoni*; a single-stringed instrument used for measuring intervals</note>—whose lowest sound is Gamma <note>original: *Γ*; the lowest note of the medieval "Guidonian" scale</note>—with the number 16, and I assign the number 8 to its octave (namely, G-sol-re-ut), and then I apply the number 4 to its fifteenth, which is the high G-sol-re-ut. By this consideration, 16 to 8 creates a "double proportion," establishing the equal-sounding octave, and 8 to 4 is similarly a "double proportion." Again, the difference of the larger numbers (which is 8) compared to the difference of the smaller numbers (which is 4) proves an equal relationship. 

And thus, the sounds themselves are to be understood through such proportioned limits of the strings, while the intervals of the strings are understood through the differences. For just as the terms hold themselves to each other in a double ratio, so does the lower and higher interval relate to the smaller and higher one. It likewise supports the fifth string and the twelfth, mediated harmoniously and sounding sweetly. Truly, with a third and tenth string interspersed, a most sweet harmony of this kind will reach the ears, though somewhat shaded and colored. 

<margin>The seventeenth</margin> 

The seventeenth is equal-sounding to the third and the tenth; it is a fifteenth plus a third, or an octave plus a tenth. It is wandering and imperfect, following the sonority of the third itself; it is mediated harmoniously by the third, fifth, octave, tenth, twelfth, and fifteenth. 

<margin>The nineteenth</margin> 

The nineteenth is an equal-sounding double octave plus a fifth, or an octave plus a twelfth; it supports the third, fifth, octave, tenth, twelfth, fifteenth, and seventeenth as harmonious mediations. 

<margin>The twentieth</margin> 

The twentieth, moreover, sounds as an octave to the thirteenth, and a double octave to the sixth; it supports many harmonious middle strings, namely the third, sixth, octave, tenth, thirteenth, fifteenth, and seventeenth. However, those intervals that exceed the fifteenth, because they go beyond the physical magnitude of geometric moderation, are in a way "deaf" and weak, sometimes destitute of the nature of their own proper sonority. 

The remaining combinations of sounds harshly offend the sense of hearing when the outer strings are struck together—for instance, the simple second, the fourth, and the seventh, as well as those intervals that sound equivalent to these simpler ones. Therefore, they are excluded from the elements of counterpoint <note>original: *contrapuncti*; the art of combining independent melodic lines</note>, for in songs they find no stable place (except perhaps in the swiftest passage of rhythmic time).

### ¶ On the eight mandates or rules of Counterpoint. Chapter III.

<margin>The first rule</margin>

# BOOK THREE

...in the octave or in the fifteenth: or even in the fifth and twelfth: which, although they are not perfectly complete, are nevertheless ascribed to the perfect concords <note>In early music theory, "perfect" intervals were the unison, octave, fifth, and their compounds. "Imperfect" referred to thirds and sixths</note> due to their sweeter sonority. But this first mandate is not necessary, but arbitrary; for they attribute perfection in all things not to the beginnings, but to the endings. For this reason, many have established the beginnings of their melodies with imperfect concords <note>original: *imperfectis concordantiis*; intervals like thirds or sixths that sound pleasant but were considered "unstable" and needing resolution</note>, as is proven by these examples.

->CANTVS&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;CANTVS&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;CANTVS<-

->TENOR&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;TENOR&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;TENOR<-

<margin>**Second rule**</margin>
For the first composition of this song takes its beginning through a third. The second through a sixth. The third through a tenth: which, even if they are imperfect elements of the harmony, are nevertheless known to be harmoniously and concordantly suitable for beginnings of this kind. The second rule is that two perfect species of the same kind cannot be established consecutively and immediately, either by ascending or descending together in a melody: for instance, two unisons, or two octaves, or two fifteenths, or even two fifths or twelfths. Although these [fifths and twelfths] are not strictly perfect, they are numbered among the perfect ones because of the sweetness they possess, following their rules and mandates. For this rule is not arbitrary, but legal—rejecting every possible exception. 

Some, however, have thought that two fifths could be sounded ascending or descending together, provided they are of different quantities and intervals: namely, one perfect <note>A "perfect fifth" is the standard interval of seven semitones</note> and the other diminished by the subtraction or lack of a semitone <note>A "diminished fifth" is six semitones, often called the *tritone* or "the devil in music" for its dissonance</note>: for example, proceeding from A-re to E-la-mi <note>A to E</note>, or from the <term>proslambanomene</term> to the <term>hypatenmeson</term> <note>These are Greek names for notes in the Greater Perfect System; here, they represent a leap from a low note to a middle note</note>. Then, subsequently and immediately ascending from B-natural-mi-grave to F-fa-ut <note>B to F, which is a naturally occurring diminished fifth</note>, or from the <term>hypatehyparon</term> to the <term>parhypatenmeson</term>: which in my opinion is false. 

<margin>
<unclear>It is possible in singing [...] the fifth desy [...]</unclear>
</margin>

For no one doubts that a fifth diminished by a semitone is unsuitable for a melody, however great and well-known that diminution may be. Hence, the harmonic mean, arranged diatonically, does not allow the order of the species of the fifth <note>original: *diapenticarum specierum*</note> to be led from the <term>proslambanomene</term>. However, the fifth itself (as organists assert) can endure a very small, hidden, and somewhat uncertain amount of diminution, which they call "participated" <note>original: *participata*; this refers to early keyboard tuning or "temperament," where intervals were slightly adjusted so that more keys would sound acceptable</note>. 

<margin>**Third rule**</margin>
The third rule is: that between two perfect concords of the same kind, whether moving in different or similar directions (higher or lower), at least one imperfect concord—such as a third or a sixth and the like—must be placed in the middle. Likewise, several similar or even dissimilar imperfect ones—such as two, three, or four thirds and one or more sixths—are quite appropriately arranged between those two perfect ones of the same kind, as the present example shows.

<summary>This page outlines the foundational rules of counterpoint, explaining that while songs can begin with "imperfect" intervals like thirds, they must avoid "parallel perfect intervals" like back-to-back octaves or fifths. It also discusses the controversy of using diminished fifths and notes how organists use "participated" tuning to adjust intervals.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, perfect concords, imperfect concords, fifths, octaves, diminished fifth, musical rules, temperament, organists</keywords>

<margin>The fourth rule</margin>
Furthermore, a counterpoint is not permitted if it contains a dissonance—such as a second, a fourth, or a seventh—between two perfect concords of the same kind moving in similar motion, whether ascending or descending. This applies regardless of whether the dissonance itself is noted with a shorter rhythmic figure, such as a <term>semiminim</term> <note>equivalent to a quarter note</note> or <term>fusa</term> <note>original: seminiminima; a very short note value</note> or even a <term>minim</term> <note>equivalent to a half note</note>, or if they take the form of a larger temporal figure like a <term>semibreve</term> <note>a whole note</note> or a <term>breve</term> <note>a double whole note</note>. For if an obvious and known dissonance does not suit the counterpoint, it cannot take the place or role of an imperfect concord; thus, even a dissonance "hidden" by its own speed can by no means support this type of imperfect concord.

The fourth rule is that several perfect and *dissimilar* concords can be used consecutively in counterpoint, whether ascending or descending: such as a fifth after a unison or after an octave, and an octave after a fifth, and others in the same manner, as is established by this harmony.

SOPRANO
[A five-line musical staff with a C-clef on the first line. The mensural notation shows an example of the "fifth rule," demonstrating that two perfect concords of the same kind can follow each other if the voices move in contrary motion.]

TENOR
[A five-line musical staff with a C-clef on the fourth line, providing the lower voice in contrary motion to the Soprano above.]

<margin>The fifth rule</margin>
The fifth rule is that two similar perfect concords can be placed consecutively and immediately in counterpoint, provided they proceed by dissimilar and contrary motions. For example, if the first of two octaves is stretched into the high register and the second is brought down into the low register—and vice versa. Similarly, when there are two fifths following each other immediately, the first of which is guided by <term>thesis</term> <note>the "falling" or lowering of the voice/beat</note> and the second by <term>arsis</term> <note>the "rising" or lifting of the voice/beat</note>, or vice versa, as this example proves.

<summary>This page outlines the fourth and fifth rules of counterpoint, specifically prohibiting "hidden" dissonances in similar motion while permitting consecutive perfect intervals if they are of different types or move in contrary motion.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

[A musical score featuring three voices in white mensural notation. The staves are five lines each, utilizing various C and F clefs. There are three separate staves arranged vertically.]

->SOPRANO&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;TENOR&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;CONTRATENOR<-
<note>original: CANTVS... TENOR... CONTRATENOR</note>

<margin>The sixth rule.</margin>
In the arrangement of this counterpoint, the first note of the soprano <note>original: cantus</note>—that is, the higher part—is an octave above the tenor in the high range. The second note, however, is an octave below the tenor, because the soprano is lowered by an octave and the tenor is raised by an octave. Similarly, the first <term>semibreve</term> <note>the equivalent of a whole note</note> of the contratenor is a fifth above the tenor. The second is a fifth above the soprano. Likewise, the last semibreve or the penultimate note of the contratenor is a fifth below the tenor. The very last note of the contratenor is a <term>long</term> <note>a note of long duration, usually four times a semibreve</note>; it is also a fifth, but above the tenor, which happens to occur in almost all melodies <note>original: cantilenae</note>. The sixth rule is that the parts of a song—namely the tenor, soprano, and contratenor—ought to be contrary to one another in their motion: so that when the soprano ascends, the tenor descends, and vice versa. The contratenor should behave in the same manner relative to one of the other parts. However, this law is flexible <note>original: arbitraria</note>. For often and in many cases, the notes of the tenor follow the ascending or descending notes of the soprano with similar motions; the same occurs in the progression of the contratenor. This happens most especially when the parts of the song chase <note>original: fugant; a reference to imitation or "fugal" style</note> each other with the same movements and figures, as this melody demonstrates.

[A musical score for two voices, labeled Soprano and Tenor on the right side. The notation is in white mensural style on two five-line staves.]

<margin>SOPRANO</margin>
<margin>TENOR</margin>

<margin>The seventh rule.</margin>
The seventh rule is that when we move from an imperfect concord <note>intervals like thirds or sixths</note> to a perfect concord <note>intervals like unisons, fifths, or octaves</note> as a conclusion to the melody or any harmonic part of it, it is necessary to converge toward the closer perfect concord by the different motions of each part. For example: when the tenor and soprano sound a major sixth <note>original: sextam maiorem</note>—namely a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: diapente</note> with a whole tone—then both proceed with contrary motions: the tenor descending by a single step and the soprano likewise raised by a single step into the high range. They will meet at the octave, which is closer to that sixth when the opposition of their motions is considered. It is characteristic of the major sixth to pass <note>original: transmeare</note> into the octave. A minor sixth, however, more frequently returns to a fifth with one part of the song remaining stationary and the other moving; yet it also passes to an octave through contrary motions. Again, when the soprano and tenor sound a third, they will meet in a unison <note>original: unisonum</note> (the first being led by the closeness of the contrary motions). Likewise, both expanding in the opposite direction are transferred to a fifth, which is easily proven by this harmony.

<summary>This page defines the sixth and seventh rules of counterpoint, focusing on the use of contrary motion between voices and the proper resolution of imperfect concords (thirds and sixths) into perfect concords (unisons, fifths, and octaves).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on counterpoint from the previous page, specifically detailing how the Soprano and Tenor parts interact through various intervals before introducing the eighth rule and the treatment of dissonances in Chapter 4.</meta>

# BOOK [THREE]

[A musical score in white mensural notation. The first system shows two voices, SOPRANO and TENOR, on a five-line staff with diamond and square-shaped notes. The second system, at the bottom of the page, shows three voices: SOPRANO, TENOR, and CONTRATENOR.]

->SOPRANO<- ->TENOR<-

¶ In this example, the Soprano <note>original: Cantus</note> and Tenor move from a third with the first <term>semibreve</term> <note>the basic unit of time, like a whole note</note> into a unison at the second semibreve. Then, from the fourth semibreve, which sounds a minor third, they meet in a fifth with the fifth semibreve by contrary motions. From the fifth semibreve to the sixth semibreve, with only the soprano moving (the tenor standing still, that is), they turn into a minor sixth. From this note, proceeding to the seventh semibreve, the motion of the soprano alone leads to a fifth against the stable tenor. But with the eighth semibreve, again by its own motion, it is stretched to assign a minor sixth to the unmoved tenor. After this, when both are drawn away from each other by contrary motions (by the interval of a tone on both sides, that is), they establish the ninth semibreve at the closer octave. And from the eleventh semibreve, led similarly into a sixth, they proceed by contrary motions to the sounding equality of the octave with the twelfth semibreve. In other matters, too, it is fitting to approach a similar consideration.

<margin>The eighth rule</margin>
The eighth and final rule is that every melody <note>original: Cantilena</note> must be finished and terminated in a perfect concord: namely, either in a unison, as was the custom of the Venetians, or in an octave, or in a fifteenth; this is what every school of musicians observes most frequently for the sake of achieving a harmonic <term>mediocrity</term> <note>here meaning a balanced middle ground or perfect proportion</note>. For, as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> bears witness, the end is the perfection of every thing.
<margin>The Philosopher</margin>

### ¶ Which dissonances are to be admitted in counterpoint, and where. Chapter 4.

We have seen fit to express which <term>dissonances</term> <note>intervals that sound harsh or "unstable" to the ear</note> are to be admitted in counterpoint with a brief description. For a straight semibreve, following the full measure of time—in the manner, naturally, of a steady pulse—cannot be subject to dissonance in counterpoint, as the masters of the art have established. Similarly, they do not admit a <term>breve</term> <note>a note twice as long as a semibreve</note> as a dissonant note; for dissonance itself, when it is a full note, corrupts the nature and sweetness of the harmony. However, that dissonance which is hidden through <term>syncopation</term> <note>suspending a note so it becomes dissonant on a strong beat before resolving</note> and again by a swift transit is admitted in counterpoint.

¶ Indeed, this happens in nearly all melodies: when we hold an imperfect concord, from which we proceed immediately through contrary motions of the parts to a perfect concord near to it, then the <term>minim</term> <note>a note half the value of a semibreve</note> or even the semibreve immediately preceding that imperfect concord will be a dissonance: specifically, either a second when it arrives at a unison from a third; or a fourth when they proceed into a fifth; or a seventh when they leap to the equal-sounding octave. And for that reason, a dissonance of this kind, being syncopated, remains hidden, bringing no injury to the ears, as is perceived in this example.

[A musical score for three voices: SOPRANO, TENOR, and CONTRATENOR, written in white mensural notation on five-line staves.]

->SOPRANO<- ->TENOR<- ->CONTRATENOR<-

¶ In this harmony: the first minim of the soprano creates a second against the tenor (a certain manifest dissonance). And the second minim of the soprano is likewise a fourth to the tenor, a most well-known dissonance; I would rarely allow these to be admitted, for their discord is notable, however swiftly [they pass]...

# BOOK THREE

when moving, it occupies only half of a <term>semibreve</term> <note>the basic unit of time in this period's music</note>. Many, however, admitted such a dissonant <term>minim</term> <note>a shorter note value, half of a semibreve</note> and semibreve, such as <margin>Dunstable, Binchois, Dufay, Brassart</margin> Dunstable, Binchois, Dufay, and Brassart. However, when the last semibreve of the soprano <note>original: cantus</note> is divided into two minims: the second minim creates a dissonant seventh with the last semibreve of the tenor, but it is hidden, led by a <term>syncopation</term> <note>original: sincopa; a rhythmic technique where a note is held across a beat, often used to prepare a dissonance</note>. You will find the same thing in the last semibreve of the <term>contratenor</term> <note>the voice part written "against" the tenor, often occupying a similar range</note>: when you have divided it into two minims, the second of them will show a dissonant fourth against the last semibreve of the tenor, but it is hidden. Therefore, it is necessary to admit such minims and even a dissonant semibreve in counterpoint. There is also a hidden dissonance in counterpoint besides syncopation: which is contained and muffled <note>original: obtundi; meaning the dissonance is softened by the surrounding harmony</note> between several concordant parts of the song. A semibreve diminished by half <note>into a minim</note> and a breve by fourfold <note>also into a minim</note> and other such things that are equivalent in value to a minim, can be sustained in counterpoint even if they are dissonant.

### On the Agreeable Sweetness of the Fourth. Chapter V.

But how is the dissonance that arises from four sounds joined together—namely the Fourth <note>original: Diatessaron</note>—admitted in two places in counterpoint? First, when the tenor and soprano <note>original: cantus</note> sound an octave together, then the middle voice called the contratenor is placed at a fifth above the tenor: led, that is, through three tones and a semitone; from the highest sound which completes the sounding octave to the lower, it will be distant by a fourth toward the bass. And so a fourth of this kind between the contratenor and the soprano, because it is harmonically mediated <note>original: harmonice mediata; referring to the division of an octave into a fifth and a fourth</note>, will harmonize perfectly, as the present composition shows.

<image-desc>A musical score in white mensural notation. There are three distinct voice parts arranged horizontally: the CANTVS (Soprano) on the left, the TENOR in the middle, and the CONTRATENOR on the right. The music illustrates a three-part harmony where the contratenor sits between the other two voices.</image-desc>

->SOPRANO&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;TENOR&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;CONTRATENOR<-

¶ In this composition: the first note and likewise the last note of the contratenor, sounding a fifth above the tenor and a fourth below the soprano, establishes a harmonic mediation. Second, when the tenor and soprano proceed through one or more sixths: then the middle voice (the contratenor) will always hold a fourth below the soprano, always observing a third above the tenor. Moreover, singers call counterpoint of this kind <term>Fauxbourdon</term> <note>literally "false bass"; a technique of parallel harmony used to create a rich, full sound</note>, in which indeed the middle part itself, the contratenor, most often follows the notes of the soprano, proceeding by a lowering of a fourth beneath them, which is more frequently observed by musicians in the settings of psalmody. And thus a fourth of this kind between the contratenor and the soprano, even led through semibreves and <term>breves</term> and <term>longs</term> <note>longer rhythmic values</note>, is harmoniously admitted in counterpoint, as is more clearly declared in this composition.

<image-desc>A second musical score in mensural notation, illustrating the technique of "Fauxbourdon." Three voices (CANTVS, TENOR, and CONTRATENOR) are shown moving in parallel motion, demonstrating the specific interval relationships described in the text.</image-desc>

->SOPRANO&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;TENOR&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;CONTRATENOR<-

¶ For it is clear from the proposed composition that all the notes of the contratenor are lowered by a fourth from the notes of the soprano, and from the notes of the tenor, they conclude a sixth to the soprano.

¶ Why the Fourth is concordant between a middle and higher sound, and discordant between a middle and lower sound.

### Chapter Six.

<margin>Boethius</margin>
And if a sixth, mediated by a third above the tenor, concordantly supports a fourth between the middle term and the high one: since it is arranged between those two concordances—the third and the sixth (although they are imperfect)—it is overshadowed by them like a smaller thing by larger ones. However, this is known to be derived from both nature and art. For higher sounds are generated by faster motions. Therefore, they are weaker than the lower ones produced by slower motion, as we described in the first book of the second part of theory. Therefore, by that very speed, the dissonance of the fourth remains hidden in the high register. It is different when it is conceived in lower sounds. For then it returns a sound unpleasant to the ears because of the slowness of the motions, which our <margin>Boethius</margin> Boethius asserts in the first book of *Music* is inherent in lower sounds. For slowness naturally claims more time for itself than speed. Here it can be more discordant in lower sounds; consequently, the dissonance is perceived as more noticeable, which <margin>Aristotle</margin> Aristotle mentions in the 21st problem of the harmonic section. For this reason, counterpoint does not tolerate this kind of dissonance of the fourth in those low sounds.
¶ On the Conformity and Diversity of the Third and Sixth.

### Chapter Seven.

It is established that the primary imperfect simple concordances—namely the third and the sixth—are created by the motions of one another within the arrangement of the resonant octave. For when two sounds are arranged equally distant by an octave, as in the tenor and the soprano <note>original: cantu; the upper voice</note>: if the soprano is lowered by a sixth, a third is immediately arranged above the tenor. When, in such an arrangement of the octave, it produces a sixth raised from the lower string: it will substitute a major or minor third relative to the soprano, according to the natural <term>diatonic</term> <note>the standard scale of seven notes</note> tenor and the progression of semitones. I add (which is more significant) that a middle string placed in the harmonic octave arrangement generates both of these intervals relative to the outer notes with a single motion; for when it is lowered by a third, it will create a third against the lower note and a sixth against the higher one. When, however, that middle sound is raised by a semitone or tone, it will prove a third against the higher note and a sixth against the lower one. Furthermore, if the soprano is lowered from the octave by a major or minor third, it will immediately fall into a minor or major sixth. Similarly, when the tenor sounds an octave below the soprano, if it ascends by a third, it will result in a sixth against the soprano; this becomes clear through a careful examination of this composition.

->SOPRANO&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;TENOR<-

<image-desc>A musical score in white mensural notation on two five-line staves. The upper staff is labeled "CANTVS" and the lower staff "TENOR". The musical example demonstrates counterpoint between the two voices, showing how intervals like thirds and sixths interact within the space of an octave.</image-desc>

¶ This also should be noted: if the <term>species</term> <note>the specific type or quality of an interval</note> or concordance whose raising or lowering produces the other is major—for instance, a third raised from the tenor or lowered from the soprano—the resulting one will be minor (namely the sixth), and vice versa. This is because in the perfect octave itself, only five tones and two minor semitones are contained. It is clear, therefore, that in the arrangement of the octave, a third is easily generated from the motion of a sixth, and a sixth is concluded from the path of a third. Hence, because both are imperfect, they seem to preserve one and the same nature and property. Furthermore

It also details the mathematical and musical relationship between thirds and sixths as "inverted" versions of each other within an octave.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of counterpoint and interval relationships from Book Three, following the analysis of the fourth (diatessaron). It shifts focus to the "sweetness" of the third compared to the sixth and begins Chapter Eight, which addresses how the outer notes of intervals are named according to the Guidonian system of solmization.</meta>

<header>BOOK THREE</header>

We stated moreover in the third chapter, under the seventh rule, that the third moves most fittingly to the **unison** and to the **fifth**, and the sixth to the **octave**, by contrary motions; for this reason, they are led poorly by a single motion <note>original: "in eptius unico deferuntur motu"; referring to parallel motion, which was restricted in formal counterpoint</note>. From this, it is established that the third is surrounded by two contiguous perfect <term>concords</term> <note>concordantia: a harmonious combination of sounds</note> (approaching them by contrary motions)—namely, the fifth and the unison—and is thus sweeter than the sixth. For although the sixth is bounded by the octave and the fifth, it does not proceed to both by contrary motions; it is converted into a fifth by a single motion, even if it transcends to the octave by two contrary motions, as is clear here.

<image-desc>A small musical notation diagram placed to the right of the main text block, consisting of three short segments of counterpoint on five-line staves using square mensural notation. It illustrates the intervallic movements described in the text, such as a third moving to a unison or fifth.</image-desc>

Furthermore, the sixth shares more of the nature of the fifth than of the octave, because it is closer and contiguous to the fifth; indeed, no middle voice falls between the fifth and the sixth. Between the sixth and the octave, however, a seventh note is naturally placed. Therefore, the sixth, being more remote from the octave, participates more in the nature of the fifth. And since the fifth is far distant in perfection from the octave, the sixth itself—sharing in the nature of that fifth—possesses a less perfect sonority than the third, which (as a middle between extremes) shares in the perfection of both the fifth and the unison. For the third is known to pass over with a primary and more direct impulse by contrary motions (as has been said) to both the fifth and the unison itself; the perfection of the octave is simply ascribed to the unison. The underlying descriptions of the notes prove this in the following way.

<image-desc>A rectangular musical diagram featuring three systems of two-voice counterpoint on five-line staves. The music uses square notation to demonstrate various interval progressions (thirds, fifths, sixths, and octaves) as explained in the preceding text. The systems are separated by horizontal divider lines.</image-desc>

### ¶ On the Naming of the Extreme Sounds in Concords. Chapter VIII.

There are also those who consider the extreme <term>strings</term> <note>chordas: here referring to the pitches or notes</note> of concords by many kinds of vowel syllable names <note>referring to the solmization syllables Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La</note>. For they say that when the <term>tenor</term> <note>the fundamental melody or base voice</note> is pronounced as *ut*, the melody <note>cantus: the upper voice</note> is in **unison**; similarly, it will be sung as a **third** with *mi* and *sol*, as a **fifth** with *mi* and *sol*, and as a **sixth** with *la* in a single <term>hexachord</term> <note>exacordo: a six-note scale system</note>. But in the **octave**, the melody itself will sound the same as the tenor, and they believe the remaining intervals should be named according to this kind of consideration. 

However, we (even if we described this very order in *The Flower of Music* <note>original: "in flore musicæ"; likely referencing Gaffurio's earlier work *Extractus Vitricus* or a related early treatise</note>) believe that this does not have to be observed by any law. This is because neither the equality nor the diversity of syllables defines concords, but rather the boundaries of the sounding terms separated by their proper intervals. For when the tenor says *ut* in **Gamma-ut** <note>the lowest note of the medieval system, G2</note>, the melody can express an octave not only as *ut* according to the <term>diatonic</term> system of **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century monk who developed solmization</note>, but also as *sol* and *re* in **G-sol-re-ut** in the low register. For every single syllable arranged in low **G-sol-re-ut** sounds a perfect octave—namely, a <term>diapason</term> <note>the Greek term for an octave</note> consonance—to **Gamma-ut**. 

Similarly, you will be able to easily lead all the concords in the <term>introductory scale</term> up to the **nineteenth**. For when the tenor rests in **A-re**, the melody can pronounce *fa* and *ut* as a **third** (namely, *f* in **F-faut**), and *la* and *mi* as a **fifth** in low **E-la-mi**, and *fa* in **F-faut** as a **minor sixth**, and *la*, *mi*, and *re* in **A-la-mi-re** as a **perfect octave**. And likewise, *sol*, *fa*, and *ut* as a **tenth** in **C-sol-faut**; and *la* and *mi* in high **E-la-mi** as a **twelfth**; and *fa* in high **F-faut** as a **thirteenth**; and *la*, *mi*, and *re* in super-high **A-la-mi-re** as a **fifteenth**; and *sol* and *fa* as a **seventeenth** in **C-sol-fa**; and finally *la* in **E-la** as a **nineteenth**, which is most clearly perceived by the description in the present figure.

# BOOK THREE

¶ And thus, a similar consideration will apply to the remaining concordances <note>Harmonious intervals or "consonances."</note> in turn: namely, not only by the arrangement of the syllables on both sides, but by sounds proportioned to each other through the appropriate measurements of the intervals.

### ### Chapter IX: The Elements of Counterpoint are Arranged by Alternating Rising and Falling Pitch Species.

Furthermore, I think it is fitting to explain more precisely what we have previously considered: specifically, how the elementary species of counterpoint are usually mixed with one another in a song <note>original: "cantilena"</note>. For when the melody <note>original: "cantus"; the upper voice.</note> holds a third with the tenor, and the diversity of their movements is calculated <note>meaning "contrary motion," where one voice goes up and the other goes down.</note>, they immediately fall together into a unison. And again, from that same third, they jump to a fifth by those same diverse movements. However, when the melody is placed a third below the tenor and is lowered by a single rising tone, then the tenor and melody will together complete an octave by way of a fifth. And conversely: when the tenor is arranged in an octave with the melody and rises by a fifth while the melody alone is lowered by one step, they will pass from the octave into a sweeter transition to a third. Likewise, from an octave to a sixth, one proceeds best by diverse movements, and from an octave to a fifth by a single movement: namely, with the melody lowered by a fourth <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> or the tenor rising by a fourth. Truly, from a sixth to a third, one arrives easily if the sound of the organizer <note>original: "organizantis"; the singer or composer who creates the counterpoint.</note> is lowered by a fourth, or the tenor alone is raised by that same fourth. For the "organizer" is the one who produces the higher part of the song. Similarly, a melody raised by a fourth from a third will correspond to a sixth against the tenor. But if the tenor, arranged in a third with the melody, is lowered by a fourth, a sixth will likewise result from the melody, which is clearly arranged in this harmony.

<margin>UPPER VOICE</margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff featuring a C-clef on the first line. The melody consists of ten diamond-shaped notes (semibreves and minims), starting on the first line and moving upwards and downwards within the staff.</image-desc>

<margin>TENOR</margin>
<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff featuring a C-clef on the fourth line. The melody consists of ten diamond-shaped notes, serving as the counterpoint to the Cantus part above.</image-desc>

¶ When you attempt to proceed to the other elements of counterpoint, you will find that what we said regarding the third moving to the unison also applies to the tenth moving to the octave. And what was said of the octave moving to the fifth, applies to the fifteenth moving to the twelfth. And what was said of the sixth moving to the third, applies likewise to the thirteenth moving to the tenth. The same holds for the others mentioned above in the introduction. Truly, a concordance assigned to perfection <note>A "perfect" consonance like a unison, fifth, or octave.</note>—which those organizing the music reach by contrary movements—is sweeter and more delightful than if it were conceived by a single movement. This is because the diverse sounds proceeding by contrary movements hasten to meet one another and mix with a harmonious sweetness; from this mixing, a concordant counterpoint is generated and produced.

### ### Chapter X: On the Diversity of the Figuration of Sounds in Counterpoint.

Musicians are accustomed to assigning concordant sounds in counterpoint to the notes of a plainchant <note>original: "cantus plani"; traditional Gregorian chant.</note> as if to a tenor and a foundation of the relationship, doing so in various ways. For sometimes they make the notes of the melody—or even the counter-tenor—equal to the notes of the plainchant arranged as the tenor, relating them note for note, and bringing only semibreves <note>A diamond-shaped note value, at this time acting as a standard pulse.</note> into the middle. This they call "simple and plain counterpoint," because all the notes are arranged with an equal measure of time, as is noted in this harmony <note>original: "concentu"</note>.

F iiii <note>A printer's mark indicating the sequence of the paper gatherings.</note>

# BOOK THREE

### COUNTERPOINT [AND] TENOR

<image-desc>Musical notation for two voices on a five-line staff. The upper voice, labeled Counterpoint, features white diamond-shaped notes (semibreves and minims). The lower voice, labeled Tenor, consists of black square notes characteristic of plainchant.</image-desc>

Sometimes those performing counterpoint <note>original: "organizantes"; refers to musicians who arrange or compose polyphonic parts.</note> also measure semibreves and minims and other smaller figures against the notes of <term>plainchant</term> <note>original: "cantus plani"; the traditional, monophonic ritual music of the Church, used here as the foundational melody.</note>. They arrange these within the measure of semibreves, a style which they call **broken and florid counterpoint** <note>original: "fractum ac floridum contrapunctum"; "fractum" implies breaking the longer notes into smaller rhythmic pieces, and "floridum" refers to the decorative, flowing nature of the melody.</note>, as seen here:

<image-desc>A musical staff showing a "broken and florid" contrapuntal example. The upper voice moves in a more decorative, rhythmic style with varied note values over the steady, square-note tenor.</image-desc>

Sometimes, by placing two semiminims <note>A note value half the length of a minim.</note> after every three minims, they assign the counterpoint to the notes of the plainchant in a regular order, in this manner:

<image-desc>Musical notation with a 'C' time signature (common time), demonstrating a rhythmic pattern where three minims are followed by two semiminims in the upper voice, set against a plainchant tenor.</image-desc>

### COUNTERPOINT [AND] TENOR

Again, using a variety of different note shapes, musicians modulate the counterpoint in various ways over the notes of the plainchant, as is established here:

<margin>
COUNTER-
POINT

TENOR
</margin>

<image-desc>Three musical staves displaying complex contrapuntal variations. The upper voice (Counterpoint) demonstrates varied "numerosities" of notes—including rhythmic groupings and leaps—above the sustained square notes of the Tenor.</image-desc>

They also sometimes modulate with a deeper accompaniment <note>original: "succentu"; a lower vocal part or accompaniment that supports the main melody from below.</note> over the notes of the plainchant. Therefore, we have rightly decided to conclude that counterpoint can be constructed from various figures, species, or elements—provided the established rules <note>original: "legalium mandatorum"; refers to the "laws" or theoretical requirements of harmony and counterpoint.</note> are observed—which we know is left to the judgment of the musicians and composers.

f j <note>This is a signature mark used by printers to keep the pages of the book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the practice of "florid counterpoint," demonstrating how composers can use diverse rhythmic values—from semibreves to semiminims—to create elaborate melodies over a steady plainchant tenor. It concludes that while there are strict rules for harmony, the specific rhythmic arrangement is left to the composer's artistic judgment.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

### ¶ Chapter XI. On the composition of the various parts of counterpoint.

The counterpoint of songs <note>original: "Cantilenarū"; refers to polyphonic vocal compositions.</note> which are composed of three or four consonant parts is considered in this order: when the <term>Tenor</term> and the <term>Cantus</term> <note>The "Cantus" is the highest voice, often called the soprano in modern terms.</note> have maintained an octave between them, the <term>Contratenor</term>—led to a fifth above the tenor or an octave below the tenor—will harmonize most excellently and sweetly. But if, with these three parts joined, you attempt to fit another part into the song—a **High Contratenor** <note>original: "Contratenoré in acutum"; literally a contratenor in the high register.</note>—you may place it at a third above the tenor, or (which pertains to a sweeter harmony) at a fifth. However, if it is placed at a third above the tenor, it will harmonize as a tenth with the lower contratenor; but if it is at a fifth, it will then be a twelfth distant from the <term>Baritone</term> <note>original: "Boritonante"; Gaffurius uses a unique spelling here to support his etymological theory.</note>. For the "Baritonans" is understood to be the part or lower progression in the composition of a song, which is also called the "low counter-tenor," from the word *barys* <note>original: "Vari"; Gaffurius is transliterating the Greek word βαρύς (barys), meaning heavy or deep.</note> which means "low" or "heavy," with the *V* changed to a *B*, as if singing the lower part of the song. An example of this consideration is presented here:

<image-desc>A musical score featuring two five-line staves with four-part counterpoint written in white mensural notation (diamond-shaped notes). The voices are labeled below the lower staff.</image-desc>

->CANTVS TENOR BARITONANS HIGH CONTRATENOR<-

¶ In this example, the first semibreve of the high contratenor, placed at a third above the tenor, is distant by a third stretched from the first note of the Baritone, and is a sixth below the first note of the Cantus. From this [high contratenor], however, the first note of the Baritone is distant by a fifteenth <note>An interval of two octaves.</note> in the low register. And thus, a good concord is drawn from both. The last semibreve of the high contratenor, however, exceeds the tenor in height by a fifth; being a twelfth away from the last note of the Baritone, it is lowered by a **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> below the last note of the Cantus. By this means, a harmonic concord arises therefrom. Truly, when the Baritone is lowered by a fifth below the tenor, and the Cantus is a sixth or an octave distant from that same tenor in height, the high contratenor led to a third above the Baritone will then harmonize, as is perceived from the first semibreve of the following example. Yet it will be mediated with a sweeter consistency if it were placed at an octave above the Baritone, as is noted in the last note of this example.

<image-desc>A second musical example in white mensural notation across two five-line staves, showing four-part harmony. Voice labels are provided below.</image-desc>

->CANTVS TENOR BARITONANS HIGH CONTRATENOR<-

¶ Because when the Baritone has an octave below the tenor, and the Cantus has come to rest at a third above the tenor—so that it is a tenth distant from the Baritone in height—the high contratenor can be established at a fifth above the Baritone; it will then be a sixth distant from the Cantus in the low register and a fourth below the tenor. And thus they will contain each other harmoniously, which is clearly proven by the first and last notes of this example.

<summary>This page explains the rules for arranging four-voice harmony, defining the interval relationships between the Cantus, Tenor, Baritone, and High Contratenor. It also provides a Greek-based etymology for the term 'Baritone' as the 'heavy' or 'low' voice.</summary>
<keywords>polyphony, four-part harmony, Baritone, Contratenor, Cantus, Tenor, intervals, music theory, etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for four-voice composition begun on the previous page, specifically detailing the harmonic "interlocking" of the High Contratenor and Tenor against the Baritone voice, and introducing the rules for a fifth voice.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

<image-desc>Musical notation with four parts: Cantus, Tenor, Baritonans, and High Contratenor. The notation uses white mensural symbols on five-line staves. Vertically aligned notes show the harmonic relationships between the voices as described in the text.</image-desc>

->CANTUS        TENOR        BARITONE    HIGH CONTRATE-<-
->NOR<-

¶ It is clear, therefore, from the aforementioned reasons and examples, that each part relates to the others in composition through diverse movements, according to the rules and elements of <term>counterpoint</term> <note>The art of combining different melodic lines to create harmony.</note>. This is done so that each part of the song <note>original: "cantilenæ"; refers to a polyphonic vocal work.</note> corresponds harmoniously with the others, such that no part produces a full <term>dissonance</term> (except for the fourth), which actually agrees sweetly with the higher and middle sounds of the combined voices, as was stated in the fifth chapter. Furthermore, the interval of a sixth <note>original: "Sexta"</note> often sounds discordant in the lower registers when held with a full and stable voice, unless the parts immediately converge from that sixth into an <term>octave</term> through diverse movements; we leave this to be investigated by the judgment of the ears. A similar discord is sometimes perceived in the interval of a thirteenth in those same lower sounds. We believe the cause of this is the "fullness of time" <note>original: "multitudinem temporis"; likely referring to the slower vibration or acoustic weight of lower frequencies.</note> dwelling in the deeper tones, through which the harshness and dissonance of an <term>imperfection</term> reaches the ears more clearly, bringing offense to the sense.

Therefore, the **High Contratenor** and the **Tenor** must always be harmonized with the **Baritone** <note>original: "Baritonante".</note>. Thus, when the High Contratenor is separated from the Baritone by an octave in the higher range, the Tenor shall be placed either a fifth or a third above the Baritone. But if the Tenor takes an octave stretched above the Baritone, then the High Contratenor will mediate this octave interval at a third, or—more sweetly—at a fifth. In this way, the various parts of the song are mingled with one another in melody and arrangement through the mutual variety of different <term>species</term> <note>Refers to the different types of intervals (perfect and imperfect).</note>.

Likewise, the Baritone can ascend a fifth above the Tenor when the **Cantus** <note>The highest voice part.</note> sounds an octave with the Tenor; the High Contratenor, placed a third above the Tenor, will then harmonize, being a third lower than the Baritone and a sixth away from the Cantus, as is perceived in the final note of the following example. Similarly, when the Cantus sounds a tenth above the Tenor, then the Baritone will melodiously hold a fifth above the Tenor, especially when the High Contratenor takes an octave stretched from the Tenor—this is more clearly observed in the first note of this musical phrase.

<image-desc>A second musical score in white mensural notation, demonstrating the harmonic configuration for four voices: Cantus, Tenor, Baritonans, and High Contratenor.</image-desc>

->CANTUS        TENOR        BARITONE    HIGH CONTRATE-<-
->NOR<-

¶ When you wish to add a fivefold part, or a fifth harmonious voice, to these four, you should take care to apply it alternately and mutually with different species according to the rules and mandates of counterpoint.

# THIRD BOOK

It further seems necessary to consider how many perfect species <note>original: "species"; here referring to types of perfect intervals like octaves and fifths.</note> of the same kind may be placed statically in a song. For this reason, multiple static octaves and multiple static fifths are suitable in <term>counterpoint</term> <note>The technique of combining independent melodic lines.</note>; and indeed, many other perfect intervals are often arranged statically. However, if one moves from a perfect concord of the <term>tenor</term> with the <term>cantus</term> <note>The highest voice, often called the soprano.</note> (or any one part with another) to another similar perfect concord, whether ascending or descending—even if a pause of a <term>minim</term> <note>A short rhythmic value; a "minim rest" is a brief silence.</note> is interposed in one of the parts—I do not at all think this should be praised. Indeed, two similar perfect concords are seen to occur simultaneously and consecutively, whether ascending or descending, despite the very small interruption of a minim’s silence. For example, if the tenor and cantus are arranged in an octave: if the cantus itself, having performed the octave, pauses for a minim (while the tenor does not pause, or even if it pauses for the same duration), it is then not fitting for another octave to follow immediately, even if it is at a different pitch level, on account of the very small portion of time (as we said before) interposed; as is clearly seen in this example.

<image-desc>Musical notation for "CANTVS" and "TENOR" in mensural notation on two five-line staves. The example shows intervals of an octave between the parts, separated by various rests represented by vertical bars of differing lengths.</image-desc>

->CANTVS&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;TENOR<-

In this example, all the first notes placed in octaves are appropriately arranged because they are static (even with a <term>semibreve</term> <note>A longer rhythmic value, usually representing a full beat or measure in this period.</note> pause interposed). However, the fifth minim of the cantus, following immediately after a minim rest, is not permitted to be in an octave with the tenor because of the lower octave immediately preceding it. There are also those who do not allow two similar perfect concords immediately ascending or descending even if a semibreve pause is interposed; although many disagree with them, since a semibreve pause maintains a full measure of time. Likewise, two or more semibreve pauses may appropriately mediate two similar perfect concords ascending or descending consecutively, whether they are performed in the tenor, the cantus, the <term>contratenor</term>, or the <term>Baritone</term>. Furthermore, what should we believe regarding those who object to singers of <term>organum</term> <note>An early form of polyphony; here referring generally to polyphonic singers.</note> pronouncing more than three identical notes (unisons) in the cantus? They assert this so that the cantus does not seem to have turned into the tenor, or the tenor into the cantus. In our opinion, this is ridiculous. For composers of songs often mark the tenor and contratenor with fast-moving figures <note>original: "diminutionibus"; the division of long notes into many fast ones.</note> and the cantus with slower ones, which more recent composers do frequently, as is established here by a very well-known example:

->CANTVS&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;TENOR<-

### ¶ Concerning Counterpoint in Ficta Music. Chapter Thirteen.

<margin>The Ambrosians</margin>
Let us now proceed a little with the *ficta* <note>original: "fictas"; literally "feigned" or "contrived." In this period, it refers to notes outside the standard hand-diagram of music, such as sharps and flats.</note> or "colored" species of counterpoint, which are considered on the <term>monochord</term> <note>A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> by the strings themselves dividing individual whole tones. For these, which are led by a chromatic measurement, demonstrate "colored" songs, which are also called *ficta*. Indeed, such an arrangement is necessary for the perfection of a harmonic instrument: both for a sweeter tempering of the harsher <term>diatonic</term> <note>The standard musical scale consisting of whole steps and half steps.</note> genus, and for finding the perfection of certain consonances within the diatonic scale. 

And therefore, one proceeds to this type of *ficta* music through a triple consideration: according to the measurement of the <term>chromatic</term> <note>original: "chromaticha"; a genus of music using smaller intervals to "color" the melody.</note> genus; according to the demonstration of the mixed genus; and according to the division of the <term>enharmonic</term> <note>original: "enarmonici"; the most complex genus in ancient Greek theory, involving very small intervals.</note> genus—all of which are called "condensations" and ornaments of the diatonic genus. However, we have judged that their measurements in instrumental harmony should be arranged mathematically. 

In the <term>Guidonian</term> <note>Referring to the system of Guido of Arezzo, the medieval monk who standardized the musical staff and hexachords.</note> introductory diatonic, *ficta* music is shown by a single interval of a tone: specifically where the <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale pattern (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la).</note> of "soft B" (B-flat) arranges its fourth string as *fa*, which splits the distance of a whole tone between *A-la-mi-re* and *B-natural-mi* (or between the *Mese* and *Paramese* <note>The Greek names for the central pitches of the ancient musical system.</note> of the conjunct <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A four-note scale segment.</note>). Therefore, musicians are accustomed to mark the strings that divide such whole-tone intervals with the sign of the "round b" <note>The symbol ♭, used to lower a pitch.</note>, pronouncing them with the syllable *fa*. 

Quite often, many also pronounce *sol* below *la* at the interval of a semitone <note>A half-step.</note>, especially when proceeding with the notes *la-sol-la*, beginning on *A-la-mi-re* and ending back on it, as in the chant *Salve Regina*. And likewise, they do so between *sol* and *fa*, beginning and ending on *G-sol-re-ut* with the transition *sol-fa-sol*, which the <margin>Ambrosians</margin> <note>Singers of the Milanese rite, known for their distinct liturgical chant.</note> are often accustomed to sing. For it is not the syllable *fa* itself that creates the semitone, but rather the measurement of the semitone interval defined between two sounds. Therefore, when you seek this *ficta* progression in the hexachords of the Guidonian tradition, you shall lower the higher notes of each whole tone by a major semitone, describing them with the syllable *fa* and the letter "round b" (♭), which creates a transition from a whole tone into a semitone. Many, however, do this at every whole-tone interval.

<summary>This page introduces the concept of musica ficta, explaining how accidental notes (sharps and flats) are used to "color" and improve the sound of the standard diatonic scale. It specifically details the use of B-flat within the Guidonian hexachord system and mentions the singing practices of the Ambrosian rite.</summary>
<keywords>musica ficta, counterpoint, monochord, chromatic, enharmonic, diatonic, hexachord, B-flat, Guido of Arezzo, Ambrosian chant, semitone</keywords>

### THIRD BOOK 47

<note>original: "xam"; likely referring to the Greek letter Chi (χ) used as a sign for a sharp or accidental.</note> they write the letter chi for a semitone voice of this kind. Truly, when *mi* is changed into *fa*, they write a round *b* <note>the flat sign (♭)</note> in place of the square $\natural$ <note>the natural or "hard" B sign</note>; however, when *fa* is changed into *mi*, they place a square $\natural$ in place of the round *b*. From this, if one has changed *mi* into *fa* in the low *E-la-mi* <note>a specific pitch in the medieval "Guidonian Hand" system</note>, one will drop by a major semitone into the low range, whose <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la) used to teach singing.</note> will acquire its beginning in low *B-mi*. 

But if you have changed *fa* into *mi* in low *C-fa-ut* through the transition of a major semitone into the high range, a hexachord of this kind will take its beginning in *A-re*. Furthermore, when you have changed *mi* into *fa* in low *B-mi* through the transition of a major semitone into the low range, you will begin that hexachord in an "acquired" *F-fa-ut*, a whole tone below the low *Gamma-ut* <note>The lowest note of the standard medieval musical scale; going below this was considered "acquired" or extra-systemic.</note>; for which reason it is not inappropriate to call the consideration of such voices "Acquired Music." 

Through the remaining strings of the <term>introductory scale</term> <note>original: "itroductorii"; the basic gamut or range of notes taught to beginners.</note>, you will derive a similar conjunction of acquired hexachords. Therefore, you will easily discover the species of consonances and tones acquired through this *ficta* music. There are also those who, by the addition of this sign ♯, wish for the note to which it is applied to be lowered by the smallest interval of a <term>diesis</term>, which belongs to the Enharmonic genus. It is, indeed:

<margin>Diesis.</margin>
A **diesis** is an interval of half a minor semitone bounded by two sounds. Therefore, in the consideration of *ficta* music, **Counterpoint** proceeds by the same commands we have set forth, and is woven together with the same concordances, as can be perceived in this arrangement.

### Concerning False Counterpoint. Chapter 14.

We call it **False Counterpoint** when two singers proceed together through the dissonant extremes of joined sounds, such as the major and minor second, the major and minor fourth, and likewise the seventh and ninth; these are entirely separated from every nation and nature of sweet harmony. 

<margin>The Ambrosians</margin>
Our **Ambrosians** <note>The clergy and singers of Milan who follow the Ambrosian rite, distinct from the Roman Gregorian rite.</note> use this in the solemn vigils of martyrs and in several chants of the Mass for the Dead, asserting that it was instituted by the Divine **Ambrose**. They claim it is a mournful song by which the Church deplores the shedding of the blood of the holy martyrs and the prayers for the dead; yet (far be it from me!) I have never found this celebrated by the "honey-tongued" Ambrose himself. Indeed, 

<margin>Guido.</margin>
as **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo, the most influential music theorist of the Middle Ages.</note> says, when Ambrose described ecclesiastical chants, he labored wonderfully in sweetness alone. Has it not been written that the simple modulation of the second, fourth, and sixth modes is appropriate for the sorrowful and weeping? Therefore, one may judge that this kind of "false counterpoint" was introduced instead by certain men who were overwhelmed by the envy of ignorant music, as the opinion of Guido himself testifies: "Many things are used that are not held by rule. Because for a long time, Music has fallen into disuse, while envy and sluggishness take away all studies." The process of "false counterpoint," therefore—which the Ambrosians themselves call "following"—is of this kind: a single singer pronounces the notes of the plainchant with a higher voice, but two or three sing along in a single...

Since this is separated from all reason of melody, I am ashamed to describe it. Sometimes they begin a <term>succentus</term> <note>original: "succentum"; a lower accompanying voice or "under-song"</note> of this kind in unison with the plainchant, proceeding from there through seconds and fourths until the end or to a certain termination where the voices meet in unison. ### ¶ How the singer ought to conduct himself while singing. Chapter 15.

Finally, for the sake of instruction and warning to new singers, we have thought it necessary to propose: that they should not bring forth their voices while singing with an unusual and unseemly gaping of the mouth, or perhaps with a ridiculous <term>cackle</term> <note>original: "cochino", likely "cachinno"; a loud or mocking laugh.</note>. Especially when they are performing melodies in the divine mysteries, they should furthermore reject trembling and noisy voices; for such voices are inconsistent with themselves in their own extension, and therefore cannot be in harmony with other voices in a proportional way due to their constant instability. It is also fitting for one voice to accommodate the other—for instance, the <term>tenor</term> <note>the voice that "holds" the main melody</note> to the chant—so that one is not confused or overwhelmed by the excessive shouting of the other. Thus, when the tenor voice reaches the high melody, it can be easily observed how the singers' voices allow the melody to harmonize with the tenor in proper proportion. For as a rule, the tenor...

<summary>This page describes a "discordant" style of polyphony involving seconds and fourths used in funeral litanies and provides moral and technical instructions for singers to maintain vocal decorum.</summary>
