--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on vocal roles and the relationship between voices. The previous page ended with a warning against unseemly behavior by singers; this page expands on the technical duties of the Baritone, Contratenor, and Tenor, as well as the emotional affects of different musical tones.</meta>

# THIRD BOOK

...to the <term>chant</term> <note>original: "cantui"</note> it lies beneath at a double interval <note>an octave</note> in the low pitch. But because it is lower, it exists as slow and strong in the time allotted to it. The chant, however, is fast and weak with a shorter delay of time. Here it is necessary that the low voice sustains the high, just as the strong sustains the weak; it must not be overcome by shouting. For confused and obscured sounds do not create harmony; rather, the outer and middle sounds joined together by reciprocal harmonic proportion do. Moreover, let the voice of the <term>Baritone</term> <note>original: "Baritonantis"</note> and the high <term>Contratenor</term> <note>original: "contratenoris"</note> transfer itself to the others with an equal pronunciation. The <term>Tenor</term>, however, which sustains the melody and is sustained by the Baritone as the foundation of the relationship, is said to hold the middle place, being circumscribed by the high melody and the lower Baritone. For this reason, they behold, observe, and respect it concordantly. For it draws the concord of both to itself and confers its own onto the others. Nor is it fitting for it to be diminished by pleasing <term>breakings</term> <note>original: "fractionibus"; the practice of dividing long notes into many fast, decorative notes</note>, since it holds the concordances for the others note by note. For it will create more discords than concords for the others by these very diminutions of the musical figures; from this, it reaches the ears of those standing by unpleasantly. An insolent and indecorous movement of the head or hands also declares a singer insane. For the hand or head do not produce a concordant sound, but a well-modulated voice. Many singing imprudently are displeasing to those whom they think they will please.

<margin>Guido</margin>
Indeed, this was the primary reason why, having left behind <term>florid</term> and <term>mensurable chant</term> <note>original: "florido ac mensurabili cantu"; complex, rhythmic polyphony</note>, <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the medieval monk who invented the staff notation we use today</note> himself turned to ecclesiastical modulation. For he says (which I report with regret) about them: "In our times, among all men, singers are the most foolish." Furthermore, let the composer of a song strive to match the sweetness of the melody to the words of the song: so that when the words are about love or the seeking of death or any lamentation, let him pronounce and arrange mournful sounds as much as possible (as the Venetians are accustomed to). For I believe it confers much to this if the song is arranged in the <term>fourth or sixth tone</term>, or even in the <term>second</term>. Since these tones are more relaxed, they are known to give birth to this kind of effect easily. But when the words speak of indignation and rebuke, it is fitting to emit harsh and harder sounds, which is usually ascribed to the <term>third and seventh tone</term>. Truly, words of praise and modesty seek somewhat middle sounds, which are fittingly inscribed in the <term>first and eighth tone</term>. But I have decided to describe the properties and powers of those tones themselves copiously in instrumental harmony. Finally, let the composer of a song beware of placing the Tenor or the Baritone as immovable through all the notes in a song. For a voice remaining still cannot harmoniously agree with another to acquire a perfect concord through contrary motions. Therefore, it does not fall into the process of a concordance that soothes the hearing. For a sound of this kind produced in instruments—as in the <term>bagpipe</term> <note>original: "utriculo"</note> which common people call a <term>piva</term>—is said to "ternare" or "philosophize," but among the common folk it is called <term>to drone</term> <note>original: "Bordonizare"; to hum like a bee or a drone string</note>. For no change occurs in it by tension or relaxation;

<margin>The Philosopher</margin>
hence, no delight arises. But because it is very low, it sustains the highest and fast sounds of the bagpipe itself; indeed, it has been said that high sounds are sustained and fortified by lower ones. For they call the bagpipe itself a "piva" from its highest sounds which always seem to lead "pivi" <note>likely an onomatopoeia for the high peeping sound of the pipe</note>. For <term>Petrus Apponensis</term> <note>Pietro d'Abano (1250–1316), a philosopher and physician who wrote on music and problems of sound</note>

<margin>Petrus Apponensis</margin>
asserts in his explanation of the tenth problem that it is a "stretched out" sound, partaking of no tension and no relaxation. Moreover, it is necessary for the <term>citharist</term> or lyricist, for the sake of expressing harmony which comes from melodic strings, to use the voices of the Lyre, and to render his own voices consonant with those voices in alternating diversity: for example, by modulating the Tenor with the strings themselves and the song with his own voice, or vice versa; or even by comparing the thickness of one to the thinness of the other, or speed to slowness, or high pitch to low pitch, so that they keep one altogether consonant sound; which, by the reason of art and

<margin>Plato</margin>
the institute of the divine <term>Plato</term>, is known to be observed in the seventh book of *The Laws*. For he says the entire variety of rhythms must be accommodated to the voices of the Lyre.

<summary>This page details the structural roles of voice parts (Tenor, Baritone, Contratenor) and emphasizes that musical "tones" or modes must match the emotional content of the lyrics. It also critiques the "droning" style of bagpipes and references classical authorities like Plato and Pietro d'Abano to support its theories on harmony.</summary>
<keywords>Tenor, Baritone, Contratenor, musical modes, affects of music, Guido d'Arezzo, bagpipe, piva, bordonizare, Petrus Apponensis, Plato, harmony</keywords>

BOOK [THREE]

# ¶ On the Definition and Distinction of Proportion. Chapter I.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'P' featuring two figures (infants or putti) playing with a dog-like creature inside the bowl of the letter.</image-desc>

According to Euclid, <term>proportion</term> <note>original: "Proportio"</note> is a certain relation of two quantities of the same kind to one another, no matter what they may be. However, one type is rational and the other is irrational. Rational proportion is the comparison of two <term>commensurable</term> <note>quantities that can be measured by the same unit</note> quantities to each other, whether in <term>discrete</term> <note>individual units, like numbers</note> or in <term>continuous</term> <note>unbroken scales, like a line</note> form. In discrete quantity, it is like six to four and other relations of numbers for which some common measure is given that divides both precisely; for the number two [binarius] taken three times counts six, and twice counts four; hence it is called their common measure. In continuous quantity, however, it is like a three-foot line to a two-foot line, of which a half-foot [semipedalis] taken a certain number of times is called the common measure. And in weights, the quantity of nine ounces to the quantity of six ounces, of which the weight of three ounces is known as the common measure. Euclid himself calls such quantities "communicating" at the beginning of his tenth book. 

Irrational proportion is the mutual relation of two <term>incommensurable</term> quantities, such as the diagonal of a square to its side, for which no common measure is found measuring both quantities precisely; this is what Campanus and <margin>Albertus</margin> Albertus clearly prove in their [works on] proportions in the exposition of the seventh proportion of Euclid's tenth book. Euclid calls these incommensurable quantities "deaf" <note>original: "surdas"; this is the origin of the mathematical term "surd" for irrational numbers</note>. But this kind of irrational proportion is separated from arithmetic consideration since (as all mathematicians agree) unity is the common measure of all numbers. 

We, however, considering a certain irrationality in harmony—and those intervals which do not produce consonant intervals on the <term>monochord</term> <note>original: "chordotono"; a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals</note> without error—have proposed them as irrational in the second [chapter] of the third [book] of the *Theorica* (not inappropriately, indeed); for no integrity of common measure is recognized in those dimensions, as we will prove in the description of the harmonic instrument. Therefore, since Arithmetic proportion consists in numbers, Geometric in continuous things, and <term>Stereometric</term> <note>the measurement of volumes and weights</note> in the weighing of loads (which is also ascribed by some to Geometry), Musical proportion (participating in these) proceeds by mutual consideration. For with numbers as guides, it arranges sounds on a sounding string—which is continuous—as we proposed in the fifth [book] of the *Theorica*; and we shall speak more broadly in that work which we are about to write on the harmonic instrument according to various genres. 

Again, the work of numbers (which belongs to the present place) encompasses sounds according to the succession of time. Hence we propose a twofold effect of musical proportion: the first in the arrangement of sounds through consonant intervals (which belongs to the theorist); the second in the temporal quantity of those sounds through the numbers of the <term>notes</term> <note>original: "notularum"</note>, which is ascribed to active or practical consideration. Therefore, in whatever genus, our discourse will dwell upon rational proportion, since in the terms of every proportion some common measure (at least unity) exists.

<margin>
Albertus
Giovanni Marliani.
Plato
Philosophers.
Aristotle.
</margin>

Proportion is also frequently considered in "powers" [of things], as Albertus and <margin>Giovanni Marliani</margin> Giovanni Marliani prove in their proportions; and also frequently in places and times, as <margin>Plato</margin> Plato in the *Timaeus* and the entire school of <margin>Philosophers</margin> philosophers agree, insofar as something is larger than another or equal to it—which is the property of quantity, as <margin>Aristotle</margin> Aristotle testifies in the *Categories* <note>original: "praedicamentis"</note>. Wherefore, [we apply] proportion first to discrete and continuous quantity; then to sounds, places, times, and weights.

<summary>This page defines the mathematical foundations of music, distinguishing between rational and irrational proportions based on Euclidean geometry. It argues that while music relies on continuous strings, its practical application (rhythm and intervals) is governed by rational numerical ratios.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, Euclid, rational proportion, irrational proportion, commensurability, monochord, arithmetic, geometry, music theory, Aristotle, Plato</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous chapter, moving from the definition of rational and irrational proportions to the specific distinctions between proportions of equality and inequality, eventually applying these mathematical concepts to musical notation.</meta>

[FOURTH BOOK]

...and must necessarily be inherent in their powers. There is also a proportion of equality, which is the mutual relation of two equal quantities, such as two to two, or a two-foot line to a two-foot line. 

The proportion of inequality, however, is the relation between two unequal quantities, such as four to three, or a three-foot line to a two-foot line. Of these unequal proportions, one is called "of greater inequality" <note>original: "maioris inæqualitatis"</note> when, for instance, a larger quantity is compared to a smaller one through the relation, such as four to three. The other is "of lesser inequality" when a smaller quantity is compared to a larger in the relation, such as three to four. Therefore, in songs <note>original: "cantilenis"</note>, the proportion of inequality will be the measurement—in both division and performance—of a greater number of similar small notes <note>original: "notularum"</note> compared with a smaller number, or a smaller number with a greater, as shown here. 

<margin>What proportion is in notes</margin>

¶ It is clear from the previous definition of proportion (as is also perceived in the arrangement of the harmony <note>original: "concentu"</note>) that all figures or notes are compared to others similar to themselves in name and quantity in whatever proportion; for example, <term>semibreves</term> <note>a diamond-shaped note value</note> of <term>minor prolation</term> <note>a rhythmic setting where a semibreve is divided into two minims</note> to semibreves of minor prolation, and <term>breves</term> <note>a longer, rectangular note value</note> of <term>perfect time</term> <note>a rhythmic setting where a breve is divided into three semibreves</note> to breves of perfect time. 

But if a proportion is arranged otherwise—for instance, a <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>a 3:2 ratio where three notes are played in the time of two</note> in the higher part of the harmony, counting three breves of perfect time against two breves of <term>imperfect time</term> <note>where a breve is divided into only two semibreves</note> placed in the tenor—you will find such a consideration of sesquialtera to be absurd. This is because those three breves of perfect time differ in their total quantity from the two breves of imperfect time. For if you look proportionally at their constituent parts which agree in quantity—namely, nine semibreves of minor prolation compared to four semibreves of that same minor prolation—you will not find the sesquialtera that was indicated by the numbers, but rather a <term>dupla sesquiquarta</term> <note>a 9:4 ratio, or two and a quarter times the original value</note> proportion. And the same thing will happen when you consider three semibreves of <term>major prolation</term> <note>where a semibreve is divided into three minims</note> to be equated to two semibreves of minor prolation according to that same sesquialtera, as the following harmony proves:

<meta>This page continues from the discussion on the basic definitions of equality and inequality in musical proportions. It transitions from theoretical definitions to the practical necessity of precise numerical notation in musical scores to avoid ambiguity in performance.</meta>

[FOURTH] BOOK
121.

Every proportion, however, must be described in an arrangement of countless notes <note>original: "notularum"</note> using specific numerical characters, so that doubt and delay in discernment may be avoided. For if you designate a proportion with only a single numerical character—for example, using only the number 3 for a <term>sesquialtera</term> proportion <note>A ratio of 3:2, where three notes are played in the time of two.</note> (if I may say so with the indulgence of others)—the number 3 itself would indicate a relationship not only to 2 (to which it is sesquialtera), but also to 1 (in a triple ratio), and to 4 (in the following sequence), and to every other single number according to the choice of the arrangement. Therefore, the terms of the proportions must be written among the notes in such a way that the altered number is placed below the standard and preceding term in this manner: **3/2**. Or, to put it more clearly, the number of reducible notes should be written above the number of the immediately preceding quantity to which it is being reduced. In this way, the proportion arranged in the songs <note>original: "cantilenis"</note> will be clear and very well known. Just as when it looks back at the preceding quantity of time or <term>prolation</term> <note>The relationship between the semibreve and the minim in mensural notation.</note>, it will follow the natural consideration of proportional numbers. So also if (for example) a higher harmony <note>original: "concentus acutioris"</note> is written among the notes and brought down to the quantity of the notes of another part (such as the <term>tenor</term>), it will observe that same natural arrangement of numbers by imitation. It is different, however, when the tenor and the harmony are arranged with unequal quantities. In the other parts of a song that are to be brought together, this same method of considering proportion must necessarily occur.

### Concerning the Five Types of Proportions of Greater and Lesser Inequality. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'M' featuring ornate foliage and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

**M**oreover, the proportion of greater inequality <note>original: "maioris inæqualitatis"</note> proceeds in five ways. For either the larger number contains the smaller number multiple times exactly, and then it is called **Multiple** <note>original: "multiplex"</note>, such as 6 to 2, 4 to 1, and 5 to 1. Or the larger contains the smaller once and, in addition, one <term>aliquot part</term> <note>A part that divides the whole exactly, like 1/2 or 1/3.</note> of that smaller number, and it is called **Epimoria** or **Superparticular**, such as 3 to 2, 4 to 3, and 5 to 4. Or the larger contains the smaller once and, in addition, one <term>aliquant part</term> <note>A part that does not divide the whole exactly, like 2/3.</note> composed of several aliquot parts, and it is called **Superpartient**, such as 5 to 3, 7 to 4, 9 to 5, and 11 to 6. Or the larger number contains the smaller multiple times along with an aliquot part of it, and it is called **Multiple Superparticular**, such as 5 to 2, 7 to 3, and 9 to 4. Or the larger contains the smaller multiple times and one part that is not an aliquot part but is made of several aliquot parts, and it is called **Multiple Superpartient**, such as 8 to 3, 11 to 4, 14 to 5, and 11 to 3. There are, therefore, five such general names for proportions of this kind, to which infinite species belong, as is established in the third book of the *Theory* <note>Referring to the author's previous theoretical work.</note> and as we shall reveal individually in the following sections. The effect of these proportions of greater inequality is that they diminish the notes before which they are placed in songs, according to the nature of the proportion itself. This is because a larger number of notes is made equal to a smaller number. There are also five types of proportions of lesser inequality <note>original: "minoris inæqualitatis"</note> opposed to the aforementioned, named with the same words but with the prefix "sub-": such as **Sub-multiple**, **Sub-superparticular**, **Sub-superpartient**, **Sub-multiple superparticular**, and **Sub-multiple superpartient**. Of these, there are also infinite species, for whose naming the prefix "sub-" is not unsuitable, as will be declared in the following. Indeed, their property is to increase the notes before which they are placed according to the nature and effect of each. Since, therefore, these types of lesser inequality are opposed to the types of greater inequality, they possess the same nature but in the opposite consideration; for the discipline of opposites is the same. Thus, Sub-multiple is opposed to Multiple, Sub-superparticular to Superparticular, and the rest in the same way. We specifically say that Sub-multiple is opposed to Multiple because what one effects, the succeeding opposite destroys, as is proven by these arrangements.

<summary>This page explains the necessity of using two numbers (fractions) to clearly denote musical proportions and defines the five categories of mathematical ratios used in music, such as multiple and superparticular.</summary>
<keywords>musical proportions, notation, inequality, multiple ratio, superparticular, superpartient, mensural notation, diminished notes, augmented notes</keywords>

## ¶ On the Multiple Genus and its Species. Chapter III.

The **multiple genus** <term>multiplex genus</term> occurs when a larger number, when compared and placed over a smaller one, contains that smaller number within itself exactly several times: such as twice, three times, four times, and so on. When the larger number contains the smaller twice, it is called a **double proportion** <term>proportio dupla</term>, such as 2 to 1, 4 to 2, and 6 to 3. This is the first species of the multiple genus, in which a larger number of notes is made equal to a smaller number <note>In practice, this means playing more notes in the same amount of time</note>, so that each individual note of the larger group is reduced by half of its value. In songs, it is represented in this way: **2**, **4**, **6**, as is noted in the following description of a harmony <note>original: "concentus"</note>.

->1. 2. 5.<-

<image-desc>Two staves of white mensural musical notation. The upper staff is labeled CANTVS on the right and features a C-clef on the bottom line. The lower staff is labeled TENOR on the right and features a C-clef on the fourth line. The music contains breves, semibreves, and minims with several ligatures and a proportion sign '2' at the beginning of the Cantus.</image-desc>

¶ But if a **sub-double proportion** <term>subdupla proportio</term> <note>The inverse of the double proportion, where notes regain their original full value</note>, which is the opposite of the double, immediately follows it among the notes—provided no sign of time <note>a mensuration sign indicating the meter</note> is placed between them to hinder its natural power—it immediately destroys the double proportion. The following notes will then be counted according to the previous quantity (the number used before the double proportion was described). This is because of the equality established between the extreme terms in these opposite proportions, as is proven by this example:

<image-desc>A musical score with three staves of white mensural notation. The first staff is labeled CANTVS and the second TENOR to the right. The third staff at the bottom is unlabeled. The staves include various C-clefs and complex rhythmic figures with proportion signs such as 4/2 and 2/4.</image-desc>

¶ Every proportion is likewise destroyed when a sign for **perfect** or **imperfect time** <note>Signs like a circle or half-circle that define the relationship between different note values</note> is placed after the proportional notes. Some people are also accustomed to describing the double proportion using only the digit for the number 2 <note>original: "binarii numeri ziphra"</note>; this seems erroneous for the same reason stated regarding the **sesquialtera** <term>a 3:2 ratio</term> in the first chapter, since a proportion is by definition the consideration of two numbers in relation to each other. Often, however, a proportion is indicated by a **rule** <term>canon</term> <note>A written instruction or "rule" describing how the music should be performed without using numbers</note> and without numbers.

For the consideration of this kind of harmony must be taken in this way: so that the notes of the higher part are not at all compared to the notes of the Tenor or Contratenor (or vice versa) according to a proportion of equality <note>a 1:1 ratio</note>, even though a similar sign is written on both sides. Instead, I judge that the notes of any part should be calculated as twice as fast as similar notes [marked] with the sign of imperfect time, as if they were in proportion to the notes preceding them. Nor do I find it difficult to accept the placement of a single number in the demonstration of a proportion; for it was mentioned before that a proportion cannot be established with fewer than two terms <note>A ratio technically requires two numbers, like 2:1, but theorists sometimes used only the top number as shorthand</note>.

->On Triple Proportion.<-

Triple proportion is the second species of the multiple genus: when a larger number of following notes, compared and placed over a smaller number of preceding ones, relates and is made equal to that smaller number by containing it within itself exactly three times: such as **3 to 1**, **6 to 2**, and **9 to 3**, and so on. In this proportion, three notes are equivalent to one note of the same name and value. Thus, each of those three is diminished by two-thirds of its own value <note>Three notes are played in the time it usually takes to play one</note>. It is represented in songs in this way: **3**, or thus **6**, or **9**, which the present harmony demonstrates.

¶ If, among the figures or notes, a subtriple proportion—which is the opposite—is placed after a triple proportion, that triple is immediately canceled; and the notes following that subtriple will be returned to the previous consideration of the notes—that is, the value they had before the triple. This also occurs with each of the opposites when they follow one another immediately; for an equality of the extremes <note>The "extremes" refer to the rhythmic state before the first proportion and after its canceling opposite</note> is always noted, as is arranged in this harmony <term>concentus</term>.

## Concerning Quadruple Proportion.

¶ Quadruple proportion is the third species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>In Renaissance music theory, proportions were classified into "genera." The "multiple" genus involves ratios where a larger number is a clean multiple of a smaller one (2:1, 3:1, 4:1, etc.)</note>. It occurs when a larger number of following notes, compared to a smaller number of preceding notes, is equaled to that smaller number by containing it precisely four times: such as 4 to 1, 8 to 2, and 12 to 3. In this proportion, four notes are calculated and equivalent to one [note] similar to them in both name and quantity, so that each one of those four is diminished by three-quarters of its quantitative value <note>Because four notes now occupy the time of one, each note is four times faster than its standard value</note>. And it is described among the notes in this way: **4/1**, **8/2**, **12/3**, as is noted in this arrangement of harmony.

<image-desc>A third set of musical staves. The "CANTVS" staff on top includes the numerals 4, 8, and 12, as well as a 10, indicating the quadruple proportions described in the text. The "TENOR" staff on the bottom shows the corresponding musical line with a final long note.</image-desc>

## ¶ Concerning Quintuple Proportion.

¶ Quintuple proportion, which is the fourth species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>In Renaissance music theory, the "multiple genus" refers to ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller number, such as 2:1, 3:1, or 4:1.</note>, occurs when the larger number of following notes contains the smaller number of preceding notes precisely five times, being equivalent in power <note>original: "potentia," referring to the rhythmic value or duration</note> to that smaller number: such as 5 to 1, 10 to 2, and 15 to 3. In this proportion, five notes are equivalent in performance and in the measure of time <term>mensura</term> to one note of similar name and quantity; so that each of those five is diminished by four-fifths of its own value. It is written among the notes in this manner: 5, 10, 15, as appears here.

<image-desc>Musical notation for two voices, CANTVS and TENOR. Above the Cantus staff are the numbers 1, 2, 3. The Cantus part uses a proportion sign with a 5 over a 1. The music is written in white mensural notation, where five smaller notes are played in the time of one larger note.</image-desc>

<margin>Note.</margin> ¶ It must also be generally accepted in other proportions of greater inequality <note>A "proportion of greater inequality" is any ratio where the first number is larger than the second (e.g., 5:1 or 3:2), resulting in the notes becoming faster.</note> of any genus that each note of the larger number is to be diminished by as many parts of its own number-value as there are units in the difference between the two terms. For example: since the number five <term>quinarius</term> completes a quintuple proportion to the unit, the proper value of each unit contained in the number five will be fivefold, so that each of them is divided into five diminished small parts. But since the difference between five and one is four <term>quaternarius</term>, each individual unit of the five is diminished equally by four-fifths of its own quantity. It is similar in the other proportions.

### ¶ Concerning Sextuple Proportion.

¶ Sextuple proportion is the fifth species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>In the Pythagorean system of ratios used in music, the "multiple genus" includes any ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller one (2:1, 3:1, etc.).</note>. It occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number exactly six times; it is equivalent to it in quantity and in the measure of time <term>mensura</term> <note>The "measure of time" refers to the beat or "tactus" used to keep time in Renaissance music.</note>: such as 6 to 1, 12 to 2, and 18 to 3. According to this arrangement, six notes are equivalent and made equal to one note of the same kind, such that each of those six is reduced by five-sixths of its quantitative value. It is represented among the notes in this way: 6, 12, 18, as demonstrated by this example.

->1 2 3<-

### ¶ Concerning Septuple Proportion.

¶ Septuple proportion is the sixth species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number is an exact multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1, 7:1).</note>. It occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number exactly seven times; it is equivalent to it in quantity and in the measure of time <term>mensura</term>: such as 7 to 1, 14 to 2, and 21 to 3. In this way, seven notes are measured against one note of the same kind, so that each of those seven is reduced by six-sevenths of its original value <note>In a 7:1 proportion, seven notes must be played in the time normally allotted to one, making each note seven times faster.</note>. It is represented in musical compositions in this way:

->7 14 21<-
->1  2  3<-

which the present musical example <term>concentus</term> clarifies.

### ¶ On Octuple Proportion.

Octuple proportion <note>An 8:1 ratio.</note>, which is the seventh species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller.</note> in order, occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number exactly eight times. It is equivalent to it in power <note>original: "potentia"; referring here to the total rhythmic duration.</note>: such as 8 to 1, 16 to 2, and 24 to 3. Indeed, this proportion makes eight notes equivalent to one note of the same kind, so that each of those eight is reduced by seven-eighths of its own original quantity. In musical compositions, it is depicted in this way:

8 16 24

### ¶ On Nonuple Proportion.

¶ Nonuple proportion <note>A 9:1 ratio.</note>, the eighth species of the multiple genus, occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains it exactly nine times. It is equal to it in quantity and measure <term>mensura</term> <note>The standard unit of time or beat in Renaissance music.</note>: such as 9 to 1, 18 to 2, and 27 to 3. For nine notes in this proportion are equivalent to one note of the same kind, so that each of those nine is reduced by eight-ninths of its original quantity. And it is described in musical notation in this way:

9 18 27
->1 2 3<-

as is clear here.

### ¶ On Decuple Proportion.

¶ Decuple proportion <note>A 10:1 ratio.</note>, which is the ninth species of the multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller.</note>, occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number exactly ten times. It is equivalent to it in power and measure <note>original: "in potentia & mensura"; referring to the total rhythmic duration.</note>: such as 10 to 1, 20 to 2, and 30 to 3. In this proportion, ten notes are measured against one of the same name and quantity, so that each of those ten is reduced by nine-tenths of its own quantitative value. In musical compositions <term>cantilenis</term>, it is depicted in this way:

10 20 30
->1 2 3<-

¶ Indeed, there are infinite species of this multiple genus <term>multiplicis generis</term> <note>A category of proportions where the larger number is a perfect multiple of the smaller.</note>: which we leave to the diligence of musicians to investigate.

### ¶ On the submultiple genus, which is the first kind of lesser inequality, and its species. Chapter Four.

The submultiple genus <term>submultiplex genus</term>, which is the first category of lesser inequality <note>A ratio where the first number is smaller than the second (e.g., 1:2), resulting in a lengthening of note values.</note>: occurs when a smaller number of notes, compared and placed over a larger number, is related to that larger number by being precisely contained within it multiple times: such as 1 to 2, 1 to 3, 1 to 4, 2 to 4, 2 to 6, 2 to 8, and so on. For the smaller number matches the larger in such a way that each individual unit of the smaller number receives an increase from as many parts of its own numerical value as there were units constituting the difference between the terms. For example: if I have derived a subtriple proportion <note>A 1:3 ratio.</note> from these numbers, 3 to 9, since the number six fills the difference, each unit of the three <insert>is</insert> increased<gloss>original: augetur</gloss> by six-third parts of its own quantity. This also happens in all the genera of lesser inequality. Indeed, there are infinite species of this submultiple genus. The first is subduple; the second, subtriple; the third, subquadruple; the fourth, subquintupla; and so forth.

### ¶ On the Subduple Proportion.

¶ Subduple proportion occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it exactly twice, being equivalent to it in quantity and measure: such as 1 to 2, 2 to 4, and 3 to 6. In this proportion, each individual note of the smaller number is equivalent to and measured against two notes that are similar to it in name and quantity, so that it acquires double its own quantity <note>A single note now takes up the time previously occupied by two notes of the same type, effectively slowing the music to half speed.</note>: and it is written in songs in this manner:
-> 1 2 3 <-
-> 2 4 6 <-
which is clear in this musical arrangement <term>concentu</term>.

This proportion measures each individual note of the smaller number against three notes of the same kind, so that each note of the smaller number grows to three times its own quantity. ### ¶ Concerning the sub-quadruple proportion.
¶ The **sub-quadruple proportion** <note>original: "subquadrupla proportio"; a 1:4 ratio where the music slows down significantly, making one note last as long as four notes did previously.</note> occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding ones, is contained within it exactly four times, being equal to it in value: as 1 to 4, and 2 to 8, and 3 to 12. In this proportion, each individual note of the smaller number is equal to four notes of the same kind, such that it receives four times its own quantity <note>The "quantity" refers to the duration or length of the note in time.</note>. It is written among the notes in this way: 1, 2, 3.

It is likewise destroyed by the **quadruple proportion** <note>A 4:1 ratio, which accelerates the music four times over.</note> opposite to it, as is evident from the progression of this harmony <note>original: "concentus"; referring to the sounding together of the different voice parts.</note>.

# Concerning the sub-quintuple proportion.

¶ The **sub-quintuple proportion** <note>original: "subquintupla proportio"; a 1:5 ratio where the music slows down so that one note now lasts as long as five notes did previously.</note> occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding ones, is contained within it exactly five times, being equal to it in duration and measure: as 1 to 5, 2 to 10, 3 to 15, and 4 to 20. In this proportion, the smaller number is made equal to the larger one, so that each individual note of the smaller number increases to five times its own proper value. It is usually indicated by this rule: "it increases fivefold" <note>original: "crescit in quintuplo"</note>. In musical compositions, however, it is marked by these numerical characters: 1, 2, 3. It is then canceled <note>original: "destruitur"; this signifies a return to the standard tempo.</note> by its opposite, the **quintuple proportion** <note>A 5:1 ratio.</note>, as the present harmony demonstrates: 5, 10, 15.

¶ We leave the remaining species of the **sub-multiple genus** <note>The category of proportions where the larger number is a direct multiple of the smaller (e.g., 1:6, 1:7).</note> to be investigated by the skill and diligence of musicians through similar reasoning.

<margin>Chapter V.</margin>
# Concerning the super-particular genus and its species.

The **super-particular genus** <note>original: "superparticulare genus"; a category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one "aliquot" (equal) part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note>, which is the second type of "greater inequality," is said to occur when the larger number of following notes is compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, and is found to contain the smaller number exactly once, plus one fractional part of it; it is equal to it in power and the measurement of time. An **aliquot part** <note>term: "pars aliquota"; a part that, when taken a certain number of times, perfectly equals the whole (for example, 2 is an aliquot part of 4).</note> is that which, when taken a certain number of times, yields its whole exactly: such as a half, which when taken twice completes its whole number, or a third, which is taken three times. And—

<summary>This page concludes the discussion of sub-multiple proportions (specifically the 1:5 ratio) and introduces the super-particular genus, which involves more complex ratios like 3:2.</summary>
<keywords>sub-quintuple proportion, super-particular genus, aliquot part, mensural notation, Cantus, Tenor, musical ratios</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions in music, transitioning from the sub-multiple genus (where numbers are direct multiples) into the "super-particular" or "epimoric" genus. The author is moving from the general theory established in his previous books to practical applications in rhythmic notation.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR

The fourth species is fourfold, and so on; this is established more extensively from the deductions made in the third book of the *Theoricæ* <note>original: "Theoricæ"; referring to the author's earlier work, *Theorica Musicae*, where he laid out the mathematical foundations of music.</note>. Indeed, the species of this genus are infinite: the first is the **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera: A ratio of 3:2, where three notes are played in the time of two.</term>; the second is **sesquitertial** <term>sesquitertia: A ratio of 4:3, where four notes are played in the time of three.</term>; the third is **sesquiquartal** <term>sesquiquarta: A ratio of 5:4.</term>; the fourth is **sesquiquintal** <term>sesquiquinta: A ratio of 6:5.</term>, and so on. You will understand their order and progression when you compare any number to the smaller number that is adjacent and closest to it: such as three to two, which is **sesquialtera**. Then four to three, which is **sesquitertial**; five to four, which is **sesquiquartal**; six to five, which is **sesquiquintal**; and the rest in the same manner. In this genus, it happens that a proportion formed from smaller numbers is actually larger, while the proportion contained by larger numbers is smaller. For example, the **sesquialtera** (3 to 2) is larger than the **sesquitertial** (4 to 3), and this is also larger than the **sesquiquartal** (5 to 4). The same logic is found in the rest: for a half-part (1/2) is larger than a third (1/3), and a third-part exceeds a fourth, and a fourth exceeds a fifth. We shall describe most clearly how much the **sesquialtera** exceeds the **sesquitertial**, and the **sesquitertial** exceeds the **sesquiquartal**, and so on, in our description of instrumental harmony through a general rule. But for now, let us follow the species of this <term>epimoric</term> <note>original: "epimorias"; another name for the super-particular genus, where the ratio is (n+1)/n.</note> genus one by one in their natural order.

### -> Concerning the sesquialtera proportion. <-

The **sesquialtera** proportion occurs when a larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number exactly once and additionally a half-part of it, equaling it in quantity and measure: such as 3 to 2, 6 to 4, 9 to 6, and 12 to 8. In this proportion, three notes are measured against two notes that are similar to them in name and value. Consequently, each of those three notes is diminished by a third part of its own proper value. In songs, it is represented in this way: 3/2, 6/4, 9/6, as the present harmony <note>original: "concentus"</note> declares.

<margin>
CANTUS
<br/>
TENOR
</margin>

<image-desc>Three staves of musical notation featuring mensural notation with various proportional signs (3/2, 6/4, 9/6). The first two staves correspond to the "CANTVS" (Soprano) part, using a C1 clef. The third staff corresponds to the "TENOR" part, using a C4 clef. The notes include breves (square notes), semibreves (diamond shapes), and minims (notes with stems), showing how three notes are grouped to fit the space of two.</image-desc>

Indeed, it must be considered that if the part by which a note is diminished in a proportion is a "figurable" part of that note—for instance, a third part of a **semibreve** in **perfect prolation** <note>original: "prolatione pfecta"; a rhythmic setting where a semibreve is naturally divided into three smaller "minim" notes.</note> (which is a **minim**), or a third part of a **breve** in **perfect time** <note>original: "tpe pfecto"; a setting where the breve is divided into three semibreves.</note> (which is a **semibreve**), or a fourth part of a **semibreve** in **lesser prolation** <note>original: "minori platione"; where the semibreve normally divides into two minims, making the "fourth part" a smaller subdivision.</note> (which is a **semiminim**)—or in any other way, such diminished notes will be easily discerned and performed, as is arranged in this example.

It explains the mathematical logic that smaller numbers create larger proportional shifts and provides a musical example of how three notes can be played in the time of two.</summary>

<margin>7</margin>
But if the quantity by which each note of the larger number is diminished is not a representable aliquot part <term>aliquot part: a fraction that divides into the whole exactly, such as 1/2 or 1/3, without a remainder.</term> of its whole—for instance, if it were a fifth part of one semibreve <term>semibreve: a standard note value in Renaissance music, the ancestor of our whole note.</term>, which is not assigned a simple note shape in our system—then the diminished quantities of those notes are joined in a kind of steady continuation. This is done so that the specific quantity of each individual note cannot easily be separated from the quantity of the next by any distinct boundary. 

Instead, one quantity and measure is continued into the other in the same way (for example) as happens with a string on an instrument that is struck while being tightened: the first strike of the string emits a lower sound, but while it is being tightened, it continues that same sound upward into a higher pitch <note>The author is describing a continuous "glissando" or slide in pitch as an analogy for rhythms that flow into one another without clear divisions.</note>. We also see this clearly with our own eyes when we look at the colors of a rainbow <note>original: "cælestis arcus"; literally "celestial bow."</note>; for the colors are so close to one another that through a continuous changing of one, a color turns into the following one with no middle boundary to distinguish them. 

The **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera: A ratio of 3:2, where three notes are played in the time normally taken by two.</term> proportion is most often denoted in songs without using numerical signs; this is done when the notes are written as "full" notes, blackened or in another color, under the "imperfect" <term>imperfect: a rhythmic system where notes are grouped in pairs rather than threes.</term> measurements of the notes, in this manner:

# BOOK FOUR

<margin>Bonadies</margin>
¶ However, my music teacher Bonadies <note>Johannes Bonadies (also known as Godendach), a 15th-century friar and theorist who taught Gaffurius.</note> established that a single full semibreve <term>semibreve: a standard note value in mensural notation, the ancestor of the modern whole note.</term> of minor prolation <term>minor prolation: a rhythmic context where a semibreve is normally divided into two minims.</term>, or a single full breve <term>breve: a note value twice as long as a semibreve.</term> of imperfect time <term>imperfect time: a rhythmic context where a breve is divided into two semibreves.</term>, or only two full notes—which do not fall under a ternary (three-part) division—should be arranged not as a *hemiolia* <term>hemiolia: a 3:2 ratio often indicated by coloring notes black.</term> but as a double dimension <term>dupla: a 2:1 ratio.</term>. When you have made only the minims <term>minim: a note value shorter than a semibreve.</term> full with black color for a *sesquialtera* proportion: since there is then no difference in appearance or shape between these "proportioned" minims and ordinary semiminims <term>semiminim: a note value half the length of a minim, often naturally black.</term>, they will all be called *sesquialtera* minims if they proceed according to a ternary division.

If, however, there are only six full minims—which could be either *sesquialtera* or simply semiminims—then if a single "void" (white) minim immediately precedes them, to which the first two full minims can be counted, then all six will be semiminims. The same applies when a single white minim immediately follows them. But if a single white minim does not immediately precede or follow them, then they will all be *sesquialtera* minims. Truly, when there are only five full minims and a single white minim immediately precedes them, the first two full notes will be semiminims, while the last three will be called *sesquialtera* minims. If a single white minim immediately follows those five, then the first three full notes will be *sesquialtera* and the last two will be semiminims, as they are counted with the following white minim. If there are seven full minims, the first four are treated as semiminims and the last three as *sesquialtera*. A demonstration of these rules is provided below.

<image-desc>The following section contains musical notation illustrating these complex rhythmic groupings, showing how black notes are grouped against white ones in the Soprano and Tenor parts.</image-desc>

¶ However, through syncopation <term>syncopation: shifting the rhythmic emphasis so that notes start on "off-beats."</term>, a full minim or another note can sometimes be shifted; it may take on a *sesquialtera* proportion or sometimes depart from it, which we believe should be left to the composer's judgment. Meanwhile, it must generally be noted that a *sesquialtera* arranged in full [black] notes should not be described with the numerical signs of its own proportions <note>i.e., one should not write the fraction 3/2 if the black notes already indicate that rhythm.</note>. This is to avoid the inconvenience of falling into "two sesquialteras." If those were applied to each other, they would create not a *sesquialtera* but a *dupla sesquiquarta* proportion (a 9:4 ratio), as shown by these numbers: 9, 6, 4. 

The composer Busnoys <note>Antoine Busnoys (c. 1430–1492), one of the most famous composers of the Burgundian School.</note> failed to consider this in the high tenor part of the *Sanctus* in his Mass *L’homme armé* <note>Original: "missa Lomearme." This is one of the most famous musical works of the 15th century.</note>. Tinctoris <note>Johannes Tinctoris, a leading music theorist of the time known for his strict rules and criticisms of fellow composers.</note> sharply criticized him for this, because...

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines displaying mensural notation. It includes diamond-shaped white and black (colorated) notes, with a "3" signature indicating a sesquialtera proportion.</image-desc>

¶ For my part, I would not condemn this practice in cases of doubt. When composed songs <term>cantilenæ: polyphonic vocal compositions.</term> are brought forward, they ought to be recognized and sung by the singers immediately and without hesitation. This is what most composers have done: not because they derive the proportion from the sign itself, but to show that the notes filled with blackness <term>coloration: the practice of filling in the body of a note with ink to change its rhythmic value, often to indicate a triple meter or proportion.</term> are distinct from others that might be ambiguous. For when six full (black) minims <term>minim: a rhythmic unit, the ancestor of the modern half-note.</term> are placed among, before, or after several regular white minims—since they could be interpreted as either sesquialtera notes or as semiminims <term>semiminim: a note value half the length of a minim, which is naturally black in this notation system.</term>—the sign of the number three is appropriately placed before them (or underneath, as some prefer) when they are meant to be sesquialtera, to aid in this recognition. This is clearly demonstrated in the present harmony <note>original: "concentu," referring to the musical example below.</note>.

¶ Furthermore, minims of this kind that are filled for sesquialtera are not usually syncopated <term>syncopation: a rhythmic device where a note begins on a weak beat and is held across a strong beat.</term> by custom; however, full semibreves <term>semibreve: a standard note value, the ancestor of the modern whole-note.</term> and breves <term>breve: a note value twice the length of a semibreve.</term> of this type are more frequently syncopated. It should also be noted that when you see three full minims, if there is a fourth full minim before or after them which must be counted with those three through syncopation, then those three will be semiminims, and that fourth full semiminim will be joined to them in the division. <margin>Dufay</margin>
<margin><insert>Dufay and Philippon de Bourge, two French authors</insert></margin>
<margin>Tinctoris</margin>

¶ There are also those who arrange for the <term>sesquialtera: a proportion where three notes are played in the space of two.</term> proportion to be expressed in the notes by the sign of <term>perfect prolation: a system where a semibreve is divided into three minims, usually indicated by a dot.</term>—namely, by interposing a dot within the sign of <term>tempus: the division of the breve into semibreves.</term> in this manner: **O** or **C**. They feel there is no difference between prolation and proportion; however, the consideration of reason did not hesitate to refute this view. For the sesquialtera proportion makes three <term>minims: the basic rhythmic unit of this period.</term> equal to two; yet perfect prolation bestows three of these same "regular" minims upon each <term>semibreve: a note value originally half of a breve.</term>, and they are not to be measured as equal to two. 

This happens just as it does with a <term>breve: a long note value.</term> in perfect time, which is resolved into three semibreves, yet these three are not made equal to the two semibreves contained in a breve of imperfect time. Gulielmus Dufay, noting this correctly, arranged it so in the <note>original: "Et in terra pax"</note> "And on earth peace," the <note>original: "Patrem"</note> "Father," and the <note>original: "Unam sanctam catholicam"</note> "One holy catholic" sections of his *Mass of Saint Anthony*. There, he marked perfect (or major) prolation and imperfect time; indeed, he assigned three regular minims to each semibreve, which were by no means equal in duration to two. 

Philippon de Bourges likewise placed this rationally in a certain "And on earth peace," arranging by the sign of perfect prolation three regular and whole minims for each semibreve, making them unequal to the two minims of imperfect (or minor) prolation. Tinctoris likewise, and many others, demonstrate this correctly in their own songs <term>cantilenis: polyphonic vocal pieces.</term>. 

Nor do I agree with the corrupt practice of many who, when they describe a sesquialtera in notes of imperfect time using only the character of the triple number—which is absurd—treat it as perfect time. They have used minor prolation in place of major, considering <term>alteration: the doubling of a note's value to fill a triple meter.</term> and <term>perfection: the triple grouping of notes.</term> in the notes. From this, it is easily seen that from the diminution of the proportion, an augmentation has been derived in the notes, as is noted in this example:

### BOOK

<warning>Visible bleed-through from the reverse side of the leaf (text such as "CANTVS" and "TENOR" mirrored on the left).</warning>

<image-desc>Two musical staves featuring mensural notation. The notation includes a C-clef on the second line and various note values (breves, semibreves, minims), some with stems, representing a musical example or exercise related to the theoretical text.</image-desc>

¶ For a <term>perfect breve: a long note value that, in this system, lasts for three beats.</term> and an <term>altered semibreve: a middle-value note that is doubled in length to fill out a triple meter.</term> are arranged only in <term>perfect time: a rhythmic framework where the pulse is divided into three parts, symbolized by a circle.</term>, the proper sign of which is a circle. However, only "greater" or <term>perfect prolation: a system where the semibreve is divided into three minims.</term> provides a perfect semibreve and an alterable <term>minim: the smallest standard note value in this period.</term>; the proper sign for this is a dot placed within the sign of time <note>original: "punctus in signo temporis affixus"</note>. In <term>imperfect time: a rhythmic framework where the pulse is divided into two parts, symbolized by a semicircle.</term>, which a semicircle indicates, a breve note always possesses only two semibreves, whether in its regular form or diminished by any proportion, unless it receives a <term>dot of augmentation: a mark that adds value to a note, similar to a modern dotted note.</term>. Nor does a semibreve ever receive the increase of <term>alteration: the doubling of a note's value to maintain a triple rhythm.</term> within that framework. And therefore, by the same logic, a semibreve in <term>minor prolation: a system where the semibreve is divided into two minims.</term> will neither acquire perfection, nor can a minim be altered.

There are also those who do not diminish rests in the <term>sesquialtera: a rhythmic ratio where three notes are played in the time of two.</term> proportion as the proportion itself demands, thinking that a certain "imperfection" is derived from the proportion itself. With all due respect to them <note>original: "quorum pace dixerim"</note>, I would say that the diminutions of proportions—to which both notes and rests are subject—must be considered differently than the "imperfections" of notes. These imperfections are recognized for the most part by the subtraction of a third part through counting, as we have discussed more extensively in the treatise on figures; in such cases, rests are not usually made imperfect. 

This should also be noted regarding the sesquialtera proportion and others: when proportioned notes exist within a perfect quantity—for instance, proportioned breves and semibreves in perfect time, or semibreves and minims in perfect prolation—those notes can vary according to the "accidents" of their perfect quantity. For a <gloss>white or hollow</gloss> <insert>hollow</insert> breve appearing before another breve will be perfect (resolvable into three semibreves), yet these will be made equal to two according to the arrangement of the proportion. Furthermore, the second semibreve between two breves will be altered. Conversely, a hollow semibreve in perfect prolation before one like itself will contain three minims (governed as two), and the second of two minims placed between two semibreves will be altered. It is established that the rests of breves and semibreves are perfect according to this consideration, as the present <term>harmony: the musical example provided on the page.</term> <note>original: "concentus"</note> declares.

->TENOR<-

If, however, the <term>sesquialtera proportion: a ratio of 3:2, where three notes are played in the time of two.</term> is arranged in notes subject to <term>imperfect quantity: a rhythmic system where values are divided into two parts rather than three.</term>, then all the notes and rests will always be imperfect. For example, if in <term>imperfect time: indicated by a semicircle, where a breve equals two semibreves.</term> this sesquialtera is assigned, making three breves equal to two, then each of those three notes always has a third of its proper value removed <note>original: "de tertia propriæ quãtitatis adimitur parte"</note>. Similarly, the rests of three breves are calculated proportionally to be equivalent to two, so that each individual breve rest is diminished by a third of its proper quantity. The same must be understood regarding three semibreves of <term>minor prolation: a system where the semibreve is divided into two minims.</term> and their rests; for in the sesquialtera proportion, it is established that they are always to be deprived of a third of their value, as is shown in the present <term>composition: a musical arrangement or harmony.</term> <note>original: "concentu"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical proportions. # BOOK

The arrangement of the <term>sesquialtera proportion: a rhythmic ratio of 3:2, where three notes are played in the time normally occupied by two.</term> in musical notes would be meaningless if the rests and the notes themselves were not considered with a similar <term>diminution: the reduction of a note's duration.</term>. For every proportion of "greater inequality" <note>original: "maioris iæqlitatis pportio," referring to a ratio where the first number is larger than the second, resulting in a faster tempo.</note> described in songs removes a proportional and fitting part from individual notes and rests according to their proper and natural value. Conversely, a proportion of "lesser inequality" <note>original: "Minoris uero iæqlitatis pportio," where the first number is smaller, slowing the tempo.</note> increases both the notes and the rests.

Similarly, many mark the sesquialtera itself using "filled" notes <note>This refers to the practice of coloration, where normally hollow white notes are blackened to indicate a change in rhythm.</note>, which they call <term>hemiolia: a Greek-derived term for the ratio of 3:2, often used interchangeably with sesquialtera in music theory.</term>. They also use the sign of <term>imperfect time: a meter where the breve is divided into two semibreves, usually marked by a C.</term> and <term>major prolation: a system where the semibreve is divided into three minims.</term> reversed, like this: Ɔ. In my opinion, I judge this practice to be erroneous. It is established, therefore, from these deductions, that the sesquialtera proportion in notes is indicated by the "fullness" [blackening] of the notes; I would rather call this a diminution equivalent to the sesquialtera proportion. This effect is "destroyed" (canceled) when hollow notes immediately follow. Again, it is known to be canceled by its own numerical characters when a <term>subsesquialtera: the inverse ratio (2:3), used to return the music to its original tempo.</term> immediately follows in opposition, as is proven by this musical harmony.

# BOOK FOUR

### ¶ Concerning the sesquitertia proportion.

¶ The sesquitertia proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once plus an additional third part of it. It is equivalent to the smaller number in value and measure—for example, 4 to 3, 8 to 6, and 12 to 9. <term>Sesquitertia: a rhythmic ratio of 4:3, where four notes are played in the time normally occupied by three.</term> This proportion makes four notes equal to three of the same name and quantity, so that each one of the four notes is diminished by a fourth of its own value. In musical songs, it is written in this way: <insert>4 8 12</insert> <insert>3 6 9</insert> <note>These numbers represent the ratios 4/3, 8/6, and 12/9.</note> It is canceled out by the <term>subsesquitertia: the inverse ratio (3:4), where three notes occupy the time of four.</term> which is its opposite and follows it immediately, as perceived in this harmony: <insert>3 6 9</insert> <insert>4 8 12</insert>.

<note>The image displays a musical example of these proportions. The upper voices demonstrate the faster 4:3 rhythm, while the lower voice (Tenor) maintains the steady pulse.</note>

-> CANTVS <-
<note>original: "CANTVS"; the melody or highest voice part.</note>

-> TENOR <-
<note>original: "TENOR"; the structural foundation of the harmony which "holds" the principal melody.</note>

¶ Some have wished that the sesquitertia itself be understood in the notes by the sign of <term>imperfect time: a meter where the breve is divided into two semibreves, usually marked by a C.</term> turned to the left like this: Ɔ. These people are sharply criticized by <note>Prosdocimus de Beldemandis (c. 1380–1428), a Paduan theorist known for his mathematical precision.</note> Prosdocimus of Padua in his *Exposition of the Brief Compendium of Jehan des Murs*, as well as by every practitioner of the art, and by <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), a famous Renaissance composer and theorist.</note> Tinctoris in his *Treatise on Proportions*.

### ¶ Concerning the sesquiquarta proportion.

¶ The sesquiquarta proportion is said to occur when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once plus a fourth part of it. It is equivalent to the smaller number in quantity and the measurement of time—for example, 5 to 4, 10 to 8, and 15 to 12. <term>Sesquiquarta: a rhythmic ratio of 5:4, where five notes are played in the time normally occupied by four.</term> In this proportion, five notes are equivalent to four of the same kind, so that each of the five notes is diminished by a fifth part of its own value. In songs, it is described in this way: <insert>5 10 15</insert> <insert>4 8 12</insert> <note>These represent the ratios 5/4, 10/8, and 15/12.</note> It is canceled out by the <term>subsesquiquarta: the inverse ratio (4:5), where four notes occupy the time of five.</term> that immediately follows it, as proven by this harmony: <insert>4 8 12</insert> <insert>5 10 15</insert>.

H iiii <note>A printer's signature used to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page defines the sesquitertia (4:3) and sesquiquarta (5:4) proportions, explaining how they mathematically diminish the value of notes. The author also references famous theorists like Prosdocimus and Tinctoris to debunk the use of a reversed 'C' sign for these ratios.</summary>

### ¶ Concerning the *sesquiquinta* proportion.
<term>Sesquiquinta: a rhythmic ratio of 6:5, where six notes are played in the time normally taken by five notes of the same value.</term>

¶ The *sesquiquinta* proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once plus an additional fifth part of it. It is equivalent to the smaller number in value and measure—for example: 6 to 5, 12 to 10, and 18 to 15. This proportion makes six notes equal to five of the same kind; thus, each of those six notes is diminished by a sixth part of its own quantitative value. In musical songs, it is written in this way:
<insert>6 12 18</insert>
<insert>5 10 15</insert>
<note>These figures represent the ratios 6/5, 12/10, and 18/15.</note>
And if the <term>subsesquiquinta: the inverse ratio (5:6), which restores the original tempo by playing five notes in the time of six.</term>, which is its opposite, follows immediately, the proportion vanishes, as the present <term>concentus: a harmony or "singing together" of multiple voices.</term> demonstrates.

# ¶ Concerning the *sesquisexta* proportion.
<term>Sesquisexta: a rhythmic ratio of 7:6, where seven notes are played in the time normally allotted to six notes of the same value.</term>

¶ The *sesquisexta* proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller preceding number, contains that smaller number once and additionally its sixth part, and is equal to it in the measurement of time—for example: 7 to 6, 14 to 12, and 21 to 18. For seven notes are equivalent to six of their own kind in this proportion; thus each of those seven is diminished by a seventh part of its own quantity. In musical songs, it is indeed described in this manner:
<insert>7 14 21</insert>
<insert>6 12 18</insert>
And it is undone by the *subsesquisexta* <note>original: "subsesquisexta"; the inverse ratio of 6:7, which restores the original tempo.</note> which is its opposite, as is established here.

<note>The musical notation provided here illustrates the 7:6 proportion. The staves show the higher "CANTVS" (melody) and the "TENOR" (the voice that holds the foundation) interacting. The numbers above and below the notes indicate the mathematical shift in speed between the two parts.</note>

# ¶ Concerning the *sesquiseptima* proportion.
<term>Sesquiseptima: a rhythmic ratio of 8:7, where eight notes are played in the time of seven.</term>

¶ The *sesquiseptima* proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller preceding number, contains it once and additionally its seventh part, being equivalent to it in value: as 8 to 7, 16 to 14, and 24 to 21. In this proportion, eight notes are equivalent and measured against seven of their own kind; so that each of those eight is diminished by an eighth part of its value. It is represented in songs in this way:
<insert>8 16 24</insert>
<insert>7 14 21</insert>
But if the *subsesquiseptima* <note>original: "subsesquiseptima"; the inverse ratio of 7:8.</note> follows it immediately, the proportion vanishes and is undone because it is its opposite; as is noted by this example.

### ¶ Concerning the *sesquioctava* proportion.
<term>Sesquioctava: a rhythmic ratio of 9:8, where nine notes are performed in the time usually taken by eight notes of the same value.</term>

¶ The *sesquioctava* proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, is made equal to it—containing that smaller number once and additionally its eighth part—for example: 9 to 8, 18 to 16, and 27 to 24. For this proportion makes nine notes equivalent to eight of their own kind, so that each of those nine is diminished by a ninth part of its own proper quantity. In musical songs, it is described in this manner: 9, 18, 27. And it is undone by the *subsesquioctava* <note>original: "subsesquioctava"; the inverse ratio of 8:9, which returns the tempo to its original speed.</note> which is its opposite, as is shown in this harmony: 8, 16, 24.

### ¶ Concerning the *sesquinona* proportion.
<term>Sesquinona: a rhythmic ratio of 10:9, where ten notes are performed in the time of nine.</term>

¶ The *sesquinona* proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller preceding number, is related and made equal to it—containing that smaller number once and additionally its ninth part—for example: 10 to 9, 20 to 18, and 30 to 27. In this proportion, ten notes are equivalent to nine of their own kind, so that each of those ten is diminished by one tenth part of itself. It is represented in songs in this manner: 10, 20. Furthermore, it is undone by the *subsesquinona* <note>original: "subsesquinona"; the inverse ratio of 9:10.</note> which is its opposite, as the present harmony proves: 9, 18.

## ¶ Concerning the subsuperparticular genus and its species: Chapter Six.

The subsuperparticular genus occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding notes, is equivalent to it in quantity and power—being contained within that larger number once, along with one of its aliquot parts <note>An "aliquot part" is a portion that divides the whole number exactly, such as 1 being an aliquot part of 2 or 3.</note>: such as 2 to 3, 3 to 4, 4 to 5, and so on. Its species are, in fact, infinite. The first is the *subsesqualtera* [2:3]. The second is the *subsesquitertia* [3:4]. The third is the *subsesquiquarta* [4:5]. The fourth is the *subsesquiquinta* [5:6], and so on; each of which must be discussed individually.

->Concerning the *subsesqualtera* proportion:<-

¶ The *subsesqualtera* proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding ones, is contained within it only once plus half of that smaller number; it is equivalent to the larger number in power and the measure of time: such as 2 to 3, 4 to 6, 6 to 9, and so on. In this proportion, two notes are equivalent to and measured against three notes of their own kind, so that each of those two notes is increased by half of its own proper quantity <note>When 2 notes take the time of 3, the music slows down because each note must be held longer.</note>. In musical songs, it is represented in this way: <insert>2 4 6 / 3 6 9</insert>. And it is undone by the *sesqualtera* <note>original: "sesqualtera"; the inverse 3:2 ratio.</note> which is its opposite, when it follows immediately after, as is perceived in this harmony: <insert>3 6 9 / 2 4 6</insert>.

<note>Vocabulary used in this section: subsuperparticular genus, subsesqualtera (2:3 ratio), proportion, CANTVS (high voice), TENOR (foundational voice), measure, of the notes, sesqualtera (3:2 ratio).</note>

<summary>This page defines the "subsuperparticular" genus of musical proportions, which slow down the tempo, specifically detailing the subsesqualtera (2:3) ratio where two notes are stretched to fill the time of three.</summary>
<keywords>subsuperparticular, subsesqualtera, rhythmic proportion, mensural notation, musical theory, augmentation, aliquot part</keywords>

### ¶ Concerning the subsesquitertia proportion.
<note>original: "subsesquitertia"; a rhythmic ratio of 3:4.</note>

¶ The subsesquitertia proportion occurs when a smaller number <note>original: "maior"; though the OCR reads "maior" (larger), the mathematical definition of a "sub-" proportion in this context refers to a smaller number of notes (3) replacing a larger number (4).</note> of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding notes, is maintained such that the smaller is contained once within the larger along with a third part of that smaller number; it is equal to the larger number in the measurement of time: such as 3 to 4, 6 to 8, 9 to 12, and so on. In this proportion, three notes are equivalent to and measured against four notes of the same name and quantity. Consequently, each of those three notes receives an increase <note>original: "augmentum"; because 3 notes must now fill the time previously occupied by 4, each note must be held for 1/3 longer than its usual duration.</note> of a third part of its own value. It is represented in musical compositions in this way:
->3 6 9<-
->4 8 12<-
And it is canceled out by a following sesquitertia <note>original: "sesquitertia"; the 4:3 ratio, which is the inverse of 3:4. This returns the music to its original speed.</note> which is its opposite, as is understood in the present example:
->4 8 12<-
->3 6 9<-

### Concerning the subsesquiquarta proportion.
<term>subsesquiquarta: A rhythmic ratio of 4:5, where four notes are stretched to occupy the time of five notes.</term>

¶ The **subsesquiquarta** proportion occurs when the smaller <note>original: "maior"; the OCR likely misread "minor" or the author erred, as 4 is the smaller number in this 4:5 ratio.</note> number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once plus a fourth part of that smaller number; it is equal to the larger number in quantity and measurement: such as 4 to 5, 8 to 10, and 12 to 15. For this proportion makes four notes equal to five notes of the same kind: such that each one of those four is increased by a fourth part of its own value. It is described in the notes in this way:
4 8 12
5 10 15
However, it is undone and disappears when it is immediately followed by a **sesquiquarta** <note>original: "sesquiquarta"; the inverse ratio of 5:4, which would return the music to its original tempo.</note> proportion, which the present harmony demonstrates.

### Concerning the subsesquiquinta proportion.
<term>subsesquiquinta: A rhythmic ratio of 5:6, where five notes are stretched to occupy the time of six notes.</term>

¶ The **subsesquiquinta** proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once plus a fifth part of that smaller number: and it is equal to the larger number: such as 5 to 6, 10 to 12, 15 to 18, and so forth. For this proportion makes five notes equal to six of its own kind in both name and quantity: such that each of those five receives an increase of a fifth part of its value. It is represented in notes in this way:
5 10 15
6 12 18
And it is undone by the **sesquiquinta** <note>original: "sesquiquinta"; the inverse ratio of 6:5.</note> which is opposite to it: which the present harmony explains.

<image-desc>A section of musical notation in mensural style featuring two parts: SOPRANO (top) and TENOR (bottom). The staves contain complex rhythmic proportions represented by numerical ratios.
