¶ We leave the remaining species of this **sub-superparticular** <term>sub-superparticular: a category of rhythmic ratios where a smaller number is compared to a larger number that contains it plus one fractional part (e.g., 3:4 or 4:5).</term> genus for the careful study of musicians to explore. Rest easy for now, most pleasant singer, if you find a perfect number of notes in the arrangement of any proportion. For often, what we do not arrange in **semibreves** <term>semibreve: a diamond-shaped note value, the ancestor of the modern whole note.</term>, you will consider in **minims** <term>minim: a note value half the length of a semibreve, similar to a modern half note.</term>. Furthermore, in those proportions that are signaled only by the removal of the preceding values, you will sometimes see an imperfect number of notes for the sake of brevity, or you will find the rhythmic calculation of notes in the tenors to be either redundant or diminished for that same reason.

### ¶ Concerning the Superpartient Genus and its Species. Chapter VII.

The **superpartient** <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus two or more fractional parts (e.g., 5:3 contains 3 once, plus 2 thirds).</term> genus, which is the third type of "greater inequality," occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number within itself exactly once, plus more than one of its equal parts. In this case, the larger group is made equal to the smaller in power and the measurement of time. 

When that extra portion of the smaller number—which is the remainder and difference between the two terms—shows two parts of said smaller number, it is called a **super-two-part** <note>original: "superbipartiens"</note> proportion. It is given a more specific name based on those parts: if those two extra parts of the smaller number are thirds, it is called the **super-two-third** proportion <note>original: "superbipartiens tertias"</note>, such as 5 to 3. If, however, they are fifths, it is called the **super-two-fifth** proportion <note>original: "superbipartiens quintas"</note>, such as 7 to 5. If they are sevenths, it is called **super-two-seventh**, such as 9 to 7. 

When that extra portion by which the larger number exceeds the smaller shows three parts of that smaller number, it is a **super-three-part** <note>original: "supertripartiens"</note> proportion. It is named more specifically thus: if those three extra parts of the smaller number are fourths, it is called the **super-three-fourth** proportion <note>original: "supertripartiens quartas"</note>, such as 7 to 4. If they are fifths, it is called **super-three-fifth**, such as 8 to 5. If they are sevenths, it is **super-three-seventh**, such as 10 to 7. 

When that difference between the terms contains four equal parts of the smaller number, it is called a **super-four-part** <note>original: "superquatripartiens"</note> proportion. It also has a more specific name: for example, when each of those four parts is a fifth of the smaller number, it is called the **super-four-fifth** proportion <note>original: "superquatripartiens quintas"</note>, such as 9 to 5. If those four parts are sevenths of the smaller number, it is called the **super-four-seventh** proportion, such as 11 to 7 and 22...

<summary>This page concludes the study of sub-superparticular ratios and introduces the "superpartient" genus of musical proportions, explaining how to name ratios like 5:3 or 7:4 based on how many "extra" parts they contain.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, proportions, superpartient genus, superbipartiens, super-two-part, supertripartiens, super-three-part, mensural notation, rhythmic ratios</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical and musical definition of the "superpartient" genus. It moves from general definitions to specific rules about how these ratios are formed based on odd and even numbers, and how they must be simplified to their lowest terms to be correctly categorized.</meta>

<header>FOURTH 64</header>

...to 14. And in the remaining cases, the method of consideration is similar. Furthermore, in this **superpartient** genus, it naturally happens that a proportion acquiring its name from an even number as the partitioner <term>partitioner (partiens): the value by which the larger number exceeds the smaller, forming the "over" part of the ratio.</term> will correspond to partitioned parts <term>partitioned parts (partitae): the fractional divisions of the smaller number (the denominator).</term> of an odd number—that is, the smaller number. For example, if it were **superbipartiens tertias** <note>a 5:3 ratio</note> in this way: 5 to 3. For the partitioning number—that is, the difference between the two—is two; hence it is "superbipartiens" <note>original: "superbipartiens," meaning "two parts over"</note>. The partitioned parts are two-thirds of the odd number three; thus it is named *superbipartiens tertias*.

However, an odd partitioning number follows partitioned parts of an even number: such as 7 to 4, which is **supertripartiens quartas** <note>a 7:4 ratio</note>. For the partitioner is the odd number three (therefore it is called "supertripartiens" <note>three parts over</note>), while the partitioned parts belong to the even number four; from this it is called *supertripartiens quartas*. Frequently, an odd partitioning number also follows parts of an odd number: such as 10 to 7, which is **supertripartiens septimas** <note>a 10:7 ratio</note>. 

But if both the partitioning number and the partitioned parts are considered according to an even number, they do not fall into the superpartient category, but rather into the **epimoric** <term>epimoric: also known as the superparticular genus, where the ratio is (n+1):n, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</term> relationship. For example, 6 to 4 is not called *superbipartiens quartas*, but **sesqualtera** <note>a 3:2 ratio; 6:4 simplifies to 3:2 because the difference (2) is exactly half of the smaller number (4).</note>. And 8 to 6 <note>original: "viii ad viiii," likely an error for "viii ad vi"</note> is not called *superbipartiens sextas*, but **sesquitertia** <note>a 4:3 ratio; 8:6 simplifies to 4:3.</note>. 

Therefore, when the excess or difference between the terms of any proportion is an aliquot part <term>aliquot part: a part that, when multiplied, exactly equals the whole (e.g., 2 is an aliquot part of 6).</term> of the smaller term (even if it contains several such parts), the proportion must be considered in the **superparticular** genus, not the superpartient. For example, 9 to 6: this is not called *supertripartiens sextas*, but **sesqualtera** <note>3:2 ratio</note>, since the larger contains the smaller once plus half of the smaller (which is the same as three-sixths). 

There are infinite species of this superpartient genus. 
1. The first is **superbipartiens** (two-parts-over). 
2. The second is **supertripartiens** (three-parts-over). 
3. The third is **superquatripartiens** (four-parts-over). 
4. The fourth is **superquincupartiens** (five-parts-over). 
5. The fifth is **supersexcupartiens** (six-parts-over). 
And so the process continues to infinity. 

In natural numbers, these first ones are considered like roots in this order:
The **first** is produced from terms between which only one number lies: as 5 to 3, for the number 4 lies between them; or 7 to 5, where 6 lies between them. In this discussion, we call these terms "numbers." 
The **second** skips two numbers: as 7 to 4, for the numbers 5 and 6 are skipped. If it arises from 8 to 5, then 6 and 7 are skipped. 
The **third species** skips three numbers between its own terms: as 9 to 5, where 6, 7, and 8 are in the middle; and 11 to 7, where 8, 9, and 10 are in the middle. 
The **fourth** encloses four numbers between its extreme terms: as 11 to 6, where 7, 8, 9, and 10 are in the middle; and 12 to 7, where 8, 9, 10, and 11 are in the middle. 

And so, whichever rank the species holds in the order, that many numbers will be found between its terms. If it is the first species, one number will be placed between its terms. If the second, two numbers. If the third, three. If the fourth, four. If the fifth, five, and so on. 
Thus:
- The first, **superbipartiens**, encloses one number between its terms.
- The second, **supertripartiens**, shows two numbers contained between its terms.
- The third, **superquatripartiens**, intercepts three numbers between its own terms.
- The fourth, **superquincupartiens**, intercepts four numbers. 
The rest proceed in this simple and natural arrangement of numbers. 

These are called **subalternate species**, for they take on the power of a genus. Hence, some treat them as genera themselves, since within *superbipartiens* there is *superbipartientertias* (5 to 3), another is *superbipartienquintas* (7 to 5), another is *superbipartienseptimas* (9 to 7), and so on.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical rules for identifying superpartient ratios (like 5:3 or 7:4) and distinguishes them from superparticular ratios (like 3:2) through simplification. It also provides a mnemonic method for finding these ratios by looking at how many integers lie between the two numbers of the ratio.</summary>

<keywords>superpartient, superbipartiens, superparticular, sesqualtera, sesquitertia, rhythmic proportions, aliquot parts, musical mathematics, ratios</keywords>

<header>BOOK</header>

...most specific types, which they call dialects <note>original: "dyalecti"; here referring to the specific nomenclature of mathematical ratios</note>. Similarly, among the **supertripartient** <note>ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus three fractional parts, e.g., 1 and 3/x</note>: One is **supertripartiens quartas** <note>1 and 3/4, or 7:4</note>, such as 7 to 4. Another is **supertripartiens quintas** <note>1 and 3/5, or 8:5</note>, such as 8 to 5. Another is **supertripartiens septimas** <note>1 and 3/7, or 10:7</note>, such as 10 to 7. Likewise, among the **superquadripartient** <note>ratios with four parts over the whole, or 1 and 4/x</note>: One is **superquadripartiens quintas** <note>9:5</note>, such as 9 to 5. Another is **superquadripartiens septimas** <note>11:7</note>, such as 11 to 7. Another is **superquadripartiens nonas** <note>13:9</note>, such as 13 to 9. Likewise, among the **superquincupartient** <note>ratios with five parts over the whole, or 1 and 5/x</note>: One is **superquincupartiens sextas** <note>11:6</note>, such as 11 to 6. Another is **superquincupartiens septimas** <note>12:7</note>, such as 12 to 7. Another is **superquincupartiens octavas** <note>13:8</note>, such as 13 to 8. In this same manner, these types of superpartient proportions are organized into genera, producing other species that are, in a sense, infinite. For a genus is an aggregation of many species participating in one and the same nature in different ways. A species, however, is called the specific quantity and quality of a genus. But regarding these, let the sequence be described as follows.

### ¶ On the superbipartiens tertias proportion.

¶ The **superbipartiens tertias** <note>literally "two parts over three," representing a 5:3 ratio</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of the following notes, compared to the smaller number, contains it once and, in addition, a certain portion of that smaller number made of two-thirds, and it is equivalent <note>in time</note> to that smaller number: as 5 to 3, 10 to 6, and 15 to 9. In this proportion, five notes are equivalent and measured against three notes of the same kind, so that each one of the five is diminished by two-fifths of its own proper quantity. It is represented in songs in this way:
->5 10 15<-
->3 6 9<-
And it is undone by the **subsuperbipartientetertias** <note>the inverse ratio, 3:5</note> which is its opposite, as is proven by the present harmony.

### ¶ On the superbipartiens quintas proportion.

¶ The **superbipartiens quintas** <note>literally "two parts over five," representing a 7:5 ratio</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of the following notes, compared to the preceding smaller number, is equivalent to it, containing it once and additionally two-fifths of that smaller number: as 7 to 5, 14 to 10, and 21 to 15. In this proportion, seven notes are made equal to five notes that are similar to them in both name and value, so that each one of the seven is diminished by two-sevenths of its own proper power. It is described in notes in this way: 7, 14, 21. And it is undone by the **subsuperbipartientequintas** proportion, which is its
->5 10 15<- 
->7 14 21<-
opposite, as is easily observed in this harmony.

### On the superbipartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **superbipartient seventh** <note>original: "superbipartiens septimas"; a ratio of 1 plus 2/7, or 9:7</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, is equivalent to it by containing the smaller number once and, additionally, two of its seventh parts: such as 9 to 7, 18 to 14, and 27 to 21. For this proportion equates nine notes to seven notes of the same kind, so that each of the nine notes is diminished by two-ninths of its proper value. In musical compositions, it is represented in this way: 9/7, 18/14, 27/21. It is undone by the **sub-superbipartient seventh** <note>the inverse ratio, 7:9</note>, which is its opposite, as is perceived in this harmony: 7/9, 14/18, 21/27.

<image-desc>Two staves of mensural music notation labeled CANTVS and TENOR. The notation illustrates the 7:4 rhythmic proportion (supertripartiens quartas) discussed in the following text, showing how seven notes in one voice occupy the same time as four in another.</image-desc>

### On the supertripartient fourth proportion.

¶ The **supertripartient fourth** <note>original: "supertripartiens quartas"; a ratio of 1 plus 3/4, or 7:4</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, related to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains the smaller number within itself once and, additionally, three-fourth parts of that smaller number. It is equivalent to it in power: such as 7 to 4, 14 to 8, and 21 to 12. In this proportion, seven notes are equivalent to and measured against four of their own kind: so that each of those seven is diminished by three-sevenths of its quantity. It is represented in songs in this way: 7/4, 14/8, 21/12. However, it is undone by the **sub-supertripartient fourth** <note>the inverse ratio, 4:7</note>, which is its opposite, as is clear here: 4/7, 8/14, 12/21.

### ¶ On the supertripartient fifth proportion.

¶ The **supertripartient fifth** <note>original: "supertripartiens quintas"; a ratio of 1 plus 3/5, or 8:5</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains the smaller number once and additionally three of its equal parts: such as 8 to 5, 16 to 10, and 24 to 15. In this proportion, eight notes are measured against five notes of the same kind, so that each of the eight notes is diminished by three-eighths <note>mathematically, to fit 8 notes into the time of 5, each note must lose 3/8 of its duration</note> of its proper quantity. In musical compositions, it is represented in this way: **8/5, 16/10, 24/15**. Furthermore, it is undone by the **sub-supertripartient fifth** <note>the inverse ratio, 5:8</note> which is its opposite, as is established here: **5/10/15**.

### ¶ On the supertripartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **supertripartient seventh** <note>original: "supertripartiens septimas"; a ratio of 1 plus 3/7, or 10:7</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, related to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains it once and additionally three-sevenths parts of that smaller number, equal to the smaller number itself: such as 10 to 7, 20 to 14, and 30 to 21. For this proportion equates ten notes to seven similar notes in the measure of time <note>term: "temporis mensura"; the underlying beat or tactus</note>, so that each of the ten notes is diminished by three-tenths of its value. It is described in the notes in this way: **10/20/30**. Moreover, it is undone by the **sub-supertripartient seventh** <note>the inverse ratio, 7:10</note> which is its opposite, like this: **7/14/21**.

### ¶ On the superquatripartient fifth proportion.

¶ The **superquatripartient fifth** <note>original: "superquatripartiens quintas"; a ratio of 1 plus 4/5, or 9:5</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains the smaller number once and additionally four of its fifth parts: such as 9 to 5, 18 to 10, and 27 to 15. For this proportion measures nine notes against five notes of the same kind, so that each of the nine notes is diminished by four-ninths <note>To fit 9 notes into the time normally occupied by 5, each note's duration is reduced by 4/9 of its original value.</note> of its proper quantity. In musical compositions, it is represented in this way: **9, 18, 27**. Furthermore, it is undone by the **sub-superquatripartient fifth** <note>the inverse ratio, 5:9</note> which is its opposite, as is shown by this harmony: **5, 10, 15**.

### ¶ On the superquatripartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **superquatripartient seventh** <note>original: "superquatripartiens septimas"; a ratio of 1 plus 4/7, or 11:7</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, refers to the smaller number once and additionally four of its seventh parts: such as 11 to 7, 22 to 14, and 33 to 21. In this proportion, eleven notes are equivalent to seven notes of the same kind, so that each of the eleven is diminished by four-elevenths <note>To fit 11 notes into the time of 7, each note loses 4/11 of its value.</note> of its proper value. It is represented in musical compositions in this way: **11, 22, 33**. Likewise, it is undone by the **sub-superquatripartient seventh** <note>the inverse ratio, 7:11</note> which is its opposite, as the present harmony proves: **7, 14, 21**.

### ¶ On the superquatripartient ninth proportion.

¶ The **superquatripartient ninth** <note>original: "superquadripartientenonas"; a ratio of 1 plus 4/9, which equals 13:9</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once and additionally four of its ninth parts, and is made equal to it: such as 13 to 9, 26 to 18, and 39 to 27. For this proportion measures thirteen notes against nine notes of the same kind, such that each of those thirteen notes is diminished by four-thirteenths <note>In this ratio, thirteen notes must be played in the time usually taken by nine. Therefore, each note loses 4/13 of its standard duration to fit the timeframe.</note> of its own quantitative value. In musical notation, it is represented in this way: **13, 26, 39**. It is undone, however, by the **sub-superquatripartient ninth** <note>The inverse ratio (9:13), which returns the music to its original tempo.</note> which is its opposite, as shown here by **9, 18, 27**.

### ¶ On the superquinquepartient sixth proportion.

¶ The **superquinquepartient sixth** <note>original: "superquincupartientesextas"; a ratio of 1 plus 5/6, which equals 11:6</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once, and additionally a certain portion made of five-sixth parts of that smaller number: such as 11 to 6, 22 to 12, and 33 to 18. In this proportion, eleven notes are equivalent to and measured against six notes that are similar in name and quantity: so that every single one of those eleven notes is diminished by five-elevenths <note>To fit 11 notes into the space of 6, each note is shortened by 5/11 of its original value.</note> of its proper quantity. It is described in songs in this way: **11, 22, 33**. Likewise, it is undone by the **sub-superquinquepartient sixth** proportion, its opposite, which the present harmony declares as **9, 18, 27**. <note>The text here lists 9, 18, 27, though the mathematical logic and the Tenor image suggest the inverse of 11:6 should be 6, 12, 18.</note>

### ¶ On the superquinquepartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **superquinquepartient seventh** <note>original: "superquincupartienteseptimas"; a ratio of 1 plus 5/7, which equals 12:7</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, related to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once and additionally five-sevenths of that smaller number: such as 12 to 7, 24 to 14, and 36 to 21. This proportion makes twelve notes equal to seven of the same kind: so that each of those twelve is diminished by five-twelfths of its proper quantity. And it is described in songs in this way: **12, 24, 36**. It is undone, however, by the **sub-superquinquepartient seventh** which is its opposite, **7, 14, 21**, as is established here.

### ¶ On the supersexpartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **supersexpartient seventh** <note>original: "supersexcupartienteseptimas"; a ratio of 1 plus 6/7, which equals 13:7</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains that smaller number once and additionally six of its seven parts: such as 13 to 7, 26 to 14, and 39 to 21. In this proportion, thirteen notes are equivalent to and measured against seven notes of the same kind: so that each of those thirteen notes is diminished by six-thirteenths <note>When 13 notes occupy the time of 7, each individual note is shortened by 6/13 of its original value.</note> of its power and quantity. In musical compositions, it is represented in this way: **13, 26, 39**. ### ¶ On the sub-superpartient genus and its species. Chapter VIII.

The **sub-superpartient** genus, which is the third type of minor inequality <note>original: "minoris inaequalitatis"; this category describes ratios where the first number is smaller than the second, effectively slowing the music down.</note> and is the opposite of the superpartient genus, occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once, and additionally some part of it made of several smaller parts, and these smaller parts equal the larger in power. Indeed, the sub-alternate species of this genus are: the **sub-superbipartient**, the **sub-supertripartient**, the **sub-superquadripartient**, the **sub-superquinquepartient**, and so on. Its most specific species, however, are the **sub-superbipartient third** <note>A ratio of 3:5</note>, the **sub-superbipartient fifth** <note>A ratio of 5:7</note>, and the **sub-superbipartient seventh** <note>A ratio of 7:9</note>. The same sub-

In these "sub" proportions, a smaller number of notes is played in the time of a larger number, resulting in an augmentation or slowing down of the rhythmic value of each note.</meta>

### ¶ On the sub-superbipartient third proportion.

¶ The **sub-superbipartient third** <note>original: "subsuperbipartietertias"; a ratio of 3:5</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once, with an additional part consisting of two-third parts of that smaller number; and they are equal to the larger number: such as 3 to 5, 6 to 10, 9 to 15, and so on. In this proportion, three notes are equivalent to and measured against five notes similar to them in both name and quantity: so that each of those three notes receives an augmentation of two-thirds of its own quantity. And it is represented in songs in this way: <insert>3 6 9 / 5 10 15</insert>. For it is undone by the **superbipartient seventh** <note>original: "superbipartienteseptimas"; The text likely contains a printer's error here, as the mathematical opposite of a 3:5 ratio is the superbipartient third (5:3), not the seventh.</note> immediately following it, which is its opposite, as is clearly perceived in this harmony.

### ¶ On the sub-supertripartient fourth proportion.

¶ The **sub-supertripartient fourth** <note>original: "Subsupertripartiensquartas"; a ratio of 4:7</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once, with an additional three-fourth parts of that smaller number; such as 4 to 7, 8 to 13 <note>Likely a historical printer's error or OCR misread for 14, as the ratio 4:7 doubles to 8:14.</note>, and 12 to 21. This proportion makes four notes equal to seven notes of the same kind, so that each of those four notes is increased <note>term: "augmented"; in music theory, this means the duration of the note is lengthened.</note> by three-quarters of its own value. It is represented in notes in this way: **4 8 12**. And it is cancelled by the **supertripartient fourth** <note>original: "suptripartienteqrtas"; a ratio of 7:4</note>, which is its opposite, as is shown in this harmony: **7 14 21**.

The notation includes proportional numbers (4/7 in the first section and 7/4 in the second) indicating a slowing down and then a returning to the original tempo.</image-desc>

### ¶ On the sub-supertripartient fifth proportion.

¶ The **sub-supertripartient fifth** <note>original: "Subsupertripartiensqntas"; a ratio of 5:8</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once, with an additional three-fifth parts of that smaller number: such as 5 to 8, 10 to 16, and 15 to 24. In this proportion, five notes are equivalent to and measured against eight notes of the same kind: so that each of those notes receives an increase of three-fifths of its own quantity. It is described in notes in this way: **5 10 15**. And it is cancelled by the **supertripartient fifth** <note>original: "supertripartientequintas"; a ratio of 8:5</note>, its opposite, as is evident here: **8 16 24**.

It features mensural notation with the proportional numbers 5 and 8 used to indicate the rhythmic shift where 5 notes are played in the time of 8.</image-desc>

BOOK [FOUR]

# On the sub-superquadripartient fifth proportion.

¶ The **sub-superquadripartient fifth** <note>original: "Subsuperquadripartienſquintas"; a ratio of 5:9</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding ones, is contained within it once along with four-fifths of its own parts: such as 5 to 9, 10 to 18, 15 to 27, and so on. This proportion makes five notes equal in duration to nine notes of the same kind; in this way, each of those five notes is increased <note>term: "augmentation"; in this context, it means the physical note is held longer, slowing the tempo.</note> by four-fifths of its own value. It is written in musical compositions in this way: **5 10 15**. And it is cancelled by the **superquadripartient fifth** <note>original: "superquadripartientequintas"; a ratio of 9:5</note>, which is its opposite, as is clear from this example: **9 18 27**.

<margin>CANTUS</margin>
<margin>TENOR</margin>

# On the sub-superquadripartient seventh proportion.

¶ The **sub-superquadripartient seventh** <note>original: "Subsuperquadripartienſſeptimas"; a ratio of 7:11</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, relative to the larger number of preceding notes, is contained within it once and, in addition, four of its seventh parts: such as 7 to 11, 14 to 22, and 21 to 33. In this proportion, seven notes are equivalent to and counted alongside eleven similar notes; thus, each of those seven increases by four-seventh parts of its own value. It is represented in songs in this way: **7 14 21**. And it is cancelled by the **superquadripartient seventh** <note>original: "superq̃tripartienteſeptimas"; likely a scribal contraction for superquadripartiente, a ratio of 11:7</note>, its opposite, as proven by this harmony: **11 22 33**.

<margin>CANTUS</margin>
<margin>TENOR</margin>

¶ However, we leave the remaining sub-altern and specific types of this genus to be considered by the diligence of musicians.

# On the multiple superparticular genus and its species. Chapter 9.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'M' depicting a scholar or cleric seated in a high-backed chair, symbolizing the authority of the teacher.</image-desc>
The **multiple superparticular** <note>original: "Multiplex ſuperparticulare"; a "compound" proportion where one number contains another multiple times plus one single part (e.g., 2 1/2 or 5:2).</note> genus—which is the fourth kind of "greater inequality" and is composed of the first two types—occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it several times plus one of its fractional parts. There are infinite species of this genus to be considered at various levels. The first level proceeds from the first multiple genus...

<summary>This page explains the 5:9 and 7:11 musical proportions, which slow down the tempo by stretching fewer notes across a longer duration, and introduces the complex "multiple superparticular" genus of ratios.</summary>
<keywords>sub-superquadripartient fifth, sub-superquadripartient seventh, ratio 5:9, ratio 7:11, multiple superparticular genus, mensural notation, Cantus, Tenor, musical augmentation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "multiple superparticular" genus from the previous page. Having established that this genus combines multiples (like double or triple) with fractional parts (like a half or a third), the author here categorizes the various "degrees" of these proportions before providing specific musical examples for the ratios 5:2 and 7:3.</meta>

<header>BOOK FOUR</header>

...superparticulars <note>term: "superparticular"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note> according to their natural arrangement, mixed in, such as **Double sesquialtera** (5 to 2), **Double sesquitertia** (7 to 3), **Double sesquiquarta** (9 to 4), **Double sesquiquinta** (11 to 5), and so forth. The second degree is conceived from the second multiple <note>In this context, the "second multiple" refers to triple (3x), as the first was double (2x).</note> of all the naturally arranged superparticulars: such as **Triple sesquialtera** (7 to 2), **Triple sesquitertia** (10 to 3), **Triple sesquiquarta** (13 to 4), **Triple sesquiquinta** (16 to 5), and so forth. The third degree joins all the relations of the superparticular genus to the third multiple [quadruple]: such as **Quadruple sesquialtera** (9 to 2), **Quadruple sesquitertia** (13 to 3), **Quadruple sesquiquarta** (17 to 4), **Quadruple sesquiquinta** (21 to 5), and so forth. In the fourth degree, individual superparticulars are attached to the fourth multiple [quintuple]: such as **Quintuple sesquialtera** (11 to 2), **Quintuple sesquitertia** (16 to 3), **Quintuple sesquiquarta** (21 to 4), **Quintuple sesquiquinta** (26 to 5), and so forth. And thus the degrees of this genus are infinite, and the species of the degrees are infinite.

### ¶ On the double sesquialtera proportion.

¶ The **double sesquialtera** <note>original: "dupla sesquialtera"; a ratio of 5:2</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains it twice and additionally a half part of itself, being equivalent to it in power and measure of time: such as **5 to 2**, **10 to 4**, **15 to 6**, and so on. In this proportion, five notes are equivalent and measured against two notes of their own kind in name and quantity; thus, each of those five notes is diminished <note>term: "diminution"; in music theory, this means the notes are performed faster so that more notes fit into the same span of time.</note> by three-fifths of its own quantity. For it is written in musical compositions in this way: **5 10 15**. And it is cancelled by the **sub-double sesquialtera** <note>original: "subdupla sesquialtera"; a ratio of 2:5</note> which is its opposite, as is perceived in this harmony: **2 4 6**.

<image-desc>Three musical staves displaying a polyphonic example in mensural notation. The first stave is labeled CANTUS to its right and contains diamond-shaped semibreves grouped in threes. Above the notes are the numbers 5, 10, and 15, while below the stave are the numbers 2, 4, and 6, illustrating the 5:2 ratio (dupla sesquialtera). The second and third staves are for the TENOR part (labeled to the right of the second stave), providing a harmonic accompaniment to the rhythmic proportion demonstrated in the Cantus.</image-desc>

### ¶ On the double sesquitertia proportion.

¶ The **double sesquitertia** <note>original: "dupla sesquitertia"; a ratio of 7:3</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding notes, contains it twice and additionally a third part of itself, being equivalent to it in power: such as **7 to 3**, **14 to 6**, **21 to 9**, and so on. This proportion makes seven notes equal to three notes of their own kind; thus, each of those seven notes is diminished by four-sevenths of its value. It is represented in musical compositions in this way: **7 14 21**. And it is cancelled by the **sub-double sesquitertia** <note>original: "subdupla sesquitertia"; a ratio of 3:7</note> which is its opposite, as is perceived in this harmony: **3 6 9**.

<summary>This page defines the complex "multiple superparticular" genus of musical proportions and provides detailed explanations and notation examples for the 5:2 and 7:3 ratios. These proportions dictate how many notes of a certain value must be played in the time usually allotted to a smaller number, effectively speeding up the tempo.</summary>
<keywords>multiple superparticular, double sesquialtera, double sesquitertia, rhythmic proportions, diminution, mensural notation, music theory, 5:2 ratio, 7:3 ratio</keywords>

## ¶ On the double sesquiquarta proportion.

¶ The **double sesquiquarta** <note>original: "dupla sesquiquarta"; a ratio of 9:4</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it twice and additionally its fourth part: such as 9 to 4, 18 to 8, 27 to 12, and so forth. In this proportion, nine notes are equal to and measured against four notes of the same kind in quantity and time measure; thus, each of these nine notes is diminished by five-ninths of its proper quantity <note>If 9 notes occupy the time of 4, each note is 4/9 its original length; thus, it has "lost" 5/9 of its value.</note>. In musical notation, it is represented in this way:

| 9 | 18 | 27 |
|---|----|----|
| 4 | 8  | 12 |

And it is undone by the **subdouble sesquiquarta** <note>original: "subdupla sesquiquarta"; the inverse ratio of 4:9</note> which is its opposite, as is established in this harmony.

## ¶ On the double sesquiquinta proportion.

¶ The **double sesquiquinta** <note>original: "dupla sesquiquinta"; a ratio of 11:5</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, includes it twice and additionally its fifth part: such as 11 to 5, 22 to 10, and 33 to 15. For this proportion equates eleven notes to five of the same kind in power and time measure; thus, each of these eleven notes is diminished by six-elevenths of its proper quantity. It is described in songs in this way:

| 11 | 22 | 33 |
|----|----|----|
| 5  | 10 | 15 |

And it is undone by the **subdouble sesquiquinta** <note>original: "subdupla sesquiquinta"; the inverse ratio of 5:11</note> which is its opposite, as is evident here.

### ¶ On the triple sesquialtera proportion.

¶ The **triple sesquialtera** <note>original: "tripla sesquialtera"; a ratio of 7:2</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it three times and additionally one-half part of it: such as 7 to 2, 14 to 4, 21 to 6, and so forth. In this proportion, seven notes are equivalent to and measured against two notes of the same kind; thus, each of those seven notes is diminished by five-sevenths of its quantity <note>If 7 notes occupy the time of 2, each note is only 2/7 of its original length; therefore, it has "lost" or been diminished by 5/7 of its value.</note>. It is represented in notes in this way: 

| 7 | 14 | 21 |
|---|----|----|
| 2 | 4  | 6  | 

Furthermore, it is undone by the **subtriple sesquialtera** <note>original: "subtripla sesquialtera"; the inverse ratio of 2:7</note> which is its opposite, as is evident here.

### ¶ On the triple sesquitertia proportion.

¶ The **triple sesquitertia** <note>original: "tripla sesquitertia"; a ratio of 10:3</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it three times and additionally its third part: such as 10 to 3, 20 to 6, and 30 to 9. For this proportion equates and measures ten notes against three of the same kind: so that each of those ten notes suffers a loss of seven-tenths of its value <note>In a 10:3 ratio, each note is 3/10 its original length, meaning it has lost 7/10 of its duration.</note>. It is described in songs <note>original: "cantilenis"</note> in this way: 

| 10 | 20 | 30 |
|----|----|----|
| 3  | 6  | 9  | 

And it is undone by the **subtriple sesquitertia** <note>original: "subtripla sesquitertia"; the inverse ratio of 3:10</note> which is its opposite, following it immediately as the present harmony <note>original: "concentus"</note> shows.

¶ The **triple sesquiquarta** <note>original: "tripla sesquiquarta"; a ratio of 13:4</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it three times and additionally a fourth part of it: such as 13 to 4, 26 to 8, 39 to 12, and so forth. In this proportion, thirteen notes are equivalent to and measured against four notes of the same kind; thus, each of those thirteen notes is diminished by nine-thirteenths of its proper quantity <note>If 13 notes occupy the time of 4, each note is only 4/13 of its original length; therefore, it has been reduced or "diminished" by 9/13 of its value.</note>. | 13 | 26 | 39 |

### ¶ On the quadruple sesquialtera proportion.

¶ The **quadruple sesquialtera** <note>original: "quadrupla sesquialtera"; a ratio of 9:2</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it four times and additionally one-half part of it: such as 9 to 2, 18 to 4, and 27 to 6. Indeed, this proportion makes nine notes equal to two of the same kind, so that each of those nine is diminished by seven-ninths of its value <note>If 9 notes occupy the time of 2, each note is only 2/9 of its original length; it has been diminished by 7/9 of its value.</note>. | 9 | 18 | 27 |

| 2 | 4  | 6  |

### ¶ On the quintuple sesquiquarta proportion.

¶ The **quintuple sesquiquarta** <note>original: "quintuplasesquiquarta"; a ratio of 5 1/4 to 1, or 21:4</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it five times and additionally a fourth part of it: such as 21 to 4, 42 to 8, 63 to 12, and so forth. In this proportion, twenty-one notes are equivalent to and are measured against <gloss>measured against</gloss> four notes of the same kind: so that each of those twenty-one is diminished by seventeen twenty-first <note>When 21 notes are played in the time of 4, each note retains only 4/21 of its original duration; thus, it loses 17/21 of its value.</note> parts of its own quantity. It is represented in notation in this manner:

| 21 | 42 | 63 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 4 | 8 | 12 |

And it is cancelled <note>original: "destruitur" (literally 'destroyed'); this indicates the return to the standard tempo by applying the inverse proportion</note> by the **sub-quintuple sesquiquarta** opposed to it, as is clear here:

| 4 | 8 | 12 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

<image-desc>A second musical example for CANTUS and TENOR. The staves show complex mensural notation, illustrating how the 21:4 proportion is resolved or contrasted within a polyphonic context. The Tenor provides a steady rhythmic pulse against which the faster notes in the Cantus are measured.</image-desc>

TENOR

¶ We leave the remaining steps of this genus, along with their species, to be considered by the diligent care of musicians through a similar process.

### ¶ On the sub-multiple superparticular genus and its species. Chapter 10.

The **sub-multiple superparticular** <term>submultiplex superparticulare: the category of proportions where a smaller number of notes is played in the time of a larger number, using the "sub-" (inverse) of the multiple-plus-a-fraction ratios</term> genus, which as a matter of minor inequality <note>In "minor inequality," the following number is smaller than the preceding one, resulting in a slowing down of the music.</note> is the opposite of the multiple superparticular, occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding ones, is contained within it several times along with one of its aliquot parts, being equivalent to that larger number.

The species of this genus are infinite, consisting of various steps. The **first step** is considered through the sub-double <note>ratio 1:2</note> combined with individual superparticulars. Its first species is the **sub-double sesquialtera** <note>ratio 2:5</note>; the second is the **sub-double sesquitertia** <note>ratio 3:7</note>; the third is the **sub-double sesquiquarta** <note>ratio 4:9</note>; the fourth is the **sub-double sesquiquinta** <note>ratio 5:11</note>, and so on.

The **second step** joins individual superparticulars to the sub-triple in this order: **sub-triple sesquialtera** <note>ratio 2:7</note>, **sub-triple sesquitertia** <note>ratio 3:10</note>, **sub-triple sesquiquarta** <note>ratio 4:13</note>, **sub-triple sesquiquinta** <note>ratio 5:16</note>, and so on.

The **third step** mixes individual superparticulars with a third multiple, namely the sub-quadruple: such as **sub-quadruple sesquialtera** <note>ratio 2:9</note>, **sub-quadruple sesquitertia** <note>ratio 3:13</note>, **sub-quadruple sesquiquarta** <note>ratio 4:17</note>, **sub-quadruple sesquiquinta** <note>ratio 5:21</note>, and so forth. Indeed, anyone can easily consider the remaining steps by following this same process.

<summary>This page defines the complex 21:4 rhythmic proportion and introduces the "sub-" genus, where fewer notes are played over a longer duration, creating a slowing effect.</summary>
<keywords>quintuple sesquiquarta, sub-multiple superparticular genus, mensural notation, musical proportions, rhythmic diminution, 21:4 ratio, 16th-century music theory</keywords>

### ¶ On the sub-double sesquialter proportion.

¶ The **sub-double sesquialter** <note>original: "subduplasesquialtera"; a ratio of 2:5. In music theory, this means 2 notes are played in the time normally allotted to 5, making the music significantly slower.</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger number of preceding ones, is contained in it twice and additionally its half part: such as 2 to 5, 4 to 10, and 6 to 15. In this proportion, two notes are equivalent to and are measured against <gloss>commensurated with</gloss> five notes of the same kind: so that each of those two notes receives an increase of three half-parts <note>When 2 notes take the time of 5, each note is 2.5 times its original length. This equals the original value (1) plus an increase of 1.5 (three halves).</note> of its own quantity. It is represented in notation in this manner:

->2 4 6<-
->5 10 15<-

And it is cancelled <note>original: "destruitur" (destroyed); this refers to the resolution of the proportion, returning the music to its original tempo</note> by the **double sesquialter** <note>ratio 5:2</note> opposed to it, following immediately after it, as is perceived in this harmony.

### ¶ On the sub-double sesquitertian proportion.

### ¶ On the sub-quadruple sesquitertia proportion.

¶ The **sub-quadruple sesquitertia** <note>original: "subquadruplasesquitertia"; a ratio of 3:13.</note> proportion occurs when the smaller number of following notes, compared to the larger preceding number, is contained within it four times plus a third part: such as 3 to 13, 6 to 26, 9 to 39, and so forth. For this proportion makes three notes equal in power and measurement of time to thirteen notes of the same kind: so that each of those three notes receives an increase of ten third-parts <note>If 3 notes are stretched to the time of 13, each note is 13/3 (or 4 and 1/3) times its original length. The text "fourteen" may be a printer's error for "thirteen" (tredecim).</note>...

### ¶ On the multiple-superpartient genus and its species. Chapter XI.

The **multiple-superpartient** <note>original: "Multiplexsuperpartiens"; a complex ratio where a larger number contains a smaller number multiple times plus more than one fractional part (e.g., 8:3 contains 3 twice, plus 2/3).</note> genus is the fifth genus of greater inequality, which exists when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it several times, along with several of its fractional parts. Indeed, there are infinite levels in this genus, encompassing all the multiples and superpartients that come before. 

For the **first level** is formed from the first multiple [double] connected to individual superpartients: such as **double-superbipartient**, **double-supertripartient**, **double-superquadripartient**, and so on. These are called sub-alternate species, for each is converted into the force of a genus with the double-superbipartients. One is **double-superbipartient-thirds** <note>ratio 8:3</note> (such as 8 to 3); another is **double-superbipartient-fifths** <note>ratio 12:5</note> (such as 12 to 5); another is **double-superbipartient-sevenths** <note>ratio 16:7</note> (such as 16 to 7); another is **double-superbipartient-ninths** <note>ratio 20:9</note> (such as 20 to 9), and so forth. Truly, they call these the "most specific" species. 

In the other levels, a similar mode of consideration is applied. The **second level** joins individual superpartients to the second multiple [triple]. Its sub-alternate and most specific order is: **triple-superbipartient-thirds** <note>ratio 11:3</note> (such as 11 to 3); **triple-superbipartient-fifths** <note>ratio 17:5</note> (17 to 5); **triple-superbipartient-sevenths** <note>ratio 23:7</note> (23 to 7), and so on. 

The **third level** adds the third multiple [quadruple] to individual superpartients. Its order is: **quadruple-superbipartient-thirds** <note>ratio 14:3</note> (such as 14 to 3); **quadruple-superbipartient-fifths** <note>ratio 22 to 5</note> (22 to 5); **quadruple-superbipartient-sevenths** <note>ratio 30:7</note> (30 to 7). 

In the **fourth level**, individual superbipartients "applaud" <note>original: "applaudunt"; here meaning "correspond to" or "are grouped with".</note> the fourth multiple [quintuple] in this order: **quintuple-superbipartient-thirds** <note>ratio 17:3</note> (such as 17 to 3); **quintuple-superbipartient-fifths** <note>ratio 27:5</note> (27 to 5); **quintuple-superbipartient-sevenths** <note>ratio 37 to 7</note> (37 to 7). Such a process is generally considered by applying sub-alternate superpartients to individual multiples: as **double-superbipartient**, **double-supertripartient**, and **double-superquadripartient**; and in the rest, a diverse consideration is fitting, about which we must speak individually.

### ->¶ On the triple-superbipartient-third proportion.<-

¶ The **triple-superbipartient-third** <note>ratio 11:3, as 11 contains 3 three times (9) plus 2/3 of 3.</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller preceding ones, is calculated to contain it three times and furthermore two of its third parts: such as 11 to 3, 22 to 6, 33 to 9, and so on. In this proportion, eleven notes are equivalent to and measured against three of their same kind: so that each of those eleven is diminished by eight-elevenths of its own quantity. For it is written in notes in this way: **11 22 33**. And it is canceled out by the **sub-triple-superbipartient-third** opposed to it: as arranged in this harmony: **3 6 9**.

### ->¶ On the triple-superbipartient-fifth proportion.<-

¶ The **triple-superbipartient-fifth** <note>The ratio 17:5. The name indicates the larger number contains the smaller number three times (triple) plus two-fifths (super-bipartient-fifth) of the smaller number. (3 × 5) + 2 = 17.</note> proportion occurs when the larger number of following notes, compared to the smaller number of preceding ones, contains it three times and additionally two of its fifth parts: such as 17 to 5, 34 to 10, 51 to 15, and so on. In this proportion, seventeen notes are equivalent to and measured against five of their own kind: so that each of those seventeen is diminished by twelve-seventeenths of its value <note>Because 17 notes now occupy the time previously held by 5, each note is significantly sped up, retaining only 5/17ths of its original duration.</note>. It is written in notes in this manner: **17 34 51**. And it is canceled out <note>original: "destruitur" (destroyed); this refers to the proportional sign that returns the music to its original tempo.</note> by the **sub-triple-superbipartient-fifth** opposed to it: such as **5 10 15**, as the present harmony <note>original: "concentus" (a singing together or concord)</note> proves.

This proportion equates fifteen notes to four of their same kind: so that each of those fifteen is diminished by eleven-fifteenths of its own quantity. It is represented in songs in this manner: **15 30 45**. And it is canceled out by the **sub-triple-supertripartient-fourth** opposed to it, as is established here: **4 8 12**.

¶ The diligent investigator will, by similar consideration, search out the remaining subaltern <note>Subaltern: secondary or subordinate categories in the logical hierarchy of the time.</note> and most specific types of this genus for themselves.

### ¶ On the sub-multiple-super-partient genus and its species. Chapter XII.

**T**he **sub-multiple-super-partient** <note>This term describes a complex ratio where a smaller number of notes replaces a larger number. The "sub-" indicates the notes will be lengthened (augmentation).</note> genus, which is the fifth type of lesser inequality, is the opposite of the multiple-super-partient. It occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding ones, is contained within that larger number once, along with some portion of it comprising several equal parts. In this case, the smaller number is equivalent to the larger in power and measure of time: so that each individual note of the smaller group receives an increase <note>original: "augmentum"; in proportions of lesser inequality, the notes become longer to fill the same amount of time.</note> from as many particles of its own number as there were notes arranging the difference between those numbers; as also happens in the other genera of lesser inequality.

Likewise, the unique sub-multiplicity encompassing these super-partient relationships distributes infinite degrees of this genus. The **first degree** is derived from the sub-double: such as the **sub-double-super-bipartient**, **sub-double-super-tripartient**, and **sub-double-super-quadripartient**. Moreover, the subaltern species of this kind are those where each passes into the force of a genus: such as the **sub-double-super-bipartient-third** <note>The ratio 3:8. The number 8 contains 3 twice, plus 2 (bipartient) thirds of 3. (2 × 3) + 2 = 8.</note>, the **sub-double-super-bipartient-fifth**, and the **sub-double-super-bipartient-seventh**; these are called the "most specific" species.

The same process of consideration appears in the others as well. The **second degree** arises from the sub-triple, such as the **sub-triple-super-bipartient**, **sub-triple-super-tripartient**, **sub-triple-super-quadripartient**, and so on. The **third degree** is conceived from the sub-quadruple, such as the **sub-quadruple-super-bipartient**, **sub-quadruple-super-tripartient**, and **sub-quadruple-super-quadripartient**. Furthermore, the "most specific" species are placed under these subalterns. Let us arrange their progression in this order.

### ¶ On the sub-double-super-bipartient-third proportion.

¶ The **sub-double-super-bipartient-third** proportion occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding ones, is contained in it twice along with two additional third parts of itself: such as 3 to 8, 6 to 16, 9 to 24, and so forth. In this proportion, three notes are equivalent to and measured against eight of their own kind: so that each one of those three is increased by five-third parts of its proper quantity <note>Because 3 notes now occupy the time previously held by 8, each note is 8/3rds of its original length. It "gains" 5/3rds of its original duration.</note>. For it is described in songs in this manner:

| 3 | 6 | 9 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

And it is canceled out <note>original: "destruitur" (destroyed); returning the tempo to normal.</note> by the **double-super-bipartient-third** opposed to it, as the harmonies <note>original: "concentus"</note> show here:

| 8 | 16 | 24 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

### ¶ On the <term>sub-double-super-two-fifths proportion</term> <note>original: "subduplasuperbipartientequintas". A ratio where a smaller number of notes is equated to a larger number that contains the smaller twice plus two-fifths of it (e.g., 5 to 12).</note>.

¶ The **sub-double-super-two-fifths** proportion occurs when a smaller number of following notes is compared to a larger number of preceding notes, and the smaller is contained twice within the larger, plus two of its fifth parts: such as 5 to 12, 10 to 24, and 15 to 36. For this proportion makes five notes equal in value and time measurement to twelve notes of the same kind; thus, each of these five notes acquires an increase of seven-fifths of its own original value <note>Mathematically, if 5 notes take the time of 12, each note is 2.4 times longer than it was before (1 + 7/5 = 12/5).</note>. It is indicated in notation in this way: **5 10 15**. Furthermore, it is reversed by the **double-super-two-fifths** proportion <note>original: "duplasuperbipartientequintas"</note> which is its opposite, as is shown here by: **12 24 36**.

### ¶ On the <term>sub-double-super-three-fourths proportion</term> <note>original: "subduplasupertripartientequartas". A ratio where a smaller number of notes is equated to a larger number that contains the smaller twice plus three-fourths of it (e.g., 4 to 11).</note>.

¶ The **sub-double-super-three-fourths** proportion occurs when a smaller number of following notes, compared to a larger number of preceding ones, is contained within that larger number twice, and three of its fourth parts besides: such as 4 to 11, 8 to 22, 12 to 33, and so on. In this proportion, four notes are equivalent to and measured against eleven notes of the same kind: so that each one of these four is increased by seven-fourths of its own value <note>11 divided by 4 equals 2.75. Therefore, one original note plus an increase of 1.75 (7/4) equals the new note value.</note>. It is described in songs in this way: **4 8 12**. And it is reversed by the **double-super-three-fourths** proportion <note>original: "duplasupertripartientequartas"</note> which is its opposite, as is perceived in this **harmony** <note>original: "concentu"</note>: **11 22 33**.

¶ However, we leave the remaining relationships of this kind <note>The author refers here to the "sub-multiple-super-partient" genus, a complex category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number multiple times plus a fraction.</note> to the diligence of musicians to be thoroughly examined.

### ¶ On the conjunction of several dissimilar proportions. Chapter XIII.

Diverse proportions following one another lead, by sound consideration, to various relationships of the subsequent notes relative to the preceding ones. For if (for example) a **triple proportion** <note>original: "tripla pportio"; a 3:1 ratio</note> in notes immediately follows a **double proportion** <note>original: "duplam"; a 2:1 ratio</note>, a **sextuple proportion** <note>original: "sexcupla"; a 6:1 ratio</note> will result immediately from the number of notes in that triple proportion compared to the first number of notes—that is, the number that was positioned before the double proportion itself.

This is clearly perceived in these numbers: **1, 2, 6**. For two is double compared to one. Six is triple compared to two. In turn, six creates a sextuple proportion when compared to one. The opposite **sub-sextuple** proportion, following immediately, is proven to cancel it out; the reason for this is the restoration of equality between the outer terms, as defined by these limits: **1, 2, 6, 1**. <note>The author is demonstrating "mathematical cancellation": moving from a value of 1 to 2 (doubling) and then 2 to 6 (tripling) makes the final note six times faster than the first. Reversing this with a 1:6 ratio brings the tempo back to the original starting speed.</note> This is also most clearly perceived in the counting and performance of the notes in the present <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "concentus", referring here to the combined sounding of the musical parts.</note>.

<vocab>proportions, music, triple, double, sextuple, soprano, tenor, relationships, harmony</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the study of individual complex ratios and introduces the mathematical theory of combining different proportions in sequence, demonstrating how a 2:1 ratio followed by a 3:1 ratio results in a 6:1 ratio.</summary>
<keywords>proportions, mathematical music theory, ratio conjunction, triple proportion, double proportion, sextuple proportion, Renaissance notation</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical proportions. While the previous page introduced the concept of combining a double and a triple proportion to create a sextuple ratio, this page expands on the mathematical "chaining" of the superparticular genus (ratios like 3:2, 4:3, etc.) and how they relate back to a foundational number.</meta>

<header>## BOOK TWO</header>

**SOPRANO** <image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff for the Soprano (Cantus) part, featuring diamond-shaped mensural notes. Numerical proportions (2, 3, 2, 10, 6, 2) are written above the staff to indicate shifts in rhythmic timing.</image-desc>

<image-desc>Continuation of the Soprano part's musical notation.</image-desc>

**TENOR** <image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff for the Tenor part, providing the rhythmic foundation against which the Soprano's proportions are measured.</image-desc>

Every proportion, therefore, when considered in relation to the preceding proportions, always relates its notes to the quantity of the preceding notes, so that they are known to correspond to the consideration of the first proportion as if to the foundation of the relationship. This we demonstrated in the final part of the third book of our *Theory of Arithmetic* <note>original: "Theoricæ Arythmeticæ"; Gaffurio likely refers to his own earlier work on the mathematical foundations of music</note> where we laid out these arrangements.

However, the first two species of the **superparticular genus** <note>original: "supparticularis gñis"; a category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one fractional part (e.g., 3:2, 4:3)</note>, namely **sesquialtera** <note>a 3:2 ratio</note> and **sesquitertia** <note>a 4:3 ratio</note>, when following one another in a song <note>original: "cantilena"</note>, relate the subsequent notes to the earlier ones in a **double** <note>2:1</note> relationship. This is perceived in the arrangement of these numbers: **2, 3, 4**. 

When **sesquiquarta** <note>a 5:4 ratio</note> is added on top of these, it measures the subsequent notes against the preceding ones according to a **double sesquialtera** <note>a 5:2 ratio</note> relationship, as established by these numbers: **2, 3, 4, 5**. 

If a **sesquiquinta** <note>a 6:5 ratio</note> is further added, it will produce a **triple proportion** <note>3:1</note> in relation to the first number of notes, as shown by this arrangement: **2, 3, 4, 5, 6**. For the number six fulfills a triple relationship to the number two. 

However, if a **sub-triple proportion** <note>a 1:3 ratio</note> follows, it will undo all the previously arranged proportions, for the equality of the outer numbers returns, as this arrangement proves: **2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 2**. This same process is carried out in the notes, as is clear from this harmony <note>original: "concentu"</note>.

**SOPRANO** <image-desc>Musical notation for the Soprano part, including a specific proportion marked with the number '4'.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains how musical proportions can be "chained" together mathematically, demonstrating how a sequence of ratios (3:2, 4:3, 5:4, 6:5) eventually builds to a triple proportion (6:2) and can be returned to the original value using a sub-triple ratio.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, sesquiquarta, sesquiquinta, arithmetic, mensural notation, Soprano, Tenor, triple proportion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical analysis of musical intervals. While the previous page discussed how the first two superparticular ratios (3:2 and 4:3) combine to form a double (2:1) ratio, this page explores why smaller ratios in the same family (like 4:3 and 5:4) fail to do so without an additional step, and how these mathematical relationships are "canceled out" in musical practice.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR

<header>## FOURTH</header>

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation on five-line staves using white mensural notation. The notes include semibreves, minims, and semiminims, with C-clefs and F-clefs visible on the left side of the staves.</image-desc>

It is furthermore established that two continuous and immediate species of the **superparticular genus** <term>superparticular genus: a category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one single part of it (e.g., 3:2, 4:3, 5:4)</term>—other than the **sesquialtera** <note>a 3:2 ratio</note> and **sesquitertia** <note>a 4:3 ratio</note>, which are the first and larger than the other **epimoric** <note>original: "epimoriis"; another name for superparticular ratios</note> ratios—cannot fulfill a **double proportion** <note>a 2:1 ratio</note>. For when they are arranged with larger numbers than those first two, they must necessarily produce a smaller proportion. For example, if I arrange a **sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note> and a **sesquiquarta** <note>a 5:4 ratio</note> using these numbers—3, 4, 5—the outer terms cannot create a double proportion between themselves. However, if the **sesquiquinta** <note>a 6:5 ratio</note> is added, the outer terms will maintain a double relationship to one another in this manner: 3, 4, 5, 6. For the number six relates to five as a **sesquiquinta**, but to three it holds a double relationship. This relationship is immediately destroyed when the opposing **subduple** <note>a 1:2 ratio, the inverse of double</note> follows it, bringing the outer terms back to equality, as seen in these numbers: 3, 4, 5, 6, 3. For the number three creates a **subduple proportion** <note>original: "subduplam ... proportionem"</note> in relation to six, but it regards the first number three with equality, comparing the later notes to the earlier ones by their number, as is perceived in this harmony:

<image-desc>Three systems of musical notation on five-line staves. Labels "CANTVS" and "TENOR" are printed in the right margin next to the first and second staves of this section.</image-desc>
<margin>SOPRANO</margin>
<margin>TENOR</margin>

This also occurs in this kind of consideration: when any proportion is extinguished by its opposite immediately following it, the remaining preceding proportions that were its members are also extinguished. This is drawn from the previous arrangements; for the **subduple** itself not only destroyed the **double proportion** of the outer terms, but also caused the **sesquitertia**, **sesquiquarta**, and **sesquiquinta** (which are members of that double) to fall silent. In various ways, these continuous relationships of any genus follow one another, which a musical singer can easily observe for themselves in the music itself.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical necessity of chaining specific ratios (3:4:5:6) to achieve a perfect octave (2:1) and describes how these proportions are resolved or "extinguished" by their musical opposites.</summary>
<keywords>superparticular genus, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, sesquiquarta, sesquiquinta, double proportion, subduple, musical mathematics, ratio resolution</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of musical ratios. While the previous page established how basic superparticular ratios like 3:2 and 4:3 combine, this page moves into the practical demonstration of these ratios using a string of numbers (6, 8, 9, 12, 18, 24) to show how they generate the standard musical intervals of the fourth, fifth, and octave, eventually returning to unity.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR

## ¶ On the Proportions that nurture musical consonances.
### Chapter fourteen.

We have decided to explain the progression and nature of those proportions which, by their own proper dimensions, prove the musical consonances. They exist in the **multiple genus** <term>multiple genus: ratios where the larger number is a direct multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 or 3:1</term>: the **Double**, **Triple**, and **Quadruple**, by which the **Octave** <note>original: "Diapason"</note>, **Octave-plus-Fifth** <note>original: "Diapason diapente"; a compound interval of a twelfth</note>, and **Double Octave** <note>original: "Disdiapason"; a fifteenth</note> are brought together. They also exist in the **superparticular genus** <term>superparticular genus: ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term>: the **Sesquialtera**, **Sesquitertia**, and **Sesquioctava**, which demonstrate the **Fifth** <note>original: "Diapenten"</note>, the **Fourth** <note>original: "Diateſſaron"</note>, and the **Whole Tone** <note>original: "tonum"</note>. Although I have spoken of these at greater length in my theoretical works, I wish here to explain them more strictly within the order of musical notation.

Therefore, when you have placed a **sesquioctava** <note>a 9:8 ratio</note> on top of an arranged **sesquitertia** <note>a 4:3 ratio</note>, the outer terms will preserve a **sesquialtera** <note>a 3:2 ratio</note> relationship to each other, as is clear in these numbers: 6, 8, and 9. For the number 8 creates a **sesquitertia** <note>the interval of a Fourth</note> in relation to 6; the number 9 creates a **sesquioctava** <note>the interval of a Tone</note> in relation to 8; and again, 9 leads to a **sesquialtera** <note>the interval of a Fifth</note> proportion in relation to 6.

When a **sesquitertia** is placed above this, it will set the outer terms in a **double** relationship, as shown by these numbers: 6, 8, 9, 12. For the number 12 proves a **sesquitertia** in relation to 9, but a **double** <note>the interval of an Octave</note> in relation to 6. Furthermore, when a **sesquialtera** is added on top, a **triple** consideration of the outer terms will immediately result, which is proved by this arrangement: 6, 8, 9, 12, 18. For the number 18 produces a **sesquialtera** <note>a 3:2 ratio</note> in relation to 12, but a **triple** <note>a 3:1 ratio, the Twelfth</note> in relation to 6.

When you have added a **sesquitertia** to a triple ratio of this kind, you will complete a **quadruple** relationship between the outer terms, as seen here: 6, 8, 9, 12, 18, 24. For the number 24 shows a **sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note> in relation to the number 18, but a **quadruple** <note>4:1 ratio, the Double Octave</note> in relation to 6. Truly, when a **sub-quadruple** <note>a 1:4 ratio</note> (its opposite) is placed below such a quadruple ratio, the quadruple itself and its members immediately vanish, as the subsequent notes are brought back to the first number, which the equality of the outer terms proves. This is evident in these numbers: 6, 8, 9, 12, 18, 24, 6. For the number 6 creates a **sub-quadruple** in relation to 24, but refers back to the first 6 by that very **equality** <note>a 1:1 ratio</note>. This is exactly how the present harmony has arranged it in the notes.

<margin>CANTUS</margin>
<image-desc>Three lines of musical notation in white mensural notation on five-line staves. The music serves as a practical demonstration of the harmonic proportions described in the text. Specific integers are printed below certain notes to indicate proportional values: 6, 8, and 9 on the first staff; 12 and 18 on the second staff; 24 and a concluding 6 on the third staff.</image-desc>

### ¶ On the production of multiple proportions from multiples and superparticulars. Chapter 15.

Finally, we must consider how the order of the species of the **multiple genus** <term>multiple genus: ratios where the larger number is a direct multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 or 3:1</term> is produced from the combination of multiplicity and **superparticularity** <term>superparticular genus: ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once plus one additional part, like 3:2 or 4:3</term>. For the first species of the multiple genus, namely the **double** <note>original: "dupla"; a 2:1 ratio, the musical octave</note>, and the first species of the superparticular, namely the **sesquialtera** <note>original: "sesqualtera"; a 3:2 ratio, the musical fifth</note>, when joined together, lead to the second multiple species in the outer terms, namely the **triple** <note>original: "triplam"; a 3:1 ratio, the musical twelfth</note>, as is clear in these numbers: 1, 2, 3. However, if a **subtriple** <note>original: "subtripla"; a 1:3 ratio</note> is then applied, the proportion is neutralized because the outer terms return to an equal relationship, as seen here: 1, 2, 3, 1. This same principle exists in the values of musical notes according to the measurement of time, as the following harmony demonstrates.

---

<image-desc>Three staves of mensural music notation. The top staff is labeled "CANTVS" on the right. The second and third staves are labeled "TENOR" on the right. The notation includes various rhythmic values and proportions indicated by numbers like 3 and 2 on the staves.</image-desc>

->CANTUS<-

¶ Indeed, the second species of the multiple genus, namely the **triple** <note>3:1</note>, and the second superparticular species, which is the **sesquitertia** <note>original: "sesquitertia"; a 4:3 ratio, the musical fourth</note>, consequently lead to the third species of the multiple genus, namely the **quadruple** <note>original: "quadruplam"; a 4:1 ratio, the double octave</note>, as shown in this series of numbers: 1, 3, 4. When arranged in musical notes, these proportions guide the subsequent notes in a quadruple relationship to those preceding them; this is immediately canceled out if a **subquadruple** <note>original: "subquadrupla"; a 1:4 ratio</note> is placed opposite it, as the present harmony proves.

<summary>This page explains how compound musical intervals are mathematically constructed by adding a simple multiple ratio (like an octave) to a superparticular ratio (like a fifth or fourth) to create larger intervals like the twelfth and fifteenth.</summary>
<keywords>multiple genus, superparticular genus, double ratio, triple ratio, quadruple ratio, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, musical proportions, mensural notation</keywords>

¶ Moreover, the third species of the **multiple genus** <term>multiple genus: a category of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1)</term>, which is the **quadruple** <note>original: "quadrupla"; a 4:1 ratio</note>, and the third species of the **superparticular** <term>superparticular genus: ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one additional part (e.g., 3:2, 4:3)</term>, which is the **sesquiquarta** <note>original: "sesquiquarta"; a 5:4 ratio</note>, when joined together, create the fourth multiple, namely the **quintuple** <note>original: "quintuplam"; a 5:1 ratio</note>. This is proven by the arrangement of these terms: 1, 4, 5. They achieve the same effect in songs, for the following notes relate to the preceding ones in a quintuple proportion; this arrangement is neutralized by the sequence of the opposing **subquintuple** <note>original: "subquintuplæ"; a 1:5 ratio</note>, as is evident in this harmony.

->CANTVS<-
->TENOR<-

<image-desc>Three more staves of mensural music notation, similar to the first set. The top two staves are labeled "CANTVS" and "TENOR" respectively. The notation includes C-clefs and rhythmic figures consistent with the discussion of musical proportions in the surrounding text.</image-desc>

¶ A similar consideration applies to the derivation of further multiple proportions from multiples and superparticulars. For the fourth species of the multiple [the quintuple, 5:1] and the fourth species of the superparticular [the sesquiquinta, 6:5] lead to the fifth multiple [the sextuple, 6:1], as is proven by these numbers: 1, 5, 6. Truly, the fifth species of the multiple genus [6:1] and the fifth superparticular [7:6] produce the sixth multiple [7:1], which is established by these numbers: 1, 6, 7. Furthermore, the sixth species of the multiple genus [7:1] and the sixth superparticular [8:7] prove the seventh multiple [8:1] by these numbers: 1, 7, 8. And the rest are considered in the same way according to the natural progression of numbers.

You have now, most illustrious reader, our commentaries on the **Practice of Music** <term>Musicæ exercitatio: likely referring to the author’s practical manual on music, the *Practica Musice* (1496)</term>, elaborated by me with perhaps no less talent and industry than you have long desired in silent longing. Indeed, when you have become weary from reading our books on **Theory** <term>Theoricæ: referring to the author's earlier work, the *Theorica musice* (1492), which dealt with the abstract mathematical side of music</term>, these may serve as a kind of feast...

<summary>This page concludes the mathematical derivation of musical proportions, showing how multiple and superparticular ratios combine to create ever-higher multiples. It ends with a personal address to the reader, positioning the work as a practical companion to the author's previous theoretical writings.</summary>
<keywords>multiple genus, superparticular genus, quintuple proportion, sextuple proportion, mensural notation, Franchinus Gaffurius, music theory, mathematical ratios</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Book Four of Franchinus Gaffurius’s "Practica Musicae." Following the mathematical proofs of musical proportions discussed on the previous page, the author here offers a closing apology and defense of his work, followed by the printer's registry and colophon.</meta>

<header>FOURTH [BOOK]</header>

...as if medicinal waters <note>original: "acidulæ"; literally "sour waters," often referring to mineral springs believed to have healing or digestive properties</note> might refresh and revive your stomach. I believed I could not be free of fault if, having taught the art of music and opened its more secret inner-chambers, I had remained silent regarding this part which is called **Practice** <note>original: "Pratica"; the application of musical theory to performance and composition</note>, which consists of and is perfected in the very act of music-making. But if any error should be detected in so great a work, let learned men not be indignant on that account; for I leave my writings to be weighed and corrected by any most skilled mathematician or musician. Truly, if someone is offended by words that are either insufficiently Latin or too modern, I would wish him to attribute it to the subject matter we are treating. He should not be unaware that many writers, if they wished to treat their subjects with precision, have frequently found it necessary to declare war on ancient word usage and the customs of the grammarians. I had not intended to say these things in a preface, because I write these things for the learned and the prudent, who would grant pardon even to one who does not beg for it. However, I have noticed the crowd of the unskilled who, cloaked in the shadow of knowledge and tasting our meditations with only the tips of their lips, immediately attack and revile with curses those things which, if read thoughtfully and without malice, they might perhaps honor with worthy and fruitful praise.
